A Lagrangian approach to totally dissipative evolutions in Wasserstein spaces
Giulia Cavagnari, Giuseppe Savaré, Giacomo Enrico Sodini

Abstract
. We introduce and study the class of totally dissipative multivalued probability vector fields (MPVF) 𝐅 {\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}} on the Wasserstein space ( 𝒫 2 ​ ( 𝖷 ) , W 2 ) (\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{X}),W_{2}) of Euclidean or Hilbertian probability measures. We show that such class of MPVFs is in one to one correspondence with law-invariant dissipative operators in a Hilbert space L 2 ​ ( Ω , ℬ , ℙ , 𝖷 ) L^{2}(\Omega,{\mathcal{B}},\mathbb{P};\mathsf{X}) of random variables, preserving a natural maximality property. This allows us to import in the Wasserstein framework many of the powerful tools from the theory of maximal dissipative operators in Hilbert spaces, deriving existence, uniqueness, stability, and approximation results for the flow generated by a maximal totally dissipative MPVF and the equivalence of its Eulerian and Lagrangian characterizations. We will show that demicontinuous single-valued probability vector fields satisfying a metric dissipativity condition as in [ 27 ] are in fact totally dissipative. Starting from a sufficiently rich set of discrete measures, we will also show how to recover a unique maximal totally dissipative version of a MPVF, proving that its flow provides a general mean field characterization of the asymptotic limits of the corresponding family of discrete particle systems. Such an approach also reveals new interesting structural properties for gradient flows of displacement convex functionals with a core of discrete measures dense in energy.



1991 Mathematics Subject Classification
Primary: 34A06, 47B44, 49Q22; Secondary: 34A12, 34A60, 28D05

1.
Introduction

The theory of evolutions of probability measures is experiencing an ever growing interest from the scientific community. On one side, this is justified by its numerous applications in modeling real-life dynamics: social dynamics, crowd dynamics for multi-agent systems, opinion formation, evolution of financial markets just to name a few. We refer the reader to the recent survey
[
48
]
for a more complete overview of the many applications of control theory for multi-agent systems, i.e. large systems of interacting particles/individuals.
On the other side, dealing with mean-field evolutions, expecially in the framework of optimal control theory in Wasserstein spaces
[
31
,
24
,
21
]
, provides interesting insights into mathematical research. We mention for instance the recent contributions
[
7
,
13
,
16
]
for the study of a well-posedness theory for differential inclusions in Wasserstein spaces,

[
5
,
14
,
49
]
for
necessary conditions for optimality in the form of a Pontryagin maximum principle,
the references
[
4
,
8
,
25
,
34
,
35
]
for the study of Hamilton-Jacobi-Bellman equations in this framework. Finally, other contributions devoted to the development of a
viability theory for control problems in the space of probability measures are e.g.
[
6
,
15
,
9
,
26
]
.

In addition to these studies, we have all the applications of the theory of gradient flows in Wasserstein spaces
[
2
]

which are impossible to summarize here even briefly.
In particular, in the case of geodesically convex (resp.
\lambda
-convex) functionals
[
39
]
, the geometric viewpoint and the variational approach introduced by
[
44
,
36
]
have been
extremely powerful to construct a semigroup of contractions (resp. Lipschitz maps)
[
2
]
, which provides a robust background for various applications.

In the present paper, we continue the project, started in
[
27
]
, to extend the theory beyond gradient flows.
Our aim is to investigate the evolution semigroups generated by a
\lambda
-dissipative multivalued probability vector field (in short, MPVF)

{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
in the Wasserstein space

(\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{X}),W_{2})
. The space
\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{X})
denotes the set of Borel probability measures with finite quadratic moment on a separable Hilbert space
\mathsf{X}
.
The geometric notion of dissipativity is
intimately related to the
L^{2}
-Kantorovich-Rubinstein-Wasserstein distance
W_{2}
between two measures
\mu_{0},\mu_{1}\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{X})
, which can be expressed
by the solution of the Optimal Transport problem

(1.1)

W_{2}^{2}(\mu_{0},\mu_{1}):=\min\Big\{\int|x_{0}-x_{1}|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{\mu}(x_{0},x_{1}):\boldsymbol{\mu}\in\Gamma(\mu_{0},\mu_{1})\Big\},

where
\Gamma(\mu_{0},\mu_{1})

denotes the set of couplings
\boldsymbol{\mu}\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{X}\times\mathsf{X})
with
marginals
\mu_{0}
and
\mu_{1}
.
It is well known that the set
\Gamma_{o}(\mu_{0},\mu_{1})
where the
minimum in (
1.1
) is attained
is a nonempty compact and convex subset of
\Gamma(\mu_{0},\mu_{1}).

We refer to
[
27
]
for a detailed discussion of the various approaches to such kind of problems; let us only mention here the Cauchy-Lipschitz approach via vector fields
[
13
,
16
]
, the barycentric approach in
[
47
,
46
,
20
]
and the variational approach to characterize limit solutions of an Explicit Euler Scheme for evolution equations driven by dissipative MPVFs in
[
27
]
.

Let us just recall here the main features of this approach.
A MPVF
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}

can be identified with a subset of
the set of probability measures

\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{T\kern-1.5ptX})
on the space-velocity tangent bundle
\mathsf{T\kern-1.5ptX}=\{(x,v)\in\mathsf{X}\times\mathsf{X}\}
, with proper domain

\mathrm{D}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}):=\{\mathsf{x}_{\sharp}\Phi:\Phi\in{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}\}
and sections
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}[\mu]:=\{\Phi\in{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}:\mathsf{x}_{\sharp}\Phi=\mu\}
,
where
\mathsf{x}(x,v):=x
is the projection on the first coordinate in
\mathsf{T\kern-1.5ptX}
.
Since every element
\Phi\in{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}

has finite quadratic moment in the tangent bundle, the
L^{2}
-norm of the velocity marginal

|\Phi|_{2}^{2}:=\int|v|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\Phi(x,v)\quad\text{is finite.}

The disintegration
\{\Phi_{x}\}_{x\in\mathsf{X}}
of
\Phi\in{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}[\mu]
with respect to
\mu

provides a Borel field of probability measures on the space of velocity vectors, which can be interpreted as
a probabilistic description of the velocity prescribed by
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
at every position/particle
x
, given the distribution
\mu
.
An important case, which is simpler to grasp, occurs when
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
is concentrated on maps
and therefore
\Phi_{x}=\delta_{\boldsymbol{f}(x)}
is a Dirac mass concentrated on the deterministic velocity
\boldsymbol{f}

(in this case we say that
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
is deterministic):
for every measure
\mu\in\mathrm{D}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})

(1.2)

the elements
\Phi\in{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}[\mu]

have the form

(\boldsymbol{i}_{\mathsf{X}},\boldsymbol{f})_{\sharp}\mu

for a vector field
\boldsymbol{f}\in L^{2}(\mathsf{X},\mu;\mathsf{X}),

where
\boldsymbol{i}_{\mathsf{X}}
denotes the identity map on
\mathsf{X}
. In this case,
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
is
dissipative
if for every
\Phi_{i}=(\boldsymbol{i}_{\mathsf{X}},\boldsymbol{f}_{i})_{\sharp}\mu_{i}\in\mathrm{D}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})
,
i=0,1
,

(1.3)

\exists\,\boldsymbol{\mu}\in\Gamma_{o}(\mu_{0},\mu_{1})\quad\text{optimal, such that}\quad\int\langle\boldsymbol{f}_{0}(x_{0})-\boldsymbol{f}_{1}(x_{1}),x_{0}-x_{1}\rangle\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{\mu}(x_{0},x_{1})\leq 0.

Notice however that, even in the deterministic case,
the realization of
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}[\mu]
as an element/subset
of
\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{T\kern-1.5ptX})
is crucial to deal with varying base measures
\mu
,
since for different
\mu_{0},\mu_{1}\in\mathrm{D}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})

the representation (
1.2
)
yields corresponding
maps
\boldsymbol{f}_{0},\boldsymbol{f}_{1}
which belong to different
L^{2}
spaces
and therefore are not easy to be compared.

When
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
is not concentrated on maps, the dissipativity condition
between two elements

\Phi_{0}\in{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}[\mu_{0}],\Phi_{1}\in{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}[\mu_{1}]

guarantees the existence
of a coupling
\boldsymbol{\vartheta}\in\Gamma(\Phi_{0},\Phi_{1})\subset\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{T\kern-1.5ptX}\times\mathsf{T\kern-1.5ptX})

such that
the “space” marginal projection

(\mathsf{x}_{0},\mathsf{x}_{1})_{\sharp}\boldsymbol{\vartheta}
is optimal,
thus belongs to
\Gamma_{o}(\mu_{0},\mu_{1})
, and
moreover

(1.4)

\int\langle v_{1}-v_{0},x_{1}-x_{0}\rangle\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{\vartheta}(x_{0},v_{0};x_{1},v_{1})\leq 0.

Such a property appears as a natural generalization of
the corresponding
condition introduced in
[
2
]

for the Wasserstein subdifferentials of geodesically convex functionals.

The geometric interpretation of this condition becomes apparent by considering its equivalent characterization
in terms of the first order expansion
of the squared Wasserstein distance:
in the case (
1.2
)
it can be written as

W_{2}^{2}((\boldsymbol{i}_{\mathsf{X}}+h\boldsymbol{f}_{0})_{\sharp}\mu_{0},(\boldsymbol{i}_{\mathsf{X}}+h\boldsymbol{f}_{1})_{\sharp}\mu_{1})\leq W_{2}^{2}(\mu_{0},\mu_{1})+o(h)\quad\text{as }h\downarrow 0.

In principle, one may interpret the flow generated by
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
in terms of
absolutely continuous (w.r.t. the Wasserstein metric)
curves
\mu:[0,+\infty)\to\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{X})
,
\mu(t)\equiv\mu_{t}
, in
\mathrm{D}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})
solving the continuity equation

\partial_{t}\mu_{t}+\nabla\cdot(\mu_{t}\,\boldsymbol{f}_{t})=0\quad\text{in }(0,\infty)\times\mathsf{X},\quad(\boldsymbol{i}_{\mathsf{X}},\boldsymbol{f}_{t})_{\sharp}\mu_{t}\in{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}},

and obeying a Cauchy condition
\mu\lower 3.0pt\hbox{$|_{t=0}$}=\mu_{0}.

However, the derivation of such a precise formulation is not a simple task and, in general, it requires more
restrictive assumptions on
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}

as

(1.5)

\begin{gathered}\mathrm{D}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})=\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{X}),\quad{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}[\mu]=(\boldsymbol{i}_{\mathsf{X}},\boldsymbol{f}[\mu])_{\sharp}\mu\quad\text{(thus ${\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}$ is single-valued),}\\
\mu_{n}\to\mu\quad\Longrightarrow\quad(\boldsymbol{i}_{\mathsf{X}},\boldsymbol{f}[\mu_{n}])_{\sharp}\mu_{n}\to(\boldsymbol{i}_{\mathsf{X}},\boldsymbol{f}[\mu])_{\sharp}\mu.\end{gathered}

We introduced in
[
27
]

the more flexible condition of EVI solutions, borrowed from the theory of gradient flows
[
2
]
and from the Bénilan notion of integral solutions to dissipative evolutions in Hilbert/Banach spaces
[
12
]
:
a continuous curve
\mu:[0,+\infty)\to\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{X})

with values in
\overline{\mathrm{D}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})}
is an EVI solution if it solves the system of Evolution Variational Inequalities

(1.6)

\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\frac{1}{2}W^{2}_{2}(\mu_{t},\nu)\leq-[\Phi,\mu_{t}]_{r}\quad\text{for every }\Phi\in{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}[\nu],\,\nu\in\mathrm{D}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})\quad\text{in }\mathscr{D}^{\prime}((0,+\infty)),

where for every

\Phi=(\boldsymbol{i}_{\mathsf{X}},\boldsymbol{f})_{\sharp}\nu\in{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
the duality pairing
[\Phi,\mu]_{r}
is defined by

[\Phi,\mu]_{r}:=\min\Big\{\int\langle\boldsymbol{f}(x_{0}),x_{0}-x_{1}\rangle\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{\mu}(x_{0},x_{1}):\boldsymbol{\mu}\in\Gamma_{o}(\nu,\mu)\Big\}.

In
[
27
]
, we studied the properties of the flow in
\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{X})
generated by
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
by means of the
explicit Euler method

and we proved that, under suitable conditions, every family of discrete approximations obtained by the explicit Euler method
converges to an EVI solution
when the step size vanishes, also providing an optimal error estimate.

The use of the explicit Euler method is simple to implement and quite powerful when the domain of
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
coincides with the whole
\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{X})

and
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
is locally bounded

[
27
, Cor. 5.23]
, i.e.

|\Phi|_{2}
remains uniformly bounded
in a suitable neighborhood of every measure

\mu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{X})
(but much more general conditions are thoroughly discussed in
[
27
]
).
Dealing with constrained evolutions or with operators which are not locally bounded requires a better understanding of the implicit Euler method.

Maximal totally dissipative
MPVF
s

One of the starting points of the present investigation (see Sections
3.3
and

8
) is the nontrivial fact that
a large class of
\lambda
-dissipative MPVFs
including the demicontinuous fields (
1.5
) satisfies a much stronger dissipativity condition, which we call
total
\lambda
-dissipativity
:
in the simplest case
\lambda=0
when (
1.2
) holds and
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
is single-valued, such a property reads as

(1.7)

\int\langle\boldsymbol{f}[\mu_{0}](x_{0})-\boldsymbol{f}[\mu_{1}](x_{1}),x_{0}-x_{1}\rangle\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{\mu}(x_{0},x_{1})\leq 0\quad\text{for \emph{every} $\boldsymbol{\mu}\in\Gamma(\mu_{0},\mu_{1})$}

and can be compared with
the notion of L-monotonicity of
[
23
, Definition 3.31]
. Total dissipativity thus holds along
arbitrary couplings between pairs of measures
\mu_{0},\mu_{1}
in the domain of
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
,
whereas the metric dissipativity condition
(
1.3
)
involves only optimal couplings.
The relaxed version of (
1.7
) allowing for
\lambda
-dissipativity
includes the class of Lipschitz probability vector fields
\boldsymbol{f}
satisfying

\Big|\boldsymbol{f}[\mu_{0}](x_{0})-\boldsymbol{f}[\mu_{1}](x_{1})\Big|\leq L\Big(|x_{0}-x_{1}|+W_{2}(\mu_{0},\mu_{1})\Big)\quad\text{for every }x_{i}\in\mathsf{X},\ \mu_{i}\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{X})

for
\lambda=2L
(see Example
3.11
).

Motivated by this remarkable property, it is natural to extend
the notion of total dissipativity
to a
general MPVF
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
.
Here there are two possible
approaches: the weakest one, modeled
on the general definition of metric dissipativity (
1.4
),
would require that
for every
\Phi_{0}\in{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}[\mu_{0}],\Phi_{1}\in{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}[\mu_{1}]
and
every

coupling
\boldsymbol{\mu}\in\Gamma(\mu_{0},\mu_{1})

(
\boldsymbol{\mu}
is not optimal)
there exists
\boldsymbol{\vartheta}\in\Gamma(\Phi_{0},\Phi_{1})

such that

(\mathsf{x}_{0},\mathsf{x}_{1})_{\sharp}\boldsymbol{\vartheta}=\boldsymbol{\mu}
and
(
1.4
)
holds.

The strongest condition, which we will systematically investigate in this paper,
requires that

(1.8)

\text{for every $\Phi_{0},\Phi_{1}\in{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}$
and every $\boldsymbol{\vartheta}\in\Gamma(\Phi_{0},\Phi_{1})$}\quad\int\langle v_{1}-v_{0},x_{1}-x_{0}\rangle\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{\vartheta}(x_{0},v_{0};x_{1},v_{1})\leq 0.

It is
clear that
total dissipativity for arbitrary MPVFs
is much stronger than
the metric dissipativity condition (
1.4
).
We address two main questions:

\langle
Q.1
\rangle

What are the structural properties of totally dissipative MPVFs satisfying the stronger condition (
1.8
)
and their relation with Lagrangian representations by dissipative
operators in the Hilbert space

\mathcal{X}:=L^{2}(\Omega,{\mathcal{B}},\mathbb{P};\mathsf{X}),

where
\mathbb{P}
is a nonatomic probability measure
on a standard Borel
space
(\Omega,{\mathcal{B}})
,
which provides the domain of the parametrization. A similar lifting approach has been used also in e.g.
[
38
,
22
,
33
,
23
,
34
,
35
]
, in particular
for functions defined in
\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{X})
and
their Fréchet differential.
This is the content of
Part
and in particular of Section
3
and
4
, with applications to the case of gradient flows in Section

5
.

\langle
Q.2
\rangle

Under which conditions
a dissipative MPVF is totally dissipative and, more generally,
it is possible to recover a unique
maximal totally dissipative “version” of the initial MPVF starting from
a sufficiently rich set of discrete measures.
This is investigated first in Section

3.3
and then more extensively in
Part
, in particular in Section
8
,
starting from the results of Sections
and
7

on the geometry of discrete measures.

Lagrangian representations

Concerning the first question

⟨

Q.1

⟩

, in Section
3.2

we will show that

there is a one-to-one correspondence between totally dissipative
MPVF
s
and law invariant
dissipative operators in the Hilbert space

\mathcal{X}:=L^{2}(\Omega,{\mathcal{B}},\mathbb{P};\mathsf{X})
;
such a correspondence preserves maximality.

This representation is very useful to import in the metric space

(\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{X}),W_{2})

all the powerful tools and results
concerning semigroups of contractions generated by maximal dissipative operators in Hilbert spaces, see e.g.
[
17
]
.
This approach overcomes most of the technical limits of the explicit Euler method adopted in
[
27
]
and allows for a more general theory of existence, well posedness, and stability of solutions.
In particular, even if the results are new and relevant
also in the finite dimensional Euclidean case, the theory does not rely on any compactness argument and thus can be fully developed in a
infinite dimensional separable Hilbert space
\mathsf{X}
.
We can in fact lift
a totally dissipative MPVF
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
to a dissipative operator
{\boldsymbol{B}}\subset\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{X}
, that we call
Lagrangian representation of
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
, defined by

(X,V)\in{\boldsymbol{B}}\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad(X,V)_{\sharp}\mathbb{P}\in{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}.

It turns out that
{\boldsymbol{B}}
is law invariant
(i.e. if
(X,V)\in{\boldsymbol{B}}
and
(X^{\prime},V^{\prime})
has the same law as
(X,V)
, then
(X^{\prime},V^{\prime})\in{\boldsymbol{B}}
as well) and admits a maximal dissipative extension
\hat{\boldsymbol{B}}
which is law invariant
and corresponds to a maximal extension
of
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
still preserving total dissipativity.
In particular,
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
is maximal in the class of totally dissipative MPVFs if and only if
{\boldsymbol{B}}
is a law invariant operator which is maximal dissipative.

Such a crucial result depends on two important properties:
first of all, if the graph of
{\boldsymbol{B}}
is strongly-weakly closed in
\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{X}
(in particular if
{\boldsymbol{B}}
is maximal)
then law invariance can also be characterized by invariance w.r.t. measure-preserving isomorphisms of
\Omega
, i.e. essentially injective maps
g:\Omega\to\Omega
such that

g_{\sharp}\mathbb{P}=\mathbb{P}=g^{-1}_{\sharp}\mathbb{P}
(Theorem
3.4
). The second property
(Theorem
3.12
)
guarantees that every dissipative operator in
\mathcal{X}
which
is invariant by measure-preserving isomorphisms has a maximal dissipative extension enjoying the same invariance (and thus also law invariance). Such a result has been obtained in

[
28
]
and exploits remarkable results of
[
10
,
11
]
providing an explicit construction of a maximal extension of a monotone operator.

The equivalence between law-invariance and invariance by measure-preserving tranformations also plays a crucial role to prove that the resolvents of
{\boldsymbol{B}}
, its Yosida approximations, and the generated semigroup of contractions
(\boldsymbol{S}_{t})_{t\geq 0}
in
\mathcal{X}

are still law invariant.
The family

(\boldsymbol{S}_{t})_{t}
thus induces
a projected semigroup of contractions in
\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{X})
defined by

(1.9)

S_{t}(\mu_{0}):=(\boldsymbol{S}_{t}X_{0})_{\sharp}\mathbb{P}\quad\text{whenever}\quad(X_{0})_{\sharp}\mathbb{P}=\mu_{0}\in\mathrm{D}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}),

which is independent of the choice of
X_{0}

parametrizing the initial law
\mu_{0}
,
which satisfies the EVI formulation (
1.6
) and the stability property (here for arbitrary
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
)

(1.10)

|\boldsymbol{S}_{t}X_{0}-\boldsymbol{S}_{t}Y_{0}|_{\mathcal{X}}\leq\mathrm{e}^{\lambda t}|X_{0}-Y_{0}|_{\mathcal{X}},\quad W_{2}(S_{t}(\mu_{0}),S_{t}(\nu_{0}))\leq\mathrm{e}^{\lambda t}W_{2}(\mu_{0},\nu_{0}).

Another crucial property of totally dissipative MPVFs concerns the
barycentric projection
, which can be obtained by taking the expected value of the disintegration

\{\Phi_{x}\}_{x\in\mathsf{X}}
of an element
\Phi\in{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
with respect to its first marginal
\mu=\mathsf{x}_{\sharp}\Phi
:

\boldsymbol{b}_{\Phi}(x):=\int v\,\mathrm{d}\Phi_{x}(v)\quad\text{for $\mu$-a.e.\penalty\ $x\in\mathsf{X}$;}\quad\boldsymbol{b}_{\Phi}\in L^{2}(\mathsf{X},\mu;\mathsf{X}).

The barycenter
\boldsymbol{b}_{\Phi}
also represents the conditional expectation

\mathbb{E}[V|X]

of
V
given (the
\sigma
-algebra generated by)
X
, for every

(X,V)\in{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
with
(X,V)_{\sharp}\mathbb{P}=\Phi
:

\mathbb{E}[V|X]=\boldsymbol{b}_{\Phi}\circ X\quad\text{in $L^{2}(\Omega,\sigma(X),\mathbb{P};\mathsf{X})$.}

It turns out that, if

{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
is maximal totally dissipative
(or, equivalently, its Lagrangian representation
{\boldsymbol{B}}
is maximal dissipative), then
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
is invariant
with respect to the barycentric projection:

(1.11)

(X,V)_{\sharp}\mathbb{P}=\Phi\in{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}\quad\Longrightarrow\quad(\boldsymbol{i}_{\mathsf{X}},\boldsymbol{b}_{\Phi})_{\sharp}\mu\in{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}},\quad(X,\mathbb{E}[V|X])\in{\boldsymbol{B}}.

Thanks to (
1.11
), for every
\mu_{0}\in\mathrm{D}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})
, the solution
\mu_{t}
expressed by the Lagrangian formula (
1.9
) can be
characterized as a Lipschitz curve in
\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{X})

satisfying the continuity equation

(1.12)

\partial_{t}\mu_{t}+\nabla\cdot(\mu_{t}\,\boldsymbol{v}_{t})=0\quad\text{in $(0,+\infty)\times\mathsf{X}$}

for a Borel vector field
\boldsymbol{v}

satisfying

(1.13)

t\mapsto\int|\boldsymbol{v}_{t}(x)|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\mu_{t}(x)\quad\text{is locally integrable in $[0,+\infty)$},\quad(\boldsymbol{i}_{\mathsf{X}},\boldsymbol{v}_{t})_{\sharp}\mu_{t}\in{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}\ \text{for a.e.\penalty\ $t>0$.}

We may also equivalently write
(
1.12
) in a weaker form
assuming that
there exists a Borel family
\Phi_{t}
,
t>0
, such that

\displaystyle\Phi_{t}\in{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}[\mu_{t}]\quad\text{for a.e.\penalty\ $t>0$,}\quad t\mapsto\int|v|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\Phi_{t}\quad\text{is locally integrable in $[0,+\infty)$,}

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\int\zeta\,\mathrm{d}\mu_{t}=\int\langle v,\nabla\zeta(x)\rangle\,\mathrm{d}\Phi_{t}(x,v)\quad\text{for every $\zeta\in\Cyl(\mathsf{X})$ and a.e.\penalty\ $t>0$.}

A more precise formulation of (
1.12
) and
(
1.13
)
can be obtained by introducing the minimal selection
{\boldsymbol{B}}^{\circ}
(i.e. the element of minimal norm) of
{\boldsymbol{B}}
:
we will prove that
for every
X\in\mathrm{D}({\boldsymbol{B}})
with

X_{\sharp}\mathbb{P}=\mu
,

{\boldsymbol{B}}^{\circ}
is associated with
a vector field
\boldsymbol{f}^{\circ}\in L^{2}(\mathsf{X},\mu;\mathsf{X})
through the formula

(1.14)

V={\boldsymbol{B}}^{\circ}X,\quad X_{\sharp}\mathbb{P}=\mu\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad V=\boldsymbol{f}^{\circ}[\mu](X).

The measure
(\boldsymbol{i}_{\mathsf{X}},\boldsymbol{f}^{\circ}[\mu])_{\sharp}\mu
can be characterized as
the unique element
\Phi\in{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}[\mu]
minimizing
|\Phi|_{2}

and the solution
\mu:[0,+\infty)\to\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{X})
provided by (
1.9
)
is also the unique Lipschitz curve satisfying the continuity equation

(1.15)

\partial_{t}\mu_{t}+\nabla\cdot(\mu_{t}\,\boldsymbol{f}^{\circ}[\mu_{t}])=0\quad\text{in $(0,+\infty)\times\mathsf{X}$}

with initial datum
\mu_{0}\in\mathrm{D}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})
.
It is remarkable that
a maximal totally dissipative MPVF always admits a minimal selection which is concentrated on a map.

It turns out that the evolution driven by
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
preserves the class of discrete measures with finite
support; if moreover
\mu_{0}=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{n=1}^{N}\delta_{x_{n}}\in\mathrm{D}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})

(or also in
\overline{\mathrm{D}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})}
if
\mathsf{X}
has finite dimension) then
the unique solution of (
1.15
) can be expressed
in the form
\mu_{t}=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{n=1}^{N}\delta_{\mathsf{x}_{n}(t)}

where
t\mapsto\mathsf{x}_{n}(t)
are locally Lipschitz curves satisfying the system of ODEs

(1.16)

\dot{\mathsf{x}}_{n}(t)=\boldsymbol{f}^{\circ}[\mu_{t}](\mathsf{x}_{n}(t))\quad\text{a.e.\penalty\ in $(0,+\infty)$},\quad\mathsf{x}_{n}(0)=x_{n},\quad n=1,\cdots,N.

Thanks to (
1.10
), if a sequence of
discrete initial measures
\mu_{0}^{N}=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{n=1}^{N}\delta_{x_{n}^{N}}

converges to a limit
\mu_{0}
in
\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{X})
as
N\to\infty
, then
the corresponding evolving measures
\mu^{N}_{t}
obtained by solving
(
1.16
) starting from
\mu_{0}^{N}
will converge to
\mu_{t}=S_{t}(\mu_{0})
. As a general fact
[
51
]
, this correspond to the propagation of chaos
for the sequence of symmetric particle systems (
1.16
).

Maximality also shows that EVI curves are unique; when they are differentiable
(in particular when
\mu_{0}\in\mathrm{D}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})
)
we can recover the representation
(
1.15
) and the Lagrangian
representation (
1.9
),
in an even more refined version involving characteristic curves.
This representation immediately yields regularity, stability, perturbation, and approximation results thanks to the corresponding statements in the Hilbertian framework.

Among the possible applications, we just recall that we can also use
the Implicit Euler Method

(corresponding to the JKO scheme for gradient flows)
to construct the flow (Corollary
4.7
).
Starting from
M_{\tau}^{0}:=\mu_{0}\in D({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})
, for every step size
\tau>0
we
can find a (unique) sequence
(M^{n}_{\tau})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}

in
D({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})
which at each step
n\in\mathbb{N}
solves

(1.17)

(\mathsf{x}-\tau\mathsf{v})_{\sharp}\Phi^{n+1}_{\tau}=M^{n}_{\tau}\quad\text{for some }\Phi^{n+1}_{\tau}\in{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}[M^{n+1}_{\tau}].

Selecting
\tau:=t/N
, the sequence
N\mapsto M^{N}_{t/N}
converges to
S_{t}(\mu_{0})
as
N\to\infty

with the a-priori error estimate

(1.18)

W_{2}(S_{t}(\mu_{0}),M^{N}_{t/N})\leq\frac{2t}{\sqrt{N}}\|\boldsymbol{f}^{\circ}[\mu_{0}]\|_{L^{2}(\mathsf{X},\mu_{0};\mathsf{X})}.

When
\mathrm{D}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})=\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{X})
and

{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
is single-valued as in (
1.5
),
it follows that maximality is equivalent to
the following demicontinuity condition:
for every sequence
(\mu_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
converging to
\mu
in

\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{X})
one has

(1.19)

\sup_{n\to\infty}\int|\boldsymbol{f}[\mu_{n}]|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\mu_{n}<\infty,\quad(\boldsymbol{i}_{\mathsf{X}},\boldsymbol{f}[\mu_{n}])_{\sharp}\mu_{n}\to(\boldsymbol{i}_{\mathsf{X}},\boldsymbol{f}[\mu])_{\sharp}\mu\quad\text{in }\mathcal{P}(\mathsf{X}\times\mathsf{X}^{w}),

where
\mathsf{X}^{w}
denotes the Hilbert space endowed with its weak topology.
Clearly, in this case
the map
\boldsymbol{f}
representing
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
coincides with
\boldsymbol{f}^{\circ}
.
Notice that (
1.19
) surely holds
if
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
is represented by a map
\boldsymbol{f}:\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{X})\to\mathrm{Lip}(\mathsf{X};\mathsf{X})
(see also the map
F^{\prime}
in
[
29
, Section 2.3]
) satisfying
the integrated Lipschitz-like condition along arbitrary couplings

(1.20)

\int\Big|\boldsymbol{f}[\mu_{0}](x_{0})-\boldsymbol{f}[\mu_{1}](x_{1})\Big|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{\mu}(x_{0},x_{1})\leq L^{2}\int|x_{0}-x_{1}|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{\mu}(x_{0},x_{1})\quad\text{for every }\boldsymbol{\mu}\in\Gamma(\mu_{0},\mu_{1}).

On the other hand,
this class of regular
dissipative PVFs
is sufficiently rich to approximate the minimal selection of any
maximal totally dissipative MPVF
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
: in fact, by using the Yosida approximation,
it is possible to find a sequence of regular PVFs
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}_{n}
associated to
Lipschitz fields
\boldsymbol{f}_{n}
according to (
1.20
)
(w.r.t. a possibly diverging sequence of Lipschitz constant
L_{n}
)
satisfying the dissipativity condition (
1.7
) and

\lim_{n\to\infty}\int\big|\boldsymbol{f}_{n}[\mu](x)-\boldsymbol{f}^{\circ}[\mu](x)\big|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\mu(x)=0\quad\text{for every }\mu\in D({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}).

So, the class of totally dissipative MPVFs arises as a natural closure
of more regular PVFs concentrated on dissipative Lipschitz maps.
This statement (Corollary
3.25
)
justifies a posteriori
the choice of the
strongest notion of
total dissipativity
given in (
1.8
).

We cannot develop here all the applications of the abstract Hilbertian theory (which we aim to
study in a future review paper) in the measure-theoretic setting:
an incomplete list contains:

•

regularizing effects
under suitable assumptions on
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
,

•

asymptotic behaviour,

•

error estimates
for the Yosida regularization and
for
time discretizations
(see also
[
27
]
),
Chernoff and Trotter formulas,

•

stability
and convergence of sequences
of
\lambda
-contractive semigroups,

•

discrete to continuous
limit and chaos propagation,

•

the case of time dependent
MPVFs,

•

applications to
the stochastic gradient descent method.

We will however devote a particular effort to clarify
some relations
between
totally dissipative
and metrically dissipative MPVFs, showing
that they are intimately connected with the possibility to construct
a MPVF starting from discrete measures.

Construction of
a maximal totally dissipative
MPVF
from a discrete core.

We investigate the second
issue

⟨

Q.2

⟩

in Section
8
,
i.e. how to recover a (unique) maximal totally dissipative “version” of
a (totally or metrically)

\lambda
-dissipative MPVF
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
defined on
a sufficiently
rich
core

\mathrm{C}
of discrete measures.
This corresponds to the derivation of a mean-field description
from a compatible family of discrete particle systems.

Just to give an idea of a simple case of core,
we consider a totally convex subset
\mathrm{D}
of
the set
\mathcal{P}_{f}(\mathsf{X})
of discrete measures with finite support:
total convexity here means that,
whenever the marginals

\mathsf{x}^{i}_{\sharp}\boldsymbol{\mu}
,
i=0,1
,
of
\boldsymbol{\mu}\in\mathcal{P}_{f}(\mathsf{X}\times\mathsf{X})

belong to
\mathrm{D}
,
then also
((1-t)\mathsf{x}^{0}+t\mathsf{x}^{1})_{\sharp}\boldsymbol{\mu}

belong to
\mathrm{D}
for every
t\in(0,1).

For every

N\in\mathbb{N}

we consider
the
collection
\mathrm{C}_{N}

of uniform discrete measures

\mu_{\boldsymbol{x}}=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{n=1}^{N}\delta_{x_{n}}

belonging to
\mathrm{D}
,
where
{\boldsymbol{x}}=(x_{1},\cdots,x_{N})
is a vector
in
\mathsf{X}^{N}
with distinct coordinates.
The set
\mathrm{C}_{N}
corresponds to
a subset
\mathsf{C}_{N}
of
\mathsf{X}^{N}

which is invariant under the action of
the group of permutations
{\mathrm{Sym}(N)}
of the components,

\sigma{\boldsymbol{x}}:=(x_{\sigma(1)},\cdots,x_{\sigma(N)}),\quad\text{for every}\quad\sigma\in{\mathrm{Sym}(N)},\ {\boldsymbol{x}}=(x_{1},\cdots,x_{N})\in\mathsf{X}^{N}.

We will suppose that
\mathsf{C}_{N}
is relatively
open in
\mathsf{X}^{N}

for every
N\in\mathbb{N}.

Examples of
\mathrm{D}
are provided by the collection of all
the discrete measures
\mu
such that
\supp(\mu)

is contained in a given convex open subset
\mathsf{U}

of
\mathsf{X}
. Another interesting case, assuming
0\in\mathsf{U}
,
is given by all
the discrete measures such that

\supp(\mu)-\supp(\mu)\subset\mathsf{U}.
The case of the whole
set
\mathcal{P}_{f}(\mathsf{X})
is
still interesting.

Suppose that we
have a deterministic single-valued PVF
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}

defined in
\mathrm{C}=\bigcup_{N}{\mathrm{C}_{N}}
(when
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
is not deterministic, the construction is more subtle).
We can then represent
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
on each
\mathrm{C}_{N}

by a vector field
\boldsymbol{f}^{N}:\mathsf{C}_{N}\to\mathsf{X}^{N}

satisfying
the invariance property

\boldsymbol{f}^{N}(\sigma{\boldsymbol{x}})=\sigma\boldsymbol{f}^{N}({\boldsymbol{x}})
, so that

{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}[\mu_{\boldsymbol{x}}]=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{n=1}^{N}\delta_{(x_{n},\boldsymbol{f}^{N}_{n}({\boldsymbol{x}}))}\quad\text{for every }{\boldsymbol{x}}\in\mathsf{C}_{N},

and, at least for a short time,
the evolution of discrete measures in
\mathrm{C}_{N}

can be described by

\mu_{t}=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{n=1}^{N}\delta_{x_{n}(t)}=\mu_{{\boldsymbol{x}}(t)}

where the vector
{\boldsymbol{x}}(t)=(x_{1}(t),\cdots x_{N}(t))\in\mathsf{C}_{N}

solves the system

(1.21)

\dot{\boldsymbol{x}}(t)=\boldsymbol{f}^{N}({\boldsymbol{x}}(t)).

We assume the following

\lambda
-dissipativity conditions on the maps
\boldsymbol{f}^{N}
:
for every pair of integers

M,N\in\mathbb{N}
with
M\mid N
,
if
{\boldsymbol{x}}\in\mathsf{C}_{M}
,
{\boldsymbol{y}}\in\mathsf{C}_{N}

and
\theta
is an optimal correspondence from

\{1,\cdots,N\}
to
\{1,\cdots,M\}

such that

\mu_{\boldsymbol{x}}=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{n=1}^{N}\delta_{x_{\theta(n)}}\quad\text{and}\quad\frac{1}{N}\sum_{n=1}^{N}|y_{n}-x_{\theta(n)}|^{2}=W_{2}^{2}(\mu_{\boldsymbol{x}},\nu_{\boldsymbol{y}}),

then

\sum_{n=1}^{N}\langle\boldsymbol{f}^{N}_{n}({\boldsymbol{y}})-\boldsymbol{f}^{M}_{\theta(n)}({\boldsymbol{x}}),y_{n}-x_{\theta(n)}\rangle\leq\lambda\sum_{n=1}^{N}|y_{n}-x_{\theta(n)}|^{2}.

We will show that

{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
is in fact totally
\lambda
-dissipative
and admits a unique maximal extension
\hat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
,
whose flow
can be interpreted as the unique mean-field limit
of the particle systems driven by (
1.21
).
This fact guarantees two interesting properties:
the local in time evolution corresponding to
(
1.21
) admits a unique
global extension which induces a semigroup
(S^{N}_{t})_{t\geq 0}

on
\overline{\mathrm{C}_{N}}

which corresponds to the restriction to
\overline{\mathrm{C}_{N}}

of the semigroup
S_{t}
generated by
\hat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}

(and characterized e.g. by the continuity equation
(
1.15
) and by (
1.16
)).
Moreover, thanks to (
1.10
)
for every
\mu_{0}\in\overline{\mathrm{C}}
and every sequence
(\mu^{N}_{0})_{N\in\mathbb{N}}

with
\mu^{N}_{0}\in\mathrm{C}_{N}
and converging to
\mu_{0}
as
N\to\infty

we have
S^{N}_{t}(\mu^{N}_{0})\to S_{t}(\mu_{0})
in
\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{X})

locally uniformly w.r.t.
t\in[0,+\infty)
.

Thanks to the stability properties of
the Lagrangian flow, Theorem
4.9
also shows that
the trajectories of the discrete particle system
uniformly converge
in a measure-theoretic sense to the characteristics of
the mean-field system.

As a byproduct, we obtain that when
the domain of a totally dissipative
MPVF
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
contains a dense core then
its maximal extension is unique and can be characterized by a suitable
explicit construction starting from the core itself
and its flow has a natural mean-field interpretation.

Our result also provides interesting applications to geodesically
convex functionals and their approximations (see Sections

5
,
9
).

First of all, if the proper domain of a lower semicontinuous
and geodesically convex functional
\phi:\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{X})\to(-\infty,+\infty]

contains a discrete core
\mathrm{C}
which is dense in energy,
then
\phi
is totally convex, i.e. it is convex along all the
linear interpolations induced by arbitrary couplings.
An important class is provided by
continuous and everywhere defined geodesically convex functionals,
which thus turn out to be totally convex.

The same property holds for any functional
\phi:\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{X})\to(-\infty,+\infty]
which arises as Mosco-like limit of a
sequence of continuous and geodesically convex functionals which are everywhere finite. In particular, such approximation is impossible for all the functionals
which are not totally convex, as the relative entropy functionals
w.r.t. log-concave measures.

The plan of the paper is as follows.

Part
develops the theory of totally dissipative MPVFs. After a quick review in
Section
of the main tools on Wasserstein spaces used in the sequel, we summarize in Subsection
2.2
the notation and the results concerning Multivalued Probability Vector Fields and EVI solutions.

In
Section
3
, we introduce the notion of
totally dissipative
MPVF and we study its consequences in terms of existence and description of Lagrangian solutions: in Subsection
3.1
we study the properties of the Yosida approximations, the resolvent operator and the minimal selection of law-invariant operators in the Hilbert space
\mathcal{X}
of parametrizations, Subsection
3.2
deals with the relation between dissipativity for such law-invariant subsets of
\mathcal{X}
and the corresponding total dissipativity for their law.
These results are used in Subsection
3.3
to study the particular case of deterministic totally dissipative PVFs.

Section
4
contains the main
existence, uniqueness, stability, and approximation results for
the Lagrangian flow generated by a totally dissipative MPVF, together with its various equivalent characterizations.

In
Section
5
, we study the behaviour of functionals
\phi:\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{X})\to(-\infty,+\infty]
which are convex along any coupling, proving the existence of gradient flows (equivalently, EVI solutions for the MPVF given by their subdifferential) still exploiting their representation in terms of a convex functional
\psi
defined in the parametrization space
\mathcal{X}
.

Part
studies the
characterization of maximal extensions of total dissipative MPVF and their relation with metric dissipativity.

Section
is devoted to study the properties of couplings between discrete measures, in particular showing that such couplings are “piece-wise” optimal.
This property is then exploited in
Section
7
where we show that a dissipative MPVF is totally dissipative along discrete couplings.

In
Section
8
we show that starting from a dissipative MPVF
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}

definied on a sufficiently rich
core

\mathrm{C}
of discrete measures, it is possible to construct a maximal totally dissipative MPVF
\hat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
, in a unique canonical way.

Section
9
is in the same spirit but in the case of a geodesically convex functional
\phi
: under analogous approximation properties, it is possible to show that
\phi
is actually totally convex and then satisfies the assumptions of Section
5
.

Finally,
Appendix
A
contains many useful results related to
\lambda
-dissipative operators in Hilbert spaces that are more commonly known for
\lambda=0
(the main reference is
[
17
]
), while
Appendix
B
lists some of the results of
[
28
]
related to Borel partitions and approximations of couplings that are used in the present work.

2.1.
Wasserstein distance in Hilbert spaces and strong-weak topology

From now on, we denote by
\mathsf{X}
a separable (possibly infinite dimensional) Hilbert space with
norm
|\cdot|
and scalar product
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle
. When it is necessary to specify it, we denote by
\mathsf{X}^{s}
(resp.
\mathsf{X}^{w}
) the Hilbert space
\mathsf{X}
endowed with its strong (resp. weak) topology. We remark that
\mathsf{X}^{w}
is
a Lusin completely regular space and that
\mathsf{X}^{s}
and
\mathsf{X}^{w}

share the same class of Borel sets
and thus of Borel probability measures. Therefore, we are allowed to adopt the simpler notation
\mathcal{P}(\mathsf{X})
and to use the heavier
\mathcal{P}(\mathsf{X}^{s})
and
\mathcal{P}(\mathsf{X}^{w})

only when we will refer to the correspondent topology.

We adopt the notation

\mathsf{T\kern-1.5ptX}
for the tangent bundle to
\mathsf{X}
, which is identified with the cartesian product
\mathsf{X}\times\mathsf{X}

with the induced norm
|(x,v)|:=\big(|x|^{2}+|v|^{2}\big)^{1/2}
and the
strong-weak topology of
\mathsf{X}^{s}\times\mathsf{X}^{w}
(i.e. the product of the strong topology on the
first component and the weak topology on the second one). The set
\mathcal{P}(\mathsf{T\kern-1.5ptX})
is defined thanks to the identification of

\mathsf{T\kern-1.5ptX}
with
\mathsf{X}\times\mathsf{X}
and it is endowed with
the narrow topology induced by the strong-weak topology in
\mathsf{T\kern-1.5ptX}
.

We will denote by
\mathsf{x},\mathsf{v}:\mathsf{T\kern-1.5ptX}\to\mathsf{X}
the projection maps defined by

(2.2)

\mathsf{x}(x,v):=x,\quad\mathsf{v}(x,v)=v.

When dealing with the product space
\mathsf{X}^{2}

we use the notation

(2.3)

\displaystyle\mathsf{s}

\displaystyle:\mathsf{X}^{2}\to\mathsf{X}^{2},\quad

\displaystyle\mathsf{s}(x_{0},x_{1}):=(x_{1},x_{0}),

(2.4)

\displaystyle\mathsf{x}^{t}

\displaystyle:\mathsf{X}^{2}\to\mathsf{X},\quad

\displaystyle\mathsf{x}^{t}(x_{0},x_{1}):=(1-t)x_{0}+tx_{1},\quad

\displaystyle t\in[0,1].

Definition 2.3
.

Given
\mu\in\mathcal{P}(\mathsf{X})
and
\Phi\in\mathcal{P}(\mathsf{T\kern-1.5ptX})

we define

(2.5)

\mathsf{m}_{2}^{2}(\mu):=\int_{\mathsf{X}}|x|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\mu(x),\quad|\Phi|_{2}^{2}:=\int_{\mathsf{T\kern-1.5ptX}}|v|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\Phi(x,v)

and the spaces

(2.6)

\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{X}):=\{\mu\in\mathcal{P}(\mathsf{X})\mid\mathsf{m}_{2}(\mu)<+\infty\},\quad\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{T\kern-1.5ptX}|\mu):=\Big\{\Psi\in\mathcal{P}(\mathsf{T\kern-1.5ptX}):\mathsf{x}_{\sharp}\Psi=\mu,\,|\Psi|_{2}<\infty\Big\}.

Given
\Psi\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{T\kern-1.5ptX}|\mu)
, the
barycenter of
\Psi
is the
function

\boldsymbol{b}_{\Psi}\in L^{2}(\mathsf{X},\mu;\mathsf{X})
defined by

(2.7)

\boldsymbol{b}_{\Psi}(x):=\int_{\mathsf{X}}v\,\mathrm{d}\Psi_{x}(v)\quad\text{for }\mu\text{-a.e. }x\in\mathsf{X},

where
\{\Psi_{x}\}_{x\in\mathsf{X}}\subset\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{X})
is the disintegration
of
\Psi
w.r.t.
\mu
.
We set
\operatorname{bar}\left(\Psi\right):=(\boldsymbol{i}_{\mathsf{X}},\boldsymbol{b}_{\Psi})_{\sharp}\mu
.
We say that
\Psi
is
concentrated on a map
(or it is

deterministic
) if
\Psi=\operatorname{bar}\left(\Psi\right)
.

For the following recalls on Wasserstein spaces we refer e.g. to
[
2
, §7]
.
On
\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{X})
we define the
L^{2}
-Wasserstein distance
W_{2}
by

(2.8)

\displaystyle W_{2}^{2}(\mu,\mu^{\prime})

\displaystyle:=\inf\left\{\int_{\mathsf{X}\times\mathsf{X}}|x-y|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{\gamma}(x,y)\mid\boldsymbol{\gamma}\in\Gamma(\mu,\mu^{\prime})\right\}.

For the sequel, the set
\Gamma_{o}(\mu,\mu^{\prime})
denotes the subset of admissible plans in
\Gamma(\mu,\mu^{\prime})
realizing the infimum in
(
2.8
).
We say that a measure
\boldsymbol{\gamma}\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{X}\times\mathsf{X})
is optimal if

\boldsymbol{\gamma}\in\Gamma_{o}(\pi^{1}_{\sharp}\boldsymbol{\gamma},\pi^{2}_{\sharp}\boldsymbol{\gamma})
. We recall that
\boldsymbol{\gamma}\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{X}\times\mathsf{X})
is optimal if and only if its support is cyclically monotone i.e.

(2.9)

\begin{gathered}\text{for every $N\in\mathbb{N}$ and
$\{(x_{n},y_{n})\}_{n=1}^{N}\subset\supp\boldsymbol{\gamma}$ with $x_{0}:=x_{N}$ we
have}\\
\sum_{n=1}^{N}\langle y_{n},x_{n}-x_{n-1}\rangle\geq 0.\end{gathered}

We recall that the metric space
(\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{X}),W_{2})
is a complete and separable metric space and the
W_{2}
-convergence (sometimes denoted with
\overset{W_{2}}{\longrightarrow}
) is stronger than the narrow convergence. More precisely, if
(\mu_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\subset\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{X})
and
\mu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{X})
, the following holds (see
[
2
, Remark 7.1.11]
)

\mu_{n}\overset{W_{2}}{\to}\mu,\text{ as }n\to+\infty\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad\begin{cases}\mu_{n}\to\mu\text{ in }\mathcal{P}(\mathsf{X}^{s}),\\
\mathsf{m}_{2}(\mu_{n})\to\mathsf{m}_{2}(\mu),\end{cases}\text{ as }n\to+\infty.\\

In the following Definition
2.4
and Proposition
2.5
, we recall the topology of
\mathcal{P}_{2}^{sw}(\mathsf{T\kern-1.5ptX})
(see
[
40
,
27
]
).

Definition 2.4

(Strong-weak topology in
\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{T\kern-1.5ptX})
)
.

We denote by
\mathcal{P}_{2}^{sw}(\mathsf{T\kern-1.5ptX})
the space
\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{T\kern-1.5ptX})
endowed with the coarsest topology which makes the following functions continuous

\Phi\mapsto\int\zeta(x,v)\,\mathrm{d}\Phi(x,v),\quad\zeta\in\mathrm{C}^{sw}_{2}(\mathsf{T\kern-1.5ptX}),

where
\mathrm{C}^{sw}_{2}(\mathsf{T\kern-1.5ptX})
is the Banach space of test functions
\zeta:\mathsf{T\kern-1.5ptX}\to\mathbb{R}
such that

\displaystyle\zeta\text{ is sequentially continuous in $\mathsf{T\kern-1.5ptX}=\mathsf{X}^{s}\times\mathsf{X}^{w}$,}

\displaystyle\forall\,\varepsilon>0\ \exists\,A_{\varepsilon}\geq 0:|\zeta(x,v)|\leq A_{\varepsilon}(1+|x|^{2})+\varepsilon|v|^{2}\quad\text{for every }(x,v)\in\mathsf{T\kern-1.5ptX}.

The following Proposition (whose proof can be found in
[
40
]
) summarizes some of the properties of the topology of
\mathcal{P}_{2}^{sw}(\mathsf{T\kern-1.5ptX})
.

Proposition 2.5
.

(1)

If
(\Phi_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\subset\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{T\kern-1.5ptX})
is
a sequence and
\Phi\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{T\kern-1.5ptX})
, then
\Phi_{n}\to\Phi
in
\mathcal{P}_{2}^{sw}(\mathsf{T\kern-1.5ptX})
as
n\to\infty
if and only if

(a)

\Phi_{n}\to\Phi
in
\mathcal{P}(\mathsf{T\kern-1.5ptX})=\mathcal{P}(\mathsf{X}^{s}\times\mathsf{X}^{w})
,

(b)

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to+\infty}\int|x|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\Phi_{n}(x,v)=\int|x|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\Phi(x,v)
,

(c)

\displaystyle\sup_{n}\int|v|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\Phi_{n}(x,v)<\infty
.

(2)

For every compact set
\mathcal{K}\subset\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{X}^{s})
and
every constant
c<\infty
the sets

\mathcal{K}_{c}:=\Big\{\Phi\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{T\kern-1.5ptX}):\mathsf{x}_{\sharp}\Phi\in\mathcal{K},\quad\int|v|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\Phi(x,v)\leq c\Big\}

are sequentially compact in

\mathcal{P}_{2}^{sw}(\mathsf{T\kern-1.5ptX})
.

For the sequel, we recall the concept and main properties of geodesics in
\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{X})
. We denote equivalently by
\mu(t)
or
\mu_{t}
the evaluation at time

t\in\mathcal{I}\subset\mathbb{R}
of a curve
\mu:\mathcal{I}\to\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{X})
.

Definition 2.6

(Geodesics)
.

A curve
\mu:[0,1]\to\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{X})
is said to be a
(constant speed) geodesic
if for all
0\leq s\leq t\leq 1

we have

W_{2}(\mu_{s},\mu_{t})=(t-s)W_{2}(\mu_{0},\mu_{1}).

We also say that
\mu
is a
geodesic from
\mu_{0}
to
\mu_{1}
.

Definition 2.7

(Geodesic and total convexity)
.

We say that
A\subset\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{X})
is a
geodesically convex
set if for any pair
\mu_{0},\mu_{1}\in A
there exists a geodesic
\mu:[0,1]\to\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{X})
from
\mu_{0}
to
\mu_{1}
such that
\mu_{t}\in A
for all
t\in[0,1]
.

We say that
A\subset\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{X})
is
totally convex
if for any pair
\mu_{0},\mu_{1}\in A
and any coupling
\boldsymbol{\gamma}\in\Gamma(\mu_{0},\mu_{1})
, we have that
(\mathsf{x}^{t})_{\sharp}\boldsymbol{\gamma}\in A
for any
t\in[0,1]
.

Remark 2.8
.

Since total convexity will play a crucial role in the present paper,
let us recall a few examples of totally convex sets in
\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{X})
, which
are induced by a lower semicontinuous and convex function

P:\mathsf{X}\to(-\infty,+\infty]

and a real number
c
: it is sufficient to consider
the set of
\mu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{X})
satisfying one of the following conditions:

P\Big(\int x\,\mathrm{d}\mu(x)\Big)\leq c,\quad\int P(x)\,\mathrm{d}\mu(x)\leq c,\quad\int P(x-y)\,\mathrm{d}\mu\otimes\mu(x,y)\leq c.

Clearly, one can replace large with strict inequalities in the previous
formulae. Choosing
P
as the indicator function of
a convex set
\mathsf{U}\subset\mathsf{X}
(i.e.
P(x)=0
if
x\in\mathsf{U}
,

P(x)=+\infty
otherwise), one obtains conditions confining
the barycenter,
\supp\mu
, or

\supp\mu-\supp\mu
to a given set
\mathsf{U}
.

The following useful result (see
[
2
, Theorem 7.2.1, Theorem 7.2.2]
) on geodesics also points out that total convexity is stronger than geodesic convexity.

Theorem 2.9

(Properties of geodesics)
.

Let
\mu_{0},\mu_{1}\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{X})
and
\boldsymbol{\mu}\in\Gamma_{o}(\mu_{0},\mu_{1})
.
Then
\mu:[0,1]\to\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{X})
defined by

(2.10)

\mu_{t}:=(\mathsf{x}^{t})_{\sharp}\boldsymbol{\mu},\quad t\in[0,1],

is a (constant speed) geodesic from
\mu_{0}
to
\mu_{1}
. Conversely, any (constant speed) geodesic
\mu
from
\mu_{0}
to
\mu_{1}
admits the representation (
2.10
) for a suitable plan
\boldsymbol{\mu}\in\Gamma_{o}(\mu_{0},\mu_{1})
.

Finally, if
\mu
is a geodesic connecting
\mu_{0}
to
\mu_{1}
, then for every
t\in(0,1)
there exists a unique optimal plan
\boldsymbol{\mu}_{t0}
between
\mu_{t}
and
\mu_{0}
(resp.
\boldsymbol{\mu}_{t1}
between
\mu_{t}
and
\mu_{1}
) and it is concentrated on a map w.r.t.
\mu_{t}
, meaning that there exist Borel maps
\boldsymbol{r}_{t},\boldsymbol{r}^{\prime}_{t}:\mathsf{X}\to\mathsf{X}
such that

\boldsymbol{\mu}_{t0}=(\boldsymbol{i}_{X},\boldsymbol{r}_{t})_{\sharp}\mu_{t},\quad\boldsymbol{\mu}_{t1}=(\boldsymbol{i}_{X},\boldsymbol{r}_{t}^{\prime})_{\sharp}\mu_{t}.

The following defines the counterpart of
\mathrm{C}^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
when
\mathbb{R}^{d}
is replaced by
\mathsf{X}
.

Definition 2.10

(The space
\Cyl(\mathsf{X})
of cylindrical functions)
.

We denote by
\Pi_{d}(\mathsf{X})
the space of linear maps
\pi:\mathsf{X}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
of the form
\pi(x)=(\langle x,e_{1}\rangle,\cdots,\langle x,e_{d}\rangle)

for an orthonormal set
\{e_{1},\cdots,e_{d}\}
of
\mathsf{X}
.
A function
\varphi:\mathsf{X}\to\mathbb{R}
belongs to the space of cylindrical functions on
\mathsf{X}
,
\Cyl(\mathsf{X})
, if it is of the form

\varphi=\psi\circ\pi

where
\pi\in\Pi_{d}(\mathsf{X})
and
\psi\in\mathrm{C}^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
.

Given
\nu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{X})
we define the tangent bundle to
\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{X})
at
\nu
by

\Tan_{\nu}\mathcal{P}_{2}(X):={}\overline{\{\nabla\varphi\mid\varphi\in\Cyl(\mathsf{X})\}}^{L^{2}(X,\nu;X)}.

If
\mathcal{I}\subset\mathbb{R}
is an open interval and
\mu:\mathcal{I}\to\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{X})
is a locally absolutely
continuous curve, we define the
metric velocity
of
\mu
at
t\in\mathcal{I}
as

|\dot{\mu}_{t}|^{2}:=\lim_{h\to 0}\frac{W_{2}^{2}(\mu_{t+h},\mu_{t})}{h^{2}},

which exists for a.e.
t\in\mathcal{I}
.

The following result (see
[
2
, Theorem 8.3.1, Proposition
8.4.5 and Proposition 8.4.6]
) characterizes
locally absolutely continuous curves in
\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{X})
.

Theorem 2.11

(Wasserstein velocity field)
.

Let
\mu:\mathcal{I}\to\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{X})
be a locally absolutely
continuous curve defined in an open interval
\mathcal{I}\subset\mathbb{R}
.
There exists a Borel vector field

\boldsymbol{v}:\mathcal{I}\times\mathsf{X}\to\mathsf{X}

and
a set
A(\mu)\subset\mathcal{I}
with

\mathscr{L}(\mathcal{I}\setminus A(\mu))=0
such that
for every
t\in A(\mu)
the following hold

(1)

\boldsymbol{v}_{t}\in\Tan_{\mu_{t}}\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{X})
;

(2)

\int_{\mathsf{X}}|\boldsymbol{v}_{t}|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\mu_{t}=|\dot{\mu}_{t}|^{2}
;

(3)

the continuity equation
\partial_{t}\mu_{t}+\nabla\cdot(\boldsymbol{v}_{t}\mu_{t})=0

holds in the sense of distributions in
\mathcal{I}\times\mathsf{X}
.

Moreover,
\boldsymbol{v}_{t}
is uniquely determined
in
L^{2}(\mathsf{X},\mu_{t};\mathsf{X})
for
t\in A(\mu)
and

\lim_{h\to 0}\frac{W_{2}((\boldsymbol{i}_{\mathsf{X}}+h\boldsymbol{v}_{t})_{\sharp}\mu_{t},\mu_{t+h})}{|h|}=0\quad\text{for every }t\in A(\mu).

2.2.
Duality pairings

In this subsection we collect the main objects involving
duality pairings between measures in
\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{T\kern-1.5ptX}).

We report here a summary of the results needed in the sequel and we refer to
[
27
]
for a wider discussion on this matter.

As usual, we denote by
\mathsf{x}^{0},\mathsf{v}^{0},\mathsf{x}^{1}:\mathsf{T\kern-1.5ptX}\times\mathsf{X}\to\mathsf{X}
the projection maps of a point
(x_{0},v_{0},x_{1})
into
x_{0}
,
v_{0}
or
x_{1}
, respectively (and similarly with
\mathsf{x}^{0},\mathsf{v}^{0},\mathsf{x}^{1},\mathsf{v}^{1}
when they are defined in
\mathsf{T\kern-1.5ptX}\times\mathsf{T\kern-1.5ptX}
).

Definition 2.12

(Metric-duality pairings)
.

For every
\Phi_{0},\Phi_{1}\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{T\kern-1.5ptX})
,
\mu_{1}\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{X})
,
\boldsymbol{\vartheta}\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{X}\times\mathsf{X})
,
t\in[0,1]
and
\Psi\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{T\kern-1.5ptX}|\mathsf{x}^{t}_{\sharp}\boldsymbol{\vartheta})
, we set

\displaystyle\Lambda(\Phi_{0},\mu_{1})

\displaystyle:=\left\{\boldsymbol{\sigma}\in\Gamma(\Phi_{0},\mu_{1})\mid(\mathsf{x}^{0},\mathsf{x}^{1})_{\sharp}\boldsymbol{\sigma}\in\Gamma_{o}(\mathsf{x}_{\sharp}\Phi_{0},\mu_{1})\right\},

\displaystyle\Lambda(\Phi_{0},\Phi_{1})

\displaystyle:=\left\{\boldsymbol{\Theta}\in\Gamma(\Phi_{0},\Phi_{1})\mid(\mathsf{x}^{0},\mathsf{x}^{1})_{\sharp}\boldsymbol{\Theta}\in\Gamma_{o}(\mathsf{x}_{\sharp}\Phi_{0},\mathsf{x}_{\sharp}\Phi_{1})\right\},

\displaystyle\Gamma_{t}(\Psi,\boldsymbol{\vartheta})

\displaystyle:=\left\{\boldsymbol{\sigma}\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{T\kern-1.5ptX}\times\mathsf{X})\mid(\mathsf{x}^{0},\mathsf{x}^{1})_{\sharp}\boldsymbol{\sigma}=\boldsymbol{\vartheta},\quad(\mathsf{x}^{t}\circ(\mathsf{x}^{0},\mathsf{x}^{1}),\mathsf{v}^{0})_{\sharp}\boldsymbol{\sigma}=\Psi\right\}.

We set

\displaystyle\left[\Phi_{0},\mu_{1}\right]_{r}

\displaystyle:=\min\left\{\int_{\mathsf{T\kern-1.5ptX}\times\mathsf{X}}\langle x_{0}-x_{1},v_{0}\rangle\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{\sigma}\mid\boldsymbol{\sigma}\in\Lambda(\Phi_{0},\mu_{1})\right\},

\displaystyle\left[\Phi_{0},\mu_{1}\right]_{l}

\displaystyle:=\max\left\{\int_{\mathsf{T\kern-1.5ptX}\times\mathsf{X}}\langle x_{0}-x_{1},v_{0}\rangle\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{\sigma}\mid\boldsymbol{\sigma}\in\Lambda(\Phi_{0},\mu_{1})\right\},

\displaystyle\left[\Phi_{0},\Phi_{1}\right]_{r}

\displaystyle:=\min\left\{\int_{\mathsf{T\kern-1.5ptX}\times\mathsf{T\kern-1.5ptX}}\langle x_{0}-x_{1},v_{0}-v_{1}\rangle\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{\Theta}\mid\boldsymbol{\Theta}\in\Lambda(\Phi_{0},\Phi_{1})\right\},

\displaystyle\left[\Phi_{0},\Phi_{1}\right]_{l}

\displaystyle:=\max\left\{\int_{\mathsf{T\kern-1.5ptX}\times\mathsf{T\kern-1.5ptX}}\langle x_{0}-x_{1},v_{0}-v_{1}\rangle\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{\Theta}\mid\boldsymbol{\Theta}\in\Lambda(\Phi_{0},\Phi_{1})\right\},

\displaystyle[\Psi,\boldsymbol{\vartheta}]_{r,t}

\displaystyle:=\min\left\{\int\langle x_{0}-x_{1},v_{0}\rangle\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{\sigma}(x_{0},v_{0},x_{1})\mid\boldsymbol{\sigma}\in\Gamma_{t}(\Psi,\boldsymbol{\vartheta})\right\},

\displaystyle[\Psi,\boldsymbol{\vartheta}]_{l,t}

\displaystyle:=\max\left\{\int\langle x_{0}-x_{1},v_{0}\rangle\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{\sigma}(x_{0},v_{0},x_{1})\mid\boldsymbol{\sigma}\in\Gamma_{t}(\Psi,\boldsymbol{\vartheta})\right\}.

The following Theorem summarizes some of the properties of duality pairings analyzed in
[
27
]
.

Theorem 2.13
.

The following properties hold.

(1)

(Inversion)
For every
\boldsymbol{\vartheta}\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{X}^{2})
,
t\in[0,1]
,

\Phi\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{T\kern-1.5ptX}|\mathsf{x}^{t}_{\sharp}\boldsymbol{\vartheta})
it holds

[\Phi,\boldsymbol{\vartheta}]_{r,t}=-[\Phi,\mathsf{s}_{\sharp}\boldsymbol{\vartheta}]_{l,1-t},

where
\mathsf{s}
is as in (
2.3
).

(2)

(Comparison)
For every
\mu_{0},\mu_{1}\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{X})
and every
\Phi_{0}\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{T\kern-1.5ptX}|\mu_{0})
,
\Phi_{1}\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{T\kern-1.5ptX}|\mu_{1})
, it holds

\displaystyle\left[\Phi_{0},\mu_{1}\right]_{r}=\inf_{\boldsymbol{\vartheta}\in\Gamma_{o}(\mu_{0},\mu_{1})}[\Phi_{0},\boldsymbol{\vartheta}]_{r,0},\quad

\displaystyle\left[\Phi_{0},\mu_{1}\right]_{l}=\sup_{\boldsymbol{\vartheta}\in\Gamma_{o}(\mu_{0},\mu_{1})}[\Phi_{0},\boldsymbol{\vartheta}]_{l,0},

\displaystyle\left[\Phi_{0},\mu_{1}\right]_{r}+\left[\Phi_{1},\mu_{0}\right]_{r}\leq\left[\Phi_{0},\Phi_{1}\right]_{r},\quad

\displaystyle\left[\Phi_{0},\mu_{1}\right]_{l}+\left[\Phi_{1},\mu_{0}\right]_{l}\geq\left[\Phi_{0},\Phi_{1}\right]_{l},

and

\left[\Phi_{0},\Phi_{1}\right]_{r}\leq\left[\Phi_{0},\mu_{1}\right]_{r}+\left[\Phi_{1},\mu_{0}\right]_{l}\leq\left[\Phi_{0},\Phi_{1}\right]_{l}.

(3)

(Restriction)
For every
\boldsymbol{\vartheta}\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{X}^{2})
, every
0\leq s<t\leq 1
and every
\Phi\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{T\kern-1.5ptX}|\mathsf{x}^{s}_{\sharp}\boldsymbol{\vartheta})
,
\Psi\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{T\kern-1.5ptX}|\mathsf{x}^{t}_{\sharp}\boldsymbol{\vartheta})
we have

(2.11)

[\Phi,\boldsymbol{\vartheta}]_{r,s}=\frac{1}{t-s}[\Phi,(\mathsf{x}^{s},\mathsf{x}^{t})_{\sharp}\boldsymbol{\vartheta}]_{r,0},\quad[\Psi,\boldsymbol{\vartheta}]_{l,t}=\frac{1}{t-s}[\Psi,(\mathsf{x}^{s},\mathsf{x}^{t})_{\sharp}\boldsymbol{\vartheta}]_{l,1}.

(4)

(Trivialization)
If
\boldsymbol{\vartheta}\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{X}^{2})
,
t\in[0,1]
,

\Phi\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{T\kern-1.5ptX}|\mathsf{x}^{t}_{\sharp}\boldsymbol{\vartheta})
and
\mathsf{x}^{t}:\mathsf{X}^{2}\to\mathsf{X}
is

\boldsymbol{\vartheta}
-essentially injective
or
\Phi
is concentrated on a map,
then
\Gamma_{t}(\Phi,\boldsymbol{\vartheta})

contains a unique element and

(2.12)

[\Phi,\boldsymbol{\vartheta}]_{r,t}=[\Phi,\boldsymbol{\vartheta}]_{l,t}=\int\langle\boldsymbol{b}_{\Phi}\left(\mathsf{x}^{t}(x_{0},x_{1})\right),x_{0}-x_{1}\rangle\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{\vartheta}(x_{0},x_{1}),

with
\boldsymbol{b}_{\Phi}
the barycenter of
\Phi
as in Definition
2.3
.

(5)

(Semicontinuity)
Let
(\Phi_{n}^{i})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\subset\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{T\kern-1.5ptX})
be
converging to
\Phi^{i}
in
\mathcal{P}_{2}^{sw}(\mathsf{T\kern-1.5ptX})
,
i=0,1
, let
(\boldsymbol{\vartheta}_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\subset\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{X}^{2})
be converging to
\boldsymbol{\vartheta}
in
\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{X}^{2})
, let
(\nu_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\subset\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{X})
be converging to
\nu
in
\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{X})
and let
t\in[0,1]
. Then

\displaystyle\liminf_{n\to\infty}\left[\Phi^{0}_{n},\nu_{n}\right]_{r}\geq\left[\Phi^{0},\nu\right]_{r},\quad

\displaystyle\limsup_{n\to\infty}\left[\Phi^{0}_{n},\nu_{n}\right]_{l}\leq\left[\Phi^{0},\nu\right]_{l},

\displaystyle\liminf_{n\to\infty}\left[\Phi^{0}_{n},\Phi^{1}_{n}\right]_{r}\geq\left[\Phi^{0},\Phi^{1}\right]_{r},\quad

\displaystyle\limsup_{n\to\infty}\left[\Phi^{0}_{n},\Phi^{1}_{n}\right]_{l}\leq\left[\Phi^{0},\Phi^{1}\right]_{l},

\displaystyle\liminf_{n\to\infty}[\Phi_{n}^{0},\boldsymbol{\vartheta}_{n}]_{r,t}\geq[\Phi^{0},\boldsymbol{\vartheta}]_{r,t},\quad

\displaystyle\limsup_{n\to\infty}[\Phi_{n}^{0},\boldsymbol{\vartheta}_{n}]_{l,t}\leq[\Phi^{0},\boldsymbol{\vartheta}]_{l,t}.

(6)

Let
\mathcal{I}\subset\mathbb{R}
be an open interval, let
\mu^{1},\mu^{2}:\mathcal{I}\to\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{X})
be locally absolutely continuous curves and let
\boldsymbol{v}^{1},\boldsymbol{v}^{2}:\mathcal{I}\times\mathsf{X}\to\mathsf{X}
be Borel vector fields such that
\|\boldsymbol{v}^{i}_{t}\|_{L^{2}(\mathsf{X},\mu^{i}_{t};\mathsf{X})}\in L^{1}_{loc}(\mathcal{I})
,
i=1,2
, and such that

\partial_{t}\mu^{i}_{t}+\nabla\cdot(\boldsymbol{v}^{i}_{t}\mu^{i}_{t})=0

holds in the sense of distributions in
\mathcal{I}\times\mathsf{X}
,
i=1,2
. Let
A({\mu^{1}}),A({\mu^{2}})\subset\mathcal{I}
be as in Theorem
2.11
. Then

(a)

for every
\nu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{X})
and every
t\in A(\mu^{i})
,
i=1,2
, it holds

\displaystyle\lim_{h\downarrow 0}\frac{W_{2}^{2}(\mu^{i}_{t+h},\nu)-W_{2}^{2}(\mu^{i}_{t},\nu)}{2h}

\displaystyle=\left[(\boldsymbol{i}_{\mathsf{X}},\boldsymbol{v}^{i}_{t})_{\sharp}\mu^{i}_{t},\nu\right]_{r},

\displaystyle\lim_{h\uparrow 0}\frac{W_{2}^{2}(\mu^{i}_{t+h},\nu)-W_{2}^{2}(\mu^{i}_{t},\nu)}{2h}

\displaystyle=\left[(\boldsymbol{i}_{\mathsf{X}},\boldsymbol{v}^{i}_{t})_{\sharp}\mu^{i}_{t},\nu\right]_{l};

(b)

there exists a subset
A\subset A({\mu^{1}})\cap A({\mu^{2}})
of full Lebesgue
measure
such that
s\mapsto W_{2}^{2}(\mu^{1}_{s},\mu^{2}_{s})
is
differentiable in
A
and for every
t\in A
it holds

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}W_{2}^{2}(\mu^{1}_{t},\mu^{2}_{t})

\displaystyle=\left[(\boldsymbol{i}_{\mathsf{X}},\boldsymbol{v}^{1}_{t})_{\sharp}\mu^{1}_{t},(\boldsymbol{i}_{\mathsf{X}},\boldsymbol{v}^{2}_{t})_{\sharp}\mu^{2}_{t}\right]_{r}=\left[(\boldsymbol{i}_{\mathsf{X}},\boldsymbol{v}^{1}_{t})_{\sharp}\mu^{1}_{t},(\boldsymbol{i}_{\mathsf{X}},\boldsymbol{v}^{2}_{t})_{\sharp}\mu^{2}_{t}\right]_{l}.

Proof.

We give a few references for the proofs. Property (1) is
[
27
, (3.27)]
. Property (2) comes from the definition and
[
27
, Corollary 3.7]
. We sketch the proof only for the last property in (2): take
\boldsymbol{\sigma}\in\Lambda(\Phi_{0},\mu_{1})
such that

\left[\Phi_{0},\mu_{1}\right]_{r}=\int_{\mathsf{T\kern-1.5ptX}\times\mathsf{X}}\langle x_{0}-x_{1},v_{0}\rangle\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{\sigma},

and consider
\boldsymbol{\Theta}\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{T\kern-1.5ptX}\times\mathsf{T\kern-1.5ptX})
such that
(\mathsf{x}^{0},\mathsf{v}^{0},\mathsf{x}^{1})_{\sharp}\boldsymbol{\Theta}=\boldsymbol{\sigma}
and
(\mathsf{x}^{1},\mathsf{v}^{1})_{\sharp}\boldsymbol{\Theta}=\Phi_{1}
. Then
\boldsymbol{\Theta}\in\Lambda(\Phi_{0},\Phi_{1})
, so that

\displaystyle\left[\Phi_{0},\Phi_{1}\right]_{r}

\displaystyle\leq\int_{\mathsf{T\kern-1.5ptX}\times\mathsf{T\kern-1.5ptX}}\langle x_{0}-x_{1},v_{0}-v_{1}\rangle\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{\Theta}

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathsf{T\kern-1.5ptX}\times\mathsf{X}}\langle x_{0}-x_{1},v_{0}\rangle\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{\sigma}+\int_{\mathsf{T\kern-1.5ptX}\times\mathsf{T\kern-1.5ptX}}\langle x_{1}-x_{0},v_{1}\rangle\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{\Theta}

\displaystyle\leq\left[\Phi_{0},\mu_{1}\right]_{r}+\left[\Phi_{1},\mu_{0}\right]_{l}.

The strategy for proving the remaining inequality in (2) is identical.

Assertion (3) follows from the fact that, if we define
T:\mathsf{T\kern-1.5ptX}\times\mathsf{X}\to\mathsf{T\kern-1.5ptX}\times\mathsf{X}
and
\mathcal{L}:\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{T\kern-1.5ptX}\times\mathsf{X})\to\mathbb{R}
as

T(x_{0},v_{0},x_{1}):=(\mathsf{x}^{s}(x_{0},x_{1}),v_{0},\mathsf{x}^{t}(x_{0},x_{1})),\quad\quad\mathcal{L}(\boldsymbol{\sigma}):=\int_{\mathsf{T\kern-1.5ptX}\times\mathsf{X}}\langle v_{0},x_{0}-x_{1}\rangle\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{\sigma}(x_{0},v_{0},x_{1}),

it is clear that

[\Phi,\boldsymbol{\mu}]_{r,s}=\inf\left\{\mathcal{L}(\boldsymbol{\sigma})\mid\boldsymbol{\sigma}\in\Gamma_{s}(\Phi,\boldsymbol{\mu})\right\},\quad[\Phi,(\mathsf{x}^{s},\mathsf{x}^{t})_{\sharp}\boldsymbol{\mu}]_{r,0}=\inf\left\{\mathcal{L}(\boldsymbol{\sigma})\mid\boldsymbol{\sigma}\in\Gamma_{0}(\Phi,(\mathsf{x}^{s},\mathsf{x}^{t})_{\sharp}\boldsymbol{\mu})\right\}.

Then, the first equality in the statement follows noting that
T_{\sharp}(\Gamma_{s}(\Phi,\boldsymbol{\mu}))=\Gamma_{0}(\Phi,(\mathsf{x}^{s},\mathsf{x}^{t})_{\sharp}\boldsymbol{\mu})
and that
\mathcal{L}(T_{\sharp}\boldsymbol{\sigma})=(t-s)\mathcal{L}(\boldsymbol{\sigma})
for every
\boldsymbol{\sigma}\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{T\kern-1.5ptX}\times\mathsf{X})
. The second equality follows from the first one and (1). Item (4) is
[
27
, Remark 3.19]
. Item (5) easily follows by
[
27
, Lemma 3.15]
. Finally, item (6) is provided by
[
27
, Theorem 3.11, Theorem 3.14, Remark 3.12]
.
∎

2.3.
Multivalued probability vector fields, metric dissipativity and EVI solutions

We recall now the
main definition of Multivalued Probability Vector Field and of metric dissipativity.

Definition 2.14

(Multivalued Probability Vector Field - MPVF)
.

A
multivalued probability vector field

{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
is a nonempty subset of

\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{T\kern-1.5ptX})
with
\mathrm{D}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}):=\mathsf{x}_{\sharp}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})=\{\mathsf{x}_{\sharp}\Phi:\Phi\in{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}\}
.
Given any
\mu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{X})
, we define the
section

{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}[\mu]

of
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
as

{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}[\mu]:=\left\{\Phi\in{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}\mid\mathsf{x}_{\sharp}\Phi=\mu\right\}.

We say that
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
is a
Probability Vector Field
(
PVF
) if
\mathsf{x}_{\sharp}
is injective in
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
, i.e.
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}[\mu]
contains a
unique element for every
\mu\in\mathrm{D}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})
.

A
selection

{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}^{\prime}
of a
MPVF

{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
is a
PVF
such that
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}^{\prime}\subset{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
and
\mathrm{D}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}^{\prime})=\mathrm{D}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})
.

A
MPVF

{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}\subset\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{T\kern-1.5ptX})
is
deterministic

or
concentrated on maps
if
every
\Phi\in{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
is deterministic (see Definition
2.3
).

Starting from a MPVF
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
,
the barycentric projection (
2.7
)
induces a deterministic MPVF which we call
\operatorname{bar}\left({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}\right)
: it is defined by

(2.13)

\operatorname{bar}\left({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}\right)[\mu]:=\left\{\operatorname{bar}\left(\Phi\right)=(\boldsymbol{i}_{\mathsf{X}},\boldsymbol{b}_{\Phi})_{\sharp}\mu:\Phi\in{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}[\mu]\right\},\quad\mu\in\mathrm{D}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}).

We will also use the notation

(2.14)

\operatorname{map}\left({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}\right)[\mu]:=\left\{\boldsymbol{f}\in L^{2}(\mathsf{X},\mu;\mathsf{X}):(\boldsymbol{i}_{\mathsf{X}},\boldsymbol{f})_{\sharp}\mu\in{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}[\mu]\right\},\quad\mu\in\mathrm{D}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}),

to extract the deterministic part of a MPVF
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
: notice
that a MPVF
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
is deterministic if and only if

{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}=\operatorname{bar}\left({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}\right)=\left\{(\boldsymbol{i}_{\mathsf{X}},\boldsymbol{f})_{\sharp}\mu:\boldsymbol{f}\in\operatorname{map}\left({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}\right)[\mu],\,\mu\in\mathrm{D}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})\right\}
.

Conversely,
for a given set
D\subset\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{X})

let us consider a continuous map

{\boldsymbol{f}}:\mathcal{S}\left(\mathsf{X},D\right)\to\mathsf{X}
where

(2.15)

\mathcal{S}\left(\mathsf{X},D\right):=\left\{(x,\mu)\in\mathsf{X}\times D\mid x\in\supp(\mu)\right\},\quad\text{with }\mathcal{S}\left(\mathsf{X}\right):=\mathcal{S}\left(\mathsf{X},\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{X})\right).

If, for every
\mu\in D
,
the integral

\displaystyle\int|\boldsymbol{f}(x,\mu)|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\mu(x)

is finite,
then
{\boldsymbol{f}}
induces a PVF
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
defined by

{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}=\big\{(\boldsymbol{i}_{\mathsf{X}},{\boldsymbol{f}}(\cdot,\mu))_{\sharp}\mu:\mu\in D\big\},\quad\mathrm{D}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})=D.

We often adopt the convention to write
\boldsymbol{f}[\mu]
for the function

\boldsymbol{f}[\mu](x):=\boldsymbol{f}(x,\mu),\quad x\in\supp(\mu),

in particular when
\boldsymbol{f}[\mu]
is just an element
of
L^{2}(\mathsf{X},\mu;\mathsf{X})
.

Definition 2.15

(Metrically
\lambda
-dissipative MPVF)
.

A
MPVF

{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}\subset\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{T\kern-1.5ptX})
is (metrically)
\lambda
-dissipative
,

\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
, if

(2.16)

\left[\Phi_{0},\Phi_{1}\right]_{r}\leq\lambda W_{2}^{2}(\mu_{0},\mu_{1})\quad\forall\,\Phi_{0},\Phi_{1}\in{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}},\ \mu_{0}=\mathsf{x}_{\sharp}\Phi_{0},\ \mu_{1}=\mathsf{x}_{\sharp}\Phi_{1}.

In case
\lambda=0
, we simply say that
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
is dissipative.

Remark 2.16
.

Thanks to Theorem
2.13
(2), (
2.16
) implies the weaker condition

(2.17)

\left[\Phi_{0},\mu_{1}\right]_{r}+\left[\Phi_{1},\mu_{0}\right]_{r}\leq\lambda W_{2}^{2}(\mu_{0},\mu_{1}),\quad\forall\,\Phi_{0},\Phi_{1}\in{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}},\ \mu_{0}=\mathsf{x}_{\sharp}\Phi_{0},\ \mu_{1}=\mathsf{x}_{\sharp}\Phi_{1}.

Given a MPVF
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}\subset\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{T\kern-1.5ptX})
, we define its
\lambda
-trasformation,
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}^{\lambda}
, and its opposite,
-{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
, as

(2.18)

\displaystyle{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}^{\lambda}

\displaystyle:=L^{\lambda}_{\sharp}{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}=\left\{L^{\lambda}_{\sharp}\Phi\,:\,\Phi\in{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}\right\},

(2.19)

\displaystyle-{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}

\displaystyle:=\left\{(\mathsf{x},-\mathsf{v})_{\sharp}\Phi:\Phi\in{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}\right\},

where
L^{\lambda}:\mathsf{T\kern-1.5ptX}\to\mathsf{T\kern-1.5ptX}
is the bijective map defined by

L^{\lambda}:=(\mathsf{x},\mathsf{v}-\lambda\mathsf{x}).

Similar to Remark
A.1
for the case of operators in Hilbert spaces, we recall the following result (cf.
[
27
, Lemma 4.6]
)

Lemma 2.17
.

{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}\subset\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{T\kern-1.5ptX})
is a
\lambda
-dissipative
MPVF
(resp. satisfies (
2.17
)) if and only if
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}^{\lambda}
is dissipative, i.e.
0
-dissipative (resp. satisfies (
2.17
) with
\lambda=0
).

Definition 2.18
.

Let
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}\subset\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{T\kern-1.5ptX})
,
\mu_{0},\mu_{1}\in\mathrm{D}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})
. We define the set

\Gamma({\mu_{0}},{\mu_{1}}|{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}):=\left\{\boldsymbol{\mu}\in\Gamma(\mu_{0},\mu_{1})\mid\mathsf{x}^{t}_{\sharp}\boldsymbol{\mu}\in\mathrm{D}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})\text{ for every }t\in[0,1]\right\}.

If
\boldsymbol{\mu}\in\Gamma({\mu_{0}},{\mu_{1}}|{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})
and
t\in[0,1]
, we define

\displaystyle[{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}},\boldsymbol{\mu}]_{r,t}:=\sup\left\{[\Phi,\boldsymbol{\mu}]_{r,t}\mid\Phi\in{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}[\mu_{t}]\right\},\qquad[{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}},\boldsymbol{\mu}]_{l,t}:=\inf\left\{[\Phi,\boldsymbol{\mu}]_{l,t}\mid\Phi\in{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}[\mu_{t}]\right\}.

In the following Theorem we discuss the behaviour of duality pairings with
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
along geodesics.

Theorem 2.19
.

Let
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
be a
MPVF
, let
\mu_{0},\mu_{1}\in\mathrm{D}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})
, let

\boldsymbol{\mu}\in\Gamma({\mu_{0}},{\mu_{1}}|{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})\cap\Gamma_{o}(\mu_{0},\mu_{1})
and let

W^{2}:=W_{2}^{2}(\mu_{0},\mu_{1})
.
If
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
satisfies (
2.17
), then the following properties hold.

(1)

[{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}},\boldsymbol{\mu}]_{l,t}\leq[{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}},\boldsymbol{\mu}]_{r,t}
for every

t\in(0,1)
;

(2)

[{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}},\boldsymbol{\mu}]_{r,s}\leq[{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}},\boldsymbol{\mu}]_{l,t}+\lambda W^{2}(t-s)

for every
0\leq s<t\leq 1
;

(3)

t\mapsto[{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}},\boldsymbol{\mu}]_{r,t}+\lambda W^{2}t
and

t\mapsto[{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}},\boldsymbol{\mu}]_{l,t}+\lambda W^{2}t
are increasing respectively in
[0,1)
and in
(0,1]
;

(4)

[{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}},\boldsymbol{\mu}]_{l,t}=[{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}},\boldsymbol{\mu}]_{r,t}
at every
point
t\in(0,1)
where one of them is continuous and thus coincide outside a countable set.

Proof.

Item (1) immediately follows from the definition. Item (2) is proven in
[
27
, Theorem 4.9]
, while (3) and (4) follow from (2).
∎

Proposition 2.20
.

If
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
is a
\lambda
-dissipative
MPVF
then
its sequential closure

(2.20)

\operatorname{cl}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}):=\Big\{\Phi\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{T\kern-1.5ptX}):\exists\,\Phi_{n}\in{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}:\Phi_{n}\to\Phi\ \text{in
}\mathcal{P}_{2}^{sw}(\mathsf{T\kern-1.5ptX})\Big\}.

is
\lambda
-dissipative as well.

Proof.

It follows from Theorem
2.13
(5). See also
[
27
, Proposition 4.15]
.
∎

We recall the definition of
\lambda
-EVI solution for a MPVF.

Definition 2.21

(
\lambda
-Evolution Variational Inequality)
.

Let
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
be a
MPVF
and let
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
.
We say that a continuous curve
\mu:\mathcal{I}\to\overline{\mathrm{D}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})}

is a
\lambda
-EVI solution
for the
MPVF

{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
if

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}W_{2}^{2}(\mu_{t},\mathsf{x}_{\sharp}\Phi)

\displaystyle\leq\lambda W_{2}^{2}(\mu_{t},\mathsf{x}_{\sharp}\Phi)-\left[\Phi,\mu_{t}\right]_{r}\text{ in }\mathscr{D}^{\prime}(\operatorname{int}\left(\mathcal{I}\right))\text{ for every }\Phi\in{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}},

where the writing
\mathscr{D}^{\prime}(\operatorname{int}\left(\mathcal{I}\right))
means that the expression has to be unerstood in the distributional sense in
\operatorname{int}\left(\mathcal{I}\right)
.

Remark 2.22
.

In light of Theorem
2.13
(6a) and recalling
[
27
, Remark 5.2]
, an absolutely continuous curve
\mu:\mathcal{I}\to\overline{\mathrm{D}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})}
is a
\lambda
-
EVI
solution
for the MPVF
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
if and only if

\begin{aligned} \lim_{h\downarrow 0}\frac{W_{2}^{2}(\mu_{t+h},\nu)-W_{2}^{2}(\mu_{t},\nu)}{2h}&\leq\lambda W_{2}^{2}(\mu_{t},\mathsf{x}_{\sharp}\Phi)-\left[\Phi,\mu_{t}\right]_{r}\end{aligned}\quad\text{for every}\ t\in A(\mu)\text{ and every}\ \Phi\in{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}},

where
A(\mu)\subset\mathcal{I}
is as in Theorem
2.11
.

3.
Invariant dissipative operators in Hilbert spaces and totally dissipative MPVFs

From now on,
\mathsf{X}
will denote a separable Hilbert space;
we will also consider
a standard Borel
space
(\Omega,{\mathcal{B}})

endowed with a nonatomic probability measure
\mathbb{P}

(see Appendix
B
and in particular Definition

B.1
)
and the Hilbert space

\mathcal{X}:=L^{2}(\Omega,{\mathcal{B}},\mathbb{P};\mathsf{X})
.
We will use capital letters
X,Y,V,\ldots
to denote elements of

\mathcal{X}
(i.e.
\mathsf{X}
-valued random variables).

We denote by
\iota:\mathcal{X}\to\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{X})

and

\iota^{2}:\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{X}\to\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{X}\times\mathsf{X})\equiv\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{T\kern-1.5ptX})

the push-forward operators

(3.1)

\iota(X):=X_{\sharp}\mathbb{P},\qquad\iota^{2}(X,V):=(X,V)_{\sharp}\mathbb{P}.

We frequently use the notations
\iota_{X}=\iota(X)
and
\iota^{2}_{X,V}=\iota^{2}(X,V)
.

Definition 3.1

(Measure-preserving isomorphisms)
.

We denote by
\mathrm{S}(\Omega)
the class of
{\mathcal{B}}
-
{\mathcal{B}}
-measurable maps

g:\Omega\to\Omega
which are essentially injective and measure
preserving, meaning that there exists a full
\mathbb{P}
-measure set

\Omega_{0}\in{\mathcal{B}}
such that
g
is injective on
\Omega_{0}
and

g_{\sharp}\mathbb{P}=\mathbb{P}
. Every
g\in\mathrm{S}(\Omega)
has an inverse

g^{-1}\in\mathrm{S}(\Omega)
(defined up to a
\mathbb{P}
-negligible set) such
that
g^{-1}\circ g=g\circ g^{-1}=\boldsymbol{i}_{\Omega}

\mathbb{P}
-a.e. in
\Omega
.

In Section
3.1
we report some properties (see

[
28
]
for details and proofs) of the resolvent operator,
the Yosida approximation and the minimal selection of a maximal

\lambda
-dissipative operator
{\boldsymbol{B}}\subset\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{X}

which is invariant by measure-preserving isomorphisms.
In Section
3.2
we study the relation between
\lambda
-dissipativity for an invariant subset
{\boldsymbol{B}}
of
\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{X}
, and correspondent total
\lambda
-dissipativity of the image/law
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
of
{\boldsymbol{B}}
in
\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{T\kern-1.5ptX})
. The particular case of deterministic MPVFs is considered in section
3.3
.
These results are then used, in Section
4
, to analyze well-posedness of the Eulerian flow for
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
generated by the corresponding Lagrangian one for
{\boldsymbol{B}}
and the generation of
\lambda
-EVI solutions in
\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{X})
.

3.1.
Law invariant dissipative operators

Given a set

{\boldsymbol{B}}\subset\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{X}

(as usual, we will identify subsets of
\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{X}
with
multivalued operators),
we define
{\boldsymbol{B}}X:=\{V\in\mathcal{X}:(X,V)\in{\boldsymbol{B}}\}
and the domain

\mathrm{D}({\boldsymbol{B}}):=\{X\in\mathcal{X}:{\boldsymbol{B}}X\neq\emptyset\}
.

When
{\boldsymbol{B}}
is maximal
\lambda
-dissipative,

{\boldsymbol{J}_{\tau}}
,
{\boldsymbol{B}}_{\tau}
and
{\boldsymbol{B}}^{\circ}
denote
respectively the resolvent operator, the Yosida approximation and the
minimal selection of
{\boldsymbol{B}}
(we refer to Appendix
A
).
Here, we just recall that
{\boldsymbol{J}_{\tau}}:=(\boldsymbol{i}_{\mathcal{X}}-\tau{\boldsymbol{B}})^{-1}
is a
(1-\lambda\tau)^{-1}
-Lipschitz map defined on the whole
\mathcal{X}
for every
0<\tau<1/\lambda^{+}
, where we set
\lambda^{+}:=\lambda\vee 0
and
1/\lambda^{+}=+\infty
if
\lambda^{+}=0
.
The minimal selection
{\boldsymbol{B}}^{\circ}:\mathrm{D}({\boldsymbol{B}})\to\mathcal{X}
of
{\boldsymbol{B}}
is also characterized by

{\boldsymbol{B}}^{\circ}X=\displaystyle\lim_{\tau\downarrow 0}\frac{{\boldsymbol{J}_{\tau}}X-X}{\tau}.

The Yosida approximation of
{\boldsymbol{B}}
is defined by
{\boldsymbol{B}}_{\tau}:=\frac{{\boldsymbol{J}_{\tau}}-I}{\tau}
.
For every
0<\tau<1/\lambda^{+}
,
{\boldsymbol{B}}_{\tau}
is maximal
\lambda/(1-\lambda\tau)
-dissipative and
\frac{2-\lambda\tau}{\tau(1-\lambda\tau)}
-Lipschitz continuous.

If
{\boldsymbol{B}}
is a maximal
\lambda
-dissipative operator,
then there exists (cf. Theorems
A.5
,
A.6
in Appendix
A
) a semigroup of
e^{\lambda t}
-Lipschitz transformations
(\boldsymbol{S}_{t})_{t\geq 0}
with
\boldsymbol{S}_{t}:\overline{\mathrm{D}({\boldsymbol{B}})}\to\overline{\mathrm{D}({\boldsymbol{B}})}
s.t. for every
X_{0}\in\mathrm{D}({\boldsymbol{B}})

the curve
t\mapsto\boldsymbol{S}_{t}X_{0}
is included in
\mathrm{D}({\boldsymbol{B}})
and it
is the unique locally Lipschitz continuous solution of the differential inclusion

\begin{cases}\dot{X}_{t}\in{\boldsymbol{B}}X_{t}\quad\text{ a.e. }t>0,\\
X\lower 3.0pt\hbox{$|_{t=0}$}=X_{0}.\end{cases}

By Theorem
A.5
(3), we also have

\lim_{h\downarrow 0}\frac{\boldsymbol{S}_{t+h}X_{0}-\boldsymbol{S}_{t}X_{0}}{h}={\boldsymbol{B}}^{\circ}(\boldsymbol{S}_{t}X_{0}),\quad\text{for every $X_{0}\in\mathrm{D}({\boldsymbol{B}})$ and every $t\geq 0$.}

Let us now consider the particular classes of
operators which are invariant by measure-preserving isomorphisms
or law-invariant.

Definition 3.2

(Invariant operators)
.

We say that a set
(or a multivalued operator)
{\boldsymbol{B}}\subset\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{X}
is
invariant by measure-preserving isomorphisms
if for every
g\in\mathrm{S}(\Omega)
it holds

(X,V)\in{\boldsymbol{B}}\,\Rightarrow\,(X\circ g,V\circ g)\in{\boldsymbol{B}}.

A set
{\boldsymbol{B}}\subset\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{X}
is
law invariant
if it holds

(X,V)\in{\boldsymbol{B}},\,\,X^{\prime},V^{\prime}\in\mathcal{X},\,\,(X,V)_{\sharp}\mathbb{P}=(X^{\prime},V^{\prime})_{\sharp}\mathbb{P}\,\Rightarrow\,(X^{\prime},V^{\prime})\in{\boldsymbol{B}}.

An operator
\boldsymbol{A}:\mathcal{X}\supset D(\boldsymbol{A})\to\mathcal{X}
,
is invariant by measure-preserving isomorphisms (resp. law invariant) if its graph is invariant by measure-preserving isomorphisms (resp. law invariant).

We recall the notation in (
2.15
) and that
\iota\big(D({\boldsymbol{B}})\big)=\Big\{X_{\sharp}\mathbb{P}:X\in\mathrm{D}({\boldsymbol{B}})\Big\}

is
the image
in
\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{X})

of the domain of

{\boldsymbol{B}}
.
The results in the following Lemma
3.3
and Theorem
3.4
are presented in
[
28
, Section 4]
to which we refer for the proofs.

Lemma 3.3

(Closed invariant sets)
.

Let
{\boldsymbol{B}}\subset\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{X}
be a closed set. Then
{\boldsymbol{B}}
is invariant by measure-preserving isomorphisms if and only if it is law invariant.

Theorem 3.4

(Representation of resolvents, Yosida approximations, and semigroups)
.

Let
{\boldsymbol{B}}\subset\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{X}
be a maximal
\lambda
-dissipative operator which is invariant by measure-preserving isomorphisms. Then for every
0<\tau<1/\lambda^{+},\ t\geq 0

the operators
{\boldsymbol{B}},{\boldsymbol{B}}_{\tau},{\boldsymbol{J}_{\tau}},\boldsymbol{S}_{t},{\boldsymbol{B}}^{\circ}
are
law invariant. Moreover there exist (uniquely defined) continuous maps
\boldsymbol{j}_{\tau}:\mathcal{S}\left(\mathsf{X}\right)\to\mathsf{X}
,
\boldsymbol{b}_{\tau}:\mathcal{S}\left(\mathsf{X}\right)\to\mathsf{X}
, and
