\boldsymbol{s}_{t}:\mathcal{S}\left(\mathsf{X},\overline{\iota\big(D({\boldsymbol{B}})\big)}\right)\to\mathsf{X}
such that

(1)

for every
\mu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{X})
, the map
\boldsymbol{j}_{\tau}(\cdot,\mu):\supp(\mu)\to\mathsf{X}
is
(1-\lambda\tau)^{-1}
-Lipschitz continuous, for
0<\tau<1/\lambda^{+}
;

(2)

for every
\mu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{X})
, the map
\boldsymbol{b}_{\tau}(\cdot,\mu):\supp(\mu)\to\mathsf{X}
is
\frac{2-\lambda\tau}{\tau(1-\lambda\tau)}
-Lipschitz continuous, for
0<\tau<1/\lambda^{+}
;

(3)

for every
\mu\in\overline{\iota\big(D({\boldsymbol{B}})\big)}
, the map
\boldsymbol{s}_{t}(\cdot,\mu):\supp(\mu)\to\mathsf{X}
is
e^{\lambda t}
-Lipschitz continuous,

and

(3.2)

\displaystyle\text{ for every $X\in\mathcal{X}$, }{\boldsymbol{J}_{\tau}}X(\omega)=\boldsymbol{j}_{\tau}(X(\omega),X_{\sharp}\mathbb{P})\text{ for $\mathbb{P}$-a.e.\penalty\ $\omega\in\Omega$,}

(3.3)

\displaystyle\text{ for every $X\in\mathcal{X}$, }{\boldsymbol{B}}_{\tau}X(\omega)=\boldsymbol{b}_{\tau}(X(\omega),X_{\sharp}\mathbb{P})\text{ for $\mathbb{P}$-a.e.\penalty\ $\omega\in\Omega$,}

(3.4)

\displaystyle\text{ for every $X\in\overline{\mathrm{D}({\boldsymbol{B}})}$, }\boldsymbol{S}_{t}X(\omega)=\boldsymbol{s}_{t}(X(\omega),X_{\sharp}\mathbb{P})\text{ for $\mathbb{P}$-a.e.\penalty\ $\omega\in\Omega$,}

together with the invariance and semigroup properties

(3.5)

\begin{gathered}\mu\in\overline{\iota\big(D({\boldsymbol{B}})\big)}\quad\Rightarrow\quad\boldsymbol{s}_{t}(\cdot,\mu)_{\sharp}\mu\in\overline{\iota\big(D({\boldsymbol{B}})\big)};\quad\mu\in{\iota\big(D({\boldsymbol{B}})\big)}\quad\Rightarrow\quad\boldsymbol{s}_{t}(\cdot,\mu)_{\sharp}\mu\in{\iota\big(D({\boldsymbol{B}})\big)},\\
\boldsymbol{s}_{t+h}(x,\mu)=\boldsymbol{s}_{h}(\boldsymbol{s}_{t}(x,\mu),\boldsymbol{s}_{t}(\cdot,\mu)_{\sharp}\mu)\quad\text{for every }(x,\mu)\in\mathcal{S}\left(\mathsf{X},\overline{\iota\big(D({\boldsymbol{B}})\big)}\right),\ t,h\geq 0.\end{gathered}

Finally, for every
\mu\in\iota\big(D({\boldsymbol{B}})\big)
, there exists a map

\boldsymbol{b}^{\circ}[\mu]\in L^{2}(\mathsf{X},\mu;\mathsf{X})
such that
for every
X\in\mathcal{X}

(3.6)

\text{ if $X_{\sharp}\mathbb{P}=\mu$ then $X\in\mathrm{D}({\boldsymbol{B}})$, }{\boldsymbol{B}}^{\circ}X(\omega)=\boldsymbol{b}^{\circ}[\mu](X(\omega))\text{ for $\mathbb{P}$-a.e.\penalty\ $\omega\in\Omega$.}

For every
\mu\in\iota\big(D({\boldsymbol{B}})\big)
,
the map
\boldsymbol{b}^{\circ}[\mu]
is
\lambda
-dissipative in a set
\mathsf{X}_{0}\subset\mathsf{X}

of full
\mu
-measure and satisfies

(3.7)

\lim_{h\downarrow 0}\int\bigg|\frac{1}{h}(\boldsymbol{s}_{t+h}(x,\mu)-\boldsymbol{s}_{t}(x,\mu))-\boldsymbol{b}^{\circ}[\boldsymbol{s}_{t}(\cdot,\mu)_{\sharp}\mu](\boldsymbol{s}_{t}(x,\mu))\bigg|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\mu(x)=0,\quad t\geq 0.

Notice that when
\mu\in\iota(\mathrm{D}({\boldsymbol{B}}))
,
(
3.5
) and (
3.7
) yield

(3.8)

\lim_{h\downarrow 0}\int\bigg|\frac{1}{h}(\boldsymbol{s}_{h}(x,\mu)-x)-\boldsymbol{b}^{\circ}[\mu](x)\bigg|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\mu(x)=0.

Remark 3.5
.

By Theorem
A.3
(1) and Lemma
3.3
,
a maximal
\lambda
-dissipative operator
{\boldsymbol{B}}\subset\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{X}
,
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
, is law invariant if and only if it is invariant by measure-preserving isomorphisms.
Hence, in this case, we will simply use the word
invariant
. Notice moreover that if
{\boldsymbol{B}}
is law invariant, then also

\mathrm{D}({\boldsymbol{B}})
is
law invariant

in the sense that if
X\in\mathrm{D}({\boldsymbol{B}})
and
Y_{\sharp}\mathbb{P}=X_{\sharp}\mathbb{P}

then also
Y
belongs to
\mathrm{D}({\boldsymbol{B}})
. It is an immediate consequence of (
3.6
).

3.2.
Totally dissipative MPVFs

The aim of this section is to study the properties of MPVFs enjoying a strong dissipativity property that we call total dissipativity.

Definition 3.6

(Total dissipativity)
.

We say that a
MPVF

{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}\subset\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{T\kern-1.5ptX})
is
totally
\lambda
-dissipative
,
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
, if
for every
\Phi_{0},\Phi_{1}\in{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}

and every
\boldsymbol{\vartheta}\in\Gamma(\Phi_{0},\Phi_{1})
we have

(3.9)

\int\langle v_{1}-v_{0},x_{1}-x_{0}\rangle\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{\vartheta}(x_{0},v_{0},x_{1},v_{1})\leq\lambda\int|x_{1}-x_{0}|^{2}\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{\vartheta}.

We say that
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
is
maximal totally
\lambda
-dissipative
if it is
maximal in the class of totally
\lambda
-dissipative
MPVF
s: if
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}^{\prime}\supset{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}

and
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}^{\prime}
is totally
\lambda
-dissipative, then
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}^{\prime}={\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}.

Of course, total
\lambda
-dissipativity implies

\lambda
-dissipativity (see Definition
2.15
).

Remark 3.7
.

Notice that for a deterministic MPVF (recall Definition
2.14
)
total
\lambda
-dissipativity is equivalent
to the following condition
(when
\lambda=0
see the analogous
notion of L-monotonicity
of
[
23
, Def. 3.31]
):
for every

\mu_{i}\in\mathrm{D}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})
and

\boldsymbol{f}_{i}\in\operatorname{map}\left({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}[\mu_{i}]\right)
,
i=0,1
, and every
\boldsymbol{\mu}\in\Gamma(\mu_{0},\mu_{1})
it holds

(3.10)

\int\langle\boldsymbol{f}_{1}(x_{1},\mu_{1})-\boldsymbol{f}_{0}(x_{0},\mu_{0}),x_{1}-x_{0}\rangle\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{\mu}(x_{0},x_{1})\leq\lambda\int|x_{1}-x_{0}|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{\mu}(x_{0},x_{1}).

We introduce now the natural notion of Lagrangian representation of
a MPVF,
based on the maps
\iota
,
\iota^{2}
introduced in (
3.1
).

Definition 3.8

(Lagrangian representations and Eulerian images)
.

Given
{\boldsymbol{B}}\subset\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{X}
and
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}\subset\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{T\kern-1.5ptX})
, we say that
{\boldsymbol{B}}
is
the
Lagrangian representation
of
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
if

{\boldsymbol{B}}=(\iota^{2})^{-1}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})=\Big\{(X,V)\in\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{X}:(X,V)_{\sharp}\mathbb{P}\in{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}\Big\}.

Conversely, if

{\boldsymbol{B}}\subset\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{X}

we say that
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
is the

Eulerian
image
of
{\boldsymbol{B}}
if

{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}=\iota^{2}({\boldsymbol{B}})=\Big\{(X,V)_{\sharp}\mathbb{P}:(X,V)\in{\boldsymbol{B}}\Big\}.

Clearly, the Lagrangian representation
{\boldsymbol{B}}
of
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
is law
invariant, moreover
{\boldsymbol{B}}
is the Lagrangian representation of
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}

if and only if
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
is the Eulerian image of
{\boldsymbol{B}}
and
{\boldsymbol{B}}
is law invariant.

Similarly to Remark
A.1
concerning operators in Hilbert
spaces, we highlight the following result which allows a
reduction of
many arguments to the dissipative case
\lambda=0
.

Lemma 3.9
.

The following hold:

(1)

{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}\subset\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{T\kern-1.5ptX})
is totally
\lambda
-dissipative if and only if
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}^{\lambda}
(cf. (
2.18
)) is totally
0
-dissipative;

(2)

{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}\subset\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{T\kern-1.5ptX})
is maximal totally
\lambda
-dissipative if and only if
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}^{\lambda}
is maximal totally
0
-dissipative;

(3)

{\boldsymbol{B}}\subset\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{X}
is invariant by measure-preserving isomorphisms (resp. law invariant) if and only if
{\boldsymbol{B}}^{\lambda}:={\boldsymbol{B}}-\lambda\boldsymbol{i}_{\mathcal{X}}
is invariant by measure-preserving isomorphisms (resp. law invariant);

(4)

{\boldsymbol{B}}\subset\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{X}
is the Lagrangian representation of
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}\subset\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{T\kern-1.5ptX})
if and only if
{\boldsymbol{B}}^{\lambda}
is the Lagrangian representation of
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}^{\lambda}
.

Proof.

The proof of claim (1) is similar to
[
27
, Lemma 4.6]
and it is based on the bijectivity of the map
L^{\lambda}:=(\mathsf{x},\mathsf{v}-\lambda\mathsf{x}):\mathsf{T\kern-1.5ptX}\to\mathsf{T\kern-1.5ptX}
. Hence, if
\Phi_{i}\in{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
and
\Phi_{i}^{\lambda}:=L^{\lambda}_{\sharp}\Phi_{i}\in{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}^{\lambda}
,
i=1,2
, then
\boldsymbol{\vartheta}\in\Gamma(\Phi_{0},\Phi_{1})
if and only if
\boldsymbol{\vartheta}^{\lambda}\in\Gamma(\Phi_{0}^{\lambda},\Phi_{1}^{\lambda})
, with
\boldsymbol{\vartheta}^{\lambda}=(\mathsf{x}^{0},\mathsf{v}^{0}-\lambda\mathsf{x}^{0},\mathsf{x}^{1},\mathsf{v}^{1}-\lambda\mathsf{x}^{1})_{\sharp}\boldsymbol{\vartheta}
. We can thus prove only the left-to-right implication, the other will follow from the same procedure. We have

\displaystyle\int\langle v_{1}-v_{0},x_{1}-x_{0}\rangle\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{\vartheta}^{\lambda}(x_{0},v_{0},x_{1},v_{1})

\displaystyle=\int\langle v_{1}-v_{0}-\lambda(x_{1}-x_{0}),x_{1}-x_{0}\rangle\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{\vartheta}(x_{0},v_{0},x_{1},v_{1})

\displaystyle=\int\langle v_{1}-v_{0},x_{1}-x_{0}\rangle\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{\vartheta}-\lambda\int|x_{1}-x_{0}|^{2}\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{\vartheta}

\displaystyle\leq 0,

by total
\lambda
-dissipativity of
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
.

Items (2), (3) and (4) are straightforward.
∎

A first basic fact is stated by the following Proposition.

Proposition 3.10
.

Let
{\boldsymbol{B}}\subset\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{X}
be the Lagrangian representation of
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}\subset\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{T\kern-1.5ptX})
according to Definition
3.8
.
Then
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
is totally
\lambda
-dissipative
if and only if
{\boldsymbol{B}}
is
\lambda
-dissipative.

Proof.

By Lemma
3.9
and Remark
A.1
, it is sufficient to prove the result in case
\lambda=0
.
Let us first assume that
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
is totally dissipative.
Let
(X_{0},V_{0}),(X_{1},V_{1})\in{\boldsymbol{B}}
.
Since
\Phi_{0}=(X_{0},V_{0})_{\sharp}\mathbb{P}\in{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
,

\Phi_{1}=(X_{1},V_{1})_{\sharp}\mathbb{P}\in{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}

and
\boldsymbol{\vartheta}:=(X_{0},V_{0},X_{1},V_{1})_{\sharp}\mathbb{P}\in\Gamma(\Phi_{0},\Phi_{1})
,
(
3.9
)
yields

\int\langle V_{1}-V_{0},X_{1}-X_{0}\rangle\,\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}=\int\langle v_{1}-v_{0},x_{1}-x_{0}\rangle\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{\vartheta}\leq 0.

In order to prove the converse implication,
let us assume that
{\boldsymbol{B}}
is dissipative and
take
\Phi_{0},\Phi_{1}\in{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
,
\boldsymbol{\vartheta}\in\Gamma(\Phi_{0},\Phi_{1})
and
(X_{0},V_{0},X_{1},V_{1})\in\mathcal{X}^{4}
such that
(X_{0},V_{0},X_{1},V_{1})_{\sharp}\mathbb{P}=\boldsymbol{\vartheta}
.
Since
\Phi_{0},\Phi_{1}\in{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
,
there exist
(X_{0}^{\prime},V_{0}^{\prime})\in{\boldsymbol{B}}
and
(X_{1}^{\prime},V_{1}^{\prime})\in{\boldsymbol{B}}
such that

(X_{0}^{\prime},V_{0}^{\prime})_{\sharp}\mathbb{P}=\Phi_{0}=(X_{0},V_{0})_{\sharp}\mathbb{P},\qquad(X_{1}^{\prime},V_{1}^{\prime})_{\sharp}\mathbb{P}=\Phi_{1}=(X_{1},V_{1})_{\sharp}\mathbb{P}.

By the law invariance of
{\boldsymbol{B}}
, we have that
(X_{0},V_{0}),(X_{1},V_{1})\in{\boldsymbol{B}}
, so that

\int\langle v_{1}-v_{0},x_{1}-x_{0}\rangle\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{\vartheta}=\langle V_{1}-V_{0},X_{1}-X_{0}\rangle_{\mathcal{X}}\leq 0

by the dissipativity of
{\boldsymbol{B}}
.
∎

Example 3.11
.

Let us consider a map
\boldsymbol{f}:\mathcal{S}\left(\mathsf{X}\right)\to\mathsf{X}

(recall (
2.15
))
such that there exists
L>0
for which we have

|\boldsymbol{f}(x_{1},\mu_{1})-\boldsymbol{f}(x_{0},\mu_{0})|\leq L\left(W_{2}(\mu_{0},\mu_{1})+|x_{0}-x_{1}|\right)\quad\text{ for every }(x_{0},\mu_{0}),\ (x_{1},\mu_{1})\in\mathcal{S}\left(\mathsf{X}\right).

We can also identify
\boldsymbol{f}
with the map sending
\mu\mapsto f(\cdot,\mu)\in\mathrm{Lip}(\mathsf{X};\mathsf{X})
(compare with the framework analyzed by Bonnet and Frankowska in
[
13
,
16
]
and with the hypoteses in
[
24
,
1
]
).
Let us define the map
\boldsymbol{B}:\mathcal{X}\to\mathcal{X}
and the (single-valued, deterministic) PVF
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}\subset\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{T\kern-1.5ptX})
as

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{B}(X)(\omega)

\displaystyle:=\boldsymbol{f}(X(\omega),\iota_{X}),\quad

\displaystyle X\in\mathsf{X},\,\omega\in\Omega,

\displaystyle{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}[\mu]

\displaystyle:=(\boldsymbol{i}_{\mathsf{X}},\boldsymbol{f}(\cdot,\mu))_{\sharp}\mu,\quad

\displaystyle\mu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{X}).

It is not difficult to check that
\boldsymbol{B}
is
2L
-Lipschitz and that

{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
is maximal
2L
-totally dissipative. Indeed, for every
X,Y\in\mathcal{X}
, we have

\displaystyle|\boldsymbol{B}X-\boldsymbol{B}Y|_{\mathcal{X}}

\displaystyle=\left(\int_{\Omega}|\boldsymbol{B}X(\omega)-\boldsymbol{B}Y(\omega)|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}(\omega)\right)^{1/2}

\displaystyle=\left(\int_{\Omega}|\boldsymbol{f}(X(\omega),\iota_{X}))-\boldsymbol{f}(Y(\omega),\iota_{Y})|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}(\omega)\right)^{1/2}

\displaystyle\leq L\left(\int_{\Omega}\left(W_{2}(\iota_{X},\iota_{Y})+|X(\omega)-Y(\omega)|\right)^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}(\omega)\right)^{1/2}

\displaystyle\leq L\left(\left(\int_{\Omega}W_{2}^{2}(\iota_{X},\iota_{Y})\,\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}(\omega)\right)^{1/2}+\left(\int_{\Omega}|X(\omega)-Y(\omega)|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}(\omega)\right)^{1/2}\right)

\displaystyle\leq 2L|X-Y|_{\mathcal{X}}

so that
\boldsymbol{B}
is
2L
-dissipative and therefore

{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
is
2L
-totally dissipative as well by
Proposition
3.10
. Maximality follows by the maximality of
\boldsymbol{B}
and the next Theorem.

Theorem 3.12

(Maximal dissipativity)
.

(1)

Every
\lambda
-dissipative operator
{\boldsymbol{B}}\subset\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{X}

which is invariant by measure-preserving isomorphisms has
a maximal
\lambda
-dissipative extension with domain included in
\overline{\operatorname{co}}\left(\mathrm{D}({\boldsymbol{B}})\right)
which is
invariant by measure-preserving isomorphisms (and therefore also law invariant).

(2)

Let us suppose that

{\boldsymbol{B}}\subset\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{X}
is the
\lambda
-dissipative Lagrangian representation
of the totally
\lambda
-dissipative
MPVF

{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}\subset\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{T\kern-1.5ptX})
.
Then
{\boldsymbol{B}}
is maximal
\lambda
-dissipative if and only if

{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
is maximal totally
\lambda
-dissipative.

(3)

If
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}\subset\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{T\kern-1.5ptX})
is a totally
\lambda
-dissipative
MPVF
with domain included in a closed and totally convex set
\mathrm{C}
, then there exists a maximal totally
\lambda
-dissipative extension of
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
with domain included in
\mathrm{C}
.

Proof.

By Lemma
3.9
and Remark
A.1
, it is sufficient to prove the result in case
\lambda=0
.
Claim (1) is
[
28
, Theorem 4.5]
. Notice that, being maximal
\lambda
-dissipative and invariant by measure-preserving isomorphisms, a maximal
\lambda
-dissipative extension of
{\boldsymbol{B}}
is also law invariant by Lemma
3.3
.

Claim (2) follows by
the equivalence result of
Proposition
3.10

and by Claim 1. In fact, if
{\boldsymbol{B}}
is
maximal dissipative it is clear that
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
is maximal. Conversely,
suppose that
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
is maximal and
{\boldsymbol{B}}

is its Lagrangian representation.
By contradiction, if
{\boldsymbol{B}}
is not maximal, Claim 1 shows that
there exists a maximal and proper extension
\hat{\boldsymbol{B}}

of
{\boldsymbol{B}}
which is law invariant.
Therefore,
\hat{\boldsymbol{B}}

induces a strict extension of
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}

which is totally dissipative.

Claim (3) is a consequence of Claim 1 and Claim 2.
∎

Remark 3.13
.

Notice that if
{\boldsymbol{B}}
is the Lagrangian representation
of a maximal totally
\lambda
-dissipative MPVF
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
,
then

\iota^{-1}\big(\overline{D({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})}\big)=\overline{D({\boldsymbol{B}})}
.
In fact, it is sufficient to prove that if
\iota(X)=\mu\in\overline{D({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})}
then
X\in\overline{D({\boldsymbol{B}})}
, since the converse
inclusion is trivial. We can thus find a sequence
\mu_{n}\in D({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})
converging to
\mu
in
\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{X})
. Applying the last
statement of Theorem
B.5

we can then find a sequence
X_{n}\in\mathcal{X}

such that
\iota(X_{n})=\mu_{n}
and

\lim_{n\to\infty}|X_{n}-X|_{\mathcal{X}}=0
.
We deduce
that
X_{n}\in D({\boldsymbol{B}})
by Remark
3.5

and therefore
X\in\overline{D({\boldsymbol{B}})}
.

The uniqueness of a maximal totally dissipative extension of a given totally dissipative MPVF is investigated in Part
and, in particular, in Theorem
8.6
of which we report a simplified version here.

Theorem 3.14
.

Let
\mathsf{U}\subset\mathsf{X}
be open, convex, non-empty and let
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}\subset\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{T\kern-1.5ptX})
be a totally
\lambda
-dissipative
MPVF
whose domain satisfies

\mathcal{P}_{f}(\mathsf{U})\subset D({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})\subset\mathcal{P}_{2}(\overline{\mathsf{U}}),

where

\mathcal{P}_{f}(\mathsf{U}):=\Big\{\mu\in\mathcal{P}(\mathsf{U})\,:\,\supp(\mu)\text{ is finite}\Big\}
.
Then there exists a unique maximal totally
\lambda
-dissipative extension
\hat{{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}}
of
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
with domain included in
\mathcal{P}_{2}(\overline{\mathsf{U}})
.

We now apply Theorem
3.12
to get
useful insights on the structure of totally dissipative MPVFs.
The first result concerns the existence of a solution to the resolvent equation,
which provides an equivalent characterization of maximality and will
be the crucial tool to implement the Implicit Euler method, see
Corollary
4.7
.

Theorem 3.15

(Solution to the resolvent equation)
.

A totally
\lambda
-dissipative
MPVF

{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}\subset\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{T\kern-1.5ptX})
is maximal
\lambda
-dissipative if and only if
for every
\mu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{X})

and every
0<\tau<1/\lambda^{+}

there exists
\Phi\in{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}

such that
(\mathsf{x}-\tau\mathsf{v})_{\sharp}\Phi=\mu.

Proof.

Let

{\boldsymbol{B}}
be the Lagrangian representation of
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
that is
\lambda
-dissipative by Proposition
3.10
.
If
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
is maximal
\lambda
-dissipative, then
{\boldsymbol{B}}

is maximal
\lambda
-dissipative as well by Theorem
3.12
(3), so that
for every
Y\in\mathcal{X}
with

Y_{\sharp}\mathbb{P}=\mu
and
0<\tau<1/\lambda^{+}

there exists

(X,V)\in{\boldsymbol{B}}
such that

X-\tau V=Y
(cf. Theorem
A.2
(1))
so that
\Phi:=(X,V)_{\sharp}\mathbb{P}\in{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}

satisfies
(\mathsf{x}-\tau\mathsf{v})_{\sharp}\Phi=\mu.

Conversely, let us now suppose
that
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
is not maximal
\lambda
-dissipative,
so that
{\boldsymbol{B}}
is not maximal
\lambda
-dissipative
and it admits a proper maximal
\lambda
-dissipative law invariant extension
\hat{\boldsymbol{B}}
by Theorem
3.12
. Let
(\tilde{X},\tilde{V})\in\hat{\boldsymbol{B}}\setminus{\boldsymbol{B}}
,
0<\tau<1/\lambda^{+}
,

\tilde{Y}:=\tilde{X}-\tau\tilde{V}
, and
\mu:=\tilde{Y}_{\sharp}\mathbb{P}.

We claim that the equation

\Phi\in{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
,
(\mathsf{x}-\tau\mathsf{v})_{\sharp}\Phi=\mu

has no solution. We argue by contradiction,
and we suppose that

\Phi\in{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
is a solution: we could find

(X,V)\in{\boldsymbol{B}}

such that setting

(X,V)_{\sharp}\mathbb{P}=\Phi

and setting

Y:=X-\tau V

we have
Y_{\sharp}\mathbb{P}=\mu.

We use the maximal
\lambda
-dissipativity of
\hat{\boldsymbol{B}}
and we denote by
\hat{\boldsymbol{J}}_{\tau}
the resolvent associated to
\hat{\boldsymbol{B}}
,
by
\hat{\boldsymbol{j}}_{\tau}
the map
induced by Theorem
3.4
as in (
3.2
),
and we set

\hat{\boldsymbol{b}}_{\tau}(x):=\frac{1}{\tau}(\hat{\boldsymbol{j}}_{\tau}(x,\mu)-x)
,
x\in\supp(\mu)
.

We have
\tilde{X}=\hat{\boldsymbol{J}}_{\tau}\tilde{Y}=\hat{\boldsymbol{j}}_{\tau}(\tilde{Y},\mu)
,
X=\hat{\boldsymbol{J}}_{\tau}Y=\hat{\boldsymbol{j}}_{\tau}(Y,\mu)
,

\tilde{V}=\frac{1}{\tau}(\tilde{X}-\tilde{Y})=\hat{\boldsymbol{b}}_{\tau}(\tilde{Y})
and

V=\frac{1}{\tau}(X-Y)=\hat{\boldsymbol{b}}_{\tau}(Y).

It follows that

(\tilde{X},\tilde{V})_{\sharp}\mathbb{P}=(\hat{\boldsymbol{j}}_{\tau}(\cdot,\mu),\hat{\boldsymbol{b}}_{\tau})_{\sharp}\mu=(X,V)_{\sharp}\mathbb{P}=\Phi\in{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}

so that
(\tilde{X},\tilde{V})

has the same law of
(X,V)

and therefore belongs to
{\boldsymbol{B}}
,
a contradiction.
∎

We now show that a maximal totally
\lambda
-dissipative MPVF is sequentially closed in the strong-weak topology of

\mathcal{P}_{2}^{sw}(\mathsf{T\kern-1.5ptX})
, recall (
2.20
).

Proposition 3.16

(Strong-weak closure)
.

The sequential strong-weak closure
\operatorname{cl}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})

of a totally
\lambda
-dissipative
MPVF

{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}

is totally
\lambda
-dissipative as well.
In particular, if
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
is maximal, then
\operatorname{cl}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})={\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
.

Proof.

As usual, it is sufficient to check the property for
\lambda=0
.
Let
\Phi^{\prime},\Phi^{\prime\prime}\in\operatorname{cl}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})
and
\boldsymbol{\vartheta}\in\Gamma(\Phi^{\prime},\Phi^{\prime\prime})
.
Denoting by
\{e_{i}\}_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
an orthonotmal system for
\mathsf{X}
, we introduce on
\mathsf{X}
and on
\mathsf{T\kern-1.5ptX}
respectively the distances

\mathsf{d}^{w}(v_{1},v_{2}):=\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}2^{-i}\big(|\langle v_{1}-v_{2},e_{i}\rangle|\land 1\big),\quad\mathsf{d}^{sw}((x_{1},v_{1}),(x_{2},v_{2})):=\Big(|x_{1}-x_{2}|_{\mathsf{X}}^{2}+\mathsf{d}^{w}(v_{1},v_{2})^{2}\Big)\Big)^{1/2}

whose induced topologies are weaker than the weak (resp. the strong-weak)
topology of
\mathsf{X}
(resp.
\mathsf{T\kern-1.5ptX}
), see also the proof of
[
40
, Proposition 3.4]
.
Denoting by
W_{2}^{sw}
the
2
-Wasserstein distance on

\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{T\kern-1.5ptX})
induced by
\mathsf{d}^{sw}
, we have

\Phi_{n}\to\Phi\quad\text{in }\mathcal{P}_{2}^{sw}(\mathsf{T\kern-1.5ptX})\quad\Rightarrow\quad W_{2}^{sw}(\Phi_{n},\Phi)\to 0.

By definition of
\operatorname{cl}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})
we can find two
sequences

(\Phi_{n}^{\prime})_{n\in\mathbb{N}},(\Phi_{n}^{\prime\prime})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
in
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}

respectively converging to
\Phi^{\prime}
and
\Phi^{\prime\prime}
in

\mathcal{P}_{2}^{sw}(\mathsf{T\kern-1.5ptX})
. We denote by
\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{n}^{\prime}\in\Gamma_{o}^{sw}(\Phi_{n}^{\prime},\Phi^{\prime})
and

\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{n}^{\prime\prime}\in\Gamma_{o}^{sw}(\Phi^{\prime\prime},\Phi_{n}^{\prime\prime})

the corresponding optimal plans for
W_{2}^{sw}
.

Denoting the elements of
\mathsf{T\kern-1.5ptX}^{4}
by

(x_{1}^{\prime},v_{1}^{\prime},x_{1},v_{1},x_{2},v_{2},x_{2}^{\prime\prime},v_{2}^{\prime\prime})
and using the gluing Lemma we can find
a plan
\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{n}\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{T\kern-1.5ptX}^{4})
such that

(\mathsf{x}_{1}^{\prime},\mathsf{v}_{1}^{\prime},\mathsf{x}_{1},\mathsf{v}_{1})_{\sharp}\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{n}=\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{n}^{\prime}
,

(\mathsf{x}_{1},\mathsf{v}_{1},\mathsf{x}_{2},\mathsf{v}_{2})_{\sharp}\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{n}=\boldsymbol{\vartheta}
,

(\mathsf{x}_{2},\mathsf{v}_{2},\mathsf{x}_{2}^{\prime\prime},\mathsf{v}_{2}^{\prime\prime})_{\sharp}\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{n}=\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{n}^{\prime\prime}
.
We also have

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}\int|x_{1}^{\prime}-x_{1}|^{2}+|x_{2}-x_{2}^{\prime\prime}|^{2}+d^{w}(v_{1}^{\prime},v_{1})^{2}+d^{w}(v_{2}^{\prime\prime},v_{2})^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{n}=0,

\displaystyle\sup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\int\Big(|v_{1}^{\prime}|^{2}+|v_{1}|^{2}+|v_{2}|^{2}+|v_{2}^{\prime\prime}|^{2}\Big)\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{n}<\infty,

so that
setting
\tilde{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}_{n}:=(\mathsf{x}_{1}^{\prime},\mathsf{x}_{2}^{\prime\prime},\mathsf{v}_{1}^{\prime},\mathsf{v}_{2}^{\prime\prime})_{\sharp}\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{n}
we have

\tilde{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}_{n}\to(\mathsf{x}_{1},\mathsf{x}_{2},\mathsf{v}_{1},\mathsf{v}_{2})_{\sharp}\boldsymbol{\vartheta}\quad\text{in }\mathcal{P}_{2}^{sw}(\mathsf{X}^{2}\times\mathsf{X}^{2}).

Since
(\mathsf{x}_{1}^{\prime},\mathsf{v}_{1}^{\prime},\mathsf{x}_{2}^{\prime\prime},\mathsf{v}_{2}^{\prime\prime})_{\sharp}\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{n}\in\Gamma(\Phi_{n}^{\prime},\Phi_{n}^{\prime\prime})
, the total dissipativity of
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
yields

(3.11)

\int\langle\mathsf{v}_{1}-\mathsf{v}_{2},\mathsf{x}_{1}-\mathsf{x}_{2}\rangle\,\mathrm{d}\tilde{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}_{n}=\int\langle\mathsf{v}_{1}^{\prime}-\mathsf{v}_{2}^{\prime\prime},\mathsf{x}_{1}^{\prime}-\mathsf{x}_{2}^{\prime\prime}\rangle\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{n}\leq 0\quad\text{for every }n\in\mathbb{N}.

Since the function
\zeta(x_{1},x_{2};v_{1},v_{2}):=\langle\mathsf{v}_{1}-\mathsf{v}_{2},\mathsf{x}_{1}-\mathsf{x}_{2}\rangle

belongs to
\mathrm{C}^{sw}_{2}(\mathsf{X}^{2}\times\mathsf{X}^{2})
,
the convergence in
\mathcal{P}_{2}^{sw}(\mathsf{X}^{2}\times\mathsf{X}^{2})
is sufficient
to pass to the limit in (
3.11
) and thus get

\int\langle\mathsf{v}_{1}-\mathsf{v}_{2},\mathsf{x}_{1}-\mathsf{x}_{2}\rangle\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{\vartheta}\leq 0.\qed

We can also prove that
the sections
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}[\mu]
of a maximal totally
dissipative MPVF are (conditionally) totally convex.
In the following statement
we consider the space
\mathsf{X}\times\mathsf{X}^{N}

whose variables are denoted by
(x,v_{1},\cdots,v_{N})
and
the corresponding projections are

\mathsf{x}(x,v_{1},\cdots,v_{N}):=x,

\mathsf{v}_{i}(x,v_{1},\cdots,v_{N}):=v_{i}.

Proposition 3.17

(Total convexity of sections of maximal totally dissipative MPVF)
.

If
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}\subset\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{T\kern-1.5ptX})
is a maximal totally

\lambda
-dissipative
MPVF
,
then for every
\mu\in D({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})

the section
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}[\mu]

satisfies the following total convexity property:

(3.12)

\begin{gathered}\text{if $\Lambda\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{X}\times\mathsf{X}^{N})$
satisfies
$(\mathsf{x},\mathsf{v}_{i})_{\sharp}\Lambda\in{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}[\mu]$
and $\alpha_{i}\geq 0$, $i=1,\cdots,N$ with $\sum_{i}\alpha_{i}=1$, then}\\
(\mathsf{x},\sum_{i}\alpha_{i}\mathsf{v}_{i})_{\sharp}\Lambda\in{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}[\mu].\end{gathered}

Proof.

Since
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
is maximal totally
\lambda
-dissipative, by Theorem
3.12
, its Lagrangian representation
{\boldsymbol{B}}\subset\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{X}
is maximal
\lambda
-dissipative.

We can find
(X,V_{1},V_{2},\cdots V_{N})\in\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{X}^{N}

such that
(X,V_{1},V_{2},\cdots V_{N})_{\sharp}\mathbb{P}=\Lambda.

We deduce that
(X,V_{i})\in{\boldsymbol{B}}
since
(X,V_{i})_{\sharp}\mathbb{P}\in{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
.
since the sections of
{\boldsymbol{B}}
are convex, we deduce that

(X,\sum_{i}\alpha_{i}V_{i})\in{\boldsymbol{B}}
as well, so that

(\mathsf{x},\sum_{i}\alpha_{i}\mathsf{v}_{i})_{\sharp}\Lambda=(X,\sum_{i}\alpha_{i}V_{i})_{\sharp}\mathbb{P}\in{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}.\qed

We can now derive a remarkable information on the structure of
a totally dissipative MPVF,
which involves the barycentric projection
introduced in (
2.13
).

Theorem 3.18

(Barycentric projection)
.

Let
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
be a
MPVF
and

\mu\in\mathrm{D}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})
such that

{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}[\mu]
is closed in
\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{T\kern-1.5ptX})
and
satisfies the total convexity property (
3.12
).
Then
\operatorname{bar}\left({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}\right)[\mu]\subset{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}[\mu]
.
In particular, if
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
is a maximal totally
\lambda
-dissipative
MPVF
,
then
\operatorname{bar}\left({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}\right)\subset{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
.

Proof.

We use an argument which is clearly inspired by the Law of Large Numbers.

Let
\{\Phi_{x}\}_{x\in\mathsf{X}}

be the disintegration of
\Phi\in{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
w.r.t. its first marginal
\mu\in\mathrm{D}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})
.
For a given integer
N

and every
x\in\mathsf{X}
we define the product measure

\Phi_{x}^{N}:=(\Phi_{x})^{\otimes N}\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{X}^{N})

and the corresponding plan

\Lambda^{N}:=\int\delta_{x}\otimes\Phi_{x}^{N}\,\mathrm{d}\mu(x)\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{X}\times\mathsf{X}^{N}).

It is clear that

\Lambda^{N}
satisfies the condition
of Proposition
3.17
:
choosing
\alpha_{i}:=1/N

we deduce that

\Psi^{N}:=(\mathsf{x},\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i}\mathsf{v}_{i})_{\sharp}\Lambda^{N}\in{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}[\mu].

Let now
\Psi:=(\boldsymbol{i}_{\mathsf{X}},\boldsymbol{b}_{\Phi})_{\sharp}\mu.

We can easily estimate
the squared Wasserstein distance between
\Psi
and
\Psi^{N}
by

\displaystyle W_{2}^{2}(\Psi^{N},\Psi)

\displaystyle\leq\int\Big|\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i}v_{i}-\boldsymbol{b}_{\Phi}(x)\Big|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\Lambda^{N}=\frac{1}{N}\int\Big|v-\boldsymbol{b}_{\Phi}(x)\Big|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\Phi

where we used the following
orthogonality for
i\neq j

\displaystyle\int\langle v_{i}-\boldsymbol{b}_{\Phi}(x),v_{j}-\boldsymbol{b}_{\Phi}(x)\rangle\,\mathrm{d}\Lambda^{N}

\displaystyle=\int\Big(\int\langle v_{i}-\boldsymbol{b}_{\Phi}(x),v_{j}-\boldsymbol{b}_{\Phi}(x)\rangle\,\mathrm{d}\Phi_{x}(v_{i})\otimes\Phi_{x}(v_{j})\Big)\,\mathrm{d}\mu(x)=0

and
the fact that

\displaystyle\int|v_{i}-\boldsymbol{b}_{\Phi}(x)|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\Lambda^{N}

\displaystyle=\int\Big(\int|v_{i}-\boldsymbol{b}_{\Phi}(x)|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\Phi_{x}(v_{i})\Big)\,\mathrm{d}\mu(x)=\int\Big|v-\boldsymbol{b}_{\Phi}(x)\Big|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\Phi.

We deduce that
\Psi^{N}\to\Psi

in
\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{T\kern-1.5ptX})
as
N\to+\infty
,
so that
\Psi\in{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}[\mu]

as well.
∎

Corollary 3.19
.

Let
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}\subset\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{T\kern-1.5ptX})
be a totally
\lambda
-dissipative
MPVF
. Then
the extended
MPVF

\tilde{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
defined
by

\tilde{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}:={\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}\cup\operatorname{bar}\left({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}\right),

with
\operatorname{bar}\left({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}\right)
as in (
2.13
), is totally
\lambda
-dissipative. In particular, for every

\Phi_{i}\in{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}[\mu_{i}]
,
i=1,2
, and every
\boldsymbol{\mu}\in\Gamma(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})
,

\int\langle\boldsymbol{b}_{\Phi_{1}}(x_{1})-\boldsymbol{b}_{\Phi_{2}}(x_{2}),x_{1}-x_{2}\rangle\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{\mu}(x_{1},x_{2})\leq\lambda\int|x_{1}-x_{2}|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{\mu}(x_{1},x_{2}).

Proof.

It is sufficient to consider an arbitrary maximal totally

\lambda
-dissipative extension
\hat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
of
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
: by the
previous Theorem
3.18

clearly
\hat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}\supset\tilde{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
.
∎

Theorem 3.20

(The minimal selection)
.

Let
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}\subset\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{T\kern-1.5ptX})
be a maximal totally
\lambda
-dissipative

MPVF
.

(1)

For every
\mu\in\mathrm{D}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})
there exists a unique vector field

\boldsymbol{f}^{\circ}[\mu]\in L^{2}(\mathsf{X},\mu;\mathsf{X})
such that

(3.13)

(\boldsymbol{i}_{\mathsf{X}},\boldsymbol{f}^{\circ}[\mu])_{\sharp}\mu\in{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}[\mu],\quad\int|\boldsymbol{f}^{\circ}[\mu]|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\mu\leq\int|v|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\Phi\quad\text{for every }\Phi\in{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}[\mu].

We denote the
minimal selection of
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
at
\mu
by

(3.14)

{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}^{\circ}[\mu]:=(\boldsymbol{i}_{\mathsf{X}},\boldsymbol{f}^{\circ}[\mu])_{\sharp}\mu.

(2)

If
{\boldsymbol{B}}
is the Lagrangian representation of
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
, then for
every
\mu\in\mathrm{D}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})
, we have

\boldsymbol{f}^{\circ}[\mu]=\boldsymbol{b}^{\circ}[\mu]\quad\mu\text{-a.e.},

where
\boldsymbol{b}^{\circ}
has been defined by (
3.6
)
and, if
0<\tau<1/\lambda^{+}
, the following hold

(3.15)

\displaystyle\int\big|\boldsymbol{b}_{\tau}(x,\mu)-\boldsymbol{f}^{\circ}[\mu](x)\big|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\mu

\displaystyle\leq\int\big|\boldsymbol{f}^{\circ}[\mu]\big|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\mu-(1-2\lambda\tau)\int\big|\boldsymbol{b}_{\tau}(x,\mu)\big|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\mu,

(3.16)

\displaystyle(1-\lambda\tau)^{2}\int\big|\boldsymbol{b}_{\tau}(x,\mu)\big|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\mu

\displaystyle\uparrow\int\big|\boldsymbol{f}^{\circ}[\mu]\big|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\mu\quad\text{as }\tau\downarrow 0

with
\boldsymbol{b}_{\tau}
as in (
3.3
).

(3)

The map
|{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}|_{2}:\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{X})\to[0,+\infty]
defined by

(3.17)

|{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}|_{2}(\mu):=\begin{cases}\displaystyle\int|\boldsymbol{f}^{\circ}[\mu]|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\mu&\text{if }\mu\in\mathrm{D}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}),\\
+\infty&\text{if }\mu\not\in\mathrm{D}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})\end{cases}

is lower semicontinuous.

(4)

Finally, if
\mathsf{Y}
is a Polish space,
\boldsymbol{\mu}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathsf{X}\times\mathsf{Y})

with marginal
\nu=\pi^{2}_{\sharp}\boldsymbol{\mu}
and the disintegration

\{\mu_{y}\}_{y\in\mathsf{Y}}
of
\boldsymbol{\mu}
w.r.t.
\nu
satisfies

(3.18)

\int_{\mathsf{X}\times\mathsf{Y}}|x|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{\mu}(x,y)+\int_{\mathsf{Y}}|{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}|_{2}(\mu_{y})\,\mathrm{d}\nu(y)<+\infty,

then the map
\boldsymbol{f}(x,y):=\boldsymbol{f}^{\circ}[\mu_{y}](x)
belongs to

L^{2}(\mathsf{X}\times\mathsf{Y},\boldsymbol{\mu};\mathsf{X})
(in particular it is uniquely defined up
to a
\boldsymbol{\mu}
-negligible set and it is
\boldsymbol{\mu}
-measurable).

Proof.

Claim (1) is an immediate consequence of the closure of
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
in

\mathcal{P}_{2}^{sw}(\mathsf{T\kern-1.5ptX})

(so that the map
\Phi\mapsto|\Phi|_{2}

has compact sublevels in the set
\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{T\kern-1.5ptX})
with fixed first marginal equal to
\mu
)
and of the previous Theorem
3.18
.

To prove the second claim,
it is enough to notice that, trivially,
\boldsymbol{b}^{\circ}(\cdot,\mu)

satisfies (
3.13
). Estimates (
3.15
) and (
3.16
)
follow
by Theorem
A.3
(5).

The third claim still follows immediately by the
closure of
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
in

\mathcal{P}_{2}^{sw}(\mathsf{T\kern-1.5ptX})

and the fact that the map
\Phi\mapsto|\Phi|_{2}^{2}

defined by (
2.5
) is lower semicontinuous
w.r.t. the topology of
\mathcal{P}_{2}^{sw}(\mathsf{T\kern-1.5ptX})
.

Let us now prove claim (4).
We first notice that (
3.18
) yields
\mu_{y}\in D({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})
for

\nu
-a.e.
y\in\mathsf{Y}
.
Let us now prove that
the map
\boldsymbol{b}_{\tau}(x,y):=\boldsymbol{b}_{\tau}(x,\mu_{y})
is

\boldsymbol{\mu}
-measurable.

Recall that the set

\mathcal{S}_{0}:=\big\{(x,\mu)\in\mathsf{X}\times\mathcal{P}(\mathsf{X}):x\in\supp(\mu)\big\}

is a
G_{\delta}
(thus Borel, cf.
[
32
, Formula (4.3)]
) subset
of
\mathsf{X}\times\mathcal{P}(\mathsf{X})
.
Since the inclusion map of
\mathsf{X}\times\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{X})
in

\mathsf{X}\times\mathcal{P}(\mathsf{X})
is continuous,
we deduce that

\mathcal{S}:=\mathcal{S}_{0}\cap\left(\mathsf{X}\times\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{X})\right)

is a
G_{\delta}
set in
\mathsf{X}\times\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{X})
.

Since the map
j(x,y):=(x,\mu_{y})
is Borel
from
\mathsf{X}\times\mathsf{Y}
to
\mathsf{X}\times\mathcal{P}(\mathsf{Y})
,
we deduce that the set

\mathcal{S}^{\prime}:=j^{-1}(\mathcal{S})=(\big\{(x,y)\in\mathsf{X}\times\mathsf{Y}:x\in\supp(\mu_{y})\big\}
is Borel in
\mathsf{X}\times\mathsf{Y}
and it is immediate to check that

\boldsymbol{\mu}
is concentrated on
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}
. Since the map
(x,\mu)\mapsto\boldsymbol{b}_{\tau}(x,\mu)
is continuous in
\mathcal{S}
(cf. Theorem
3.4
) then its composition with
j
(which is the map
(x,y)\mapsto\boldsymbol{b}_{\tau}(x,\mu_{y}))
is
\boldsymbol{\mu}
-measurable.
Passing to the limit as
\tau\downarrow 0
and using (
3.15
) and (
3.16
)
we conclude that
\boldsymbol{b}_{\tau}\to\boldsymbol{f}
in
L^{2}(\mathsf{X}\times\mathsf{Y},\boldsymbol{\mu};\mathsf{X})
so that also
\boldsymbol{f}
is
\boldsymbol{\mu}
-measurable.
∎

We now show that discrete measures are sufficient to reconstruct a
maximal totally
\lambda
-dissipative MPVF. For a general Polish space
X
, we set

(3.19)

\mathcal{P}_{f}(X):=\Big\{\mu\in\mathcal{P}(X)\,:\,\supp(\mu)\text{ is finite}\Big\},

(3.20)

\mathcal{P}_{f,N}(X):=\Big\{\mu\in\mathcal{P}_{f}(X):N\mu(A)\in\mathbb{N}\quad\text{for every }A\subset X\Big\},\quad\mathcal{P}_{f,\infty}(X):=\bigcup_{N\in\mathbb{N}}\mathcal{P}_{f,N}(X).

Corollary 3.21
.

Let
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}\subset\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{T\kern-1.5ptX})
be a maximal totally
\lambda
-dissipative
MPVF
and let

\mathrm{D}_{f,\infty}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}):=\mathcal{P}_{f,\infty}(\mathsf{X})\cap\mathrm{D}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}).

Then for every
\mu\in\mathrm{D}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})
there exists a sequence

\mu_{n}\in\mathrm{D}_{f,\infty}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})
such that

{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}^{\circ}[\mu_{n}]\to{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}^{\circ}[\mu]
in
\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{T\kern-1.5ptX})
as
n\to\infty
, where
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}^{\circ}
has been defined in (
3.14
).
Moreover, a measure
\Phi\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{T\kern-1.5ptX})
with
\mathsf{x}_{\sharp}\Phi\in\overline{\mathrm{D}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})}
belongs to
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
if and only if
for every
\mu\in\mathrm{D}_{f,\infty}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})
and every
\boldsymbol{\gamma}\in\Gamma(\Phi,\mu)
we have

\int\langle v-\boldsymbol{f}^{\circ}(y,\mu),x-y\rangle\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{\gamma}(x,v,y)\leq\lambda\int|x-y|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{\gamma}(x,v,y).

Proof.

We denote by
\boldsymbol{B}\subset\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{X}

the Lagrangian representation of
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
and
we set
D:=\iota^{-1}(\mathcal{P}_{f,\infty}(\mathsf{X}))
.
Since
\mathcal{P}_{f,\infty}(\mathsf{X})
is dense in
\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{X})
, by e.g. the last part of Theorem
B.5
we have that
D
is dense in
\mathcal{X}
and by Theorem

3.4
(see in particular (
3.2
)) it satisfies

\boldsymbol{J}_{\tau}(D)\subset D
.
We can thus apply Corollary
A.16
.
∎

3.3.
Totally dissipative PVFs concentrated on maps

We devote this section to the study of the important case
of deterministic MPVFs and, in particular, of single-valued and everywhere defined PVFs.
Recall that
for a deterministic PVF, total
\lambda
-dissipativity can be equivalently stated as in Remark
3.7
.

We start with a result on deterministic MPVFs which gives a sufficient condition to pass from dissipativity to total dissipativity. Its proof, in a more general framework, is deferred to Section
8
(see in particular Theorem
8.5
).

Theorem 3.22
.

Assume that
\dim(\mathsf{X})\geq 2
. Let
\mathsf{U}\subset\mathsf{X}
be an open, convex, non-empty subset of
\mathsf{X}
and let
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}\subset\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{T\kern-1.5ptX})
be a deterministic
\lambda
-dissipative
MPVF
with domain
D({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})=\mathcal{P}_{f}(\mathsf{U})
. Then
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
is totally
\lambda
-dissipative.

Definition 3.23

(Demicontinuity)
.

A single-valued
PVF

{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
is demicontinuous if
the map
\mu\mapsto{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}[\mu]
satisfies

\mu_{n}\to\mu\quad\text{in }\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{X})\quad\Rightarrow\quad{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}[\mu_{n}]\to{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}[\mu]\quad\text{in }\mathcal{P}_{2}^{sw}(\mathsf{T\kern-1.5ptX}).

A single-valued
PVF

{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
is hemicontinuous if its domain is totally convex and, for every
\boldsymbol{\gamma}\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{X}\times\mathsf{X})
with marginals in
\mathrm{D}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})
, the restriction of
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
to the
set
\{\mathsf{x}^{t}_{\sharp}\boldsymbol{\gamma}:t\in[0,1]\}
is demicontinuous.

Theorem 3.24

(Characterization of deterministic totally dissipative PVF)
.

Let
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
be a
single-valued
totally
\lambda
-dissipative

PVF
.

(1)

If
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
is maximal, then it is deterministic and

{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}[\mu]=(\boldsymbol{i}_{\mathsf{X}},\boldsymbol{f}^{\circ}[\mu])_{\sharp}\mu
for every
\mu\in D({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})
, where
\boldsymbol{f}^{\circ}
is the minimal selection of
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
as in Theorem
3.20
.

(2)

If
\mathrm{D}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})=\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{X})
, then
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
is maximal if and only if
it is deterministic and demicontinuous
(or, equivalently, deterministic and hemicontinuous)

(3)

If
\mathrm{D}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})=\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{X})
and

{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}[\mu]=(\boldsymbol{i}_{\mathsf{X}},\boldsymbol{f}[\mu])_{\sharp}\mu

for every
\mu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{X})
, then
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
is maximal
if and only if for every
\zeta\in\mathrm{C}_{2}^{sw}(\mathsf{T\kern-1.5ptX})

and for every sequence
\mu_{n}\to\mu
in
\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{T\kern-1.5ptX})

\lim_{n\to\infty}\int\zeta(x,\boldsymbol{f}[\mu_{n}](x))\,\mathrm{d}\mu_{n}(x)=\int\zeta(x,\boldsymbol{f}[\mu](x))\,\mathrm{d}\mu(x).

Proof.

Claim (1) is an obvious consequence of Theorem
3.18
.

Claim (2): let us first assume that

{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
is maximal and let
{\boldsymbol{B}}
be its Lagrangian representation.
Since
D({\boldsymbol{B}})=\mathcal{X}
,
{\boldsymbol{B}}
is locally bounded (see Theorem
A.3
(3)) so that
if a sequence
\mu_{n}
is converging to
\mu
in

\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{X})
and
\Phi_{n}={\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}[\mu_{n}]
, we can assume that
there exists a constant
C>0
such that

\int|v|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\Phi_{n}(x,v)\leq C\quad\text{for every }n\in\mathbb{N}.

The compactness
criterium of Proposition
2.5

shows that
(\Phi_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
is relatively compact in

\mathcal{P}_{2}^{sw}(\mathsf{T\kern-1.5ptX})
.
On the other hand,
since
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}=\operatorname{cl}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})

by Proposition
3.16
, we know
that any accumulation point of
\Phi_{n}
belongs to
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}

and therefore it should coincide with
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}[\mu]
.

In order to prove the converse implication,
it is sufficient to consider the case

\lambda=0
and
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
deterministic and hemicontinuous;
we try to reproduce the argument of
[
17
]
in the measure
theoretic framework.

We first observe that
the Lagrangian representation
{\boldsymbol{B}}
of
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}

is everywhere defined and single-valued, since

\iota(X)=\mu
, and
\iota^{2}(X,V)={\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}[\mu]=(\boldsymbol{i}_{\mathsf{X}},\boldsymbol{f})_{\sharp}\mu
yield

V=\boldsymbol{f}\circ X
.

Let
(Y,W)\in\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{X}

satisfying

\int\langle{\boldsymbol{B}}X-W,X-Y\rangle_{\mathcal{X}}\,\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}\leq 0\quad\text{for every }X\in\mathcal{X}.

Replacing
X
with
Y_{t}:=(1-t)Y+tX
,
t\in(0,1)
and setting

\mu_{t}:=\iota(Y_{t})
,
\boldsymbol{f}_{t}:=\boldsymbol{f}[\mu_{t}]
,
V_{t}:=\boldsymbol{f}_{t}\circ Y_{t}={\boldsymbol{B}}(Y_{t})
we get

\int\langle\boldsymbol{f}_{t}(Y_{t})-W,Y_{t}-Y\rangle_{\mathcal{X}}\,\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}=\frac{t}{1-t}\int\langle\boldsymbol{f}_{t}(Y_{t})-W,X-Y_{t}\rangle_{\mathcal{X}}\,\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}\leq 0\quad\text{for every }X\in\mathcal{X},

so that

(3.21)

\int\langle V_{t}-W,X-Y_{t}\rangle_{\mathcal{X}}\,\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}\leq 0\quad\text{for every }X\in\mathcal{X}.

Let us now set
\boldsymbol{\vartheta}_{t}:=(X,Y_{t},V_{t})_{\sharp}\mathbb{P}\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{X}^{2}\times\mathsf{X})
. Denoting by
\mathsf{x},\mathsf{y},\mathsf{v}
the projections
of the points of
\mathsf{X}^{3}
to their components, since
(\mathsf{y},\mathsf{v})_{\sharp}\boldsymbol{\vartheta}_{t}={\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}[\mu_{t}]
,
by hemicontinuity assumption we know that

(\mathsf{y},\mathsf{v})_{\sharp}\boldsymbol{\vartheta}_{t}\to(Y,\boldsymbol{f}_{0}\circ Y)_{\sharp}\mathbb{P}={\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}[\mu_{0}],\quad\text{in $\mathcal{P}_{2}^{sw}(\mathsf{X}\times\mathsf{X})$ as $t\downarrow 0$}.

On the other hand,
(\mathsf{x},\mathsf{y})_{\sharp}\boldsymbol{\vartheta}_{t}=\iota^{2}(X,Y_{t})
converges to
\iota^{2}(X,Y)
in
\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{X}^{2})
so that
by compactness, we can also find a sequence
n\mapsto t(n)\downarrow 0

such that
\boldsymbol{\vartheta}_{t(n)}\to\boldsymbol{\vartheta}

in
\mathcal{P}_{2}^{sw}(\mathsf{X}^{2}\times\mathsf{X})
. Clearly

(\mathsf{y},\mathsf{v})_{\sharp}\boldsymbol{\vartheta}=(\boldsymbol{i}_{\mathsf{X}},\boldsymbol{f}_{0})_{\sharp}\mu_{0}
is concentrated on a graph,
so that
\boldsymbol{\vartheta}=(X,Y,\boldsymbol{f}_{0}\circ Y)_{\sharp}\mathbb{P}
.

Since

\int\langle\boldsymbol{f}_{t}(Y_{t}),X-Y_{t}\rangle\,\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}=\int\langle v,x-y\rangle\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{\vartheta}_{t}

and the function
\zeta(x,y,v):=\langle v,x-y\rangle

belongs to
\mathrm{C}_{2}^{sw}(\mathsf{X}^{2}\times\mathsf{X})
we deduce that

\lim_{n\to\infty}\int\langle\boldsymbol{f}_{t(n)}(Y_{t(n)}),X-Y_{t(n)}\rangle\,\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}=\int\langle v,x-y\rangle\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{\vartheta}=\int\langle\boldsymbol{f}_{0}(Y),X-Y\rangle\,\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}.

Thus, we can pass to the limit in (
3.21
) obtaining

\int\langle\boldsymbol{f}_{0}(Y)-W,X-Y\rangle_{\mathcal{X}}\,\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}\leq 0\quad\text{for every }X\in\mathcal{X},

in particular it holds for
X=\boldsymbol{f}_{0}(Y)-W+Y
.
We deduce that
W=\boldsymbol{f}_{0}\circ Y={\boldsymbol{B}}Y
so that
{\boldsymbol{B}}
is maximal
and
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
is maximal as well.

Claim (3) is just the equivalent way to express the demicontinuity
of
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
, recalling Definition
2.4
.
∎

An important example of single-valued, everywhere defined
demicontinuous PVF is provided by the Yosida approximation:
starting from a maximal totally
\lambda
-dissipative MPVF
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}

and its Lagrangian representation
{\boldsymbol{B}}
,
for every
\tau\in(0,1/\lambda^{+})
we consider its Yosida approximation
{\boldsymbol{B}}_{\tau}
and define the corresponding (single-valued) PVF

(3.22)

{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}_{\tau}:=\iota^{2}({\boldsymbol{B}}_{\tau}).

Notice that
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}_{\tau}
is maximal totally
\lambda/(1-\lambda\tau)
-dissipative (see Theorem
A.3
). Moreover, by Theorem
3.24
(1), (
3.3
) and (
3.22
) we get that

{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}_{\tau}[\mu]=(\boldsymbol{i}_{\mathsf{X}},\boldsymbol{f}_{\tau}[\mu])_{\sharp}\mu,\quad\text{for all }\mu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{X}),

where

\boldsymbol{f}_{\tau}:\mathcal{S}\left(\mathsf{X}\right)\to\mathsf{X}
are given by

\boldsymbol{f}_{\tau}[\mu](\cdot):=\boldsymbol{b}_{\tau}(\cdot,\mu)

with
\boldsymbol{b}_{\tau}
as in (
3.3
); notice
\boldsymbol{f}_{\tau}
admits a continuous version
defined in
\mathcal{S}\left(\mathsf{X}\right)
and
\boldsymbol{f}_{\tau}(\cdot,\mu)

belongs to
\mathrm{Lip}(\supp(\mu);\mathsf{X})

for every
\mu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{X})

and clearly admits a Lipschitz extension
to
\mathsf{X}

(see Theorem
3.4
).
Setting
L_{\tau}:=\frac{1}{\tau}(2-\lambda\tau)/(1-\lambda\tau)
, by
L_{\tau}
-Lipschitz continuity of
{\boldsymbol{B}}_{\tau}
and the representation (
3.3
), we get the following Lipschitz condition

(3.23)

\int\Big|\boldsymbol{f}_{\tau}(x_{0},\mu_{0})-\boldsymbol{f}_{\tau}(x_{1},\mu_{1})\Big|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{\mu}(x_{0},x_{1})\leq L_{\tau}^{2}\int|x_{0}-x_{1}|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{\mu}(x_{0},x_{1})\quad\text{for every }\boldsymbol{\mu}\in\Gamma(\mu_{0},\mu_{1}),

which clearly implies demicontinuity of
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}_{\tau}
.
We have thus proved the following result, recalling also Theorem
3.20
(2).

Corollary 3.25
.

Let
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}\subset\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{T\kern-1.5ptX})
be a maximal totally
\lambda
-dissipative
MPVF
.
There exist sequences
\lambda_{n},L_{n}\in\mathbb{R}
and a sequence of maps

\boldsymbol{f}_{n}:\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{X})\to\mathrm{Lip}(\mathsf{X},\mathsf{X})

satisfying the Lipschitz condition (
3.23
) with
L_{n}
in place of
L_{\tau}

inducing a sequence of single-valued
maximal totally
\lambda_{n}
-dissipative
PVF
s
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}_{n}
, and
satisfying

\lim_{n\to\infty}\int\big|\boldsymbol{f}_{n}[\mu](x)-\boldsymbol{f}^{\circ}[\mu](x)\big|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\mu(x)=0\quad\text{for every }\mu\in D({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}),

where
\boldsymbol{f}^{\circ}
is as in Theorem
3.20
.

4.
Lagrangian and Eulerian flow generated by a totally dissipative MPVF

In this section, making use of the results obtained in the previous Section
3
,
we study well-posedness for
\lambda
-EVI solutions driven by a maximal totally
\lambda
-dissipative MPVF
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
. These curves are characterized (time by time) as the law of the unique semigroup of Lipschitz transformations
\boldsymbol{S}_{t}
of the Lagrangian representation
{\boldsymbol{B}}
of
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
.
As in the previous Section, we will consider a standard Borel
space
(\Omega,{\mathcal{B}})

endowed with a nonatomic probability measure
\mathbb{P}

and the Hilbert space

\mathcal{X}:=L^{2}(\Omega,{\mathcal{B}},\mathbb{P};\mathsf{X})
.

Definition 4.1

(Lagrangian flow)
.

Let
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}\subset\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{T\kern-1.5ptX})
be a
maximal totally
\lambda
-dissipative
MPVF
.
We call
Lagrangian flow

the family of maps
\boldsymbol{s}_{t}:\mathcal{S}(\mathsf{X},\overline{\mathrm{D}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})})\to\mathsf{X}

defined by Theorem

3.4

starting from the Lagrangian representation

{\boldsymbol{B}}
of
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
.

The Lagrangian flow induces
a semigroup of
(\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{X}),W_{2})
-Lipschitz transformations

S_{t}:\overline{\mathrm{D}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})}\to\overline{\mathrm{D}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})}

defined by

S_{t}(\mu_{0}):=\boldsymbol{s}_{t}(\cdot,\mu_{0})_{\sharp}\mu_{0}
.

We say that the continuous
curve
\mu:[0,+\infty)\to\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{X})

is a
Lagrangian solution

of the flow generated by
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}

if
\mu_{t}=S_{t}(\mu_{0})=\boldsymbol{s}_{t}(\cdot,\mu_{0})_{\sharp}\mu_{0}

for every
t\geq 0
.

Notice that, if
\mu
is a Lagrangian solution, the semigroup property (
3.5
)
of the Lagrangian flow
\boldsymbol{s}_{t}
yields in particular

\mu_{t}=\boldsymbol{s}_{t-s}(\cdot,\mu_{s})_{\sharp}\mu_{s}\quad\text{for every }0\leq s\leq t.

In particular, to construct a Lagrangian solution starting from
\mu_{0}\in\mathrm{D}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})

it is sufficient to choose an arbitrary map
X_{0}\in\mathcal{X}
satisfying
(X_{0})_{\sharp}\mathbb{P}=\mu_{0}

and set
\mu_{t}:=(X_{t})_{\sharp}\mu_{0}
for the (unique) locally Lipschitz solution

X\in\mathrm{Lip}_{\rm loc}([0,\infty);\mathcal{X})
of

\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}X_{t}={\boldsymbol{B}}^{\circ}X_{t}\quad\text{a.e.\penalty\ in $(0,+\infty)$,}\quad X\lower 3.0pt\hbox{$|_{t=0}$}=X_{0}.

An immediate consequence of Theorem

3.4

is the following result.

Theorem 4.2

(Existence of Lagrangian solutions)
.

If
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}\subset\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{T\kern-1.5ptX})
is a maximal totally
\lambda
-dissipative
MPVF
then for
every
\mu_{0}\in\overline{\mathrm{D}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})}

there exists a unique Lagrangian solution
\mu:[0,+\infty)\to\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{X})

starting from
\mu_{0}
.

If
\mu_{0}\in\mathrm{D}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})
,
then

\mu_{t}\in\mathrm{D}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})

for every
t\geq 0
,
the curve
\mu:[0,+\infty)\to\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{X})

is locally Lipschitz continuous, and

(4.1)

\int|\boldsymbol{f}^{\circ}(x,\mu_{t})|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\mu_{t}(x)\leq e^{\lambda t}\int|\boldsymbol{f}^{\circ}(x,\mu_{0})|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\mu_{0}(x)\quad\text{for every }t\geq 0,

where
\boldsymbol{f}^{\circ}

is defined in Theorem
3.20

and induces a map
(x,t)\mapsto\boldsymbol{f}^{\circ}(x,\mu_{t})

which is
\boldsymbol{\mu}
-measurable
with respect to
\boldsymbol{\mu}=\int\mu_{t}\,\mathrm{d}t
in every
set
\mathsf{X}\times(0,T)
,
T>0
.

Moreover,

\mu
is the unique
Eulerian solution

of the flow generated by
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
in the following sense:

\mu:[0,+\infty)\to\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{X})
is the unique
distributional solution
of

(4.2)

\partial_{t}\mu_{t}+\nabla\cdot(\mu_{t}\,\boldsymbol{f}^{\circ}(\cdot,\mu_{t}))=0\quad\text{in $(0,+\infty)\times\mathsf{X}$}

among the class of locally absolutely continuous curves satisfying

\mu_{t=0}=\mu_{0}\in\mathrm{D}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})

and

(4.3)

\int_{0}^{T}\int|\boldsymbol{f}^{\circ}(x,\mu_{t})|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\mu_{t}\,\mathrm{d}t<\infty\quad\text{for every }T>0.

Finally, for every
\mu_{0}\in\overline{\mathrm{D}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})}
and
t>0
we have

(1)

if
\supp(\mu_{0})

is finite, then
\supp(\mu_{t})
is finite and its
cardinality is nonincreasing w.r.t.
t
.
In particular, if
\mu_{0}\in\mathcal{P}_{f,N}(\mathsf{X})
for some
N\in\mathbb{N}

(recall (
3.20
))
then
\mu_{t}\in\mathcal{P}_{f,N}(\mathsf{X})
for every
t\geq 0
;

(2)

if
\supp(\mu_{0})
is compact, then

\supp(\mu_{t})
is compact;

(3)

if
\supp(\mu_{0})
is bounded, then

\supp(\mu_{t})
is bounded and

\operatorname{diam}(\supp(\mu_{t}))\leq\mathrm{e}^{\lambda t}\operatorname{diam}(\supp(\mu_{0}))
;

(4)

if

\int_{\mathsf{X}}|x|^{p}\,\mathrm{d}\mu_{0}(x)<+\infty
for some
p\geq 1
,
then

\int_{\mathsf{X}}|x|^{p}\,\mathrm{d}\mu_{t}(x)<+\infty
and

\int\big|x-y\big|^{p}\,\mathrm{d}\mu_{t}\otimes\mu_{t}\leq\mathrm{e}^{p\lambda t}\int\big|x-y\big|^{p}\,\mathrm{d}\mu_{0}\otimes\mu_{0}.

Proof.

The existence and the regularity properties of Lagrangian solutions
follow by Theorem

3.4
, while (
4.1
) follows by Theorem
A.5
(4).

Property (
3.7
) clearly implies
(
4.2
).

Concerning uniqueness of solutions to
(
4.2
)
satisfying
(
4.3
), we have

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}W_{2}^{2}(\mu^{1}_{t},\mu^{2}_{t})

\displaystyle\leq 2\int\langle\boldsymbol{f}^{\circ}(x_{1},\mu^{1}_{t})-\boldsymbol{f}^{\circ}(x_{2},\mu^{2}_{t}),x_{1}-x_{2}\rangle\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{\mu}_{t}

\displaystyle\leq 2\lambda\int|x_{1}-x_{2}|^{2}\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{\mu}_{t}

\displaystyle=2\lambda W_{2}^{2}(\mu^{1}_{t},\mu^{2}_{t})

for a.e.
t\geq 0
and every

\boldsymbol{\mu}_{t}\in\Gamma_{o}(\mu^{1}_{t},\mu^{2}_{t})
, by Theorem
2.13
(6b) thanks to (
4.3
), the total
\lambda
-dissipativity of
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
and (
3.13
). Hence, by Grönwall inequality, we get

W_{2}(\mu^{1}_{t},\mu^{2}_{t})\leq e^{\lambda t}W_{2}(\mu^{1}_{0},\mu^{2}_{0}).

The
\boldsymbol{\mu}
-measurability of the map
(x,t)\mapsto\boldsymbol{f}^{\circ}(x,\mu_{t})
follows by continuity of
t\mapsto\mu_{t}
together with Theorem
3.20
(4) with
\mathsf{Y}=[0,T]
. Indeed, (
3.18
) holds thanks to (
4.1
).

The last assertions (1-4) come from the fact that
\mu_{t}=\boldsymbol{s}_{t}(\cdot,\mu_{0})_{\sharp}\mu_{0}
and this map is
e^{\lambda t}
-Lipschitz continuous (cf. Theorem
3.4
(3)).
∎

Remark 4.3

(A sticky-particle interpretation)
.

We may interpret property (1) of the previous Theorem
4.2

by saying that the flows of totally dissipative MPVFs model sticky particle evolutions,
(see also
[
41
]
). This fact reflects at a dynamic level the
barycentric projection property stated in Theorem
3.18
.
In contrast, we immediately see that the example of
\frac{1}{2}
-dissipative PVF, with
\mathsf{X}=\mathbb{R}
, analysed in
[
47
, Section 7.1]
,
[
20
, Section 6]
and later discussed in
[
27
, Section 7.5, Example 7.11]
, cannot be maximally total
\frac{1}{2}
-dissipative since it produces a
\frac{1}{2}
-EVI solution which splits the mass for positive times if e.g.
\mu_{0}=\delta_{0}
.
Notice indeed that, as highlighted in the following Theorem
4.4
, if
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
is maximal totally dissipative then Lagrangian and EVI solutions coincide.

It is remarkable that
the Lagrangian flow
\boldsymbol{s}_{t}

provides an explicit representation
of the flow of Lipschitz transformations
generated by the unique
\lambda
-EVI solution,
see
[
27
, Definition 5.21]
and Definition
2.21
.

Theorem 4.4

(EVI solutions and contraction)
.

If
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}\subset\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{T\kern-1.5ptX})
is a maximal totally
\lambda
-dissipative

MPVF
, then
for every
\mu_{0}\in\overline{\mathrm{D}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})}
, the curve
\mu:[0,+\infty)\to\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{X})
,

\mu_{t}:=S_{t}(\mu_{0})
, is the unique
\lambda
-EVI solution starting from

\mu_{0}
and

S_{t}
is a semigroup of
e^{\lambda t}
-Lipschitz transformations
satisfying

W_{2}(S_{t}(\mu_{0}^{\prime}),S_{t}(\mu_{0}^{\prime\prime}))\leq e^{\lambda t}W_{2}(\mu_{0}^{\prime},\mu_{0}^{\prime\prime})\quad\text{for every }\mu_{0}^{\prime},\mu_{0}^{\prime\prime}\in\overline{\mathrm{D}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})},\,t\geq 0.

Proof.

The proof is an immediate consequence
of

[
27
, Theorem 5.22(e)]

and Theorem
4.2
. Indeed notice that
[
27
, Theorem 5.22(e)]
can be applied even if the absolutely continuous curve
\mu
satisfies the differential inclusion

(4.4)

(\boldsymbol{i}_{\mathsf{X}},\boldsymbol{v}_{t})_{\sharp}\mu_{t}\in{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}[\mu_{t}]

w.r.t. to any Borel vector field
\boldsymbol{v}_{t}
s.t.
(\mu,\boldsymbol{v})
solves the continuity equation and
t\mapsto|\boldsymbol{v}_{t}|_{L^{2}(\mathsf{X},\mu_{t};\mathsf{X})}\in L^{1}_{loc}(0,+\infty)
. For instance it holds for the vector field
\boldsymbol{f}^{\circ}
. Indeed, the proof of
[
27
, Theorem 5.22(e)]
relies on
[
27
, Theorem 5.17(2)]
which holds even if the differential inclusion (
4.4
), with
\boldsymbol{v}
the Wasserstein vector field, is replaced by a general velocity field
\boldsymbol{v}
as above. See also
[
27
, Remark 3.12]
.
∎

As a further consequence,
in the case of maximal
\lambda
-totally dissipative MPVF all the various definitions of solutions
coincide.

Theorem 4.5
.

Let
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}\subset\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{T\kern-1.5ptX})
be a maximal totally
\lambda
-dissipative
MPVF
,
let
\mu_{0}\in\overline{\mathrm{D}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})}

and let
\mu:[0,+\infty)\to\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{X})

be a continuous curve
starting from
\mu_{0}
.
The following properties are equivalent:

(1)

\mu
is a Lagrangian solution.

(2)

\mu
is a
\lambda
-
EVI
solution.

If moreover

\mu_{0}\in\mathrm{D}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})

or there exists
a sequence
t_{n}\downarrow 0
for which
\mu(t_{n})\in\mathrm{D}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})
,
the above conditions are also equivalent to the following ones:

(3)

there exists
a Borel vector field

\boldsymbol{w}_{t}

satisfying

(4.5)

t\mapsto\int|\boldsymbol{w}_{t}(x)|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\mu_{t}(x)\quad\text{is locally integrable in
$(0,+\infty)$},\quad(\boldsymbol{i}_{\mathsf{X}},\boldsymbol{w}_{t})_{\sharp}\mu_{t}\in{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}\quad\text{for a.e.\penalty\ $t>0$}

and the pair
(\mu,\boldsymbol{w})
satisfies the continuity equation

(4.6)

\partial_{t}\mu_{t}+\nabla\cdot(\mu_{t}\,\boldsymbol{w}_{t})=0\quad\text{in $(0,+\infty)\times\mathsf{X}$};

(4)

there exists a Borel family
\Phi_{t}
,
t>0
, such that

(4.7)

\displaystyle\Phi_{t}\in{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}[\mu_{t}]\quad\text{for a.e.\penalty\ $t>0$,}\quad t\mapsto\int|v|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\Phi_{t}\quad\text{is locally integrable in
$(0,+\infty)$,}

(4.8)

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}\Big(\int\partial_{t}\zeta(t,x)\,\mathrm{d}\mu_{t}+\int\langle v,\nabla\zeta(t,x)\rangle\,\mathrm{d}\Phi_{t}(x,v)\Big)\,\mathrm{d}t=0\quad\text{for every $\zeta\in\Cyl((0,+\infty)\times\mathsf{X})$;}

(5)

\mu_{t}\in\mathrm{D}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})

for every
t>0
,

\mu
is locally Lipschitz in
(0,+\infty)
and
it satisfies

t\mapsto\int|\boldsymbol{f}^{\circ}(x,\mu_{t})|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\mu_{t}\quad\text{is locally bounded in }(0,+\infty),

and (
4.2
).

Proof.

The equivalence between
(1) and (2) is a consequence of
Theorem
4.4
.

We can now consider the case when
\mu_{0}\in\mathrm{D}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})

(the argument for the case
\mu(t_{n})\in\mathrm{D}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})
along an infinitesimal sequence

t_{n}
is completely analogous).
Theorem
4.2

clearly yields (1)
\Rightarrow

(5). The implication (5)
\Rightarrow

(3) is obvious.
Theorem

3.18

shows that (3) and (4) are equivalent. Indeed (3) implies (4) by choosing
\Phi_{t}:=(\boldsymbol{i}_{\mathsf{X}},\boldsymbol{w}_{t})_{\sharp}\mu_{t}
and (4) implies (3) by choosing
\boldsymbol{w}_{t}:=\boldsymbol{b}_{\Phi_{t}}
.
The implication (3)
\Rightarrow
(2)
follows by Theorem 5.4(1) of

[
27
]
.
∎

In the case when
\mu_{0}\in\mathcal{P}_{f}(\mathsf{X})
has finite support (recall (
3.19
),
(
3.20
)),
we can obtain a more refined characterization, which
also yields a regularization effect when
\mathsf{X}
has finite dimension
and recovers the characterization (
1.16
)
anticipated in the Introduction.
Recall that
by Theorem
4.2
(1) any
Lagrangian solution starting from
\mu_{0}\in\mathcal{P}_{f,N}(\mathsf{X})

must stay in
\mathcal{P}_{f,N}(\mathsf{X})
for every time
t\geq 0
.

Corollary 4.6

(Regularization effect and Wasserstein velocity field for discrete measures)
.

Let
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}\subset\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{T\kern-1.5ptX})
be a maximal totally
\lambda
-dissipative
MPVF
,
let
\mu_{0}\in\overline{\mathrm{D}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})}\cap\mathcal{P}_{f,N}(\mathsf{X})
for some
N\in\mathbb{N}

and let
\mu:[0,+\infty)\to\mathcal{P}_{f,N}(\mathsf{X})

be a continuous curve
starting from
\mu_{0}
.
Assume moreover that at least one of the following properties holds:

(1)

\mu_{0}\in\mathrm{D}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})
,

(2)

\mathrm{D}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})\cap\mathcal{P}_{f,N}(\mathsf{X})
has non empty relative interior in

\mathcal{P}_{f,N}(\mathsf{X})
,

(3)

\mathsf{X}
has finite dimension.

Then conditions (1),…,(5)
of Theorem

4.5
are equivalent
and, in this case, the minimal selection
\boldsymbol{f}^{\circ}
of
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
(cf. Theorem
3.20
)
coincides with the Wasserstein velocity field
\boldsymbol{v}
of
\mu
(cf. Theorem
2.11
)
and
\mu
also satisfies the right-differentiability property

(4.9)

\boldsymbol{v}_{t}=\lim_{h\downarrow 0}\frac{1}{h}\Big(\boldsymbol{t}_{t}^{t+h}-\boldsymbol{i}_{\mathsf{X}}\Big)=\boldsymbol{f}^{\circ}[\mu_{t}]\quad\text{in }L^{2}(\mathsf{X},\mu_{t};\mathsf{X})\quad\text{for every }t>0,

where
\boldsymbol{t}_{t}^{t+h}

is the optimal transport map pushing
\mu_{t}
into
\mu_{t+h}
.

Finally,
\mu
is a Lagrangian solution for
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
starting from
\mu_{0}

if and only if
there are curves

\mathsf{x}_{n}\in\mathrm{C}([0,+\infty);\mathsf{X})
,
n=1,\cdots,N

which are locally Lipschitz in
(0,+\infty)
such that

\mu_{t}=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{n=1}^{N}\delta_{\mathsf{x}_{n}(t)}

for every
t\geq 0

and the curves
(\mathsf{x}_{n}(t))_{n=1}^{N}
solve
the system of ODEs

(4.10)

\dot{\mathsf{x}}_{n}(t)=\boldsymbol{f}^{\circ}(\mathsf{x}_{n},\mu_{t})\quad\text{a.e.\penalty\ in }(0,+\infty).

Proof.

Case (a) is part of Theorem
4.5
. In order to prove the first equivalence statement in cases (b) and (c), we briefly
anticipate an argument that we will develop more extensively in Section

8
:
we
introduce the standard Borel space
\Omega:=[0,1)
endowed with the Lebesgue measure
(still denoted by
\mathbb{P}
), the Lagrangian representation
{\boldsymbol{B}}
of
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
,
and we consider the closed subspace
\mathcal{X}_{N}\subset\mathcal{X}

of maps
X:\Omega\to\mathsf{X}
which are constant on each interval

[(k-1)/N,k/N)
,
k=1,\cdots,N.

Thanks to Theorem
3.4
,
\mathcal{X}_{N}
is invariant with respect to the action
of the resolvent map
\boldsymbol{J}_{\tau}
. We can thus apply Proposition
A.8
obtaining that the operator
{\boldsymbol{B}}_{N}:={\boldsymbol{B}}\cap(\mathcal{X}_{N}\times\mathcal{X}_{N})
is maximal
\lambda
-dissipative in
\mathcal{X}_{N}
and, if we select a Lagrangian parametrization
X_{0}\in\overline{\mathrm{D}({\boldsymbol{B}}_{N})}
of
\mu_{0}
, still by Proposition
A.8
(ii), we get that
\boldsymbol{S}_{t}X_{0}
, the semigroup generated by
{\boldsymbol{B}}
, coincides with
\boldsymbol{S}_{t}^{N}X_{0}
, the semigroup generated by
{\boldsymbol{B}}_{N}
and, under any of the conditions (b) and (c),
\boldsymbol{S}^{N}
has a regularizing effect (see Theorem
A.7
, Corollary
A.9
and notice that, in case (c),
\mathcal{X}_{N}
has finite dimension)
so that
\boldsymbol{S}^{N}_{t}X_{0}\in\mathrm{D}({\boldsymbol{B}}_{N})\subset\mathrm{D}({\boldsymbol{B}})

for every
t>0
.
We immediately obtain that the conditions (1), …, (5) of
Theorem
4.5
are equivalent.

In order to check (
4.9
) we can use
(
3.8
) observing that,
for sufficiently small
h
,
(\boldsymbol{i}_{\mathsf{X}},\boldsymbol{s}_{h})_{\sharp}\mu_{t}
is an optimal coupling
between
\mu_{t}
and
\mu_{t+h}
, being
\mu_{t}\in\mathcal{P}_{f,N}(\mathsf{X})
,
see the next Lemma
.

Finally, in order to check the last representation formula, it is sufficient to write

\mu_{0}
as
\frac{1}{N}\sum_{n=1}^{N}x_{n}
for suitable points
x_{n}\in\mathsf{X}

and to set
\mathsf{x}_{n}(t):=\boldsymbol{s}_{t}(x_{n},\mu_{0})
.
∎

A further application concerns the convergence of the Implicite Euler Scheme
(also called JKO method in the framework of gradient flows, see
Proposition
5.3
). We just recall here
the main Crandall-Liggett estimate, referring to

[
43
,
42
]
for more refined a-priori and a-posteriori error estimates.

Corollary 4.7

(Implicit Euler Scheme)
.

Let
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}\subset\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{T\kern-1.5ptX})
be a maximal totally
\lambda
-dissipative
MPVF
.
For every
\mu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{X})

and every
0<\tau<1/\lambda^{+}
there exists a unique
\Phi\in{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}

such that

(4.11)

(\mathsf{x}-\tau\mathsf{v})_{\sharp}\Phi=\mu.

Moreover
M_{\tau}:=\mathsf{x}_{\sharp}\Phi=\boldsymbol{j}_{\tau}(\cdot,\mu)_{\sharp}\mu
, where
\boldsymbol{j}_{\tau}
is as in Theorem
3.4
applied to the Lagrangian representation of
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
.
If
\mu_{0}\in\overline{\mathrm{D}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})}
, then setting
M^{0}_{\tau}:=\mu_{0},

M^{n+1}_{\tau}:=\boldsymbol{j}_{\tau}(\cdot,M^{n}_{\tau})_{\sharp}M^{n}_{\tau}
,
n\in\mathbb{N}
, we have

(4.12)

\lim_{N\to\infty}M^{N}_{t/N}=\mu_{t}\quad\text{for every $t\geq 0$},

where
\mu_{t}=S_{t}(\mu_{0})
with
S_{t}
as in Definition
4.1
.
Moreover, for every
T\geq 0
there exist
N(\lambda,T)\in\mathbb{N}

and
C(\lambda,T)>0
(with
C(0,T)=2T
)
such that

(4.13)

W_{2}(M^{N}_{t/N},\mu_{t})\leq\frac{C(\lambda,T)}{\sqrt{N}}\,\Big|\boldsymbol{f}^{\circ}[\mu_{0}]\Big|_{L^{2}(\mathsf{X},\mu_{0};\mathsf{X})},

for every
0\leq t\leq T
,
n\geq N(\lambda,T)
and every
\mu_{0}\in\mathrm{D}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})
,
where
\boldsymbol{f}^{\circ}
is as in Theorem
4.2
.

Proof.

The existence of
\Phi
satisfying (
4.11
) follows by Theorem
3.15
. Uniqueness follows by the well posedness of
{\boldsymbol{J}_{\tau}}
and its invariance by law, stated in Theorem
3.4
.
The approximation in (
4.12
) follows by the Lagrangian one

\boldsymbol{S}_{t}X=\lim_{N\to\infty}({\boldsymbol{J}_{t/N}})^{N}(X)

holding for any
X\in\overline{\mathrm{D}({\boldsymbol{B}})}
(see Theorem
A.6
),
{\boldsymbol{B}}
the Lagrangian representation of
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
.

Finally, (
4.13
) follows by Theorem
A.6
.
∎

We conclude this section with two results concerning the uniqueness
and the stabiility
of the characteristic system representing the solution of
(
4.5
) and (
4.6
).

Using the notation of Theorem
3.4
, we preliminary observe that choosing
\mu_{0}\in\mathrm{D}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})

the Lipschitz maps
\boldsymbol{s}_{t}(x):=\boldsymbol{s}_{t}(x,\mu_{0})
belong
to
\mathrm{Lip}(\supp(\mu_{0});\mathsf{X})
and the curve
t\mapsto\boldsymbol{s}_{t}
is Lipschitz in

L^{2}(\mathsf{X},\mu_{0};\mathsf{X})
with derivative
\boldsymbol{b}^{\circ}_{t}(\boldsymbol{s}_{t})
where

\boldsymbol{b}^{\circ}_{t}(\cdot):=\boldsymbol{b}^{\circ}(\cdot,(\boldsymbol{s}_{t})_{\sharp}\mu_{0})
.
It follows that for every
T>0
and for

\mu_{0}
-a.e.
x
the curve

t\mapsto\boldsymbol{s}_{t}(x)
belongs to
H^{1}(0,T;\mathsf{X})

and satisfies
\dot{\boldsymbol{s}}_{t}(x)=\boldsymbol{b}^{\circ}_{t}(\boldsymbol{s}_{t}(x))
.
We can thus associate to
(\boldsymbol{s}_{t})_{t\geq 0}
a
\mu_{0}
-measurable map

(4.14)

\mathrm{s}:\mathsf{X}\to H^{1}(0,T;\mathsf{X}),\quad\mathrm{s}[x](t):=\boldsymbol{s}_{t}(x,\mu_{0}).

In a similar way, if
X_{0}\in\mathcal{X}
with
\iota(X_{0})=\mu_{0}
,
we can define

(4.15)

\mathrm{X}(\omega,t):=\boldsymbol{s}_{t}(X_{0}(\omega),\mu_{0}),\quad\mathrm{X}[\omega]:=\mathrm{s}\circ X_{0},

obtaining a distiguished Caratheodory representative of

\boldsymbol{S}_{t}X_{0}
which satisfies

(4.16)

\mathrm{X}(\omega,t)=(\boldsymbol{S}_{t}X_{0})(\omega)\quad\text{for $\mathbb{P}$-a.e.\penalty\ $\omega\in\Omega$,
for every $t>0$}

and

(4.17)

\mathrm{X}(\omega,\cdot)\in H^{1}(0,T;\mathsf{X})\quad\text{for $\mathbb{P}$-a.e.\penalty\ $\omega$},\quad\int\Big(\int_{0}^{T}|\partial_{t}{\mathrm{X}}(\omega,t)|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}t\Big)\,\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}(\omega)\leq Te^{2\lambda_{+}T}|\boldsymbol{B}^{\circ}X_{0}|_{\mathcal{X}}^{2},

since

\displaystyle\int\Big(\int_{0}^{T}|\partial_{t}{\mathrm{X}}(\omega,t)|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}t\Big)\,\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}(\omega)

\displaystyle=\int\Big(\int_{0}^{T}|\boldsymbol{b}^{\circ}_{t}({\mathrm{X}}(\omega,t))|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}t\Big)\,\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}(\omega)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{T}|\boldsymbol{B}^{\circ}{\mathrm{X}}(\cdot,t)|_{\mathcal{X}}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\leq Te^{2\lambda_{+}T}|\boldsymbol{B}^{\circ}X_{0}|_{\mathcal{X}}^{2},

where we have used Theorem
A.5
(4).
It follows that
\mathrm{X}
can be identified with a

\mathbb{P}
-measurable map
\omega\mapsto\mathrm{X}[\omega]

which belongs to
L^{2}(\Omega,{\mathcal{B}},\mathbb{P};H^{1}(0,T;\mathsf{X}))
.

Theorem 4.8

(Uniqueness of the characteristic fields)
.

Let
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}\subset\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{T\kern-1.5ptX})
be a maximal totally
\lambda
-dissipative
MPVF
, let us fix
T>0
and let us suppose that
(\mu,\boldsymbol{v})
is
a solution to (
4.5
) and (
4.6
)
in the interval
[0,T]
starting from
\mu_{0}\in\mathrm{D}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})
.
Let
\boldsymbol{\eta}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathrm{C}([0,T];\mathsf{X}))
be a probability measure
concentrated on absolutely continuous curves and satisfying
the following properties:

(1)

(\mathsf{e}_{t})_{\sharp}\boldsymbol{\eta}=\mu_{t}
for every
t\in[0,T]
,
where
\mathsf{e}_{t}(\gamma):=\gamma(t)
for every
\gamma\in\mathrm{C}([0,T];\mathsf{X})
;

(2)

\boldsymbol{\eta}
-a.e.
\gamma
is an integral solution of the
differential equation

\dot{\gamma}(t)=\boldsymbol{v}_{t}(\gamma(t))
a.e. in
[0,T]
.

Then
\boldsymbol{\eta}=\mathrm{s}_{\sharp}\mu_{0}
, where
\mathrm{s}

is defined as in (
4.14
).
In particular
\boldsymbol{\eta}
is unique
and
\boldsymbol{v}_{t}(x)=\boldsymbol{b}^{\circ}_{t}(x)

\mu_{t}
-a.e. in
\mathsf{X}
.

Proof.

We can find a Borel map

\mathrm{Z}:\Omega\to\mathrm{C}([0,T];\mathsf{X})

such that
\mathrm{Z}_{\sharp}\mathbb{P}=\boldsymbol{\eta}
. Let
\boldsymbol{B}
be the Lagrangian representation of
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
.
We can then define
X_{t}:=\mathsf{e}_{t}\circ\mathrm{Z}
.
Since
(X_{t})_{\sharp}\mathbb{P}=\mu_{t}\in\mathrm{D}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})
by Theorem
4.5
(5), recalling Remark
3.5
we see that
X_{t}\in D({\boldsymbol{B}})\subset\mathcal{X}
.
It is also clear that for
\mathbb{P}
-a.e.
\omega
we have

X_{t+h}(\omega)-X_{t}(\omega)=\int_{t}^{t+h}\boldsymbol{v}_{s}(X_{s}(\omega))\,\mathrm{d}s

and therefore

|X_{t+h}-X_{t}|_{\mathcal{X}}\leq\int_{t}^{t+h}\|\boldsymbol{v}_{s}\|_{L^{2}(\mathsf{X},\mu_{s};\mathsf{X})}\,\mathrm{d}s

so that
t\mapsto X_{t}
belongs to
H^{1}(0,T;\mathcal{X})
.
At every differentiability point we have

\dot{X}_{t}=\boldsymbol{v}_{t}(X_{t})
so that

(X_{t},\dot{X}_{t})_{\sharp}\mathbb{P}=(\boldsymbol{i}_{\mathsf{X}},\boldsymbol{v}_{t})_{\sharp}\mu_{t}\in{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}[\mu_{t}]

and eventually
\dot{X}_{t}\in{\boldsymbol{B}}X_{t}
.
We conclude that
X_{t}(\omega)=\boldsymbol{s}_{t}(X_{0}(\omega))

and therefore
\boldsymbol{\eta}=\mathrm{s}_{\sharp}\mu_{0}
.
∎

Theorem 4.9

(Stability of the Lagrangian flows)
.

Under the same conditions of the previous Theorem
4.8
,
let
(\mu^{n}_{0})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}

be a sequence in
\mathrm{D}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})
satisfying the following properties:

(1)

(\mu^{n}_{0})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
converges to
\mu_{0}
in
\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{X})
, as
n\to\infty
;

(2)

\sup_{n}|{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}|_{2}(\mu^{n}_{0})<\infty
, where
|{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}|_{2}(\cdot)
is defined in (
3.17
).

If
\mathrm{s}^{n},\mathrm{s}:\mathsf{X}\to\mathrm{C}([0,T];\mathsf{X})

are the Lagrangian maps defined as in
(
4.14
) starting from
\mu^{n}_{0}

and
\mu_{0}
respectively,
then

(\boldsymbol{i}_{\mathsf{X}},\mathrm{s}^{n})_{\sharp}\mu^{n}_{0}\to(\boldsymbol{i}_{\mathsf{X}},\mathrm{s})_{\sharp}\mu_{0}
in

\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{X}\times\mathrm{C}([0,T];\mathsf{X}))
as
n\to\infty
.

Proof.

By the last part of Theorem
B.5
, we can select a sequence
(X_{0}^{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
in
\mathcal{X}

strongly converging to
X_{0}
such that

\iota(X_{0}^{n})=\mu_{0}^{n}
and
\iota(X_{0})=\mu_{0}.

We now consider the family of

\mathbb{P}
-measurable maps

\mathrm{X}^{n}:\Omega\to H^{1}(0,T;\mathsf{X})\subset\mathrm{C}([0,T];\mathsf{X})

defined as in (
4.15
)
starting from
X^{n}_{0}
and the corresponding
\mathrm{X}
defined starting from
X_{0}
.
Our thesis follows if we prove that

\mathrm{X}^{n}\to\mathrm{X}

in
L^{2}(\Omega,{\mathcal{B}},\mathbb{P};\mathrm{C}([0,T];\mathsf{X})).

The equivalence (
4.16
) and
the contraction estimates on
\boldsymbol{S}_{t}
(cf. (
A.8
))
show that

\displaystyle\|\mathrm{X}^{n}-\mathrm{X}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega;L^{2}(0,T;\mathsf{X}))}^{2}

\displaystyle=\int\Big(\int_{0}^{T}|\mathrm{X}^{n}(\omega,t)-\mathrm{X}(\omega,t)|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}t\Big)\,\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}(\omega)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{T}\Big(\int|\mathrm{X}^{n}(\omega,t)-\mathrm{X}(\omega,t)|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}(\omega)\Big)\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{T}|\boldsymbol{S}_{t}X^{n}_{0}-\boldsymbol{S}_{t}X_{0}|_{\mathcal{X}}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\leq T\mathrm{e}^{2\lambda_{+}T}|X^{n}_{0}-X_{0}|^{2}_{\mathcal{X}}\to 0\quad\text{as }n\to\infty.

Moreover, recalling (
4.17
), we have

\sup_{n}\|(\mathrm{X}^{n})^{\prime}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega;L^{2}(0,T;\mathsf{X}))}^{2}\leq Te^{2\lambda_{+}T}\sup_{n}|\boldsymbol{B}^{\circ}X^{n}_{0}|^{2}_{\mathcal{X}}<\infty\quad\text{for every }n\in\mathbb{N},

so that
\mathrm{X}^{n}
is uniformly bounded in

L^{2}(\Omega,{\mathcal{B}},\mathbb{P};H^{1}(0,T;\mathsf{X}))

by some finite constant
S>0
. The interpolation inequality (cf.
[
18
, p.233 (iii)]
)

\|Y\|^{2}_{\mathrm{C}([0,T];\mathsf{X})}\leq C\|Y\|_{L^{2}(0,T;\mathsf{X})}\|Y\|_{H^{1}(0,T;\mathsf{X})}\quad\text{for every }Y\in H^{1}(0,T;\mathsf{X}),

gives that
the sequence
\mathrm{X}^{n}

strongly converges to
\mathrm{X}

in
L^{2}(\Omega,{\mathcal{B}},\mathbb{P};\mathrm{C}([0,T];\mathsf{X}))
, since

\displaystyle\|\mathrm{X}^{n}-\mathrm{X}

\displaystyle\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\Omega,{\mathcal{B}},\mathbb{P};\mathrm{C}([0,T];\mathsf{X}))}=\int\|\mathrm{X}^{n}[\omega]-\mathrm{X}[\omega]\|_{\mathrm{C}([0,T];\mathsf{X})}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}

\displaystyle\leq C\int\|\mathrm{X}^{n}[\omega]-\mathrm{X}[\omega]\|_{L^{2}(0,T;\mathsf{X})}\|\mathrm{X}^{n}[\omega]-\mathrm{X}[\omega]\|_{H^{1}(0,T;\mathsf{X})}\,\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}

\displaystyle\leq C\Big(\int\|\mathrm{X}^{n}[\omega]-\mathrm{X}[\omega]\|_{L^{2}(0,T;\mathsf{X})}^{2}\,\,\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}\Big)^{1/2}\Big(\int\|\mathrm{X}^{n}[\omega]-\mathrm{X}[\omega]\|_{H^{1}(0,T;\mathsf{X})}^{2}\,\,\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}\Big)^{1/2}

\displaystyle\leq C(S+\|\mathrm{X}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega,{\mathcal{B}},\mathbb{P};H^{1}(0,T;\mathsf{X}))})\|\mathrm{X}^{n}-\mathrm{X}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega;L^{2}(0,T;\mathsf{X}))}.

∎

5.
Totally convex functionals in
\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{X})

In this section we analyze the case of a proper and lower
semicontinuous functional
\phi
which satisfies a strong convexity property.

Definition 5.1

(Total (
-\lambda
)-convexity)
.

Let
\phi:\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{X})\to(-\infty,+\infty]
and let
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
. We say that
\phi
is
totally
(-\lambda)
-convex
if it is
(-\lambda)
-convex along any coupling, i.e.

\phi(\mathsf{x}^{t}_{\sharp}\boldsymbol{\mu})\leq(1-t)\phi(\mathsf{x}^{0}_{\sharp}\boldsymbol{\mu})+t\phi(\mathsf{x}^{1}_{\sharp}\boldsymbol{\mu})+\frac{\lambda}{2}t(1-t)\int_{\mathsf{X}\times\mathsf{X}}|x-y|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{\mu}(x,y)

for every
\boldsymbol{\mu}\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{X}\times\mathsf{X})
,
t\in[0,1]
.

Notice that, in particular, if
\phi
is totally
(-\lambda)
-convex then it is
(-\lambda)
-convex along
generalized geodesics
[
2
, Definition 9.2.4]
and thus also
geodesically

(-\lambda)
-convex.
It is also easy to check that

\phi
is totally
(-\lambda)
-convex if and only if

\phi^{\lambda}(\mu):=\phi(\mu)+\frac{\lambda}{2}\int|x|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\mu\quad\text{is {totally convex}}.

We recall that the Wasserstein subdifferential
\boldsymbol{\partial}\phi\subset\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{T\kern-1.5ptX})
of
\phi
is defined as
the set of
\Psi\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{T\kern-1.5ptX})
such that

\mathsf{x}_{\sharp}\Psi=\mu\in\mathrm{D}(\phi),\quad\phi(\nu)-\phi(\mu)\geq-\left[\Psi,\nu\right]_{l}+o\left(W_{2}(\mu,\nu)\right)\quad\text{as }\nu\to\mu\text{ in }\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{X}).

When

\phi
is geodesically
(-\lambda)
-convex,
then it is possibile to show that

\Psi
belongs to

\boldsymbol{\partial}\phi
if and only if

\Psi
and
\mu=\mathsf{x}_{\sharp}\Psi\in D(\phi)
satisfy

(5.1)

\phi(\nu)-\phi(\mu)\geq-\left[\Psi,\nu\right]_{l}-\frac{\lambda}{2}W_{2}^{2}(\mu,\nu)\quad\text{for every }\nu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{X}).

It is easy to check that
-\boldsymbol{\partial}\phi
(cf.(
2.19
))
is a
\lambda
-dissipative MPVF (see also
[
27
, Section
7.1]
), but in general not totally
\lambda
-dissipative.

Let us now consider a totally
\lambda
-convex, proper and lower semicontinuous
functional
\phi
.
We fix a standard Borel space
(\Omega,{\mathcal{B}})
endowed with a nonatomic probability measure
\mathbb{P}
, with

\mathcal{X}:=L^{2}(\Omega,{\mathcal{B}},\mathbb{P};\mathsf{X})
and we consider the Lagrangian parametrization of
\phi
given by

(5.2)

\text{$\psi:\mathcal{X}\to(-\infty,+\infty]$ \quad defined as }\quad\psi(X):=\phi(\iota_{X})\quad\text{ for every }X\in\mathcal{X}.

Clearly,
\psi
is proper, l.s.c. and
(-\lambda)
-convex, i.e.
X\mapsto\psi(X)+\frac{\lambda}{2}|X|^{2}
is convex.

As a preliminary result, we study the (opposite of the) subdifferential of
\psi
, showing in particular that it is an invariant maximal
\lambda
-dissipative operator. This allows to consider its resolvent operator
\boldsymbol{J}_{\tau}
and compare, in Proposition
5.3
, the scheme generated by
\boldsymbol{J}_{\tau}
with the Wasserstein JKO scheme (
[
37
]
) for the functional
\phi
in
\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{X})
. We then show relations between
-\partial\psi
and
-\boldsymbol{\partial}\phi
, dealing in particular with the respective elements of minimal norm. Finally, in Theorem
5.6
, we show that the Lagrangian solution to the flow generated by the maximal totally
\lambda
-dissipative MPVF
\iota^{2}(-\partial\psi)
is the unique Wasserstein gradient flow for
\phi
and the unique
\lambda
-EVI solution for
-\boldsymbol{\partial}\phi
. Analogously to Theorem
4.4
, this Wasserstein semigroup can be characterized as the law of the semigroup of Lipschitz transformations
\boldsymbol{S}_{t}
of
-\partial\psi
.

Proposition 5.2

(Total subdifferential)
.

Let
\phi:\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{X})\to(-\infty,+\infty]
be a proper, lower semicontinuous and totally

(-\lambda)
-convex functional and let
\psi
be as in
(
5.2
).

(1)

The opposite of the subdifferential of
\psi
,

-\partial\psi
, is an invariant maximal

\lambda
-dissipative operator in
\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{X}
.

(2)

The

total subdifferential

-\boldsymbol{\partial}_{\mathrm{t}}\phi:=\iota^{2}(-\partial\psi)

is maximal totally

\lambda
-dissipative.

(3)

An element
\Psi\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{T\kern-1.5ptX})
satisfying

\mu=\mathsf{x}_{\sharp}\Psi\in D(\phi)
belongs to
-\boldsymbol{\partial}_{\mathrm{t}}\phi

if and only if for every
