\nu\in D(\phi)
and every
plan
\boldsymbol{\vartheta}\in\Gamma(\Psi,\nu)
we have

(5.3)

\phi(\nu)-\phi(\mu)\geq\int\Big(\langle v,x-y\rangle-\frac{\lambda}{2}|x-y|^{2}\Big)\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{\vartheta}(x,v,y).

In particular
\boldsymbol{\partial}_{\mathrm{t}}\phi\subset\boldsymbol{\partial}\phi
.

Proof.

As usual it is sufficient to check the case
\lambda=0
.

Claim (1):
by maximality of the
\lambda
-dissipative operator
-\partial\psi
in
\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{X}
(cf. Theorem
A.3
(1) and
Corollary
A.4
) and thanks to Theorem

3.4
, it is enough to prove that
-\partial\psi

is invariant by measure-preserving isomorphisms.

Let
(X,V)\in-\partial\psi
and let
g\in\mathrm{S}(\Omega)
. We have

\psi(Y)-\psi(X)\geq\langle V,X-Y\rangle_{\mathcal{X}}\quad\text{ for every }Y\in\mathcal{X}.

For every
Z\in\mathcal{X}
, choosing
Y:=Z\circ g^{-1}
we get

\displaystyle\psi(Z)-\psi(X\circ g)

\displaystyle=\psi(Z\circ g^{-1})-\psi(X)\geq\langle V,X-Z\circ g^{-1}\rangle_{\mathcal{X}}

\displaystyle=\langle V\circ g,X\circ g-Z\rangle_{\mathcal{X}}.

This shows that
(X\circ g,V\circ g)\in-\partial\psi
.

Claim (2) follows immediately by Theorem

3.12
(3).

Claim (3): let us first show that an element
\Psi
satisfying
(
5.3
) belongs to
-\boldsymbol{\partial}_{\mathrm{t}}\phi
: it is sufficient to
take a pair
(X,V)\in\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{X}
such that

\iota^{2}_{X,V}=\Psi
. For every
Y\in D(\psi)
, setting

\nu:=\iota_{Y}\in D(\phi)
and
\boldsymbol{\vartheta}:=(X,V,Y)_{\sharp}\mathbb{P}
,
we get

\psi(Y)-\psi(X)=\phi(\nu)-\phi(\mu)\geq\int\langle v,x-y\rangle\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{\vartheta}(x,v,y)=\langle V,X-Y\rangle_{\mathcal{X}},

which shows that
V\in-\partial\psi(X)
and therefore
\Psi\in\iota^{2}(-\partial\psi)=-\boldsymbol{\partial}_{\mathrm{t}}\phi
.

In order to prove the converse implication, we just take

\Psi=\iota^{2}(X^{\prime},Y^{\prime})\in\iota^{2}(-\partial\psi)
for some

(X^{\prime},Y^{\prime})\in-\partial\psi
,
\nu\in D(\phi)
, and

\boldsymbol{\vartheta}\in\Gamma(\Psi,\nu)
.
We can find elements
X,V,Y\in\mathcal{X}
such that

(X,V,Y)_{\sharp}\mathbb{P}=\boldsymbol{\vartheta}
.
In particular
Y_{\sharp}\mathbb{P}=\nu
so that

\psi(Y)=\phi(\nu)
and

(X,V)_{\sharp}\mathbb{P}=\Psi
so that
(X,V)\in-\partial\psi
,
since
-\partial\psi
is law invariant and the law of
(X,V)

coincides with the law of
(X^{\prime},Y^{\prime})
.
Since
\psi(X)=\phi(\iota_{X})=\phi(\mu)
and
(X,V)\in-\partial\psi
, we get (
5.3
)

\phi(\nu)-\phi(\mu)=\psi(Y)-\psi(X)\geq\langle V,X-Y\rangle_{\mathcal{X}}=\int\langle v,x-y\rangle\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{\vartheta}(x,v,y).\qed

In view of the invariance and the maximal
\lambda
-dissipativity of
-\partial\psi
, by Theorem
3.20
(1,2) we have that the subdifferential of
\psi
contains elements concentrated on maps, in the sense that for every
X\in\mathrm{D}(\partial\psi)
there exist
\boldsymbol{f}\in L^{2}(\mathsf{X},\iota_{X};\mathsf{X})
such that
\boldsymbol{f}\circ X\in-\partial\psi(X)
. An analogous result has been obtained in
[
33
, Theorem 3.19(iii)]

for real-valued functionals when
\mathsf{X}
has finite dimension (cf. also
[
35
, Lemma 8, Proposition 5]
).

The next result gives a correspondence between the minimal selection
and the resolvent operators of
-\partial\psi
and
\phi
.
It is remarkable that
the minimal selection
\boldsymbol{\partial}^{\circ}\phi
of
\boldsymbol{\partial}\phi
is an element of
the smaller set
\boldsymbol{\partial}_{\mathrm{t}}\phi
and therefore coincides with

\boldsymbol{\partial}_{\mathrm{t}}^{\circ}\phi
.
This fact guarantees that the “Eulerian-Wasserstein” approach to the gradient flow
of
\phi
coincides with the “Lagrangian-Hilbertian” construction.

In the following,
\boldsymbol{J}_{\tau}
denotes the resolvent of the invariant
maximal
\lambda
-dissipative operator
-\partial\psi
for
0<\tau<1/\lambda^{+}

with the corresponding map
\boldsymbol{j}_{\tau}
introduced in Theorem

3.4
.

Proposition 5.3

(JKO scheme, Wasserstein and total subdifferential)
.

Let
\phi:\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{X})\to(-\infty,+\infty]
be a proper, lower semicontinuous and totally
(-\lambda)
-convex functional and let
\psi
be as in (
5.2
). Then:

(1)

For every
\mu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{X})
and
0<\tau<1/\lambda^{+}

the measure
\mu_{\tau}:=\boldsymbol{j}_{\tau}(\cdot,\mu)_{\sharp}\mu

is the unique
solution
of the JKO scheme for
\phi
starting from
\mu
, i.e.
\mu_{\tau}
is the unique minimizer of

(5.4)

\nu\mapsto\frac{1}{2\tau}W_{2}^{2}(\mu,\nu)+\phi(\nu).

Equivalently, if
\mu=\iota(X)
for some
X\in\mathcal{X}
, then

\mu_{\tau}=\iota(\boldsymbol{J}_{\tau}X)
.

(2)

For every
\mu=\iota_{X}\in\mathrm{D}(\boldsymbol{\partial}_{\mathrm{t}}\phi)
,
the element of minimal norm
\boldsymbol{\partial}_{\mathrm{t}}^{\circ}\phi[\mu]

(equivalently, the
law of the element of minimal norm of
\partial\psi(X)
)
is the element of minimal norm of
\boldsymbol{\partial}\phi[\mu]
.

(3)

We have that
\iota(\mathrm{D}(\partial\psi))=\mathrm{D}(\boldsymbol{\partial}_{\mathrm{t}}\phi)=\mathrm{D}(\boldsymbol{\partial}\phi)

and the minimal selection
-\boldsymbol{\partial}^{\circ}\phi
of

-\boldsymbol{\partial}\phi
is concentrated on a map and it is
totally
\lambda
-dissipative.

(4)

The
MPVF

\iota^{2}(-\partial\psi)
is the unique maximal totally
\lambda
-dissipative extension of
-\boldsymbol{\partial}^{\circ}\phi
with domain included in
\overline{\mathrm{D}(\phi)}
.

Proof.

By Theorem
5.2
and Theorem
3.4
, we
have that
\mu_{\tau}
does not depend on the choice of
X\in\mathcal{X}

such that
\iota_{X}=\mu
; if
\nu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{X})
,

\nu\neq\mu_{\tau}
, we can thus find
(X^{\prime},Y)\in\mathcal{X}^{2}
such that

\iota^{2}_{X^{\prime},Y}=(X^{\prime},Y)_{\sharp}\mathbb{P}\in\Gamma_{o}(\mu,\nu)
,

\mu_{\tau}=(\boldsymbol{J}_{\tau}X^{\prime})_{\sharp}\mathbb{P}
, and
Y\neq\boldsymbol{J}_{\tau}X^{\prime}
, since
Y_{\sharp}\mathbb{P}=\nu\neq\mu_{\tau}=\boldsymbol{J}_{\tau}X^{\prime}
.
By the properties of the resolvent operator
\boldsymbol{J}_{\tau}
(cf. Corollary
A.4
), we have that

\phi(\mu_{\tau})+\frac{1}{2\tau}W_{2}^{2}(\mu_{\tau},\mu)\leq\psi(\boldsymbol{J}_{\tau}X^{\prime})+\frac{1}{2\tau}|\boldsymbol{J}_{\tau}X^{\prime}-X^{\prime}|_{\mathcal{X}}^{2}<\psi(Y)+\frac{1}{2\tau}|Y-X^{\prime}|_{\mathcal{X}}^{2}=\phi(\nu)+\frac{1}{2\tau}W_{2}^{2}(\mu,\nu),

which shows that
\mu_{\tau}
is a strict minimizer of
(
5.4
).

To prove (2), first of all notice that, thanks to
[
2
, Lemma 10.3.8]
,
\phi
is a
regular functional
according to
[
2
, Definition 10.3.9]
.
Let
-\partial^{\circ}\psi(X)
be the element of minimal norm in
-\partial\psi(X)
and let us denote by
\Phi_{\mu}:=(X,-\partial^{\circ}\psi(X))_{\sharp}\mathbb{P}\in-\boldsymbol{\partial}\phi[\iota_{X}]
by Proposition
5.2
. Denoting
\mu:=\iota_{X}
, we have

|\Phi_{\mu}|_{2}^{2}=|-\partial^{\circ}\psi(X)|_{\mathcal{X}}^{2}=\lim_{\tau\downarrow 0}\frac{\psi(X)-\psi(\boldsymbol{J}_{\tau}X)}{\tau}=\lim_{\tau\downarrow 0}\frac{\phi(\mu)-\phi(\mu_{\tau})}{\tau}=|-\boldsymbol{\partial}^{\circ}\phi(\mu)|_{2}^{2},

where
-\boldsymbol{\partial}^{\circ}\phi(\mu)
denotes the unique element of minimal norm in
-\boldsymbol{\partial}\phi[\mu]
(cf.
[
2
, Theorem 10.3.11]
), the last equality comes from
[
2
, Remark 10.3.14]
and the second equality comes from Corollary
A.4
. This proves (2).

Also (3) follows by Corollary
A.4
, while the fact that
-\boldsymbol{\partial}^{\circ}\phi=-\boldsymbol{\partial}_{\mathrm{t}}^{\circ}\phi[\mu]
is concentrated on a map follows by Theorem
3.20
(1) being
-\boldsymbol{\partial}_{\mathrm{t}}\phi[\mu]
maximal totally
\lambda
-dissipative by Proposition
5.2
(2).
To prove (4) it is enough to notice that, if
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}}
is a maximal totally
\lambda
-dissipative extension of
-\boldsymbol{\partial}^{\circ}\phi
with domain included in
\overline{\mathrm{D}(\phi)}
, then its Lagrangian representation
{\boldsymbol{B}}
has domain included in
\overline{\mathrm{D}(\psi)}
and it is
\lambda
-dissipative with every element of the minimal selection of
-\partial\psi
(cf. Theorem
3.12
). By (
A.3
) we thus get that
{\boldsymbol{B}}\subset-\partial\psi
and thus, being both maximal
\lambda
-dissipative, they coincide.
∎

Remark 5.4

(Comparison with similar notions of subdifferentiability)
.

Part of Proposition
5.3
can be compared with
the deep results obtained by
[
33
]

for the
Fréchet subdifferential of general
(not necessarily
\lambda
-convex) real-valued functionals when
\mathsf{X}
has finite dimension.
Using our notation,

[
33
]
restricts the analysis to
elements of
the Wasserstein-Fréchet subdifferential

\boldsymbol{\partial}\phi
of
\phi
which can be expressed by maps;
it is proven in
[
33
, Theorem 3.21, Corollary 3.22]
that such a subset of

\boldsymbol{\partial}\phi(\mu)

is nonempty if and only if the Fréchet subdifferential of
\psi
at
X
with
\mu=\iota_{X}
is nonemtpty. Moreover in
[
33
, Theorem 3.14]

it is proven that, given
\mu\in\mathrm{D}(\phi)
, all the maps

\boldsymbol{f}
belonging to
\Tan_{\mu}\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{X})
for which
(\boldsymbol{i}_{\mathsf{X}},\boldsymbol{f})_{\sharp}\mu
belongs to

\boldsymbol{\partial}\phi(\mu)

correspond to elements

\boldsymbol{f}\circ X
in
\partial\psi(X)
;
in particular

[
33
, Corollary 3.22]
shows that the element of minimal norm of the Fréchet subdifferential of
\psi
at
X
can be written as
\boldsymbol{f}^{\circ}\circ X
, where
\boldsymbol{f}^{\circ}
is the element of minimal norm of the Fréchet subdifferential of
\phi
at
\iota_{X}
(compare in particular with items (2),(3) in Proposition
5.3
).
On the other hand, working with general MPVFs and
elements in
\partial\psi(X)

which not necessarily have the form
\boldsymbol{f}\circ X

allows to prove
the law invariance of
\partial\psi
and to work with functions
\phi
whose proper domain

\mathrm{D}(\phi)
is strictly contained in
\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{X})
.

We also mention that the lifting technique we are using here is of fundamental relevance for the concept of L-derivative considered in
[
23
, Definition 5.22]
,
[
22
, Definition 6.1]
, and inspired by

[
38
]
.
Using our notation, in
[
23
,
22
]
a function
\phi:\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{X})\to\mathbb{R}
is said to be L-differentiable at
\mu=\iota_{X}\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{X})
, for
X\in\mathcal{X}
, if the lifted function
\psi:\mathcal{X}\to\mathbb{R}
is Fréchet differentible at
X
. The notion of L-differentiability can also be used to define a notion of convexity (called L-convexity) for functionals
\phi:\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{X})\to\mathbb{R}
which are continuously differentiable:
we refer the interested reader to
[
23
, Section 5.5.1, Definition 5.70]
and we only mention that
for such a class of regular functionals this definition is equivalent to total convexity.

For clarity of explanation, we anticipate here a result linking geodesic convexity to total convexity whose proof, in a more general setting, is deferred to Section
9
(see in particular Theorem
9.1
).

Theorem 5.5
.

Assume that
\dim(\mathsf{X})\geq 2
. Let
\mathsf{U}\subset\mathsf{X}
be open, convex, non-empty and let
\phi:\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{X})\to(-\infty,+\infty]
be a proper, lower semicontinuous and geodesically
(-\lambda)
-convex functional whose domain satisfies
\mathcal{P}_{f}(\mathsf{U})\subset\mathrm{D}(\phi)
and such that
\mathcal{P}_{f}(\mathsf{U})
is
dense in energy,
meaning that for every
\mu\in\mathrm{D}(\phi)
there exists
(\mu_{n})_{n}\subset\mathcal{P}_{f}(\mathsf{U})
such that

\mu_{n}\to\mu\quad\text{ and }\quad\phi(\mu_{n})\to\phi(\mu).

Then
\phi
is totally
(-\lambda)
-convex. In particular, every continuous and geodesically
(-\lambda)
-convex functional
\phi:\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{X})\to\mathbb{R}
is totally
(-\lambda)
-convex.

Theorem 5.6

(Gradient flows of totally convex functionals)
.

Let
\phi:\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{X})\to(-\infty,+\infty]
be a proper, lower semicontinuous and totally

(-\lambda)
-convex functional and let
\psi
be as in
(
5.2
). For every
\mu_{0}\in\overline{\mathrm{D}(\phi)}
, let us
denote by
(S_{t})_{t\geq 0}
the family of semigroups in
\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{X})
induced by the Lagrangian flow associated to
the maximal total
\lambda
-dissipative
MPVF

-\boldsymbol{\partial}_{\mathrm{t}}\phi=\iota^{2}(-\partial\psi)
(cf. Definition

4.1
).
Then the locally Lipschitz curve
\mu:[0,+\infty)\to\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{X})
,
\mu_{t}:=S_{t}(\mu_{0})
, is the unique
gradient flow
for
\phi
starting from
\mu_{0}
, in the sense that

(\boldsymbol{i}_{\mathsf{X}},\boldsymbol{v}_{t})_{\sharp}\mu_{t}=-\boldsymbol{\partial}^{\circ}\phi[\mu_{t}]=-\boldsymbol{\partial}_{\mathrm{t}}^{\circ}\phi[\mu_{t}]\quad\text{ for a.e. }t>0,

where
\boldsymbol{v}
is the Wasserstein velocity field of
\mu
coming from
Theorem
2.11
and therefore satisfies all the properties of

[
2
, Thm. 11.2.1]
.

Moreover,
t\mapsto S_{t}(\mu_{0})
is also the unique
(-\lambda)
-
EVI
solution for the
MPVF

-\boldsymbol{\partial}\phi
starting from
\mu_{0}\in\overline{\mathrm{D}(\phi)}
and
S_{t}
is a semigroup of
e^{\lambda t}
-Lipschitz transformations satisfying

W_{2}(S_{t}(\mu_{0}),S_{t}(\mu_{1}))\leq e^{\lambda t}W_{2}(\mu_{0},\mu_{1})\quad\text{for any }\mu_{0},\,\mu_{1}\in\overline{\mathrm{D}(\phi)}.

Proof.

Since

\phi
is lower semicontinuous and

(-\lambda)
-convex along generalized geodesics,
in particular it is coercive
thanks to
[
40
, Theorem 4.3]
:
we can apply
[
2
, Theorem 11.2.1]

to get that
there exists a unique gradient flow

\mu:[0,+\infty)\to\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{X})
for
\phi
starting from

\mu_{0}
. By
[
27
, Theorem 5.22(e)]
this also shows that

\mu
is the unique
(-\lambda)
-EVI solution for

-\boldsymbol{\partial}\phi
starting from
\mu_{0}
.

Since
\boldsymbol{\partial}^{\circ}\phi=\boldsymbol{\partial}^{\circ}_{\mathrm{t}}\phi

by Proposition
5.3
, we
can apply Theorem
4.2
and Theorem
4.4

to show that
\mu
coincides with
S_{t}(\mu_{0})
, first for every

\mu_{0}\in D(\boldsymbol{\partial}\phi)
and then also in its closure, thanks to
the regularization effect.
∎

We conclude the section with a pivotal example of a functional
\phi
to which the results of this section can be applied.

Example 5.7
.

Let
P,W:\mathsf{X}\to(-\infty,+\infty]
be proper, lower semicontinuous and
(-\lambda)
-convex functions, with
W
even. We define the functional
\phi:\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{X})\to(-\infty,+\infty]
as

\phi(\mu):=\int_{\mathsf{X}}P\,\mathrm{d}\mu+\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathsf{X}\times\mathsf{X}}W(x-y)\,\mathrm{d}(\mu\otimes\mu)(x,y),\quad\mu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{X}).

Notice that
W(0)
is finite so that, if
x_{0}\in\mathrm{D}(P)
, then

\phi(\delta_{x_{0}})=P(x_{0})+\frac{1}{2}W(0)<+\infty
, so that

\phi
is proper. Moreover, by
[
2
, Propositions 9.3.2 and
9.3.5]
, we have that
\phi
is lower semicontinuous and totally

(-\lambda\land 0)
-convex.

Part II.

thm:easy-but-not-obvious
Let
\mu_{0},\mu_{1}\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{X})
be two measures with finite support,

\boldsymbol{\gamma}\in\Gamma(\mu_{0},\mu_{1})

and

\mu_{t}:=(\mathsf{x}^{t})_{\sharp}\boldsymbol{\gamma}
,
t\in[0,1]
. Then the following properties hold.

(1)

For every
s\in[0,1]
there exists
\delta>0
such that
for every
t\in[0,1]
with
|t-s|\leq\delta

\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{s,t}:=(\mathsf{x}^{s},\mathsf{x}^{t})_{\sharp}\boldsymbol{\gamma}

is an optimal plan between
\mu_{s}
and
\mu_{t}
, so that

W_{2}^{2}(\mu_{s},\mu_{t})=\int|y-x|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{s,t}=|t-s|^{2}\int|y-x|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{\gamma}(x,y).

(2)

There exist a finite number of points

t_{0}=0<t_{1}<t_{2}<\cdots<t_{K}=1
such that
for every
k=1,\cdots,K
,
\mu|_{[t_{k-1},t_{k}]}
is
a minimal constant speed geodesic and

W_{2}^{2}(\mu_{t^{\prime}},\mu_{t^{\prime\prime}})=|t^{\prime\prime}-t^{\prime}|^{2}\int|y-x|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{\gamma}(x,y)\quad\text{for every }t^{\prime},t^{\prime\prime}\in[t_{k-1},t_{k}].

(3)

The length of the curve
t\mapsto\mu_{t}
coincides
with

\Big(\int|y-x|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{\gamma}\Big)^{1/2}
.

Proof.

The first statement follows by Lemma
, since
every measure
\mu_{s}
has finite support and for every
t\in[0,1]

\displaystyle\sup\big\{|y-x|:(x,y)\in\supp\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{s,t}\big\}

\displaystyle=|t-s|\sup\big\{|y-x|:(x,y)\in\supp\boldsymbol{\gamma}\big\}

\displaystyle\leq|t-s|\max\{|y-x|:x\in\supp\mu_{0},\ y\in\supp\mu_{1}\big\}.

In order to prove the second claim, we define an increasing sequence

(t_{n})_{n=0}^{\infty}\subset[0,1]
by induction as follows:

•

t_{0}:=0
;

•

if
t_{n}<1
then
t_{n+1}:=\sup\Big\{t\in(t_{n},1]:W_{2}^{2}(\mu_{t_{n}},\mu_{t})=|t-t_{n}|^{2}\int|y-x|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{\gamma}\Big\}
;

•

if
t_{n}=1
then
t_{n+1}=1
.

The sequence is well defined thanks to the
first claim. It is easy to see that there exists
K\in\mathbb{N}
such that

t_{K}=1
. If not,
t_{n}
would be strictly increasing with limit

t_{\infty}\leq 1
as
n\to\infty
. By the first claim, there exists

r>0
such that the restriction of
\mu
to
[t_{\infty}-r,t_{\infty}]

is a minimal geodesic, so that whenever
t_{n}\geq t_{\infty}-r
we should
get
t_{n+1}=t_{\infty}
, a contradiction.

Claim
(3)
follows immediately by
(2)
.
∎

6.2.
Injectivity of interpolation maps

Given two pairs of points
(a^{\prime},b^{\prime})
and
(a^{\prime\prime},b^{\prime\prime})
in
\mathsf{X}^{2}

it is easy to check that

(6.1)

(1-t)a^{\prime}+tb^{\prime}\neq(1-t)a^{\prime\prime}+tb^{\prime\prime}\quad\text{for every }t\in(0,1)\quad\Leftrightarrow\quad b^{\prime\prime}-b^{\prime}\not\in\Big\{-s(a^{\prime\prime}-a^{\prime}):s>0\Big\}.

In particular,
given a set
A\subset\mathsf{X}
we consider the
set of directions

(6.2)

\operatorname{dir}(A):=\Big\{s(a^{\prime}-a^{\prime\prime}):s\in\mathbb{R},\ a^{\prime},a^{\prime\prime}\in A\Big\}=\bigcup_{s\in\mathbb{R}}s\big(A-A\big).

Definition 6.3
.

Given
A,B\subset\mathsf{X}
we say that
the chords of
B
are not aligned with the directions of
A
if

(6.3)

(B-B)\cap\operatorname{dir}(A)=\{0\}.

In this case, for every
t\in(0,1)
the map

\mathsf{x}^{t}:\mathsf{X}^{2}\to\mathsf{X}
is injective on
A\times B
.

When
\mathsf{X}
has at least dimension
2
,
it is remarkable that in the discrete setting,
it is always possible to perturb
the elements of a finite set
B
in order to satisfy condition (
6.3
) with respect to a fixed finite set
A
.
In particular, we can always find a suitable small perturbation
of the points in
B
, so that the chords of the perturbed set are not aligned with the directions of the fixed set

A
.

Proposition 6.4

(Injectivity by small perturbations)
.

Assume that
\dim\mathsf{X}\geq 2
and
A\subset\mathsf{X}
be a finite
set.
For every finite set of distinct points

B=\{b_{n}\}_{n=1}^{N}\subset\mathsf{X}

there exists a finite set
B^{\prime}:=\{b_{n}^{\prime}\}_{n=1}^{N}
of distinct points
with
|b_{n}^{\prime}-b_{n}|<1

such that,
setting

(6.4)

b_{n}(s):=(1-s)b_{n}+sb_{n}^{\prime},\quad B(s):=\{b_{n}(s)\}_{n=1}^{N},

we have that
\#B(s)=N
for all
s\in[0,1]
and

(6.5)

(B(s)-B(s))\cap\operatorname{dir}(A)=\{0\}\quad\text{for every }s\in(0,1].

In particular, for every
t\in(0,1)
the
restriction of the map
\mathsf{x}^{t}
to

A\times B(s)
is injective for every
s\in(0,1]
.

Proof.

We split the proof of the Proposition in two steps.

Claim 1
:

there exists a finite set of distinct points
B^{\prime\prime}:=\{b_{n}^{\prime\prime}\}_{n=1}^{N}

with
|b_{n}^{\prime\prime}-b_{n}|<1

satisfying

(6.6)

(B^{\prime\prime}-B^{\prime\prime})\cap\operatorname{dir}(A)=\{0\}.

We can argue by induction with respect to the cardinality
N
of
the set
B
.
The statement is obvious in case
N=1
(it is sufficient to
choose
b^{\prime\prime}_{1}:=b_{1}
).

Let us assume that the property holds for all the sets of
cardinality
N-1\geq 1
. We can thus find a finite set of distinct points

B_{N-1}^{\prime\prime}=\{b_{n}^{\prime\prime}\}_{n=1}^{N-1}

satisfying
(B^{\prime\prime}_{N-1}-B^{\prime\prime}_{N-1})\cap\operatorname{dir}(A)=\{0\}
.
We look for a point
b^{\prime\prime}_{N}\in U\setminus B_{N-1}^{\prime\prime}
, where
U:=\{x\in\mathsf{X}:|x-b_{N}|<1\}
,
such that
B^{\prime\prime}_{N}:=B^{\prime\prime}_{N-1}\cup\{b^{\prime\prime}_{N}\}

satisfies (
6.6
).
b^{\prime\prime}_{N}
should therefore satisfy

b^{\prime\prime}_{N}\in U,\quad b^{\prime\prime}_{N}-b^{\prime\prime}_{n}\not\in\operatorname{dir}(A)\quad\text{for every }n\in\{1,\cdots,N-1\}.

Such a point surely exists, since
\operatorname{dir}(A)
is a closed set with
empty interior (here we use the fact that the dimension of
\mathsf{X}
is
at least
2
)
and the union
\bigcup_{n=1}^{N-1}\big(b^{\prime\prime}_{n}+\operatorname{dir}(A)\big)
has empty interior as well, so that it
cannot contain the open set
U
.

Claim 2
:
If
B^{\prime\prime}
satisfies the properties of the
previous claim, then there exists
\delta\in(0,1]
such that setting

(6.7)

b^{\prime}_{n}:=(1-\delta)b_{n}+\delta b_{n}^{\prime\prime},

the set
B^{\prime}=\{b_{n}^{\prime}\}_{n=1}^{N}
satisfies the thesis.

We denote by
\mathsf{a}
the cardinality
\#A
of
A
and
we first make a simple remark:
for every
z,z^{\prime\prime}\in\mathsf{X}

(6.8)

\#\{s\in[0,1]:z(s):=(1-s)z+sz^{\prime\prime}\in\operatorname{dir}(A)\}>\mathsf{a}^{2}\quad\Rightarrow\quad z,z^{\prime\prime}\in\operatorname{dir}(A).

Indeed, the set
A-A
contains at most

\mathsf{a}^{2}
distinct
elements,
so that if the left hand side of (
6.8
) is true, then
there are at least two distinct values
s_{1},s_{2}\in[0,1]
,

r_{1},r_{2}\in\mathbb{R}
and a
vector
w\in A-A
such that

(1-s_{1})z+s_{1}z^{\prime\prime}=r_{1}w,\ (1-s_{2})z+s_{2}z^{\prime\prime}=r_{2}w
.
We then get

z(s)=z(s_{1})+\frac{s-s_{1}}{s_{2}-s_{1}}(z(s_{2})-z(s_{1}))=r_{1}w+\frac{(s-s_{1})(r_{2}-r_{1})}{s_{2}-s_{1}}w\in\operatorname{dir}(A)\quad\text{for
every }s\in[0,1],

hence (
6.8
).
As a particular consequence of (
6.8
) we get that
if
z^{\prime\prime}
does not belong to
\operatorname{dir}(A)
,
then the set
\{s\in(0,1]:z(s):=(1-s)z+sz^{\prime\prime}\in\operatorname{dir}(A)\}
is
finite, so that

(6.9)

\forall\,z,z^{\prime\prime}\in\mathsf{X}:\ z^{\prime\prime}\not\in\operatorname{dir}(A)\quad\Rightarrow\quad\exists\,\delta>0:\ (1-s)z+sz^{\prime\prime}\not\in\operatorname{dir}(A)\quad\text{for
every }s\in(0,\delta].

Let us now apply property (
6.9
) to all the pairs
(z,z^{\prime\prime})
of the
form

z=b_{n}-b_{m},\ z^{\prime\prime}=b_{n}^{\prime\prime}-b_{m}^{\prime\prime}
,
n,m\in\{1,\cdots,N\}
, with

n\neq m
. Since
b_{n}^{\prime\prime}-b_{m}^{\prime\prime}\not\in\operatorname{dir}(A)
we
deduce that
there exists
\delta_{n,m}>0
such that

(6.10)

(1-s)(b_{n}-b_{m})+s(b_{n}^{\prime\prime}-b_{m}^{\prime\prime})\not\in\operatorname{dir}(A)\quad\text{for every
}s\in(0,\delta_{n,m}].

Setting

\tilde{\delta}:=\min\{|b_{n}-b_{m}|\,:\,n,m\in\{1,\dots,N\},\,n\neq m\}>0

and choosing
\delta:=\min_{n,m}\{\delta_{n,m},\,\tilde{\delta}/3\}>0
, then it is not difficult to check that
B^{\prime}
satisfies
the thesis, with
b_{n}^{\prime}
as in
(
6.7
).
Indeed,
|b_{n}-b_{n}^{\prime}|=\delta|b_{n}-b_{n}^{\prime\prime}|<1
,
and for every
s\in[0,1]
and
n
we get

b_{n}(s):=(1-s)b_{n}+sb_{n}^{\prime}=(1-s)b_{n}+s(1-\delta)b_{n}+s\delta b_{n}^{\prime\prime}=(1-\delta s)b_{n}+\delta sb_{n}^{\prime\prime}

so that

\displaystyle b_{n}(s)-b_{m}(s)=(1-\delta s)(b_{n}-b_{m})+\delta s(b_{n}^{\prime\prime}-b_{m}^{\prime\prime})\not\in\operatorname{dir}(A)

thanks to (
6.10
) and the fact that
s\delta\leq\delta_{n,m}
.
∎

7.
Total dissipativity of MPVFs along discrete measures

We will consider the following subsets of the space
\mathcal{P}_{f}(X)
of probability measures with finite support
in a general Polish space
X
: for every
N\in\mathbb{N}

(7.1)

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}_{f,N}(X):={}

\displaystyle\Big\{\mu\in\mathcal{P}_{f}(X):N\mu(A)\in\mathbb{N}\ \forall\,A\subset X\Big\},

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}_{\#N}(X):={}

\displaystyle\Big\{\mu\in\mathcal{P}_{f}(X):N\mu(A)\in\{0,1\}\ \forall\,A\subset X\Big\}\ \

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle\Big\{\mu\in\mathcal{P}_{f,N}(X):\#\supp(\mu)=N\Big\}.

Notice that every measure
\mu\in\mathcal{P}_{f,N}(X)
can be expressed in
the form

\mu=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{n=1}^{N}\delta_{x_{n}}\quad\text{for some points
}x_{1},\cdots,x_{N}\in X.

The measure
\mu
belongs to
\mathcal{P}_{\#N}(X)
if the points
x_{1},\cdots,x_{n}
are
distinct.

If
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
is a MPVF,
\mu_{0},\mu_{1}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathsf{X})
, we correspondingly set

(7.2)

\mathrm{D}_{\star}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}):=\mathrm{D}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})\cap\mathcal{P}_{\star}(\mathsf{X}),\quad\Gamma_{\star}(\mu_{0},\mu_{1}):=\Gamma(\mu_{0},\mu_{1})\cap\mathcal{P}_{\star}(\mathsf{X}\times\mathsf{X}),

where
\star
is replaced by one of the symbols
f,c,b
,
(f,N)
,
\#N
above.

For every
\mu_{0},\mu_{1}\in\mathcal{P}_{f}(\mathsf{X})

we introduce the

L^{\infty}
-Wasserstein distance by

(7.3)

W_{\infty}(\mu_{0},\mu_{1}):=\min\Big\{\big|\mathsf{x}^{0}-\mathsf{x}^{1}|_{L^{\infty}(\mathsf{X}\times\mathsf{X},\boldsymbol{\mu};\mathsf{X})}:\boldsymbol{\mu}\in\Gamma(\mu_{0},\mu_{1})\Big\}.

In the following, we investigate the results recalled in Theorem
2.19
in the case of marginals
\mu_{0},\mu_{1}
with finite support, but removing the optimality requirement over the coupling
\boldsymbol{\mu}
.
Recall that the set
\Gamma({\mu_{0}},{\mu_{1}}|{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})
has been introduced in
Definition
2.18
.

Lemma 7.1
.

Let
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
be a
MPVF
satisfying (
2.17
) and let

\mu_{0},\mu_{1}\in\mathrm{D}_{f}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})
with
\boldsymbol{\mu}\in\Gamma({\mu_{0}},{\mu_{1}}|{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})

satisfy at least one of the following conditions:

(1)

for every
t\in(0,1)
,
\mathsf{x}^{t}
is
\boldsymbol{\mu}
-essentially injective;

(2)

for every
t\in(0,1)
,
there exists an element
\Phi_{t}\in{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}[\mathsf{x}^{t}_{\sharp}\boldsymbol{\mu}]

which is concentrated on a map.

Then

(7.4)

[{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}},\boldsymbol{\mu}]_{r,s}-[{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}},\boldsymbol{\mu}]_{l,t}\leq\lambda(t-s)W^{2},\quad W^{2}:=\int|x_{0}-x_{1}|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{\mu},\quad\text{for every }0\leq s<t\leq 1.

In particular,

t\mapsto[{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}},\boldsymbol{\mu}]_{r,t}+\lambda W^{2}t
and

t\mapsto[{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}},\boldsymbol{\mu}]_{l,t}+\lambda W^{2}t
are increasing
respectively in
[0,1)
and in
(0,1]
,

[{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}},\boldsymbol{\mu}]_{l,t}=[{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}},\boldsymbol{\mu}]_{r,t}
at every
t\in(0,1)
where one of them is continuous, hence they coincide outside a countable set of discontinuities.

Proof.

By Theorem
2.19
it is not restrictive to assume
\lambda=0
;
we can also assume
s=0
and
t=1
thanks to (
2.11
).
We set
\mu_{t}:=\mathsf{x}^{t}_{\sharp}\boldsymbol{\mu}
and we select
an element
\Phi_{t}\in{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}[\mu_{t}]

(in case (2) we can also suppose
that
\Phi_{t}
is concentrated on a map).

Applying Theorem
, we can find
points
t_{0}=0<t_{1}<\cdots<t_{K}=1
such that

\boldsymbol{\mu}^{k}:=(\mathsf{x}^{t_{k-1}},\mathsf{x}^{t_{k}})_{\sharp}\boldsymbol{\mu}\in\Gamma({\mu_{t_{k-1}}},{\mu_{t_{k}}}|{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})\cap\Gamma_{o}(\mu_{t_{k-1}},\mu_{t_{k}})\quad\text{for every }k=1,\cdots,K.

In particular, from (
2.11
) and Theorem
2.19
(2), we get

[\Phi_{t_{k-1}},\boldsymbol{\mu}]_{r,t_{k-1}}=\frac{1}{t_{k}-t_{k-1}}[\Phi_{t_{k-1}},\boldsymbol{\mu}^{k}]_{r,0}\leq\frac{1}{t_{k}-t_{k-1}}[\Phi_{t_{k}},\boldsymbol{\mu}^{k}]_{l,1}=[\Phi_{t_{k}},\boldsymbol{\mu}]_{l,t_{k}}.

Since, for
1\leq k<K
,
\mathsf{x}^{t_{k}}
is
\boldsymbol{\mu}
-essentially injective
(if assumption (1) holds) or
\Phi_{t_{k}}

is concentrated on its barycenter (if assumption (2) holds),
Theorem
2.13
(4) yields

[\Phi_{t_{k}},\boldsymbol{\mu}]_{l,t_{k}}=[\Phi_{t_{k}},\boldsymbol{\mu}]_{r,t_{k}}
so that

[\Phi_{0},\boldsymbol{\mu}]_{r,0}\leq[\Phi_{1},\boldsymbol{\mu}]_{l,1}.

Taking the supremum w.r.t.
\Phi_{0}\in{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}[\mu_{0}]
and
the infimum w.r.t.
\Phi_{1}\in{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}[\mu_{1}]
we obtain (
7.4
).
The last part of the statement follows as in the proof of Theorem
2.19
.
∎

Theorem 7.2

(Self-improving dissipativity along discrete couplings)
.

Assume that
\dim\mathsf{X}\geq 2
. Let
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
be a
MPVF
satisfying
(
2.17
),
N\in\mathbb{N}
,
let
\mu_{0},\mu_{1}\in\mathrm{D}_{f}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})
,

\boldsymbol{\mu}\in\Gamma(\mu_{0},\mu_{1})
and let
\mu_{t}=\mathsf{x}^{t}_{\sharp}\boldsymbol{\mu}
,
t\in[0,1]
.
Assume that one of the following conditions is
satisfied:

(1)

\boldsymbol{\mu}\in\mathcal{P}_{f,N}(\mathsf{X}\times\mathsf{X})

and for every
t\in(0,1)

\mu_{t}
belongs to the
relative interior of

\mathrm{D}_{f,N}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})
in
\mathcal{P}_{f,N}(\mathsf{X})
;

(2)

for every
t\in(0,1)

\mu_{t}
belongs to the interior of

\mathrm{D}_{f}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})
in the metric space
(\mathcal{P}_{f}(\mathsf{X}),W_{\infty})
.

Then

(7.5)

[{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}},\boldsymbol{\mu}]_{r,s}-[{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}},\boldsymbol{\mu}]_{l,t}\leq\lambda(t-s)W^{2},\quad W^{2}:=\int|x_{0}-x_{1}|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{\mu},\quad\text{for every }0\leq s<t\leq 1.

Proof.

We carry out the proof in case (1), the proof in case (2)
is analogous.
By Theorem
2.19
it is not restrictive to assume
\lambda=0
; we can also assume
s=0
and
t=1
thanks to (
2.11
). By Theorem
we can find
0<\delta<1/2
and
\tau\in(\delta,1-\delta)
s.t.
\mathsf{x}^{\delta},\mathsf{x}^{\tau}
and
\mathsf{x}^{1-\delta}
are
\boldsymbol{\mu}
-essentially injective and
(\mathsf{x}^{0},\mathsf{x}^{\delta})_{\sharp}\boldsymbol{\mu}
, (
\mathsf{x}^{1-\delta},\mathsf{x}^{1})_{\sharp}\boldsymbol{\mu}
are optimal. In this way, since by Theorem
2.19
the relation (
7.5
) is true both for the case
s=0,t=\delta
and
s=1-\delta,t=1
, we only need to prove it for
s=\delta
and
t=1-\delta
.

We set
A=\supp(\mu_{\delta})\cup\supp(\mu_{1-\delta})
and
B=\supp(\mu_{\tau})
. By compactness, we can find
\varepsilon>0
such that every measure in
\mathcal{P}_{f,N}(\mathsf{X})
in the
W_{2}
-neighborhood of radious
\varepsilon>0
around
\mu_{t}
is contained in
\mathrm{D}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})
for every
\delta\leq t\leq 1-\delta
.

Applying Proposition
6.4

we can find a map
\boldsymbol{b}:B\to\mathsf{X}
with values
in the open ball of radious
\varepsilon
centered at
0

such that setting
\boldsymbol{b}^{s}(x):=x+s\boldsymbol{b}(x)
for every
s\in[0,1]

and
x\in B
,
the set
B^{s}:=\boldsymbol{b}^{s}(B)
satisfies

(B^{s}-B^{s})\cap\operatorname{dir}(A)=\{0\}
and
\#B^{s}=\#\supp(\mu_{\tau})
for every
s\in(0,1]
.
Considering the measures
\nu_{s}:=(\boldsymbol{b}^{s})_{\sharp}\mu_{\tau}
,
we can pick
\Psi_{s}\in{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}[\nu_{s}]
with barycenter
\boldsymbol{v}_{s}:B^{s}\to\mathsf{X}
, i.e.

\boldsymbol{v}_{s}(y):=\int v\,\mathrm{d}\Psi_{s}(y,v).

Now for every
(x_{0},x_{1})\in\supp((\mathsf{x}^{\delta},\mathsf{x}^{1-\delta})_{\sharp}\boldsymbol{\mu})
we
set

x_{a}:=\mathsf{x}^{a}(x_{0},x_{1}),\quad\boldsymbol{b}^{s,\tau}:=\boldsymbol{b}^{s}(x_{a}),\quad\boldsymbol{v}^{s,\tau}:=\boldsymbol{v}^{s}(\boldsymbol{b}^{s,\tau}),

where
a=\frac{\tau-\delta}{1-2\delta}
. Notice that
x_{a}\in B=\supp(\mu_{\tau})
, so the above definitions are well-posed. Let us consider
\Phi_{\delta}\in{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}[\mu_{\delta}]
,
\Phi_{1-\delta}\in{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}[\mu_{1-\delta}]
and
\boldsymbol{\sigma}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathsf{T\kern-1.5ptX}\times\mathsf{T\kern-1.5ptX})
s.t.
(\mathsf{x}^{0},\mathsf{x}^{1})_{\sharp}\boldsymbol{\sigma}=(\mathsf{x}^{\delta},\mathsf{x}^{1-\delta})_{\sharp}\boldsymbol{\mu}
,
(\mathsf{x}^{0},\mathsf{v}^{0})_{\sharp}\boldsymbol{\sigma}=\Phi_{\delta}
and
(\mathsf{x}^{1},\mathsf{v}^{1})_{\sharp}\boldsymbol{\sigma}=\Phi_{1-\delta}
. For every
(x_{0},v_{0},x_{1},v_{1})\in\supp(\boldsymbol{\sigma})
we have

(7.6)

\begin{split}\langle&v_{0}-v_{1},x_{0}-x_{1}\rangle=\langle v_{0}-\boldsymbol{v}^{s,\tau},x_{0}-x_{1}\rangle+\langle v_{1}-\boldsymbol{v}^{s,\tau},x_{1}-x_{0}\rangle\\
&=\frac{1}{a}\langle v_{0}-\boldsymbol{v}^{s,\tau},x_{0}-x_{a}\rangle+\frac{1}{1-a}\langle v_{1}-\boldsymbol{v}^{s,\tau},x_{1}-x_{a}\rangle\\
&=\frac{1}{a}\langle v_{0}-\boldsymbol{v}^{s,\tau},x_{0}-\boldsymbol{b}^{s,\tau}\rangle+\frac{1}{1-a}\langle v_{1}-\boldsymbol{v}^{s,\tau},x_{1}-\boldsymbol{b}^{s,\tau}\rangle\\
&\qquad+\frac{1}{a}\langle v_{0}-\boldsymbol{v}^{s,\tau},\boldsymbol{b}^{s,\tau}-x_{a}\rangle+\frac{1}{1-a}\langle v_{1}-\boldsymbol{v}^{s,\tau},\boldsymbol{b}^{s,\tau}-x_{a}\rangle\\
&=\frac{1}{a}\langle v_{0}-\boldsymbol{v}^{s,\tau},x_{0}-\boldsymbol{b}^{s,\tau}\rangle+\frac{1}{1-a}\langle v_{1}-\boldsymbol{v}^{s,\tau},x_{1}-\boldsymbol{b}^{s,\tau}\rangle\\
&\qquad+\frac{1}{a(1-a)}\langle\boldsymbol{v}^{1,\tau}-\boldsymbol{v}^{s,\tau},\boldsymbol{b}^{s,\tau}-x_{a}\rangle+\frac{1}{a(1-a)}\langle(1-a)v_{0}+av_{1}-\boldsymbol{v}^{1,\tau},\boldsymbol{b}^{s,\tau}-x_{a}\rangle\\
&=\frac{1}{a}\langle v_{0}-\boldsymbol{v}^{s,\tau},x_{0}-\boldsymbol{b}^{s,\tau}\rangle+\frac{1}{1-a}\langle v_{1}-\boldsymbol{v}^{s,\tau},x_{1}-\boldsymbol{b}^{s,\tau}\rangle\\
&\qquad+\frac{s}{(1-s)a(1-a)}\langle\boldsymbol{v}^{1,\tau}-\boldsymbol{v}^{s,\tau},\boldsymbol{b}^{1,\tau}-\boldsymbol{b}^{s,\tau}\rangle+\frac{s}{a(1-a)}\langle(1-a)v_{0}+av_{1}-\boldsymbol{v}^{1,\tau},\boldsymbol{b}^{1,\tau}-x_{a}\rangle.\end{split}

We have that

(7.7)

\begin{split}\int\langle v_{0}-\boldsymbol{v}^{s,\tau}(x_{0},x_{1}),x_{0}-\boldsymbol{b}^{s,\tau}(x_{0},x_{1})\rangle\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{\sigma}&=[\Phi_{\delta},\boldsymbol{\mu}^{s,\tau}]_{r,0}-[\Psi_{s},\boldsymbol{\mu}^{s,\tau}]_{l,1},\\
\int\langle v_{1}-\boldsymbol{v}^{s,\tau}(x_{0},x_{1}),x_{1}-\boldsymbol{b}^{s,\tau}(x_{0},x_{1})\rangle\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{\sigma}&=[\Phi_{1-\delta},\tilde{\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{s,\tau}]_{r,0}-[\Psi_{s},\tilde{\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{s,\tau}]_{l,1},\\
\int\langle\boldsymbol{v}^{1,\tau}(x_{0},x_{1})-\boldsymbol{v}^{s,\tau}(x_{0},x_{1}),\boldsymbol{b}^{1,\tau}(x_{0},x_{1})-\boldsymbol{b}^{s,\tau}(x_{0},x_{1})\rangle\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{\sigma}&=[\Psi_{1},\boldsymbol{\vartheta}^{s,\tau}]_{r,0}-[\Psi_{s},\boldsymbol{\vartheta}^{s,\tau}]_{l,1},\end{split}

where
\boldsymbol{\mu}^{s,\tau}=(\mathsf{x}^{0},\boldsymbol{b}^{s,\tau})_{\sharp}\boldsymbol{\sigma}
,
\tilde{\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{s,\tau}=(\mathsf{x}^{1},\boldsymbol{b}^{s,\tau})_{\sharp}\boldsymbol{\sigma}
,
\boldsymbol{\vartheta}^{s,\tau}=(\boldsymbol{b}^{1,\tau},\boldsymbol{b}^{s,\tau})_{\sharp}\boldsymbol{\sigma}
and the equalities with the pseudo scalar products come from the fact that all those plans are concentrated on a map w.r.t. their first marginal. Indeed, we can use Theorem
2.13
(4) thanks to the
\boldsymbol{\mu}
-essential injectivity of
\mathsf{x}^{\delta},\mathsf{x}^{\tau}
,
\mathsf{x}^{1-\delta}
, and use the fact that the cardinality of
B^{s}
is constant w.r.t.
s
. By construction, these plans satisfy the hypotheses of Lemma
7.1
so that all the expressions at the right-hand side of (
7.7
) are nonpositive. Combining this fact with (
7.6
), we end up with

\displaystyle\int\langle v_{0}-v_{1},x_{0}-x_{1}\rangle\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{\sigma}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{s}{a(1-a)}\int\langle(1-a)v_{0}+av_{1}-\boldsymbol{v}^{1,\tau},\boldsymbol{b}^{1,\tau}-x_{a}\rangle\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{\sigma}.

Passing to the limit as
s\downarrow 0

we obtain

\int\langle v_{0}-v_{1},x_{0}-x_{1}\rangle\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{\sigma}\leq 0.

Passing to the supremum w.r.t.
\Phi_{\delta}\in{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}[\mu_{\delta}]
and to the infimum w.r.t.
\Phi_{1-\delta}\in{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}[\mu_{1-\delta}]
, we get

[{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}},(\mathsf{x}^{\delta},\mathsf{x}^{1-\delta})_{\sharp}\boldsymbol{\mu}]_{r,0}-[{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}},(\mathsf{x}^{\delta},\mathsf{x}^{1-\delta})_{\sharp}\boldsymbol{\mu}]_{l,1}\leq 0,

which is (
7.5
) with
s=\delta
and
t=1-\delta
thanks to Theorem
2.13
(3).
∎

Remark 7.3
.

If
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}\subset\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{T\kern-1.5ptX})
is a
\lambda
-dissipative
{\rm MPVF}
with
\mathrm{D}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})=\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{X})
, then
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
is
\lambda
-dissipative along discrete couplings thanks to Theorem
7.2
and Theorem
2.13
, i.e.

\left[\Phi,\Psi\right]_{r}\leq\lambda\int_{\mathsf{X}\times\mathsf{X}}|x-y|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{\gamma}(x,y)

for every
\Phi,\Psi\in{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
and any
\boldsymbol{\gamma}\in\Gamma(\mathsf{x}_{\sharp}\Phi,\mathsf{x}_{\sharp}\Psi)
such that
\mathsf{x}_{\sharp}\Phi,\mathsf{x}_{\sharp}\Psi
belong to
\mathcal{P}_{f}(\mathsf{X})
.

Let us introduce the notion of “collisionless couplings”.

Definition 7.4

(Convexity along collisionless couplings)
.

Let
\mu_{0},\mu_{1}\in\mathcal{P}_{f}(\mathsf{X})
.
We say that

\boldsymbol{\mu}\in\Gamma(\mu_{0},\mu_{1})
is
collisionless
if
\mathsf{x}^{t}
is
\boldsymbol{\mu}
- essentially injective for every

t\in[0,1]
.

We say that a set
C\subset\mathcal{P}_{f}(\mathsf{X})
is
convex along
collisionless couplings
if
for every collisionless
\boldsymbol{\nu}\in\mathcal{P}_{f}(\mathsf{X}^{2})
, with

\mathsf{x}^{0}_{\sharp}\boldsymbol{\nu},\mathsf{x}^{1}_{\sharp}\boldsymbol{\nu}\in C
, and every

t\in(0,1)
we have

\mathsf{x}^{t}_{\sharp}\boldsymbol{\nu}\in C
.

Notice that if
\mu_{0},\mu_{1}\in\mathcal{P}_{\#N}(\mathsf{X})

a coupling
\boldsymbol{\mu}
in
\Gamma(\mu_{0},\mu_{1})
is collisionless
if and only if

(7.8)

\boldsymbol{\mu}\in\Gamma_{\#N}(\mathsf{X}^{2}),\quad\mathsf{x}^{t}_{\sharp}\boldsymbol{\mu}\in\mathcal{P}_{\#N}(\mathsf{X})\quad\text{for every }t\in(0,1).

Theorem 7.5

(Self-improving dissipativity along collisionless couplings)
.

Assume that
\dim\mathsf{X}\geq 2
,
N\in\mathbb{N}
, let
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
be a
MPVF
satisfying
(
2.17
) and such that

\mathrm{D}_{\#N}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})
is convex along collisionless couplings.
If

\mu_{0},\mu_{1}

belong to the
interior of

\mathrm{D}_{\#N}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})
in the metric space

(\mathcal{P}_{\#N}(\mathsf{X}),W_{2})

and

\boldsymbol{\mu}\in\Gamma_{\#N}(\mu_{0},\mu_{1})
then

(7.9)

[{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}},\boldsymbol{\mu}]_{r,0}-[{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}},\boldsymbol{\mu}]_{l,1}\leq\lambda W^{2},\quad W^{2}:=\int|x_{0}-x_{1}|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{\mu}.

Proof.

The proof is very similar to the one of Theorem
7.2
, we
keep the same notation.

Since
\boldsymbol{\mu}\in\mathcal{P}_{\#N}(\mathsf{X}^{2}),

\mathsf{x}^{0}
,
\mathsf{x}^{1}
are

\boldsymbol{\mu}
-essentially injective, so that we can select

\delta=0
.
We can then choose
\tau\in(0,1)
such that
\mathsf{x}^{\tau}
is
\boldsymbol{\mu}
-essentially injective and
\varepsilon>0
sufficiently small so that
the ball of radious
\varepsilon
centered at
\mu_{t}
is
contained in
\mathrm{D}_{\#N}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})
for every
t\in[0,1]
. We can then proceed with the
same perturbation argument of the previous proof. In the last part
of the proof, we use the fact that
\mathrm{D}_{\#N}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})
is
convex along collisionless couplings.
∎

The following result shows that in case of a deterministic
demicontinuous PVF (recall Definition
3.23
)

\lambda
-dissipativity yields total
\lambda
-dissipativity.
Similarly, we can lift the Lipschitz continuity along optimal couplings to arbitrary couplings.

Theorem 7.6

(Deterministic demicontinuous dissipative PVFs are totally dissipative)
.

Let
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}\subset\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{T\kern-1.5ptX})
be a deterministic demicontinuous
\lambda
-dissipative
PVF
with
\mathrm{D}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})=\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{X})
, of the form

(7.10)

{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}[\mu]:=(\boldsymbol{i}_{\mathsf{X}},\boldsymbol{f}(\cdot,\mu))_{\sharp}\mu,\quad\mu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{X}),

for a map

\boldsymbol{f}:\mathcal{S}\left(\mathsf{X}\right)\to\mathsf{X}
, where
\mathcal{S}\left(\mathsf{X}\right)
is as in (
2.15
).
Then
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
is maximal totally

\lambda
-dissipative.

If moreover
there exists
L>0
for which the following condition holds: for every
\mu_{0},\mu_{1}\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{X})
there exists
\boldsymbol{\mu}\in\Gamma_{o}(\mu_{0},\mu_{1})
satisfying

(7.11)

\int_{\mathsf{X}\times\mathsf{X}}\left|\boldsymbol{f}(x_{1},\mu_{1})-\boldsymbol{f}(x_{0},\mu_{0})\right|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{\mu}(x_{0},x_{1})\leq L^{2}\int_{\mathsf{X}\times\mathsf{X}}|x_{1}-x_{0}|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{\mu}(x_{0},x_{1}),

then (
7.11
) holds for every
\boldsymbol{\mu}\in\Gamma(\mu_{0},\mu_{1})
.

Proof.

By Lemma
7.1
(2) and the fact that
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
is single-valued and
concentrated on a map
\boldsymbol{f}:\mathcal{S}\left(\mathsf{X}\right)\to\mathsf{X}
, recalling Theorem
2.13
(4) we know that
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
satisfies
(
3.9
), or, equivalently,
(
1.7
) for every
\mu_{0},\mu_{1}\in\mathcal{P}_{f}(\mathsf{X}).

We use
an approximation procedure to get the general formulation
for every
\mu_{0},\mu_{1}\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{X})
and every
\boldsymbol{\mu}\in\Gamma(\mu_{0},\mu_{1})
: we take sequences
(\mu_{0}^{n})_{n},(\mu_{1}^{n})_{n}\subset\mathcal{P}_{f}(\mathsf{X})
such that
W_{2}(\mu_{0}^{n},\mu_{0})\to 0
and

W_{2}(\mu_{1}^{n},\mu_{1})\to 0
and optimal plans
\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{0}^{n}\in\Gamma_{o}(\mu_{0}^{n},\mu_{0})
and
\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{1}^{n}\in\Gamma_{o}(\mu_{1},\mu_{1}^{n})
. Let
\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{n}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathsf{X}^{4})
be such that

\pi^{1,2}_{\sharp}\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{n}=\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{0}^{n}
,
\pi^{2,3}_{\sharp}\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{n}=\boldsymbol{\mu}
and
\pi^{3,4}_{\sharp}\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{n}=\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{1}^{n}
.
Notice that we also have that
\boldsymbol{\mu}_{n}:=\pi^{1,4}_{\sharp}\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{n}

belongs to
\Gamma(\mu_{0}^{n},\mu_{1}^{n})
and converges to
\boldsymbol{\mu}
in

\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{X}^{2})

as
n\to\infty
.
Thanks to the demicontinuity of
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
and the fact that
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
is
concentrated on
\boldsymbol{f}
,
we obtain that

\boldsymbol{\vartheta}_{n}:=(\boldsymbol{i}_{\mathsf{X}\times\mathsf{X}},\boldsymbol{f}(x_{0},\mu_{0})\times\boldsymbol{f}(x_{1},\mu_{1}))_{\sharp}\boldsymbol{\mu}_{n}

converges to
\boldsymbol{\vartheta}_{n}:=(\boldsymbol{i}_{\mathsf{X}\times\mathsf{X}},\boldsymbol{f}(x_{0},\mu_{0})\times\boldsymbol{f}(x_{1},\mu_{1}))_{\sharp}\boldsymbol{\mu}
in

\mathcal{P}_{2}^{sw}(\mathsf{X}^{2}\times\mathsf{X}^{2})
.
We can then pass to the limit in the inequality

\int\langle\boldsymbol{f}(x_{1},\mu_{1})-\boldsymbol{f}(x_{0},\mu_{0}),x_{1}-x_{0}\rangle\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{\mu}_{n}(x_{0},x_{1})=\int\langle v_{1}-v_{0},x_{1}-x_{0}\rangle\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{\vartheta}_{n}(x_{0},x_{1},v_{0},v_{1})\leq 0

obtaining

\int\langle\boldsymbol{f}(x_{1},\mu_{1})-\boldsymbol{f}(x_{0},\mu_{0}),x_{1}-x_{0}\rangle\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{\mu}(x_{0},x_{1})=\int\langle v_{1}-v_{0},x_{1}-x_{0}\rangle\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{\vartheta}(x_{0},x_{1},v_{0},v_{1})\leq 0.

We can eventually apply Theorem
3.24

to get the maximality of
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
.

Concerning the second part of the Theorem,
let us first show that the condition
(
7.11
) holds
for every
\mu_{0},\mu_{1}\in\mathcal{P}_{f}(\mathsf{X})
and
every
\boldsymbol{\mu}\in\Gamma(\mu_{0},\mu_{1})
:
by Theorems
and
2.9
there exists some
K\in\mathbb{N}
and points
0=t_{0}<t_{1}<\dots<t_{K-1}<t_{K}=1
such that
(\mathsf{x}^{t_{i-1}},\mathsf{x}^{t_{i}})_{\sharp}\boldsymbol{\mu}
is the unique element of
\Gamma_{o}(\mathsf{x}^{t_{i-1}}_{\sharp}\boldsymbol{\mu},\mathsf{x}^{t_{i}}_{\sharp}\boldsymbol{\mu})
for every
i=1,\dots,K
. We thus have for every
i=1,\dots,K
that

\left(\int_{\mathsf{X}}\left|\boldsymbol{f}(\mathsf{x}^{t_{i}},\mathsf{x}^{t_{i}}_{\sharp}\boldsymbol{\mu})-\boldsymbol{f}(\mathsf{x}^{t_{i-1}},\mathsf{x}^{t_{i-1}}_{\sharp}\boldsymbol{\mu})\right|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{\mu}\right)^{1/2}\leq L(t_{i}-t_{i-1})\left(\int_{\mathsf{X}\times\mathsf{X}}|x_{1}-x_{0}|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{\mu}(x_{0},x_{1})\right)^{1/2}.

Summing up these inequalities for
i=1,\dots,K
and using the
triangular inequality in
L^{2}(\mathsf{X}\times\mathsf{X},\boldsymbol{\mu};\mathsf{X})
, we get that
(
7.11
) holds for every
\mu_{0},\mu_{1}\in\mathcal{P}_{f}(\mathsf{X})
and
every
\boldsymbol{\mu}\in\Gamma(\mu_{0},\mu_{1})
.

By using the same approximation procedure (and the same notation)
of the first part of this
proof,
we show that (
7.11
) holds for every
\mu_{0},\mu_{1}\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{X})
and every
\boldsymbol{\mu}\in\Gamma(\mu_{0},\mu_{1})
:
in fact we have the estimate

\displaystyle\left(\int_{\mathsf{X}\times\mathsf{X}}\left|\boldsymbol{f}(x_{1},\mu_{1})-\boldsymbol{f}(x_{0},\mu_{0})\right|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{\mu}(x_{0},x_{1})\right)^{1/2}=\|\boldsymbol{f}(\pi^{3},\mu_{1})-\boldsymbol{f}(\pi^{2},\mu_{0})\|_{L^{2}(\mathsf{X}^{2},\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{n};\mathsf{X})}

\displaystyle\qquad\leq\|\boldsymbol{f}(\pi^{3},\mu_{1})-\boldsymbol{f}(\pi^{4},\mu_{1}^{n})\|_{L^{2}(\mathsf{X}^{2},\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{n};\mathsf{X})}+\|\boldsymbol{f}(\pi^{4},\mu_{1}^{n})-\boldsymbol{f}(\pi^{1},\mu_{0}^{n})\|_{L^{2}(\mathsf{X}^{2},\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{n};\mathsf{X})}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+\|\boldsymbol{f}(\pi^{1},\mu_{0}^{n})-\boldsymbol{f}(\pi^{2},\mu_{0})\|_{L^{2}(\mathsf{X}^{2},\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{n};\mathsf{X})}

\displaystyle\qquad\leq L\Big(W_{2}(\mu_{1}^{n},\mu_{1})+W_{2}(\mu_{0},\mu_{0}^{n})\Big)+L\left(\int_{\mathsf{X}^{2}}|x-y|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{\mu}_{n}(x,y)\right)^{1/2}.

Passing to the limit as
n\to+\infty
, we get that (
7.11
)
holds for every
\mu_{0},\mu_{1}\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{X})
and every
\boldsymbol{\mu}\in\Gamma(\mu_{0},\mu_{1})
.
∎

8.
Construction of a totally
\lambda
-dissipative MPVF from a
discrete core

We have seen at the end of Section
3.2
(Corollary

3.21
)
that a maximal totally
\lambda
-dissipative MPVF is determined by its restriction to the set of uniform discrete
measures.

In this section, we want to investigate a closely related question,
which plays a crucial role in the construction of a maximal totally

\lambda
-dissipative MPVF: if we assign a MPVF
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
on a sufficiently
rich subset of discrete measures, is it possible to uniquely construct
a maximal extension of
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
?
In the Hilbert setting, such kind of problems are well understood if
the domain of the initial operator is open and convex (see in particular
[
50
]
, Proposition
A.12
and Theorem
A.13
). However, dealing with open sets
at the level of

\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{X})
will prevent the use of discrete measures.
We will circumvent this difficulty by a suitable localization of the open condition
in each subset
\mathcal{P}_{\#N}(\mathsf{X})
, which relies on the notion of

discrete core.

In order to allow for the greatest flexibility,
we consider collections of discrete measures indexed by
an unbounded directed subset
{\mathfrak{N}}\subset\mathbb{N}
with respect to
the partial order given by

(8.1)

\text{$m\prec n\quad\Leftrightarrow\quad m\mid n$},

where
m\mid n
means that
n/m\in\mathbb{N}
. We write
m\mathrel{\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\prec$\cr\kern 4.30554pt\cr$\smash{\raisebox{2.79857pt}{\rotatebox{90.0}{\scalebox{1}[-1]{$\nshortmid$}}}}$\cr\kern-2.15277pt\cr}}}}n
if
m\prec n
and
m\neq n
.
Typical examples are the set of all natural integers
{\mathfrak{N}}:=\mathbb{N}
or
the dyadic one
{\mathfrak{N}}:=\{2^{n}:n\in\mathbb{N}\}
.
We set

(8.2)

\mathcal{P}_{f,{\mathfrak{N}}}(\mathsf{X}):=\bigcup_{N\in{\mathfrak{N}}}\mathcal{P}_{f,N}(\mathsf{X}),\quad\mathcal{P}_{\#{\mathfrak{N}}}(\mathsf{X}):=\bigcup_{N\in{\mathfrak{N}}}\mathcal{P}_{\#N}(\mathsf{X}),

observing that, for every
N\in{\mathfrak{N}}
,
\mathcal{P}_{f,N}(\mathsf{X})
is closed in

\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{X})
and
\mathcal{P}_{\#N}(\mathsf{X})
is a relatively
open and dense subset of
\mathcal{P}_{f,N}(\mathsf{X})
.

Definition 8.1

(
{\mathfrak{N}}
-core)
.

Let
{\mathfrak{N}}
be an unbounded directed subset of
\mathbb{N}
w.r.t. the order
relation
\prec
as in (
8.1
).
A discrete
{\mathfrak{N}}
-core
is a set
\mathrm{C}\subset\mathcal{P}_{\#{\mathfrak{N}}}(\mathsf{X})
such that
\overline{\mathrm{C}}\subset\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{X})
is totally convex and
the family
\mathrm{C}_{N}:=\mathrm{C}\cap\mathcal{P}_{\#N}(\mathsf{X})
,
N\in{\mathfrak{N}}
, satisfies the
following properties:

(1)

\mathrm{C}_{N}
is nonempty and relatively open in
\mathcal{P}_{\#N}(\mathsf{X})

(or, equivalently, in
\mathcal{P}_{f,N}(\mathsf{X})
);

(2)

\mathrm{C}_{N}
coincides with the relative interior in
\mathcal{P}_{f,N}(\mathsf{X})
of
\overline{\mathrm{C}}\cap\mathcal{P}_{\#N}(\mathsf{X})
.

In the next result we will present several equivalent characterizations of

{\mathfrak{N}}
-cores, which we fully justify in the next section, adopting a
Lagrangian viewpoint (see Lemma
8.12
).

Lemma 8.2

(Equivalent characterizations of
{\mathfrak{N}}
-cores)
.

Let
\mathrm{C}\subset\mathcal{P}_{\#{\mathfrak{N}}}(\mathsf{X})
;
then the following properties are equivalent:

(1)

\mathrm{C}
is a
{\mathfrak{N}}
-core;

(2)

there exists a subset
\mathrm{D}
of
\mathcal{P}_{f,{\mathfrak{N}}}(\mathsf{X})
such that, for every
N\in{\mathfrak{N}}
, the set
\mathrm{D}_{N}:=\mathrm{D}\cap\mathcal{P}_{f,N}(\mathsf{X})
satisfies the following two conditions

(a)

\mathrm{D}_{N}
is relatively open in
\mathcal{P}_{f,N}(\mathsf{X})
,

(b)

\mathrm{D}_{N}
is convex along couplings in
\mathcal{P}_{f,N}(\mathsf{X}\times\mathsf{X})
,

and
\mathrm{C}=\mathrm{D}\cap\mathcal{P}_{\#{\mathfrak{N}}}(\mathsf{X})
;

(3)

there exists
a totally convex and closed
subset
\mathrm{E}
of
\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{X})
such that

(a)

for every

N\in{\mathfrak{N}}
the sets

\mathring{\mathrm{E}}_{N}:=\text{relative interior of }\big(\mathrm{E}\cap\mathcal{P}_{f,N}(\mathsf{X})\big)\text{ in $\mathcal{P}_{f,N}(\mathsf{X})$}

are not empty,

(b)

\mathrm{E}\cap\mathcal{P}_{f,{\mathfrak{N}}}(\mathsf{X})
is dense in
\mathrm{E}
,

and
\mathrm{C}=\bigcup_{N\in{\mathfrak{N}}}{\mathring{\mathrm{E}}}_{N}\cap\mathcal{P}_{\#N}(\mathsf{X})
;

(4)

the family of sets
\mathrm{C}_{N}=\mathrm{C}\cap\mathcal{P}_{\#N}(\mathsf{X})
satisfies

(a)

\mathrm{C}_{N}
is relatively open in
\mathcal{P}_{\#N}(\mathsf{X})

(or, equivalently, in
\mathcal{P}_{f,N}(\mathsf{X})
),

(b)

\mathrm{C}_{N}
is convex along collisionless couplings (cf. Definition
7.4
),

(c)

if
M,N\in{\mathfrak{N}}
,
M\mid N
then

\overline{\mathrm{C}_{M}}=\overline{\mathrm{C}_{N}}\cap\mathcal{P}_{f,M}(\mathsf{X})
,

(d)

\overline{\mathrm{C}_{N}}
is convex along couplings in
\mathcal{P}_{f,N}(\mathsf{X}\times\mathsf{X})
.

In the above cases
the sets
\mathrm{C}_{N}
,
\mathrm{D}_{N}
,
\mathring{\mathrm{E}}_{N}
,
\mathrm{C}
,
\mathrm{D}
and

\mathrm{E}
are linked by the following
relations

(8.3)

\displaystyle\mathrm{C}_{N}=\mathrm{D}_{N}\cap\mathcal{P}_{\#N}(\mathsf{X})=\mathring{\mathrm{E}}_{N}\cap\mathcal{P}_{\#N}(\mathsf{X}),\quad\mathrm{C}=\bigcup_{N\in{\mathfrak{N}}}\mathrm{C}_{N},,

(8.4)

\displaystyle\mathrm{D}_{N}=\mathring{\mathrm{E}}_{N}=\text{ relative interior of
$\overline{\mathrm{C}_{N}}$
in $\mathcal{P}_{f,N}(\mathsf{X})$,}\quad\mathrm{D}=\bigcup_{N\in{\mathfrak{N}}}\mathrm{D}_{N}=\bigcup_{N\in{\mathfrak{N}}}\mathring{\mathrm{E}}_{N},

(8.5)

\displaystyle\overline{\mathrm{C}_{N}}=\overline{\mathrm{D}_{N}}=\mathrm{E}\cap\mathcal{P}_{f,N},

(8.6)

\displaystyle\overline{\mathrm{C}}=\overline{\mathrm{D}}=\mathrm{E}.

Lemma 8.3
.

Let
\mathrm{C}\subset\mathcal{P}_{\#{\mathfrak{N}}}(\mathsf{X})
; if
\dim(\mathsf{X})\geq 2
, then condition
(4*) in Lemma
8.2
follows by (1*)-(3*).

Our first result shows how to recover a totally

\lambda
-dissipative MPVF starting from a (metrically)

\lambda
-dissipative MPVF
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}

whose domain is a
{\mathfrak{N}}
-core
\mathrm{C}
.

Theorem 8.4

(From dissipativity to total dissipativity)
.

Let
\mathsf{X}
be a separable Hilbert space, let
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}\subset\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{T\kern-1.5ptX})
be a
{\rm MPVF}
and let
\mathrm{C}\subset\mathcal{P}_{\#{\mathfrak{N}}}(\mathsf{X})
be a
{\mathfrak{N}}
-core. Let us assume either one of the the following hypotheses:

(1)

{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
is
\lambda
-dissipative,
\mathrm{D}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})=\mathrm{C}
and
\dim(\mathsf{X})\geq 2
;

(2)

{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
is totally
\lambda
-dissipative and
\mathrm{C}\subset\mathrm{D}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})\subset\overline{\mathrm{C}}
.

For every
N\in{\mathfrak{N}}
consider the
MPVF

\hat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}_{N}

defined by the following formula:

\Phi\in\hat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}_{N}[\mu]
if and only if
\Phi\in\mathcal{P}_{f,N}(\mathsf{T\kern-1.5ptX})
,

\mu\in\overline{\mathrm{C}_{N}}

and
for every

\nu\in\mathrm{C}_{N}
,
\Psi\in{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}[\nu]
,

\boldsymbol{\vartheta}\in\Gamma_{f,N}(\Phi,\nu)

we have

(8.7)

\int\langle v_{0}-\boldsymbol{b}_{\Psi}(x_{1}),x_{0}-x_{1}\rangle\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{\vartheta}(x_{0},v_{0},x_{1})\leq\lambda\int|x_{0}-x_{1}|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{\vartheta}(x_{0},v_{0},x_{1}).

We have the following properties:

(1)

For every
N\in{\mathfrak{N}}
, the elements of
\hat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}_{N}
satisfy (
3.9
) for couplings
\boldsymbol{\vartheta}\in\mathcal{P}_{f,N}(\mathsf{T\kern-1.5ptX}\times\mathsf{T\kern-1.5ptX})

and
\mathrm{D}(\hat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}_{N})
contains

\mathrm{C}_{N}
.

(2)

For every
\mu\in\overline{\mathrm{C}_{N}}
,
\boldsymbol{f}\in\operatorname{map}\left(\hat{{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}}_{N}\right)[\mu]
(cf. (
2.14
))
if and only if for every
\nu\in\mathrm{C}_{N}
,
\Psi\in{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}[\nu]
,
\boldsymbol{\mu}\in\Gamma_{f,N}(\mu,\nu)
we have

(8.8)

\int\langle\boldsymbol{f}(x_{0})-\boldsymbol{b}_{\Psi}(x_{1}),x_{0}-x_{1}\rangle\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{\mu}(x_{0},x_{1})\leq\lambda\int|x_{0}-x_{1}|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{\mu}(x_{0},x_{1}).

Moreover, in order for
\boldsymbol{f}
to belong to

\operatorname{map}\left({\hat{{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}}_{N}}\right)[\mu]
, it is sufficient to check
(
8.8
) only for all the measures
\nu\in\mathrm{C}_{N}

and all the couplings
\boldsymbol{\mu}\in\Gamma(\mu,\nu)

such that
\boldsymbol{\mu}
is the unique element
of
\Gamma_{o}(\mu,\nu)
.

(3)

M\mid N
implies
\mathrm{D}(\hat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}_{M})\subset\mathrm{D}(\hat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}_{N})
.

(4)

The
MPVF

(8.9)

\hat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}_{\infty}[\mu]:=\bigcup_{M\in{\mathfrak{N}}}\bigcap_{M\mid N}\hat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}_{N}[\mu]\quad\text{with domain}\quad\mathrm{D}(\hat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}_{\infty})=\bigcup_{M\in\mathbb{N}}\mathrm{D}(\hat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}_{M})\supset\mathrm{C}

is totally
\lambda
-dissipative.

(5)

There exists a unique maximal totally
\lambda
-dissipative

MPVF

\hat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
extending
\hat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}_{\infty}
whose domain is contained in

\overline{\mathrm{C}}
. For every
\mu\in\overline{\mathrm{C}}
,

\hat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}[\mu]
is characterized by
all the measures
\Phi\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{T\kern-1.5ptX}|\mu)

satisfying

(8.10)

\int\langle v-\boldsymbol{f}(y),x-y\rangle\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{\vartheta}(x,v,y)\leq\lambda\int|x-y|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{\vartheta}

for every
\boldsymbol{\vartheta}\in\Gamma(\Phi,\nu)
with

\nu\in\mathrm{D}(\hat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}_{\infty})
and
(\boldsymbol{i}_{\mathsf{X}},\boldsymbol{f})_{\sharp}\nu\in\hat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}_{\infty}
.
The
MPVF

\hat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
also coincides with the strong closure of

\hat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}_{\infty}
in
\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{T\kern-1.5ptX}).

Finally, if
\mu\in\mathrm{C}
then the minimal selection
\hat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}{\vphantom{{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}}}^{\circ}
of
\hat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
satisfies

\hat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}{\vphantom{{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}}}^{\circ}[\mu]\in\hat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}_{\infty}[\mu].

We discuss two particular cases in more detail:
the first one occurs when
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
is
a deterministic
\lambda
-dissipative
MPVF: as in Theorem
7.6
we obtain
that
\lambda
-dissipativity implies
total
\lambda
-dissipativity; here however, we deal with a MPVF (not necessarily single-valued)
defined in a much smaller domain.

Theorem 8.5

(Deterministic dissipative MPVFs on a core are totally dissipative)
.

Let us suppose that
\dim\mathsf{X}\geq 2
and

{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}\subset\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{T\kern-1.5ptX})
is a
deterministic

\lambda
-dissipative
MPVF
whose domain is a

{\mathfrak{N}}
-core
\mathrm{C}
.
Then
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
is totally
\lambda
-dissipative,
\hat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}_{\infty}
(cf. (
8.9
)) is a totally
\lambda
-dissipative extension of
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
and, for every
\mu\in\bigcup_{N\in{\mathfrak{N}}}\overline{\mathrm{C}_{N}}
,
\boldsymbol{f}\in\operatorname{map}\left(\hat{{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}}_{\infty}\right)[\mu]
if and only if

(8.11)

\int\langle\boldsymbol{f}(x_{0})-\boldsymbol{g}(x_{1}),x_{0}-x_{1}\rangle\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{\mu}(x_{0},x_{1})\leq\lambda\int|x_{0}-x_{1}|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{\mu}(x_{0},x_{1})

for all the measures
\nu\in\mathrm{C}
,
\boldsymbol{g}\in\operatorname{map}\left({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}\right)[\nu]
, and all the couplings
\boldsymbol{\mu}\in\Gamma(\mu,\nu)

such that
\boldsymbol{\mu}
is the unique element
of
\Gamma_{o}(\mu,\nu)
.
The
MPVF

\hat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
of Theorem
8.4
(5) provides the unique maximal totally
\lambda
-dissipative extension of
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
with domain included in
\overline{\mathrm{C}}
.
If moreover
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
is single-valued and the restriction of
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
to each set
\mathrm{C}_{N}
,
N\in{\mathfrak{N}}
, is demicontinous, then
the restrictions of
\hat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}_{\infty}
and
\hat{{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}}{\vphantom{{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}}}^{\circ}
to
\mathrm{C}
coincide with
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
.

A second case occurs when we know that
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
is totally
\lambda
-dissipative.

Theorem 8.6

(Unique maximal extension of totally dissipative MPVF)
.

If
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
is a
totally
\lambda
-dissipative
{\rm MPVF}

whose domain contains a dense
{\mathfrak{N}}
-core
\mathrm{C}
.
Then the
MPVF

\hat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
constructed
as in Theorem
8.4
provides the unique maximal totally

\lambda
-dissipative extension of
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
with domain included in
\overline{\mathrm{C}}
.

We devote the remaining part of this section to the proof of the above
main theorems. We adopt a Lagrangian viewpoint, lifting the MPVF
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
to the Hilbert space
\mathcal{X}:=L^{2}(\Omega,{\mathcal{B}},\mathbb{P};\mathsf{X})
and
parametrizing probability measures by random variables in
\mathcal{X}
as we
did in Section
3.2
.

8.1.
Lagrangian representations of
{\mathfrak{N}}
-cores

For the whole section,
we fix a standard Borel
space
(\Omega,{\mathcal{B}})
endowed with
a nonatomic probability measure
\mathbb{P}
(see Definition
B.1
).

Given
{\mathfrak{N}}
an unbounded directed subset of
\mathbb{N}
w.r.t. the order relation
\prec
as in (
8.1
),
we consider a
{\mathfrak{N}}
-segmentation of
(\Omega,{\mathcal{B}},\mathbb{P})
(see Definition
B.3
) that we denote by
(\mathfrak{P}_{N})_{N\in{\mathfrak{N}}}
.
We define
{\mathcal{B}}_{N}:=\sigma\left(\mathfrak{P}_{N}\right)
,
N\in{\mathfrak{N}}
, and we denote by
(\Omega,{\mathcal{B}},\mathbb{P},(\mathfrak{P}_{N})_{N\in{\mathfrak{N}}})
, with
\mathfrak{P}_{N}=\{\Omega_{N,n}\}_{n\in I_{N}}
and
I_{N}:=\{0,\dots,N-1\}
, the
{\mathfrak{N}}
-refined
probability space as in Definition
B.3
induced by
(\mathfrak{P}_{N})_{N\in{\mathfrak{N}}}
on
(\Omega,{\mathcal{B}},\mathbb{P})
. We set

\mathcal{X}:=L^{2}(\Omega,{\mathcal{B}},\mathbb{P};\mathsf{X}),\quad\mathcal{X}_{N}:=L^{2}(\Omega,{\mathcal{B}}_{N},\mathbb{P};\mathsf{X}),\quad N\in{\mathfrak{N}},\quad\mathcal{X}_{\infty}:=\bigcup_{N\in{\mathfrak{N}}}\mathcal{X}_{N},

and we recall that
\mathcal{X}_{\infty}
is dense in
\mathcal{X}
by Proposition

B.4
.

Even if the choice of a general standard Borel space allows for a great
generality, it would not be restrictive to
focus on the canonical example below, at least at a first reading.

Example 8.7
.

The canonical example of
{\mathfrak{N}}
-refined standard Borel probability space is

([0,1),\mathcal{B}([0,1)),\lambda,(\mathfrak{I}_{N})_{N\in{\mathfrak{N}}}),

where
\lambda
is the one dimensional Lebesgue measure restricted to
[0,1)

and
\mathfrak{I}_{N}=(I_{N,k})_{k\in I_{N}}
with

I_{N,k}:=[k/N,(k+1)/N)
,
k\in I_{N}
and
N\in{\mathfrak{N}}
.
The space
\mathcal{X}_{N}
can then be identified with the class of functions
which are (essentially) constant in each subintervals

I_{N,k}
,
k\in I_{N}
, of the partition
\mathfrak{I}_{N,k}
.

As in Section
3
, we parametrize measures in
\mathcal{P}(\mathsf{X})
by random variables in
(\Omega,{\mathcal{B}},\mathbb{P})
and we use the notation
\iota:\mathcal{X}\to\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{X})
for the map sending
X\in\mathcal{X}
to
\iota(X)=X_{\sharp}\mathbb{P}=\iota_{X}\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{X})
. Recall that

(8.12)

W_{2}(\iota_{X},\iota_{Y})\leq|X-Y|_{\mathcal{X}}\quad\text{for every }X,Y\in\mathcal{X}.

If
(X,V)\in\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{X}
recall the notation
\iota^{2}_{X,V}=(X,V)_{\sharp}\mathbb{P}\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{T\kern-1.5ptX})
.

We can identify
\mathcal{X}_{N}
with the space

\mathsf{X}^{N}
of vectors

{\boldsymbol{x}}:I_{N}\to\mathsf{X}
such that

X(\omega)={\boldsymbol{x}}(n)
whenever
\omega\in\Omega_{N,n}
.
In this case we set
X={\mathscr{I}_{N}}({\boldsymbol{x}})
.
Clearly
\iota(\mathcal{X}_{N})=\mathcal{P}_{f,N}(\mathsf{X})

and
\iota(\mathcal{X}_{\infty})=\mathcal{P}_{f,{\mathfrak{N}}}(\mathsf{X})
.

The isomorphism
{\mathscr{I}_{N}}

preserves the scalar product on
\mathsf{X}^{N}

\langle{\boldsymbol{x}},{\boldsymbol{y}}\rangle_{\mathsf{X}^{N}}:=N^{-1}\sum_{n=0}^{N-1}\langle{\boldsymbol{x}}(n),{\boldsymbol{y}}(n)\rangle=\mathbb{E}\big[\langle{\mathscr{I}_{N}}({\boldsymbol{x}}),{\mathscr{I}_{N}}({\boldsymbol{y}})\rangle\big]=\langle{\mathscr{I}_{N}}({\boldsymbol{x}}),{\mathscr{I}_{N}}({\boldsymbol{y}})\rangle_{\mathcal{X}}\quad{\boldsymbol{x}},{\boldsymbol{y}}\in\mathsf{X}^{N}.

The conditional expectation
\Pi_{N}=\mathbb{E}[\cdot|{\mathcal{B}}_{N}]
provides the orthogonal
projection of an arbitrary map
X\in\mathcal{X}
onto
\mathcal{X}_{N}
:

\Pi_{N}[X](\omega)=N\int_{\Omega_{N,n}}X\,\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}\quad\text{if }\omega\in\Omega_{N,n}.

Notice that

\text{if $M\mid N$ then ${\mathcal{B}}_{M}\subset{\mathcal{B}}_{N}$ and $\Pi_{M}=\Pi_{M}\circ\Pi_{N}$}.

For every
X={\mathscr{I}_{N}}({\boldsymbol{x}})\in\mathcal{X}_{N}
the probability measure
\iota_{X}=X_{\sharp}\mathbb{P}
takes the form

\iota_{X}=N^{-1}\sum_{n=0}^{N-1}\delta_{{\boldsymbol{x}}(n)}\in\mathcal{P}_{f,N}(\mathsf{X})
.

We denote by
{\mathsf{O}}_{N}\subset\mathsf{X}^{N}
the subset
of the injective maps and by
\mathcal{O}_{N}:={\mathscr{I}_{N}}({\mathsf{O}}_{N})\subset\mathcal{X}_{N}
.
Clearly,
\iota(\mathcal{O}_{N})=\mathcal{P}_{\#N}(\mathsf{X})
.
Since the complement of
{\mathsf{O}}_{N}
is the union
of a finite number of proper closed subspaces with empty interior

S_{ij}:=\{{\boldsymbol{x}}\in\mathsf{X}^{N}:{\boldsymbol{x}}(i)={\boldsymbol{x}}(j)\}
,
i\neq j
, of
\mathsf{X}^{N}
, then
{\mathsf{O}}_{N}
is open and dense in
\mathsf{X}^{N}
.

Every permutation
\sigma\in{\mathrm{Sym}(I_{N})}
acts on
\mathsf{X}^{N}

via
\sigma{\boldsymbol{x}}(n):={\boldsymbol{x}}(\sigma(n))
and can be thus extended to

\mathcal{X}_{N}
via
\sigma({\mathscr{I}_{N}}({\boldsymbol{x}})):={\mathscr{I}_{N}}(\sigma({\boldsymbol{x}}))
.
It is not difficult to see that,
for every
X,Y\in\mathcal{X}_{N}
,

\iota_{X}=\iota_{Y}
is equivalent to
Y=\sigma X
for some

\sigma\in{\mathrm{Sym}(I_{N})}
.

As in Section
3
, we denote by
\mathrm{S}(\Omega)
the class of

{\mathcal{B}}
-
{\mathcal{B}}
-measurable maps
g:\Omega\to\Omega
which are
essentially injective and measure-preserving, meaning that there
exists a full
\mathbb{P}
-measure set
\Omega_{0}\in{\mathcal{B}}
such that
g
is
injective on
\Omega_{0}
and
g_{\sharp}\mathbb{P}=\mathbb{P}
. Moreover, for every
N\in{\mathfrak{N}}
, we denote by
\mathrm{S}_{N}(\Omega):=\mathrm{S}(\Omega,{\mathcal{B}},\mathbb{P};{\mathcal{B}}_{N})
the subset of
\mathrm{S}(\Omega)
of
{\mathcal{B}}_{N}
-
{\mathcal{B}}_{N}
measurable maps.

Remark 8.8
.

Clearly, if
X={\mathscr{I}_{N}}({\boldsymbol{x}})\in\mathcal{X}_{N}
and
g\in\mathrm{S}_{N}(\Omega)
then

X\circ g\in\mathcal{X}_{N}

and there exists a unique permutation
\sigma=\sigma_{g}\in{\mathrm{Sym}(I_{N})}

such that
X\circ g=\sigma_{g}X={\mathscr{I}_{N}}({\boldsymbol{x}}\circ\sigma_{g})
.
Conversely, if
\sigma\in{\mathrm{Sym}(I_{N})}
there exists

g\in\mathrm{S}_{N}(\Omega)

such that
\sigma=\sigma_{g}
, as shown in Lemma
B.2
.
We set
G[\sigma]:=\big\{g\in\mathrm{S}_{N}(\Omega):\sigma_{g}=\sigma\big\}.

There is an interesting relation between projections and
permutations.

Lemma 8.9
.

Let
N,M\in{\mathfrak{N}}
be such that
M\mid N
.
If
\mathcal{K}
is a convex subset of
\mathcal{X}_{N}
invariant
by the action of
{\mathrm{Sym}(I_{N})}
, then

(8.13)

\mathcal{K}\cap\mathcal{X}_{M}=\Pi_{M}\big(\mathcal{K}\big).

Moreover,

(8.14)

\overline{\mathcal{K}}\cap\mathcal{X}_{M}=\overline{\mathcal{K}\cap\mathcal{X}_{M}}

and, denoting by
\mathring{\mathcal{K}}
the
relative interior of
\mathcal{K}
in
\mathcal{X}_{N}
, if

\mathring{\mathcal{K}}
is not empty then we have

(8.15)

\mathring{\mathcal{K}}\cap\mathcal{X}_{M}\text{ coincides with the relative interior of }\mathcal{K}\cap\mathcal{X}_{M}\text{ in }\mathcal{X}_{M}.

Proof.

Let us first compute the explicit representation of the orthogonal
projection
\Pi_{M}(X)
for every
X\in\mathcal{X}_{N}
.
If
K:=N/M
we consider
the cyclic permutation
\sigma:I_{N}\to I_{N}

defined by

\sigma(n):=\begin{cases}mK+k+1&\text{if }n=mK+k,\ m\in I_{M},\ 0\leq k<K-1,\\
mK&\text{if }n=mK+K-1,\ m\in I_{M},\end{cases}

and its powers
\sigma^{p}
,
p\in I_{K}
.
It is not difficult to check that

\sigma^{K}=\sigma^{0}=\boldsymbol{i}_{I_{N}}

and for every
Y\in\mathcal{X}_{M}
we have

\sigma^{p}Y=Y
for every
p\in I_{K}
.
Therefore for every
X\in\mathcal{X}_{N}
we obtain the representation

\Pi_{M}(X)=\frac{1}{K}\sum_{p=0}^{K-1}\sigma^{p}X.

If
\mathcal{K}
is a convex subset of
\mathcal{X}_{N}
invariant
by the action of
{\mathrm{Sym}(I_{N})}
,
we get
\Pi_{M}(X)\in\mathcal{K}
for every
X\in\mathcal{K}
,
so that
\Pi_{M}(\mathcal{K})=\mathcal{K}\cap\mathcal{X}_{M}
, hence we proved (
8.13
).

In order to check (
8.14
), we observe
that in general

\overline{\mathcal{K}\cap\mathcal{X}_{M}}\subset\overline{\mathcal{K}}\cap\mathcal{X}_{M}
;
on the other hand
\overline{\mathcal{K}}\cap\mathcal{X}_{M}=\Pi_{M}(\overline{\mathcal{K}})\subset\overline{\Pi_{M}(\mathcal{K})}=\overline{\mathcal{K}\cap\mathcal{X}_{M}}

by (
8.13
).

Similarly, if we denote by
\mathring{\mathcal{K}}_{M}
the relative
interior
of
\mathcal{K}\cap\mathcal{X}_{M}
in
\mathcal{X}_{M}
,
as a general fact
\mathring{\mathcal{K}}\cap\mathcal{X}_{M}\subset\mathring{\mathcal{K}}_{M}
so that

\mathring{\mathcal{K}}_{M}
is not empty, since by (
8.13
)
\mathring{\mathcal{K}}\cap\mathcal{X}_{M}=\Pi_{M}(\mathring{\mathcal{K}})
is not empty.
On the other hand, by (
8.14
),

\overline{\mathring{\mathcal{K}}\cap\mathcal{X}_{M}}=\overline{\mathring{\mathcal{K}}}\cap\mathcal{X}_{M}=\overline{\mathcal{K}}\cap\mathcal{X}_{M}=\overline{\mathcal{K}\cap\mathcal{X}_{M}}=\overline{\mathring{\mathcal{K}}_{M}}

so that the open convex sets
\mathring{\mathcal{K}}\cap\mathcal{X}_{M}
and

\mathring{\mathcal{K}}_{M}
have the same closure and therefore coincide.
∎

We will now show that
if the sections
\mathcal{A}\cap\mathcal{X}_{N}
of a set
\mathcal{A}\subset\mathcal{X}_{\infty}

are invariant by the action of
{\mathrm{Sym}(I_{N})}
, then
\overline{A}
is
law invariant.

Lemma 8.10
.

Let
\mathcal{A}\subset\mathcal{X}_{\infty}
be a set such that

\mathcal{A}_{N}:=\mathcal{A}\cap\mathcal{X}_{N}
are invariant
w.r.t.
{\mathrm{Sym}(I_{N})}
for every
N\in{\mathfrak{N}}
. Then

\overline{\mathcal{A}}

is law invariant.

Remark 8.11
.

The same statement applies to subsets of
\mathcal{X}_{\infty}\times\mathcal{X}_{\infty}
.

Proof.

Since
\overline{\mathcal{A}}
is a closed set, by Lemma
3.3
,
it is sufficient to prove that
it is invariant by measure-preserving isomorphisms:
for every
X\in\overline{\mathcal{A}}
and
g\in\mathrm{S}(\Omega)
we want to show that
X\circ g\in\overline{\mathcal{A}}
.
It is enough to prove that there exist

Z_{n}\in\mathcal{A}
s.t.
Z_{n}\to X\circ g
. Let
X_{n}
be a sequence in
\mathcal{A}
such that
X_{n}\to X
; since
\mathcal{A}\subset\mathcal{X}_{\infty}
, for every
n\in\mathbb{N}
, there exists some
N_{n}\in{\mathfrak{N}}
such that
X_{n}\in\mathcal{A}_{N_{n}}
. Let
(b_{k})_{k}\subset{\mathfrak{N}}
be the sequence given by Proposition
B.4
; by Theorem
B.5
(1) applied to
(\Omega,{\mathcal{B}},\mathbb{P},(\mathfrak{P}_{b_{k}})_{k\in\mathbb{N}})
and
\boldsymbol{\gamma}:=(\boldsymbol{i}_{\Omega},g)_{\sharp}\mathbb{P}
, we can find a strictly increasing sequence
(M_{j})_{j}\subset\mathbb{N}
and maps
g_{j}\in\mathrm{S}_{b_{M_{j}}}(\Omega)
such that

(U,W)_{\sharp}(\boldsymbol{i}_{\Omega},g_{j})_{\sharp}\mathbb{P}\to(U,W)_{\sharp}(\boldsymbol{i}_{\Omega},g)_{\sharp}\mathbb{P}\,\text{ in }\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{X}^{2})

for every
U,W\in\mathcal{X}
. Since
M_{j}
is strictly increasing and (
B.1
) holds, then we can find a strictly increasing sequence
n\mapsto j(n)
such that
g_{j(n)}\in\mathrm{S}_{N_{n}}(\Omega)
. Thus setting
g^{\prime}_{n}:=g_{j(n)}
,
n\in\mathbb{N}
, by the invariance of
\mathcal{A}_{N_{n}}
, we get that
Z_{n}:=X_{n}\circ g^{\prime}_{n}\in\mathcal{A}_{N_{n}}\subset\mathcal{A}
and of course we have

(8.16)

(U,W)_{\sharp}(\boldsymbol{i}_{\Omega},g^{\prime}_{n})_{\sharp}\mathbb{P}\to(U,W)_{\sharp}(\boldsymbol{i}_{\Omega},g)_{\sharp}\mathbb{P}\,\text{ in }\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{X}^{2})

for every
U,W\in\mathcal{X}
.
We are left to show that

(8.17)

Z_{n}\to X\circ g\text{ in }\mathcal{X}.

Since
|Z_{n}-X\circ g^{\prime}_{n}|_{\mathcal{X}}=|X_{n}-X|_{\mathcal{X}}
, in order to get (
8.17
)
it is enough to show that
X\circ g^{\prime}_{n}\to X\circ g
which, on the
other hand, is implied by
X\circ g^{\prime}_{n}\rightharpoonup X\circ g
, since
|X\circ g^{\prime}_{n}|_{\mathcal{X}}=|X|_{\mathcal{X}}=|X\circ g|_{\mathcal{X}}
.
Let
Y\in\mathcal{X}
and let us take
U=Y,W=X
in (
8.16
) so that

\langle X\circ g^{\prime}_{n},Y\rangle_{\mathcal{X}}=\int_{\mathsf{X}^{2}}\langle x,y\rangle\,\mathrm{d}((Y,X)\circ(\boldsymbol{i}_{\Omega},g^{\prime}_{n}))_{\sharp}\mathbb{P}\to\int_{\mathsf{X}^{2}}\langle x,y\rangle\,\mathrm{d}((Y,X)\circ(\boldsymbol{i}_{\Omega},g))_{\sharp}\mathbb{P}=\langle X\circ g,Y\rangle_{\mathcal{X}},

since
\varphi(x,y):=\langle x,y\rangle
is a real valued function on

\mathsf{X}^{2}
with less than quadratic growth (see e.g.
[
2
, Proposition
7.1.5, Lemma 5.1.7]
). This shows that
X\circ g^{\prime}_{n}\rightharpoonup X\circ g
as desired, thus (
8.17
) and so
X\circ g\in\overline{\mathcal{A}}
.
∎

If
\mathrm{C}
is a
{\mathfrak{N}}
-core and
N\in{\mathfrak{N}}
, we set

(8.18)

\displaystyle{\mathcal{C}_{N}}:=\Big\{X\in\mathcal{X}_{N}:\iota_{X}\in\mathrm{C}_{N}\Big\},

\displaystyle{\displaystyle\mathcal{C}_{\infty}}:=\Big\{X\in\mathcal{X}_{\infty}:\iota_{X}\in\mathrm{C}\Big\}=\bigcup_{N\in{\mathfrak{N}}}{\mathcal{C}_{N}}

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}_{N}:=\operatorname{co}({\mathcal{C}_{N}}),

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}_{\infty}:=\bigcup_{N\in{\mathfrak{N}}}\mathcal{D}_{N},\quad\mathcal{E}_{\infty}:=\overline{{\mathcal{C}_{\infty}}}.

Notice that
{\mathcal{C}_{N}}
is in fact a subset of
\mathcal{O}_{N}

and
\mathcal{D}_{N}
is a subset of
\mathcal{X}_{N}
.

Lemma 8.12
.

Assume that
\mathrm{C}\subset\mathcal{P}_{\#{\mathfrak{N}}}(\mathsf{X})
satisfies property
(d)
in Lemma
8.2
. Then for every
N\in{\mathfrak{N}}
it holds:

(1)

{\mathcal{C}_{N}}
and
\mathcal{D}_{N}
are relatively
open subsets of
\mathcal{X}_{N}
,
invariant with respect to the action of permutations of
{\mathrm{Sym}(I_{N})}
.

(2)

The relative interior of
\overline{{\mathcal{C}_{N}}}
in

\mathcal{X}_{N}
coincides
with

\mathcal{D}_{N}
, in particular
{\mathcal{C}_{N}}
is dense in
\mathcal{D}_{N}

and
\overline{{\mathcal{C}_{N}}}=\overline{\mathcal{D}_{N}}
.

(3)

\mathcal{D}_{N}\cap\mathcal{O}_{N}={\mathcal{C}_{N}}
.

(4)

If
M\in{\mathfrak{N}}
and
M\mid N
then

\mathcal{D}_{M}=\mathcal{D}_{N}\cap\mathcal{X}_{M}=\Pi_{M}(\mathcal{D}_{N})
and

\overline{\mathcal{D}_{M}}=\overline{\mathcal{D}_{N}}\cap\mathcal{X}_{M}=\Pi_{M}(\overline{\mathcal{D}_{N}})
.

(5)

{\mathcal{C}_{\infty}}\subset\mathcal{D}_{\infty}\subset\overline{\mathcal{D}_{\infty}}=\overline{{\mathcal{C}_{\infty}}}=\mathcal{E}_{\infty}
and

\mathcal{E}_{\infty}
is convex.

(6)

\mathcal{D}_{N}=\mathcal{D}_{\infty}\cap\mathcal{X}_{N}=\Pi_{N}(\mathcal{D}_{\infty})
and

\overline{\mathcal{D}_{N}}=\mathcal{E}_{\infty}\cap\mathcal{X}_{N}=\Pi_{N}(\mathcal{E}_{\infty})
.

(7)

\mathcal{E}_{\infty}=\overline{\mathcal{D}_{\infty}}=\overline{{\mathcal{C}_{\infty}}}
is law invariant.

Proof.

(1)
The set
{\mathcal{C}_{N}}
is relatively open, since
the map
X\mapsto\iota_{X}
is Lipschitz
from
\mathcal{X}_{N}
to
\mathcal{P}_{f,N}(\mathsf{X})
, thanks to (
8.12
), and

\mathrm{C}_{N}
is relatively open in
\mathcal{P}_{f,N}(\mathsf{X})
.

\mathcal{D}_{N}
is relatively open in
\mathcal{X}_{N}
since
it is the convex hull of an open set.

(2)
Since
\mathcal{D}_{N}=\operatorname{co}({\mathcal{C}_{N}})
we immediately get

\mathcal{D}_{N}\subset\overline{{\mathcal{C}_{N}}}
.
On the other hand, since
\mathcal{D}_{N}\supset{\mathcal{C}_{N}}

we also have

\overline{\mathcal{D}_{N}}=\overline{{\mathcal{C}_{N}}}
. Being
\mathcal{D}_{N}

open and convex, it coincides with the interior of its closure.

(3)
It is clear that
{\mathcal{C}_{N}}\subset\mathcal{D}_{N}\cap\mathcal{O}_{N}
.
Let now show that any element

X={\mathscr{I}_{N}}({\boldsymbol{x}})\in\mathcal{D}_{N}\cap\mathcal{O}_{N}
belongs to
{\mathcal{C}_{N}}
.
If
\mathsf{B}_{N}
is the open unit ball in
\mathsf{X}^{N}

it is easy to see that
there
exists
a sufficiently small
\varepsilon>0
such that the open set

\mathcal{B}_{\varepsilon}:=\{({\mathscr{I}_{N}}({\boldsymbol{x}}+\varepsilon{\boldsymbol{z}}),{\mathscr{I}_{N}}({\boldsymbol{x}}-\varepsilon{\boldsymbol{z}})):{\boldsymbol{z}}\in\mathsf{B}_{N}\}

is contained in
\big(\mathcal{D}_{N}\cap\mathcal{O}_{N}\big)^{2}
.
Since
{\mathcal{C}_{N}}
is relatively open and
dense in
\mathcal{D}_{N}\cap\mathcal{O}_{N}
the intersection of

\mathcal{B}_{\varepsilon}
with
\big({\mathcal{C}_{N}}\big)^{2}
is not empty.

It follows that
we can find
{\boldsymbol{z}}\in\mathsf{B}_{N}
such
that

{\mathscr{I}_{N}}({\boldsymbol{x}}+\varepsilon{\boldsymbol{z}}),{\mathscr{I}_{N}}({\boldsymbol{x}}-\varepsilon{\boldsymbol{z}})\in{\mathcal{C}_{N}}

and therefore
X\in{\mathcal{C}_{N}}
since
\mathrm{C}_{N}

is convex along collisionless couplings.

(4)
If we apply Lemma
8.9

to the closure of
\mathcal{D}_{N}
we obtain

\Pi_{M}(\overline{\mathcal{D}_{N}})=\overline{\mathcal{D}_{N}}\cap\mathcal{X}_{M}
.
On the other hand, using property (3*) in Lemma
8.2

and the density of
{\mathcal{C}_{N}}
in
\mathcal{D}_{N}

we have

\overline{\mathcal{D}_{N}}\cap\mathcal{X}_{M}=\overline{{\mathcal{C}_{N}}}\cap\mathcal{X}_{M}=\overline{{\mathcal{C}_{M}}}=\overline{\mathcal{D}_{M}}
.

Similarly, still using Lemma
8.9
, we obtain

\Pi_{M}(\mathcal{D}_{N})=\mathcal{D}_{N}\cap\mathcal{X}_{M}
.
Applying (
8.15
) to
\overline{\mathcal{D}_{N}}
, since by (2) the relative interior of
\overline{\mathcal{D}_{N}}
in
\mathcal{X}_{N}
is
\mathcal{D}_{N}
, we get that
\mathcal{D}_{N}\cap\mathcal{X}_{M}
coincides with the relative interior in
\mathcal{X}_{M}
of
\overline{\mathcal{D}_{N}}\cap\mathcal{X}_{M}=\overline{\mathcal{D}_{M}}
which is equal to
\mathcal{D}_{M}
, again by claim (2).

(5)
Since
\overline{{\mathcal{C}_{N}}}=\overline{\mathcal{D}_{N}}
for every
N\in{\mathfrak{N}}
by claim (2), we have that
\overline{\mathcal{D}_{\infty}}\subset\overline{{\mathcal{C}_{\infty}}}
so that equality follows by the
trivial inclusion
{\mathcal{C}_{\infty}}\subset\mathcal{D}_{\infty}
. Since

\overline{\cup\overline{{\mathcal{C}_{N}}}}=\overline{{\mathcal{C}_{\infty}}}
, to prove that
\overline{{\mathcal{C}_{\infty}}}
is convex,
it is enough to show that
\cup\overline{{\mathcal{C}_{N}}}
is
convex. If
X,Y\in\cup\overline{{\mathcal{C}_{N}}}
, we can find
M,N\in{\mathfrak{N}}
such that
X\in\overline{{\mathcal{C}_{N}}}
and
Y\in\overline{{\mathcal{C}_{M}}}
, so that by claim (4), both
X
and
Y

belong to
\overline{{\mathcal{C}_{MN}}}
which is convex by assumption.

(6)
The first property follows by
the identity
\mathcal{D}_{N}=\mathcal{D}_{L}\cap\mathcal{X}_{N}=\Pi_{N}(\mathcal{D}_{L})
for any
L\in{\mathfrak{N}}
such that
N\mid L
(cf. claim (4)) and the fact that

\mathcal{D}_{\infty}=\cup\Big\{\mathcal{D}_{L}:L\in{\mathfrak{N}},\ N\mid L\Big\}
, since
{\mathfrak{N}}
is
a directed set.

Setting
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}:=\cup_{N\in{\mathfrak{N}}}\overline{\mathcal{D}_{N}}
and
starting from the second identity of claim (4),
the same argument shows that

\overline{\mathcal{D}_{N}}=\mathcal{D}^{\prime}\cap\mathcal{X}_{N}=\Pi_{N}(\mathcal{D}^{\prime})
.
Since
\overline{\mathcal{D}_{N}}
is closed, we get that
\Pi_{N}(\mathcal{D}^{\prime})
is closed, so that
\Pi_{N}(\overline{\mathcal{D}^{\prime}})\subset\overline{\Pi_{N}(\mathcal{D}^{\prime})}=\Pi_{N}(\mathcal{D}^{\prime})
.
Since clearly
\Pi_{N}(\mathcal{D}^{\prime})\subset\Pi_{N}(\overline{\mathcal{D}^{\prime}})
, we get that
\overline{\mathcal{D}_{N}}=\mathcal{D}^{\prime}\cap\mathcal{X}_{N}=\Pi_{N}(\mathcal{D}^{\prime})=\Pi_{N}(\overline{\mathcal{D}^{\prime}})
.
We also have
\overline{\mathcal{D}^{\prime}}\cap\mathcal{X}_{N}=\Pi_{N}(\overline{\mathcal{D}^{\prime}}\cap\mathcal{X}_{N})\subset\Pi_{N}(\overline{\mathcal{D}^{\prime}})=\mathcal{D}^{\prime}\cap\mathcal{X}_{N}
, so that we get
\overline{\mathcal{D}^{\prime}}\cap\mathcal{X}_{N}=\mathcal{D}^{\prime}\cap\mathcal{X}_{N}
.
The thesis then follows since
\overline{\mathcal{D}^{\prime}}=\overline{\mathcal{D}_{\infty}}=\mathcal{E}_{\infty}
.

(7)
The fact that
\mathcal{E}_{\infty}
is law invariant follows from Lemma
8.10
and
the previous claim, which shows that
\mathcal{E}_{\infty}\cap\mathcal{X}_{M}=\overline{\mathcal{D}_{M}}
which
is invariant w.r.t.
{\mathrm{Sym}(I_{M})}
by claim (1).
∎

As an immediate consequence of Lemma
8.12
we have the following result.

Corollary 8.13

(Cores are totally convex)
.

If
\mathrm{C}
is as in Lemma
8.12
, then
\overline{\mathrm{C}}
is totally convex.

We can now justify the equivalent characterization of
{\mathfrak{N}}
-cores of Lemma
8.2
.

Proof of Lemma
8.2
.

Claim 1:
(d)
implies
(a)
,
(b)
and
(c)
.

The fact that
(d)
implies
(b)
and
(c)
follows by setting
\mathrm{D}:=\iota(\mathcal{D}_{\infty})
defined in (
8.18
) and
\mathrm{E}:=\overline{\mathrm{C}}
, as a consequence of Lemma
8.12
and Corollary
8.13
. We prove that
(d)
implies
(a)
: by Corollary
8.13
, we have that
\overline{\mathrm{C}}
is totally convex. Notice that the sets
\mathrm{C}_{N}
are nonempty for every
N\in{\mathfrak{N}}
thanks to (3*) and the fact that
\mathrm{C}
is nonempty. Finally, by Lemma
8.12
, we have that the relative interior in
\mathcal{P}_{f,N}(\mathsf{X})
of
\overline{\mathrm{C}}\cap\mathcal{P}_{\#N}(\mathsf{X})
is given by
\mathrm{D}_{N}\cap\mathcal{P}_{\#N}(\mathsf{X})=\mathrm{C}_{N}
(cf. Lemma
8.12
(3)).

Claim 2:
(b)
implies
(d)
.

If
\mathrm{D}
is a subset of
\mathcal{P}_{f,{\mathfrak{N}}}(\mathsf{X})
satisfying conditions

(1^{\prime}),(2^{\prime})
and
\mathrm{C}=\mathrm{D}\cap\mathcal{P}_{\#{\mathfrak{N}}}
, we see that
\mathrm{C}_{N}=\mathrm{D}_{N}\cap\mathcal{P}_{\#N}(\mathsf{X})
for every
N\in{\mathfrak{N}}
. Clearly
\mathrm{C}_{N}
is relatively open and convex along collisionless couplings in
\mathcal{P}_{f,N}(\mathsf{X})
. Also, since
\mathcal{P}_{\#N}(\mathsf{X})
is obviously dense in
\mathcal{P}_{f,N}(\mathsf{X})
and
\mathrm{D}_{N}
