is open, we see that
\mathrm{C}_{N}
is dense
\mathrm{D}_{N}
i.e.
\overline{\mathrm{C}_{N}}=\overline{\mathrm{D}_{N}}
. It is also clear that
\overline{\mathrm{C}_{N}}
is convex along couplings in
\mathcal{P}_{f,N}(\mathsf{X}\times\mathsf{X})
. Finally

\overline{\mathrm{D}_{N}}\cap\mathcal{P}_{f,M}(\mathsf{X})=\overline{\mathrm{D}_{M}}
thanks to the convexity of
\mathrm{D}_{N}
and

\mathrm{D}_{M}
, as an application of
(
8.15
) to their Lagrangian representations.

Claim 3:
(c)
implies
(b)
.

Let
\mathrm{E}
be a totally convex and closed subset of
\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{X})
satisfying conditions
(1^{\prime\prime}),(2^{\prime\prime})
and
\mathrm{C}=\cup_{N\in{\mathfrak{N}}}\mathring{\mathrm{E}}_{N}\cap\mathcal{P}_{\#N}(\mathsf{X})
. We define
\mathrm{D}_{N}
and
\mathrm{D}
as in (
8.4
). The only thing to check
is that

(8.19)

\mathrm{D}\cap\mathcal{P}_{f,N}(\mathsf{X})=\mathrm{D}_{N}.

Denote by
\mathcal{E}_{\infty}
the Lagrangian parametrization of
\mathrm{E}
(hence, law invariant) and denote by
\mathcal{E}_{N}:=\mathcal{E}_{\infty}\cap\mathcal{X}_{N}
, which is closed and convex. The relative interior
\mathring{\mathcal{E}}_{N}
of
\mathcal{E}_{N}
in
\mathcal{X}_{N}
provides a Lagrangian parametrization of
\mathring{\mathrm{E}}_{N}=\mathrm{D}_{N}
. Hence, proving (
8.19
) is equivalent to prove that
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}\cap\mathcal{X}_{N}=\mathring{\mathcal{E}}_{N}
, where
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}:=\bigcup_{N\in{\mathfrak{N}}}\mathring{\mathcal{E}}_{N}
.
Using (
8.15
), if
M\mid N
we get
\mathring{\mathcal{E}}_{N}\cap\mathcal{X}_{M}=\mathring{\mathcal{E}}_{M}
, also observing that
\mathcal{E}_{N}
is invariant by the action of
{\mathrm{Sym}(I_{N})}
, as a consequence of the law invariance of
\mathcal{E}_{\infty}
. Therefore we deduce that

\mathcal{D}^{\prime}\cap\mathcal{X}_{M}=\mathring{\mathcal{E}}_{M}
.

Claim 4:
(a)
implies
(c)
.

It is clear that setting
\mathrm{E}:=\overline{\mathrm{C}}
we have that
\mathrm{E}
it totally convex and closed. Moreover, since
\mathring{\mathrm{E}}_{N}
contains the relative interior in
\mathcal{P}_{f,N}(\mathsf{X})
of
E\cap\mathcal{P}_{\#N}(\mathsf{X})
(coinciding with
\mathrm{C}_{N}
),
\mathring{\mathrm{E}}_{N}
is not empty. Since the intersection of
\mathring{\mathrm{E}}_{N}
with
\mathcal{P}_{\#N}(\mathsf{X})
is given by
\mathrm{C}_{N}
, we immediately see that
\cup_{N}(\mathring{\mathrm{E}}_{N}\cap\mathcal{P}_{\#N}(\mathsf{X}))=\mathrm{C}
. Finally

\overline{\mathrm{E}\cap\mathcal{P}_{f,{\mathfrak{N}}}(\mathsf{X})}=\overline{\cup_{N}\overline{\mathrm{E}\cap\mathcal{P}_{\#N}(\mathsf{X})}}=\overline{\cup_{N}\overline{\mathrm{C}_{N}}}\\
=\overline{\mathrm{C}},

where we have used again that the intersection of
\mathring{\mathrm{E}}_{N}
with
\mathcal{P}_{\#N}(\mathsf{X})
is given by
\mathrm{C}_{N}
and that the closure of
\mathrm{E}\cap\mathcal{P}_{\#N}(\mathsf{X})
coincides with the closure of its (relative) interior.
∎

Proof of Lemma
8.3
.

Assume that (1*)-(3*) hold. We need to prove that
\overline{\mathrm{C}_{N}}
is convex along couplings in
\mathcal{P}_{f,N}(\mathsf{X}\times\mathsf{X})
for every
N\in{\mathfrak{N}}
. This is equivalent to prove the convexity of
\overline{{\mathcal{C}_{N}}}
so that it is sufficient to show that, for every
X_{0},X_{1}\in{\mathcal{C}_{N}}
and
t\in[0,1]
, their linear interpolation
X_{t}:=(1-t)X_{0}+tX_{1}
belongs to
\overline{{\mathcal{C}_{N}}}
. By Proposition
6.4
, we can find small perturbations
X_{1}(s)
of
X_{1}
,
s\in[0,1]
, such that
X_{1}(s)\in{\mathcal{C}_{N}}
,
X_{1}(s)\to X_{1}
as
s\downarrow 0
, and the perturbed interpolation
X_{s,t}:=(1-t)X_{0}+tX_{1}(s)
belongs to
{\mathcal{C}_{N}}
for every
t\in[0,1]
and
s>0
. It follows that the coupling
\boldsymbol{\mu}_{s}=\iota^{2}_{X_{0},X_{1}(s)}
belongs to
\mathcal{P}_{\#N}(\mathsf{X}\times\mathsf{X})
and it is collisionless for every
s>0
and therefore
\mu_{s,t}=\mathsf{x}^{t}_{\sharp}\boldsymbol{\mu}_{s}
belongs to
\mathrm{C}_{N}
for every
t
. Since
\mu_{s,t}=\iota(X_{s,t})
we have
X_{s,t}\in{\mathcal{C}_{N}}
. Passing to the limit as
s\downarrow 0
we conclude that
X_{t}\in\overline{{\mathcal{C}_{N}}}
.
∎

8.2.
Lagrangian representations of discrete MPVFs: construction of
\hat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}_{N}

Let us now study in more detail the Lagrangian representations of a
MPVF
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}\subset\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{T\kern-1.5ptX})
defined on a
{\mathfrak{N}}
-core.
If
\Phi\in{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}

we can consider the (not empty) set of all the maps
(X,V)\in\mathcal{X}^{2}
such that

(X,V)_{\sharp}\mathbb{P}=\Phi
.
A particular case arises when the first marginal
\mu=\mathsf{x}_{\sharp}\Phi
of
\Phi
belongs to

\mathcal{P}_{f,N}(\mathsf{X})
. In this case,
X
has the form
X={\mathscr{I}_{N}}({\boldsymbol{x}})\in\mathcal{X}_{N}
, so that
\mu=X_{\sharp}\mathbb{P}=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{k\in I_{N}}\delta_{{\boldsymbol{x}}(k)}
, and we can construct
V
from the representation of
\Phi
given by

\Phi=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{k\in I_{N}}\Phi_{k},\quad\mathsf{x}_{\sharp}\Phi_{k}=\delta_{{\boldsymbol{x}}(k)},

for a family
\{\Phi_{k}\}_{k\in I_{N}}\subset\mathcal{P}(\mathsf{T\kern-1.5ptX})
, by setting
V(\omega):=V_{k}(\omega)
if
\omega\in\Omega_{N,k}
, where
V_{k}\in L^{2}(\Omega_{N,k},\mathbb{P}|_{\Omega_{N,k}};\mathsf{X})
are maps such that

(V_{k})_{\sharp}\mathbb{P}|_{\Omega_{N,k}}=\frac{1}{N}\,\mathsf{v}_{\sharp}\Phi_{k}
.

In the general case, when
\Phi\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{T\kern-1.5ptX})
, it is easy to check that if
(X,V)_{\sharp}\mathbb{P}=\Phi
and
Y\in\mathcal{X}
then

(8.20)

[\Phi,\iota^{2}_{X,Y}]_{r,0}\leq\langle V,X-Y\rangle_{\mathcal{X}}.

A particular important case arises when
X\in\mathcal{O}_{N}
and
Y\in\mathcal{X}_{N}
: in this case
\Phi_{k}
is uniquely determined by the disintegration of
\Phi
w.r.t.
\mu
, and
V|_{\Omega_{N,k}}
coincides with
V_{k}
, with
V_{k}
as above, and

(8.21)

\langle V,X-Y\rangle_{\mathcal{X}}=\langle\Pi_{N}V,X-Y\rangle_{\mathcal{X}},\quad\Pi_{N}V(\omega)=\boldsymbol{b}_{\Phi}({\boldsymbol{x}}(k))\quad\text{if }\omega\in\Omega_{N,k},

where
\boldsymbol{b}_{\Phi}
is the barycenter of
\Phi
as in Definition
2.3
.
It is easy to check that

(8.22)

[\Phi,\iota^{2}_{X,Y}]_{r,0}=\langle V,X-Y\rangle_{\mathcal{X}}=\langle\Pi_{N}V,X-Y\rangle_{\mathcal{X}}\quad\text{if }(X,V)_{\sharp}\mathbb{P}=\Phi,\ X\in\mathcal{O}_{N},\ Y\in\mathcal{X}_{N},

since
\iota^{2}_{X,Y}
is concentrated on a map.

Proposition 8.14
.

Assume the same hypotheses of Theorem
8.4
. Let us define the sets

(8.23)

{\boldsymbol{F}}:=\Big\{(X,V)\in{\mathcal{C}_{\infty}}\times\mathcal{X}:(X,V)_{\sharp}\mathbb{P}\in{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}\Big\},\quad\boldsymbol{F}_{N}:=\Big\{(X,\Pi_{N}V):X\in{\mathcal{C}_{N}},\ (X,V)\in{\boldsymbol{F}}\Big\},

where
N\in{\mathfrak{N}}
. Then
{\boldsymbol{F}}_{N}\subset\mathcal{X}_{N}\times\mathcal{X}_{N}
is
\lambda
-dissipative, has open domain
\mathrm{D}({\boldsymbol{F}}_{N})={\mathcal{C}_{N}}
, and it is invariant by permutations: if
(X,V)\in{\boldsymbol{F}}_{N}
and
\sigma\in{\mathrm{Sym}(I_{N})}
, then
(\sigma X,\sigma V)\in{\boldsymbol{F}}_{N}
.

Proof.

In case (ii) of Theorem
8.4
, the dissipativity of
{\boldsymbol{F}}_{N}
immediately follows. In case (i) of Theorem
8.4
, (
8.22
) and the
\lambda
-dissipativity of
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
along
couplings in
\mathcal{P}_{\#N}(\mathsf{X}\times\mathsf{X})
, given by Theorem

7.5
,
yield

(8.24)

(X,V),\ (Y,W)\in\boldsymbol{F}_{N}\quad\Rightarrow\quad\langle V-W,X-Y\rangle_{\mathcal{X}}\leq\lambda|X-Y|_{\mathcal{X}}^{2},

so that
\boldsymbol{F}_{N}
is
\lambda
-dissipative. In any of the cases (i) and (ii) of Theorem
8.4
, if
(X,V)\in\boldsymbol{F}_{N}
and
\sigma\in{\mathrm{Sym}(I_{N})}
, then
there exists
W\in\mathcal{X}
such that
(X,W)_{\sharp}\mathbb{P}\in{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
and
V=\Pi_{N}W
. By Lemma
B.2
, we can write
\sigma X=X\circ g\in{\mathcal{C}_{N}}

for some
g\in G[\sigma]

and
(X\circ g,W\circ g)_{\sharp}\mathbb{P}\in{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
. To conclude, it suffices to notice that
\Pi_{N}(W\circ g)=\sigma V
.
∎

Proposition 8.15
.

Under the same assumptions of Theorem
8.4
,
for every
N\in{\mathfrak{N}}
the
\lambda
-dissipative operator
\boldsymbol{F}_{N}

admits a unique maximal
\lambda
-dissipative extension
{\hat{{\boldsymbol{F}}}_{N}}

in
\mathcal{X}_{N}\times\mathcal{X}_{N}

with
\mathcal{D}_{N}\subset\mathrm{D}({\hat{{\boldsymbol{F}}}_{N}})\subset\overline{\mathcal{D}_{N}}
.
The operator
{\hat{{\boldsymbol{F}}}_{N}}
can be equivalently characterized by

(8.25)

(X,V)\in{\hat{{\boldsymbol{F}}}_{N}}\quad\Leftrightarrow\quad X\in\overline{\mathcal{D}_{N}},\ V\in\mathcal{X}_{N},\ \langle V-W,X-Y\rangle_{\mathcal{X}}\leq\lambda|X-Y|_{\mathcal{X}}^{2}\quad\forall\,(Y,W)\in\boldsymbol{F}_{N},

and, whenever
X\in\mathcal{D}_{N}
,

{\hat{{\boldsymbol{F}}}_{N}}X=\overline{\operatorname{co}}\left(\bar{\boldsymbol{F}}_{N}X\right)
,
where

(8.26)

\bar{{\boldsymbol{F}}}_{N}X:=\Big\{V\in\mathcal{X}_{N}:\exists\,(X_{n},V_{n})\in\boldsymbol{F}_{N}:X_{n}\to X,\ V_{n}\rightharpoonup V\Big\}.

{\hat{{\boldsymbol{F}}}_{N}}
is invariant with respect to permutations, i.e.

(8.27)

(X,V)\in{\hat{{\boldsymbol{F}}}_{N}},\ \sigma\in{\mathrm{Sym}(I_{N})}\quad\Rightarrow\quad(\sigma X,\sigma V)\in{\hat{{\boldsymbol{F}}}_{N}}

and for every
X,Y\in\mathcal{D}_{N}
,
we have

(8.28)

V\in{\hat{{\boldsymbol{F}}}_{N}}X,\ \Psi\in{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}[\iota_{Y}]\quad\Rightarrow\quad\langle V,X-Y\rangle_{\mathcal{X}}+[\Psi,\iota^{2}_{Y,X}]_{r,0}\leq\lambda|X-Y|_{\mathcal{X}}^{2}.

Finally, if
M\mid N=KM
,
X\in\overline{\mathcal{D}_{M}}
, and

(X,V)\in{\hat{{\boldsymbol{F}}}_{N}}
then

\Pi_{M}V\in{\hat{{\boldsymbol{F}}}_{M}}X
.
Conversely, if
X\in\mathcal{D}_{M}
and

W\in{\hat{{\boldsymbol{F}}}_{M}}X
then
there exists
V\in\mathcal{X}_{N}
such that

(8.29)

(X,V)\in{\hat{{\boldsymbol{F}}}_{N}},\quad W=\Pi_{M}V.

Remark 8.16
.

It is worth noticing that the Eulerian image of

\hat{\boldsymbol{F}}_{N}

is the MPVF
\hat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}_{N}

defined in Theorem
8.4
.

Proof.

(
8.25
) and (
8.26
) follow by the fact that

\mathcal{D}_{N}
is convex and open and
the domain of
\boldsymbol{F}_{N}
is dense in
\mathcal{D}_{N}
,
see Lemma
8.12

and Theorem
A.13
in the Appendix.

Using (
8.25
) it is immediate to check that
{\hat{{\boldsymbol{F}}}_{N}}

satisfies (
8.27
), since for every
(X,V)\in{\hat{{\boldsymbol{F}}}_{N}}

and
(Y,W)\in\boldsymbol{F}_{N}

\displaystyle\langle\sigma V-W,\sigma X-Y\rangle_{\mathcal{X}}

\displaystyle=\langle V-\sigma^{-1}W,X-\sigma^{-1}Y\rangle_{\mathcal{X}}\leq\lambda|X-\sigma^{-1}Y|_{\mathcal{X}}^{2}=\lambda|\sigma X-Y|_{\mathcal{X}}^{2},

since
\boldsymbol{F}_{N}
and the scalar product in
\mathcal{X}_{N}
are invariant by
the action of permutations in
{\mathrm{Sym}(I_{N})}
.

If
(X,V)\in\boldsymbol{F}_{N}
,
(
8.28
) follows immediately
since there exists
W\in\mathcal{X}
such that
\Phi:=(X,W)_{\sharp}\mathbb{P}\in{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
,
V=\Pi_{N}W
, and
(
8.22
) yields

\langle V,X-Y\rangle_{\mathcal{X}}=[\Phi,\iota^{2}_{X,Y}]_{r,0}

so that

(8.30)

\langle V,X-Y\rangle_{\mathcal{X}}+[\Psi,\iota^{2}_{Y,X}]_{r,0}=[\Phi,\iota^{2}_{X,Y}]_{r,0}+[\Psi,\iota^{2}_{Y,X}]_{r,0}\leq\lambda|X-Y|_{\mathcal{X}}^{2}.

Notice that in case (i) of Theorem
8.4
, the last inequality in (
8.30
) follows by (
7.5
), while this is obvious in case (ii) of Theorem
8.4
.

If
X\in\mathcal{D}_{N}

and
V\in\bar{\boldsymbol{F}}_{N}X
according to (
8.26
),
then there exist

(X_{n},V_{n})\in\boldsymbol{F}_{N}
,
X_{n}\in{\mathcal{C}_{N}}
,
such that
X_{n}\to X

and
V_{n}\rightharpoonup V
. We can pass to the limit in (
8.30
)
written for
(X_{n},V_{n})
and using Theorem
2.13
(5)
we obtain that
(X,V)
satisfies
(
8.30
) as well.
Finally, since (
8.30
) holds for every

V\in\bar{\boldsymbol{F}}_{N}X
, it also holds for every

V\in\overline{\operatorname{co}}\left(\bar{\boldsymbol{F}}_{N}X\right)
, hence (
8.28
).

Let us now suppose that
M\mid N
,

(X,V)\in{\hat{{\boldsymbol{F}}}_{N}}
and

X\in\mathcal{D}_{M}
. We want to show that
W:=\Pi_{M}V
belongs to

{\hat{{\boldsymbol{F}}}_{M}}X
by using (
8.25
).
If
(Y,U)\in\boldsymbol{F}_{M}
with
Y\in{\mathcal{C}_{M}}
,
we have
U=\Pi_{M}U^{\prime}
with
(Y,U^{\prime})_{\sharp}\mathbb{P}=:\Phi\in{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
, so that
(
8.28
) yields

(8.31)

\langle V,X-Y\rangle_{\mathcal{X}}+[\Phi,\iota^{2}_{Y,X}]_{r,0}\leq\lambda|X-Y|_{\mathcal{X}}^{2}.

Since
Y\in\mathcal{O}_{M}
and
X\in\mathcal{X}_{M}
, we have

[\Phi,\iota^{2}_{Y,X}]_{r,0}=\langle U,Y-X\rangle_{\mathcal{X}}
by (
8.22
);
since
X-Y\in\mathcal{X}_{M}
, we also have

\langle V,X-Y\rangle_{\mathcal{X}}=\langle\Pi_{M}V,X-Y\rangle_{\mathcal{X}}
and we get

(8.32)

\langle W,X-Y\rangle_{\mathcal{X}}+\langle U,Y-X\rangle_{\mathcal{X}}=\langle V,X-Y\rangle_{\mathcal{X}}+[\Phi,\iota^{2}_{Y,X}]_{r,0}\leq\lambda|X-Y|_{\mathcal{X}}^{2}.

Hence, by (
8.25
)
(X,W)\in{\hat{{\boldsymbol{F}}}_{M}}
.
In particular, the above property shows that
if
\boldsymbol{G}:\mathcal{D}_{N}\to\mathcal{X}_{N}
is an arbitrary single-valued selection of
{\hat{{\boldsymbol{F}}}_{N}}
,
the restriction of
\Pi_{M}\circ\boldsymbol{G}
to
\mathcal{D}_{M}

is a selection of
{\hat{{\boldsymbol{F}}}_{M}}
.
We fix such a selection.
To conclude we need to prove that the property holds also if
X\in\overline{\mathcal{D}_{M}}
. Recall that by Lemma
8.12
(3),
\overline{\mathrm{D}(\boldsymbol{F}_{M})}=\overline{{\mathcal{C}_{M}}}=\overline{\mathcal{D}_{M}}
. Then if
X\in\overline{\mathcal{D}_{M}}
, by Corollary
A.14
we have that

W
belongs to
{\hat{{\boldsymbol{F}}}_{M}}X
if and only if

\langle W-\left(\Pi_{M}\circ\boldsymbol{G}\right)|_{\mathcal{D}_{M}}(Y),X-Y\rangle_{\mathcal{X}}\leq\lambda|X-Y|_{\mathcal{X}}^{2}\quad\text{for every }Y\in\mathcal{D}_{M},

i.e., if and only if

(8.33)

\langle W-\boldsymbol{G}Y,X-Y\rangle_{\mathcal{X}}\leq\lambda|X-Y|_{\mathcal{X}}^{2}\quad\text{for every }Y\in\mathcal{D}_{M}.

If
V\in{\hat{{\boldsymbol{F}}}_{N}}X
, then using Corollary
A.14
we have

\langle V-\boldsymbol{G}Y,X-Y\rangle_{\mathcal{X}}\leq\lambda|X-Y|_{\mathcal{X}}^{2}\quad\text{for every }Y\in\mathcal{D}_{N}\supset\mathcal{D}_{M},

hence (
8.33
) holds and we get
\Pi_{M}V\in{\hat{{\boldsymbol{F}}}_{M}}X
.

Let us now show the converse implication.
If
X\in\mathcal{D}_{M}
and

W\in{\hat{{\boldsymbol{F}}}_{M}}X
, we need to prove that
W\in\Pi_{M}\left({\hat{{\boldsymbol{F}}}_{N}}X\right)
.
Since
\overline{\mathrm{D}(\boldsymbol{G})}=\overline{\mathcal{D}_{N}}
, by Corollary
A.14
and Theorem
A.13
applied to
\boldsymbol{B}=\boldsymbol{G}
, we get
\Pi_{M}\left({\hat{{\boldsymbol{F}}}_{N}}X\right)=\Pi_{M}\left(\hat{\boldsymbol{G}}X\right)=\Pi_{M}\left(\overline{\operatorname{co}}\left(\bar{\boldsymbol{G}}X\right)\right)
, where

\bar{\boldsymbol{G}}X:=\Big\{Z\in\mathcal{X}_{N}:\exists\,X_{n}\in\mathcal{D}_{N}:X_{n}\to X,\ \boldsymbol{G}X_{n}\rightharpoonup Z\Big\}.

Similarly, denoting by
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{G}}:=\left(\Pi_{M}\circ\boldsymbol{G}\right)|_{\mathcal{D}_{M}}
, by Corollary
A.14
and Theorem
A.13
we get

\displaystyle{\hat{{\boldsymbol{F}}}_{M}}X

\displaystyle=\hat{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{G}}}X=\overline{\operatorname{co}}\left(\overline{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{G}}}X\right)=\overline{\operatorname{co}}\left(\left\{Z\in\mathcal{X}_{M}:\exists\,X_{n}\in\mathcal{D}_{M}:X_{n}\to X,\ \boldsymbol{\mathcal{G}}X_{n}\rightharpoonup Z\right\}\right)

\displaystyle\subset\Pi_{M}\left(\overline{\operatorname{co}}\left(\bar{\boldsymbol{G}}X\right)\right),

where the proof of the last equality can be pursued as follows. We first observe that

\displaystyle\left\{Z\in\mathcal{X}_{M}:\exists\,X_{n}\in\mathcal{D}_{M}:X_{n}\to X,\ \boldsymbol{\mathcal{G}}X_{n}\rightharpoonup Z\right\}

\displaystyle\subset\Pi_{M}(\left\{W\in\mathcal{X}_{N}:\exists\,X_{n}\in\mathcal{D}_{N}:X_{n}\to X,\ \boldsymbol{G}X_{n}\rightharpoonup W\right\})=\Pi_{M}(\bar{\boldsymbol{G}}X),

by using the local boundedness of
\boldsymbol{G}
as a selection of
\hat{\boldsymbol{G}}
(see Theorem
A.3
(3)) and the fact that
\Pi_{M}
is a linear and continuous operator. Then we notice that

\overline{\operatorname{co}}\left(\Pi_{M}(\bar{\boldsymbol{G}}X)\right)=\overline{\Pi_{M}(\operatorname{co}(\bar{\boldsymbol{G}}X))}=\Pi_{M}(\overline{\operatorname{co}}\left(\bar{\boldsymbol{G}}X\right)),

where the first equality follows by linearity of
\Pi_{M}
and, for the second, we exploit again the local boundedness of
\bar{\boldsymbol{G}}
as a selection of
\hat{\boldsymbol{G}}
and the linearity and continuity of
\Pi_{M}
. Hence the conclusion.
∎

It is remarkable that

{\hat{{\boldsymbol{F}}}_{N}}
can also be characterized by those
(X,V)\in\overline{\mathcal{D}_{N}}\times\mathcal{X}_{N}
satisfying inequality (
8.25
)

restricted to those
Y\in{\mathcal{C}_{N}}
for which
\iota^{2}(X,Y)

is the unique optimal coupling between
\iota(X)
and
\iota(Y)
.

Proposition 8.17
.

We assume the same hypothesis of Theorem
8.4
.
Let
X\in\overline{\mathcal{D}_{N}}
and
V\in\mathcal{X}_{N}

be satisfying

(8.34)

\begin{gathered}\langle V-W,X-Y\rangle_{\mathcal{X}}\leq\lambda|X-Y|_{\mathcal{X}}^{2}\\
\text{for every }(Y,W)\in\boldsymbol{F}_{N}\text{ s.t. }\iota^{2}(X,Y)\text{ is the unique element of }\Gamma_{o}(\iota_{X},\iota_{Y}).\end{gathered}

Then
(X,V)\in{\hat{{\boldsymbol{F}}}_{N}}.

Proof.

Let us consider an arbitrary element
(Y,W)\in{\boldsymbol{F}}_{N}
; in particular

Y\in{\mathcal{C}_{N}}
. Since
{\mathcal{C}_{N}}\subset\mathcal{D}_{N}

and
\mathcal{D}_{N}
coincides with the interior of the convex set

\overline{\mathcal{D}_{N}}
(relatively to
\mathcal{X}_{N}
),
we deduce that all the points

Y_{t}:=(1-t)X+tY
belong to
\mathcal{D}_{N}
for
t\in(0,1].

Since for
t
in a neighborhood of
1
we have that
Y_{t}\in{\mathcal{C}_{N}}\subset\mathcal{O}_{N}
,
we deduce that
Y_{t}\in\mathcal{O}_{N}
with possible finite exceptions
(observe that if two lines
t\mapsto(1-t)x_{i}+ty_{i}
,
i=1,2
,
in
\mathcal{X}
coincide at two distinct values of
t
then they coincide everywhere).
Therefore there exists
\varepsilon>0
such that
Y_{t}\in\mathcal{O}_{N}

for every
t\in(0,\varepsilon).
Since
\mathcal{D}_{N}\cap\mathcal{O}_{N}={\mathcal{C}_{N}}
(cf. Lemma
8.12
), we
deduce that
Y_{t}\in{\mathcal{C}_{N}}
for every
t\in(0,\varepsilon).

By Theorem
, we can thus find

\tau\in(0,\varepsilon)
such that

Y_{\tau}\in{\mathcal{C}_{N}}
and
\iota^{2}(X,Y_{\tau})
is the unique
optimal coupling between
\iota_{X}
and
\iota_{Y_{\tau}}
. Let
W_{\tau}\in{\boldsymbol{F}}_{N}(Y_{\tau})
, then

\displaystyle\langle V-W,X-Y\rangle_{\mathcal{X}}

\displaystyle=\langle W_{\tau}-W,X-Y\rangle_{\mathcal{X}}+\langle V-W_{\tau},X-Y\rangle_{\mathcal{X}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{1-\tau}\langle W_{\tau}-W,Y_{\tau}-Y\rangle_{\mathcal{X}}+\frac{1}{\tau}\langle V-W_{\tau},X-Y_{\tau}\rangle_{\mathcal{X}}

\displaystyle\leq\lambda|X-Y|_{\mathcal{X}}^{2},

where we have used (
8.34
) and the
\lambda
-dissipativity of
{\boldsymbol{F}}_{N}
.
Since
(Y,W)
is an arbitrary element of
{\boldsymbol{F}}_{N}
, we deduce that
(X,V)\in\hat{\boldsymbol{F}}_{N}
by (
8.25
).
∎

Let us now show that, under the particular assumptions of Theorem

8.5
,

{\hat{{\boldsymbol{F}}}_{N}}
coincides with
{\boldsymbol{F}}
on

{\mathcal{C}_{N}}
.

Corollary 8.18
.

Under the assumptions of Theorem
8.4
, assume also that the
MPVF

{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
is deterministic. Then

{\hat{{\boldsymbol{F}}}_{N}}
is an extension of
{\boldsymbol{F}}_{N}={\boldsymbol{F}}
on
{\mathcal{C}_{N}}
, for every
N\in{\mathfrak{N}}
.
Under the further assumptions
that
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
is a single-valued
PVF
and demicontinuous on each
\mathrm{C}_{N}
, then
{\boldsymbol{F}}_{N}

coincides with
\hat{\boldsymbol{F}}_{N}
on
{\mathcal{C}_{N}}
.

Proof.

The first statement is an immediate consequence of Proposition
8.15
; the equality
{\boldsymbol{F}}_{N}={\boldsymbol{F}}
on
{\mathcal{C}_{N}}
follows from the fact that
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
is a deterministic MPVF by assumption. Let us now assume that
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
is single-valued
and its restriction to
\mathrm{C}_{N}
is demicontinuous.
Let
X
be an element of
{\mathcal{C}_{N}}
,
\mu=\iota(X)
;
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}[\mu]
contains a unique element
\Phi
which may be represented as
\operatorname{bar}\left(\Phi\right)=(\boldsymbol{i}_{\mathsf{X}},\boldsymbol{b}_{\Phi})_{\sharp}\mu
so that there is a unique element
V=\boldsymbol{b}_{\Phi}\circ X\in\mathcal{X}_{N}
such that
(X,V)_{\sharp}\mathbb{P}=\Phi
. This shows that
{\boldsymbol{F}}X
is single-valued.
If
W\in\bar{\boldsymbol{F}}_{N}X
, we can find a sequence
(X_{n},{\boldsymbol{F}}X_{n})=(X_{n},\boldsymbol{f}_{n}\circ X_{n})
converging in the strong-weak topology of
\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{X}
to
(X,W)
, for maps
\boldsymbol{f}_{n}\in L^{2}(\mathsf{X},\mu_{n};\mathsf{X})
with
\mu_{n}=\iota_{X_{n}}
. On the other hand, since
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
is demicontinuous and deterministic, we have that
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}[\iota_{X_{n}}]=(\boldsymbol{i}_{\mathsf{X}},\boldsymbol{f}_{n})_{\sharp}\mu_{n}\to{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}[\iota_{X}]=(\boldsymbol{i}_{\mathsf{X}},\boldsymbol{f})_{\sharp}\mu
in
\mathcal{P}_{2}^{sw}(\mathsf{T\kern-1.5ptX})
for a map
\boldsymbol{f}\in L^{2}(\mathsf{X},\mu;\mathsf{X})
. If
\psi\in\mathrm{C}_{b}(\mathsf{X};\mathsf{X})
, we can test the convergence in
\mathcal{P}_{2}^{sw}(\mathsf{T\kern-1.5ptX})
against
\zeta(x,y):=\langle\psi(x),y\rangle
so that

\langle\psi(X_{n}),\boldsymbol{f}_{n}\circ X_{n}\rangle_{\mathcal{X}}=\int_{\mathsf{X}}\zeta\,\mathrm{d}(\boldsymbol{i}_{\mathsf{X}},\boldsymbol{f}_{n})_{\sharp}\mu_{n}\to\int_{\mathsf{X}}\zeta\,\mathrm{d}(\boldsymbol{i}_{\mathsf{X}},\boldsymbol{f})_{\sharp}\mu=\langle\psi(X),\boldsymbol{f}\circ X\rangle_{\mathcal{X}}.

On the other hand
\psi(X_{n})\to\psi(X)
and
\boldsymbol{f}_{n}\circ X_{n}\rightharpoonup W
so that we deduce that

\langle\psi(X),\boldsymbol{f}\circ X\rangle_{\mathcal{X}}=\langle\psi(X),W\rangle_{\mathcal{X}}\quad\text{ for every }\psi\in\mathrm{C}_{b}(\mathsf{X};\mathsf{X}).

By arbitrariety of
\psi
we deduce that
W=\boldsymbol{f}\circ X={\boldsymbol{F}}X
. We thus deduce that
\bar{\boldsymbol{F}}_{N}X
coincides with
{\boldsymbol{F}}X
and then it contains a unique element
V
, and therefore by (
8.26
)
\hat{\boldsymbol{F}}_{N}X=\operatorname{co}({\boldsymbol{F}}_{N}X)=V
as well.
∎

A similar result holds under the assumptions of Theorem

8.6
.
Let us first recall that,
by Corollary
3.19
,
if
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
is totally
\lambda
-dissipative also

\tilde{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}:={\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}\cup\operatorname{bar}\left({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}\right)

is totally
\lambda
-dissipative.

Corollary 8.19
.

Under the assumptions of Theorem
8.6
,
let
\tilde{\boldsymbol{B}}
be
the Lagrangian representation of
\tilde{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}={\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}\cup\operatorname{bar}\left({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}\right)
,
and let
{\boldsymbol{B}}^{\prime}
be any
\lambda
-dissipative extension of

\tilde{{\boldsymbol{B}}}
.
For every
N\in{\mathfrak{N}}
,
Y\in\overline{\mathcal{D}_{N}}
,

(Y,W)\in{{\boldsymbol{B}}}^{\prime}
,
we have

(Y,\Pi_{N}W)\in{\hat{{\boldsymbol{F}}}_{N}}
and, in particular,

(8.35)

\langle V-\Pi_{N}W,X-Y\rangle_{\mathcal{X}}\leq\lambda|X-Y|_{\mathcal{X}}^{2}\quad\text{for every
$(X,V)\in{\hat{{\boldsymbol{F}}}_{N}}$, $Y\in\overline{\mathcal{D}_{N}}$,
$(Y,W)\in{{\boldsymbol{B}}^{\prime}}$},

where
{\hat{{\boldsymbol{F}}}_{N}}
is constructed as in Proposition
8.15
starting from the restriction of
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
to
\mathrm{C}
.

Proof.

Observe that, by construction,
{\boldsymbol{F}}
(constructed
starting from the restriction of the MPVF
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
to
\mathrm{C}
)
and
{\boldsymbol{F}}_{N}
are subsets of
\tilde{\boldsymbol{B}}
hence of
{\boldsymbol{B}}^{\prime}
;
this implies that
{\boldsymbol{F}}_{N}
is dissipative with

{{\boldsymbol{B}}^{\prime}}
in the sense that

(8.36)

\langle X-Y,V-W\rangle_{\mathcal{X}}\leq\lambda|X-Y|_{\mathcal{X}}^{2}\quad\text{for every $(X,V)\in{\boldsymbol{F}}_{N}$, $(Y,W)\in{{\boldsymbol{B}}^{\prime}}$}.

Restricting (
8.36
) to
Y\in\overline{\mathcal{D}_{N}}
,
the very definition of
{\hat{{\boldsymbol{F}}}_{N}}
in (
8.25
) yields

(Y,\Pi_{N}W)\in{\hat{{\boldsymbol{F}}}_{N}}
;
in particular, we get (
8.35
).
∎

In the general case, we can still
improve the compatibility result obtained in (
8.28
) between
{\hat{{\boldsymbol{F}}}_{N}}
and
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
with the following.

Lemma 8.20
.

We keep the same assumptions of Theorem
8.4
,
and let
N\in{\mathfrak{N}}
. Then

(8.37)

\langle V,X-Y\rangle_{\mathcal{X}}+[\Psi,\iota^{2}_{Y,X}]_{r,0}\leq\lambda|X-Y|_{\mathcal{X}}^{2}

for every
(X,V)\in{\hat{{\boldsymbol{F}}}_{N}}
,
Y\in\mathrm{D}({\hat{{\boldsymbol{F}}}_{N}})
and every
\Psi\in{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}[\iota_{Y}]
.

Proof.

We start by proving (
8.37
) in case
X\in\mathcal{D}_{N}
. Let
Y_{s}:=(1-s)Y+sX\in\mathcal{D}_{N}
for every
s\in(0,1]
; then, by (
8.28
), we have

\langle V,X-Y_{s}\rangle_{\mathcal{X}}+[{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}},\iota^{2}_{Y_{s},X}]_{r,0}\leq\lambda|X-Y_{s}|_{\mathcal{X}}^{2}.

Using (
2.11
) we can rewrite the above equation as

\langle V,X-Y_{s}\rangle_{\mathcal{X}}+(1-s)[{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}},\iota^{2}_{Y,X}]_{r,s}\leq\lambda|X-Y_{s}|_{\mathcal{X}}^{2}.

Using (
7.5
) in case (i) of Theorem
8.4
, or the total
\lambda
-dissipativity of
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
in case (ii) of Theorem
8.4
, we get

\langle V,X-Y_{s}\rangle_{\mathcal{X}}+(1-s)[{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}},\iota^{2}_{Y,X}]_{r,0}-s(1-s)\lambda|X-Y|_{\mathcal{X}}^{2}\leq\lambda|X-Y_{s}|_{\mathcal{X}}^{2}.

Passing to the limit as
s\downarrow 0
, we obtain

(8.38)

\langle V,X-Y\rangle_{\mathcal{X}}+[\Psi,\iota^{2}_{Y,X}]_{r,0}\leq\lambda|X-Y|_{\mathcal{X}}^{2}\quad\forall X\in\mathcal{D}_{N},\,V\in{\hat{{\boldsymbol{F}}}_{N}}X,Y\in\mathrm{D}({\hat{{\boldsymbol{F}}}_{N}}),\Psi\in{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}[\iota_{Y}].

We come now to the general case; let
(X,V)\in{\hat{{\boldsymbol{F}}}_{N}}
,
Y\in\mathrm{D}({\hat{{\boldsymbol{F}}}_{N}})
and
\Psi\in{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}[\iota_{Y}]
. We define
Z=(X+Y)/2\in\overline{\mathcal{D}_{N}}
and, given
T\in\mathcal{D}_{N}
and
V_{T}\in{\hat{{\boldsymbol{F}}}_{N}}T
, we set
Z_{t}:=(1-t)Z+Tt\in\mathcal{D}_{N}
for every
t\in(0,1]
; we take, for every
t\in(0,1]
, some
V_{t}\in{\hat{{\boldsymbol{F}}}_{N}}Z_{t}
. Clearly

(X,V),(Z_{t},V_{t}),(T,V_{T})\in{\hat{{\boldsymbol{F}}}_{N}}\quad\forall\,t\in(0,1].

We compute

\displaystyle\quad\,\,\langle V,X-Y\rangle_{\mathcal{X}}+[\Psi,\iota^{2}_{Y,X}]_{r,0}=

\displaystyle=\langle V-V_{t},X-Y\rangle_{\mathcal{X}}-\langle V_{t},Y-X\rangle_{\mathcal{X}}+[\Psi,\iota^{2}_{Y,X}]_{r,0}

\displaystyle=2\langle V-V_{t},X-Z\rangle_{\mathcal{X}}-2\langle V_{t},Y-Z\rangle_{\mathcal{X}}+2[\Psi,\iota^{2}_{Y,Z}]_{r,0}

\displaystyle=2\langle V-V_{t},X-Z_{t}\rangle_{\mathcal{X}}-2\langle V_{t},Y-Z_{t}\rangle_{\mathcal{X}}+2[\Psi,\iota^{2}_{Y,Z_{t}}]_{r,0}

\displaystyle\quad+2\langle V-V_{t},Z_{t}-Z\rangle_{\mathcal{X}}-2\langle V_{t},Z_{t}-Z\rangle_{\mathcal{X}}-2[\Psi,\iota^{2}_{Y,Z_{t}}]_{r,0}+2[\Psi,\iota^{2}_{Y,Z}]_{r,0}

\displaystyle=2\langle V-V_{t},X-Z_{t}\rangle_{\mathcal{X}}-2\langle V_{t},Y-Z_{t}\rangle_{\mathcal{X}}+2[\Psi,\iota^{2}_{Y,Z_{t}}]_{r,0}

\displaystyle\quad+4\langle V_{T}-V_{t},Z_{t}-Z\rangle_{\mathcal{X}}+2\langle V-2V_{T},Z_{t}-Z\rangle_{\mathcal{X}}-2[\Psi,\iota^{2}_{Y,Z_{t}}]_{r,0}+2[\Psi,\iota^{2}_{Y,Z}]_{r,0}

\displaystyle=2\langle V-V_{t},X-Z_{t}\rangle_{\mathcal{X}}+2\langle V_{t},Z_{t}-Y\rangle_{\mathcal{X}}+2[\Psi,\iota^{2}_{Y,Z_{t}}]_{r,0}

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{4t}{1-t}\langle V_{T}-V_{t},T-Z_{t}\rangle_{\mathcal{X}}+2t\langle V-2V_{T},T-Z\rangle_{\mathcal{X}}-2[\Psi,\iota^{2}_{Y,Z_{t}}]_{r,0}+2[\Psi,\iota^{2}_{Y,Z}]_{r,0}

\displaystyle\leq 2t\langle V-2V_{T},T-Z\rangle_{\mathcal{X}}-2[\Psi,\iota^{2}_{Y,Z_{t}}]_{r,0}+2[\Psi,\iota^{2}_{Y,Z}]_{r,0}

\displaystyle\quad+2\lambda|X-Z_{t}|_{\mathcal{X}}^{2}+2\lambda|Y-Z_{t}|_{\mathcal{X}}^{2}+\frac{4t}{1-t}\lambda|T-Z_{t}|_{\mathcal{X}}^{2},

where we have used again (
2.11
), the
\lambda
-dissipativity of
{\hat{{\boldsymbol{F}}}_{N}}
and (
8.38
) applied to
Z_{t}\in\mathcal{D}_{N}
,
V_{t}\in{\hat{{\boldsymbol{F}}}_{N}}Z_{t}
. Passing to the
\limsup
as
t\downarrow 0
, we get

\langle V,X-Y\rangle_{\mathcal{X}}+[\Psi,\iota^{2}_{Y,X}]_{r,0}\leq 2[\Psi,\iota^{2}_{Y,Z}]_{r,0}-2\liminf_{t\downarrow 0}[\Psi,\iota^{2}_{Y,Z_{t}}]_{r,0}+\lambda|X-Y|_{\mathcal{X}}^{2}\leq\lambda|X-Y|_{\mathcal{X}}^{2}

by Theorem
2.13
(5).
∎

8.3.
Lagrangian representation of the maximal extension

The last and crucial step in the construction
of
\hat{{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}}
of Theorem
8.4
exploits an important invariance property of the resolvents
of
{\hat{{\boldsymbol{F}}}_{N}}
with respect to
N
.

Proposition 8.21
.

We keep the same assumptions of Theorem

8.4
.
For every
X\in\mathcal{X}_{\infty}
and every
0<\tau<1/\lambda^{+}

there exists a unique
X_{\tau}\in\mathcal{X}_{\infty}
such that

(8.39)

X\in\mathcal{X}_{N}\Rightarrow X_{\tau}\in\mathrm{D}({\hat{{\boldsymbol{F}}}_{N}})\subset\mathcal{X}_{N}\,\text{ and }\,X_{\tau}-X\in\tau\,{\hat{{\boldsymbol{F}}}_{N}}X_{\tau}.

Moreover

(8.40)

|X_{\tau}(\omega^{\prime})-X_{\tau}(\omega^{\prime\prime})|\leq\frac{1}{1-\lambda\tau}|X(\omega^{\prime})-X(\omega^{\prime\prime})|\quad\text{for every }\omega^{\prime},\omega^{\prime\prime}\in\Omega.

Proof.

Since
X\in\mathcal{X}_{\infty}
, there exits
N\in{\mathfrak{N}}
such that
X\in\mathcal{X}_{N}
.
Since
{\hat{{\boldsymbol{F}}}_{N}}
is maximal
\lambda
-dissipative, recalling Theorem
A.2
(1),
there exists a unique solution
X_{\tau,N}\in\mathrm{D}({\hat{{\boldsymbol{F}}}_{N}})
of

X_{\tau,N}-X\in\tau\,{\hat{{\boldsymbol{F}}}_{N}}(X_{\tau,N}).

The invariance of
{\hat{{\boldsymbol{F}}}_{N}}
by permutations, stated in (
8.27
), shows
that

(\sigma X)_{\tau,N}=\sigma(X_{\tau,N})
for every
\sigma\in{\mathrm{Sym}(I_{N})}
.
In particular, by
\lambda
-dissipativity of
{\hat{{\boldsymbol{F}}}_{N}}
we have

\displaystyle\langle\sigma X_{\tau,N}-\sigma X-(X_{\tau,N}-X),\sigma X_{\tau,N}-X_{\tau,N}\rangle_{\mathcal{X}}\leq\lambda\tau|\sigma X_{\tau,N}-X_{\tau,N}|_{\mathcal{X}}^{2}

so that

(1-\lambda\tau)\,|\sigma X_{\tau,N}-X_{\tau,N}|_{\mathcal{X}}\leq|\sigma X-X|_{\mathcal{X}}\quad\text{for every }\sigma\in{\mathrm{Sym}(I_{N})}.

If
\omega^{\prime}\in\Omega_{N,i}
,
\omega^{\prime\prime}\in\Omega_{N,j}
,
i,j\in I_{N}
, and
we choose as
\sigma
the transposition which
shifts
i
with
j
, we get

\frac{2}{N}(1-\lambda\tau)^{2}|X_{\tau,N}(\omega^{\prime})-X_{\tau,N}(\omega^{\prime\prime})|^{2}\leq\frac{2}{N}|X(\omega^{\prime})-X(\omega^{\prime\prime})|^{2}

which yields (
8.40
).

Let us now suppose that
X\in\mathcal{X}_{M}
with
M\mid N
.
Then
X_{\tau,N}
belongs to
\mathcal{X}_{M}
by (
8.40
), so that
X_{\tau,N}\in\overline{\mathcal{D}_{N}}\cap\mathcal{X}_{M}=\overline{\mathcal{D}_{M}}
by Lemma
8.12
(4). By Proposition
8.15
, for every
Y\in\mathcal{D}_{M}
and
W\in{\hat{{\boldsymbol{F}}}_{M}}Y

we can find
V\in{\hat{{\boldsymbol{F}}}_{N}}Y

such that
W=\Pi_{M}V
, so that by
\lambda
-dissipativity of
{\hat{{\boldsymbol{F}}}_{N}}
we have

(8.41)

\langle X_{\tau,N}-X-\tau V,X_{\tau,N}-Y\rangle_{\mathcal{X}}\leq\lambda\tau|X_{\tau,N}-Y|_{\mathcal{X}}^{2}.

Since
X_{\tau,N}-Y\in\mathcal{X}_{M}
, we can replace

V
with
W=\Pi_{M}V
in (
8.41
), thus obtaining that

X_{\tau,N}-X\in\tau{\hat{{\boldsymbol{F}}}_{M}}(X_{\tau,N})
by Corollary
A.14
,
i.e.
X_{\tau,N}=X_{\tau,M}
.
If
M,N
are arbitrary and
X\in\mathcal{X}_{M}\cap\mathcal{X}_{N}
, then
setting
R:=MN
the previous argument shows that

X_{\tau,M}=X_{\tau,R}=X_{\tau,N}
.
∎

Corollary 8.22
.

We keep the same assumptions of Theorem
8.4
,
let
M\in{\mathfrak{N}}
and let
X\in\mathrm{D}({\hat{{\boldsymbol{F}}}_{M}})
. Then

(1)

X\in\mathrm{D}({\hat{{\boldsymbol{F}}}_{N}})
for every
N\in{\mathfrak{N}}
s.t.
M\mid N
.

(2)

\hat{\boldsymbol{F}}{\vphantom{{\boldsymbol{F}}}}^{\circ}X:=\lim_{\tau\downarrow 0}\frac{X_{\tau}-X}{\tau}\in{\hat{{\boldsymbol{F}}}_{M}}X
. In particular
\hat{\boldsymbol{F}}{\vphantom{{\boldsymbol{F}}}}^{\circ}X\in{\hat{{\boldsymbol{F}}}_{N}}X
for every
N\in{\mathfrak{N}}
s.t.
M\mid N
.

(3)

|\hat{\boldsymbol{F}}{\vphantom{{\boldsymbol{F}}}}^{\circ}X|_{\mathcal{X}}\leq|V|_{\mathcal{X}}
for every
V\in{\hat{{\boldsymbol{F}}}_{N}}X
and for every
N\in{\mathfrak{N}}
s.t.
M\mid N
.

(4)

(1-\lambda\tau)|X_{\tau}-X|_{\mathcal{X}}\leq\tau|\hat{\boldsymbol{F}}{\vphantom{{\boldsymbol{F}}}}^{\circ}X|_{\mathcal{X}}
for every
0<\tau<1/\lambda^{+}
.

Moreover, for every
X,Y\in\bigcup_{N\in{\mathfrak{N}}}\mathrm{D}({\hat{{\boldsymbol{F}}}_{N}})
, we have

(8.42)

\langle\hat{\boldsymbol{F}}{\vphantom{{\boldsymbol{F}}}}^{\circ}X-\hat{\boldsymbol{F}}{\vphantom{{\boldsymbol{F}}}}^{\circ}Y,X-Y\rangle_{\mathcal{X}}\leq\lambda|X-Y|_{\mathcal{X}}^{2}.

Proof.

By Theorem
A.3
(5) there exists the limit

\lim_{\tau\downarrow 0}\frac{X_{\tau}-X}{\tau}=\hat{\boldsymbol{F}}{\vphantom{{\boldsymbol{F}}}}^{\circ}X\in{\hat{{\boldsymbol{F}}}_{M}}X

and (4) holds. If
N\in{\mathfrak{N}}
is s.t.
M\mid N
, then
X\in\mathrm{D}({\hat{{\boldsymbol{F}}}_{M}})\subset\overline{\mathcal{D}_{M}}\subset\overline{\mathcal{D}_{N}}
, by Lemma
8.12
. Moreover by Proposition
8.21
, we have that

\frac{X_{\tau}-X}{\tau}\in{\hat{{\boldsymbol{F}}}_{N}}X_{\tau}\quad\forall\,0<\tau<1/\lambda^{+}.

In particular

\langle\frac{X_{\tau}-X}{\tau}-W,X_{\tau}-Y\rangle_{\mathcal{X}}\leq\lambda|X_{\tau}-Y|_{\mathcal{X}}^{2}\quad\forall(Y,W)\in{\boldsymbol{F}}_{N}\quad\forall\,0<\tau<1/\lambda^{+},

so that, passing to the limit as
\tau\downarrow 0
, we get

\langle\hat{\boldsymbol{F}}{\vphantom{{\boldsymbol{F}}}}^{\circ}X-W,X-Y\rangle_{\mathcal{X}}\leq\lambda|X-Y|_{\mathcal{X}}^{2}\quad\forall(Y,W)\in{\boldsymbol{F}}_{N},

since
X_{\tau}\to X
as
\tau\downarrow 0
by Theorem
A.3
(4). This proves that
(X,\hat{\boldsymbol{F}}{\vphantom{{\boldsymbol{F}}}}^{\circ}X)\in{\hat{{\boldsymbol{F}}}_{N}}
and, in particular, that
X\in\mathrm{D}({\hat{{\boldsymbol{F}}}_{N}})
. This proves (1) and (2), while (3) immediately follows, also using Theorem
A.3
(5).

Finally, if
X,Y\in\bigcup_{N\in{\mathfrak{N}}}\mathrm{D}({\hat{{\boldsymbol{F}}}_{N}})
, then there exist
N,M\in{\mathfrak{N}}
s.t.
X\in\mathrm{D}({\hat{{\boldsymbol{F}}}_{N}})
and
Y\in\mathrm{D}({\hat{{\boldsymbol{F}}}_{M}})
so that, taking
R:=MN
, we have

(X,\hat{\boldsymbol{F}}{\vphantom{{\boldsymbol{F}}}}^{\circ}X),(Y,\hat{\boldsymbol{F}}{\vphantom{{\boldsymbol{F}}}}^{\circ}Y)\in{\hat{{\boldsymbol{F}}}_{R}}

by (2). The
\lambda
-dissipativity of
{\hat{{\boldsymbol{F}}}_{R}}
gives (
8.42
).
∎

We can therefore define the operator
{\hat{{\boldsymbol{F}}}_{\infty}}\subset\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{X}

(8.43)

{\hat{{\boldsymbol{F}}}_{\infty}}:=\Big\{(X,V)\in\mathcal{X}_{\infty}\times\mathcal{X}_{\infty}:\exists\,M\in{\mathfrak{N}}:(X,V)\in{\hat{{\boldsymbol{F}}}_{N}}\ \forall\,N\in{\mathfrak{N}},\ M\mid N\Big\}.

Equivalently,
{\hat{{\boldsymbol{F}}}_{\infty}}
has domain

\mathrm{D}({\hat{{\boldsymbol{F}}}_{\infty}})=\bigcup_{N\in{\mathfrak{N}}}\mathrm{D}({\hat{{\boldsymbol{F}}}_{N}})
and

(8.44)

{\hat{{\boldsymbol{F}}}_{\infty}}X=\bigcup_{M\in{\mathfrak{N}}}\bigcap_{M\mid N}{\hat{{\boldsymbol{F}}}_{N}}X\quad\text{for every }X\in\mathrm{D}({\hat{{\boldsymbol{F}}}_{\infty}}).

Notice that
{\hat{{\boldsymbol{F}}}_{\infty}}
is the Lagrangian representation of the MPVF
\hat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}_{\infty}
defined by Theorem
8.4
.

We can summarize the previous results in the following statement.

Corollary 8.23
.

We keep the same assumptions of Theorem
8.4
.
The operator
{\hat{{\boldsymbol{F}}}_{\infty}}
defined by (
8.43
) or
(
8.44
) satisfies the following properties:

(1)

{\hat{{\boldsymbol{F}}}_{\infty}}
is
\lambda
-dissipative with domain

\mathrm{D}({\hat{{\boldsymbol{F}}}_{\infty}})=\bigcup_{N\in{\mathfrak{N}}}\mathrm{D}({\hat{{\boldsymbol{F}}}_{N}})
and
{\mathcal{C}_{\infty}}\subset\mathcal{D}_{\infty}\subset\mathrm{D}({\hat{{\boldsymbol{F}}}_{\infty}})\subset\overline{\mathrm{D}({\hat{{\boldsymbol{F}}}_{\infty}})}=\overline{{\mathcal{C}_{\infty}}}=\overline{\mathcal{D}_{\infty}}
.

(2)

The map

\hat{\boldsymbol{F}}{\vphantom{{\boldsymbol{F}}}}^{\circ}

defined by Corollary
8.22

provides the minimal selection
({\hat{{\boldsymbol{F}}}_{\infty}})^{\circ}
.

(3)

For every
X\in\mathcal{X}_{\infty}
and every

0<\tau<1/\lambda^{+}
there exists a unique

X_{\tau}\in\mathrm{D}({\hat{{\boldsymbol{F}}}_{\infty}})
such that

X_{\tau}-X\in\tau\,{\hat{{\boldsymbol{F}}}_{\infty}}X_{\tau}
.

Proof.

Claim (1) follows by Proposition
8.15
and Lemma
8.12
.
Claim (2) comes by (
8.43
) and Corollary
8.22
.
Claim (3) is a consequence of Proposition
8.21
and the
\lambda
-dissipativity of
{\hat{{\boldsymbol{F}}}_{\infty}}
.
∎

Corollary 8.24
.

Under the assumptions of Theorem
8.4
, there exists a unique maximal extension

\hat{\boldsymbol{F}}
of
{\hat{{\boldsymbol{F}}}_{\infty}}
and it satisfies the following:

(1)

\mathrm{D}(\hat{\boldsymbol{F}})\subset\overline{\mathrm{D}({\hat{{\boldsymbol{F}}}_{\infty}})}=\overline{{\mathcal{C}_{\infty}}}
,

(8.45)

\mathcal{X}_{N}\cap\mathrm{D}(\hat{\boldsymbol{F}})=\mathrm{D}({\hat{{\boldsymbol{F}}}_{N}}),\quad\mathcal{X}_{\infty}\cap\mathrm{D}(\hat{\boldsymbol{F}})=\mathrm{D}({\hat{{\boldsymbol{F}}}_{\infty}}),

and, if
X\in\mathcal{X}_{\infty}
and
0<\tau<1/\lambda^{+}
, then

(8.46)

\boldsymbol{J}_{\tau}X=X_{\tau},

where
\boldsymbol{J}_{\tau}
is the resolvent operator of
\hat{\boldsymbol{F}}
and
X_{\tau}
is as in Proposition
8.21
.

(2)

When restricted to
\mathrm{D}({\hat{{\boldsymbol{F}}}_{N}})
(resp.
\mathrm{D}({\hat{{\boldsymbol{F}}}_{\infty}})
), the minimal selection of
\hat{\boldsymbol{F}}
coincides with
({\hat{{\boldsymbol{F}}}_{N}})^{\circ}
(resp.
({\hat{{\boldsymbol{F}}}_{\infty}})^{\circ}=\hat{\boldsymbol{F}}{\vphantom{{\boldsymbol{F}}}}^{\circ}
as in Corollary
8.23
(2)).

(3)

The following characterization holds

(8.47)

(X,V)\in\hat{\boldsymbol{F}}\quad\Leftrightarrow\quad X\in\overline{{\mathcal{C}_{\infty}}},\ \langle V-W,X-Y\rangle_{\mathcal{X}}\leq\lambda|X-Y|_{\mathcal{X}}^{2}\quad\text{for every }(Y,W)\in{\hat{{\boldsymbol{F}}}_{\infty}};

or, equivalently,

(8.48)

(X,V)\in\hat{\boldsymbol{F}}\quad\Leftrightarrow\quad X\in\overline{{\mathcal{C}_{\infty}}},\ \langle V-\hat{\boldsymbol{F}}{\vphantom{{\boldsymbol{F}}}}^{\circ}Y,X-Y\rangle_{\mathcal{X}}\leq\lambda|X-Y|_{\mathcal{X}}^{2}\quad\text{for every }Y\in\mathrm{D}(\hat{{\boldsymbol{F}}}_{\infty}).

(4)

\hat{\boldsymbol{F}}=\overline{{\hat{{\boldsymbol{F}}}_{\infty}}}^{\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{X}}
.

Proof.

Thanks to Corollary
8.23
, the existence and uniqueness of the maximal extension
\hat{\boldsymbol{F}}
of
{\hat{{\boldsymbol{F}}}_{\infty}}
with domain

\mathrm{D}(\hat{\boldsymbol{F}})\subset\overline{\mathrm{D}({\hat{{\boldsymbol{F}}}_{\infty}})}
and characterized by (
8.47
) follows by Lemma
A.15
, with
D=\mathcal{X}_{\infty}
.

Notice that (
8.46
) holds since, by Corollary
8.23
(3), when
X\in\mathcal{X}_{\infty}
then
X_{\tau}
plays the role of the resolvent for
{\hat{{\boldsymbol{F}}}_{\infty}}
and we just proved that
\hat{\boldsymbol{F}}
is a maximal extension of
{\hat{{\boldsymbol{F}}}_{\infty}}
.
We prove the equivalences in (
8.45
): let
X\in\mathcal{X}_{N}\cap\mathrm{D}(\hat{\boldsymbol{F}})
and
0<\tau<1/\lambda^{+}
, then

\frac{\boldsymbol{J}_{\tau}X-X}{\tau}

belongs to
{\hat{{\boldsymbol{F}}}_{N}}X_{\tau}
thanks to Proposition
8.21
and (
8.46
), moreover it
is bounded being
X\in\mathrm{D}(\hat{\boldsymbol{F}})
(cf. Theorem
A.3
(5)). By maximality of
{\hat{{\boldsymbol{F}}}_{N}}
and applying again Theorem
A.3
(5), we deduce that
X\in\mathrm{D}({\hat{{\boldsymbol{F}}}_{N}})
, hence
\mathcal{X}_{N}\cap\mathrm{D}(\hat{\boldsymbol{F}})\subset\mathrm{D}({\hat{{\boldsymbol{F}}}_{N}})
. The reverse inclusion is trivial.

Claim (2) comes from Claim (1) and Theorem
A.3
(5). The assertion involving
{\hat{{\boldsymbol{F}}}_{\infty}}
comes from Corollary
8.23
(2) and the proof of Lemma
A.15
.

The characterization in (
8.48
) is a consequence of Corollary
A.16
, applied to
{\boldsymbol{B}}=\hat{\boldsymbol{F}}
with
D=\mathcal{X}_{\infty}
, and of (
8.45
).

Finally, Claim (4) comes by Lemma
A.15
and the density of
\mathcal{X}_{\infty}
in
\mathcal{X}
.
∎

Remark 8.25
.

Notice that Corollary
8.24
(2) makes the notation
\hat{\boldsymbol{F}}{\vphantom{{\boldsymbol{F}}}}^{\circ}
, used in Corollary

8.22
, coherent with the
one used in Appendix
A
to denote the minimal selection of
\hat{\boldsymbol{F}}
.

Theorem 8.26
.

Under the assumptions of Theorem
8.4
,

\hat{\boldsymbol{F}}
is a law invariant maximal
\lambda
-dissipative operator according to Definition

3.2
and
the Eulerian images

\hat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}_{N},\hat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}_{\infty},\hat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
of

{\hat{{\boldsymbol{F}}}_{N}},{\hat{{\boldsymbol{F}}}_{\infty}},\hat{\boldsymbol{F}}
respectively (cf. Definition
3.8
) satisfy
the properties stated in Theorem
8.4
.

Moreover, if
Y\in\mathrm{D}({\boldsymbol{F}})
and
\Psi\in{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}[\iota_{Y}]
, we
have

(8.49)

\langle V,X-Y\rangle_{\mathcal{X}}+[\Psi,\iota^{2}_{Y,X}]_{r,0}\leq\lambda|X-Y|_{\mathcal{X}}^{2}\quad\text{for every }(X,V)\in\hat{\boldsymbol{F}}.

Finally, if
X\in{\mathcal{C}_{N}}
for some
N\in{\mathfrak{N}}
and
\Phi\in{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}[\iota_{X}]
, then

(8.50)

|\hat{\boldsymbol{F}}{\vphantom{{\boldsymbol{F}}}}^{\circ}X|_{\mathcal{X}}^{2}\leq\int_{\mathsf{X}}|\boldsymbol{b}_{\Phi}|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\iota_{X},

where
\boldsymbol{b}_{\Phi}
is the barycenter of
\Phi
as in Definition
2.3
.

Proof.

We can apply Lemma
8.10

(see also Remark
8.11
)
to the set
\hat{\boldsymbol{F}}_{\infty}\subset\mathcal{X}_{\infty}\times\mathcal{X}_{\infty}
.
By construction, if
(X,V)\in\hat{\boldsymbol{F}}_{\infty}\cap(\mathcal{X}_{M}\times\mathcal{X}_{M})

there exists some
M^{\prime}\in{\mathfrak{N}}
such that
(X,V)\in\hat{\boldsymbol{F}}_{N}
for all

N
multiple of
M^{\prime}
. In particular, choosing
M^{\prime\prime}\in{\mathfrak{N}}
so that

M\mid M^{\prime\prime}
and
M^{\prime}\mid M^{\prime\prime}
,

(X,V)\in\hat{\boldsymbol{F}}_{N}
for all

N
multiple of
M^{\prime\prime}
. On the other hand, all the permutations

\sigma\in{\mathrm{Sym}(I_{M})}
induce admissible permutations of
{\mathrm{Sym}(I_{N})}
; therefore, by (
8.27
), we have that

(\sigma X,\sigma Y)
belongs to
\hat{\boldsymbol{F}}_{N}

for every
N
multiple of
M^{\prime\prime}
. We deduce that
(\sigma X,\sigma Y)\in\hat{\boldsymbol{F}}_{\infty}
so that
\hat{\boldsymbol{F}}_{\infty}\cap(\mathcal{X}_{M}\times\mathcal{X}_{M})

is invariant by
{\mathrm{Sym}(I_{M})}
.
Since
\hat{\boldsymbol{F}}
is the closure of
\hat{\boldsymbol{F}}_{\infty}
by Corollary
8.24
(4),
Lemma
8.10
yields that
\hat{\boldsymbol{F}}
is law
invariant.

Let us now consider the Eulerian images
\hat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}_{N},\hat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}_{\infty}
and
\hat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
. Since
\hat{\boldsymbol{F}}
is law-invariant and maximal
\lambda
-dissipative, by Proposition
3.10
and Theorem
3.12
, we have that the MPVF
\hat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
is maximal totally
\lambda
-dissipative.
Since
\hat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
is an
extension of
\hat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}_{\infty}
, we deduce that
\hat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}_{\infty}
is
totally
\lambda
-dissipative as well. The remaining part of the
statement of Theorem
8.4
follows by Proposition
8.15
, Proposition
8.17
, Corollary

8.22
, Corollary
8.23
, Corollary
8.24
and the definitions
of
{\hat{{\boldsymbol{F}}}_{\infty}}
and
\hat{\boldsymbol{F}}
.

We now prove (
8.49
). Let
Y\in\mathrm{D}({\boldsymbol{F}})
and let
N\in{\mathfrak{N}}
be such that
Y\in{\mathcal{C}_{N}}
; let
((X_{n},V_{n}))_{n}
as before. If
\Psi\in{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}[\iota_{Y}]
, then, for every
n\in\mathbb{N}
, we can find
M_{n}\in{\mathfrak{N}}
such that

(X_{n},V_{n})\in{\hat{{\boldsymbol{F}}}_{N}},Y\in\mathrm{D}({\hat{{\boldsymbol{F}}}_{N}})\quad\forall\,N\in{\mathfrak{N}},\,M_{n}\prec N.

By Lemma
8.20
, we have

\langle V_{n},X_{n}-Y\rangle_{\mathcal{X}}+[\Psi,\iota^{2}_{Y,X_{n}}]_{r,0}\leq\lambda|X_{n}-Y|_{\mathcal{X}}^{2}\quad\forall n\in\mathbb{N}.

Passing to the liminf as
n\to+\infty
and using Theorem
2.13
(5) we obtain (
8.49
).

Let now
X\in{\mathcal{C}_{N}}\subset\mathcal{D}_{N}
for some
N\in{\mathfrak{N}}
, and observe that, since
\mathcal{D}_{N}
is open by Lemma
8.12
, then
\boldsymbol{J}_{\tau}X\in\mathcal{D}_{N}
for
0<\tau<1/\lambda^{+}
sufficiently small, since
\boldsymbol{J}_{\tau}X\to X
as
\tau\downarrow 0
, where
\boldsymbol{J}_{\tau}
is the resolvent of
\hat{\boldsymbol{F}}
. We can thus apply (
8.28
) and get

\frac{1}{\tau}\langle\boldsymbol{J}_{\tau}X-X,\boldsymbol{J}_{\tau}X-X\rangle_{\mathcal{X}}+[\Phi,\iota^{2}_{X,\boldsymbol{J}_{\tau}X}]_{r,0}\leq\lambda|X-J_{\tau}X|_{\mathcal{X}}^{2}.

Since we have shown that
\hat{\boldsymbol{F}}
is an invariant maximal
\lambda
-dissipative operator, by Theorem
3.4
, there exists a Lipschitz function
f
such that
\boldsymbol{J}_{\tau}X=f\circ X
; thus
\iota^{2}_{X,\boldsymbol{J}_{\tau}X}
is concentrated on a map so that, by Theorem
2.13
(4), we have

[\Phi,\iota^{2}_{X,\boldsymbol{J}_{\tau}X}]_{r,0}=\langle\boldsymbol{b}_{\Phi},X-\boldsymbol{J}_{\tau}X\rangle_{\mathcal{X}}.

We hence get

\frac{1}{\tau}|\boldsymbol{J}_{\tau}X-X|_{\mathcal{X}}^{2}\leq|X-\boldsymbol{J}_{\tau}X|_{\mathcal{X}}\,\bigg(|\boldsymbol{b}_{\Phi}|+\lambda|X-\boldsymbol{J}_{\tau}X|_{\mathcal{X}}\bigg);

dividing by
|X-\boldsymbol{J}_{\tau}X|_{\mathcal{X}}
and passing to the limit as
\tau\downarrow 0
, we obtain (
8.50
) (cf. Theorem
A.3
(5)).

∎

We conclude this section with the proofs of Theorems
8.5
.
and
8.6

Proof of Theorem
8.5
.

Let us first check that
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}\subset\hat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}_{\infty}
.
It is sufficient to prove that if
\mu\in\mathrm{C}_{M}
and
M\mid N
,
M,N\in{\mathfrak{N}}
,
then every element
\Phi=(\boldsymbol{i}_{\mathsf{X}},\boldsymbol{f})_{\sharp}\mu\in{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}[\mu]

belongs to
\hat{{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}}_{N}[\mu]
.
Adopting a Lagrangian viewpoint (thanks to Theorem
8.26
), if
X\in{\mathcal{C}_{M}}

we want to show that
V=\boldsymbol{f}\circ X
belongs to
{\hat{{\boldsymbol{F}}}_{N}}X
.
This follows easily by
the fact that
{\mathcal{C}_{M}}\subset\overline{\mathcal{D}_{N}}
,
the
\lambda
-dissipativity of
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}

and Proposition
8.17
.
Being
\hat{{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}}_{\infty}
totally
\lambda
-dissipative, the inclusion
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}\subset\hat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}_{\infty}
shows that
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
is totally
\lambda
-dissipative and
\hat{{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}}_{\infty}
is a totally
\lambda
-dissipative extension of
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
.
By construction,
\hat{{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}}
is a maximal totally
\lambda
-dissipative extension of
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
and its uniqueness follows as a particular case of Theorem
8.6
.
The characterization in (
8.11
) follows by definition of
\hat{{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}}_{\infty}
and Proposition
8.17
.
Let us now check the second statement, under the assumptions that
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
is also single-valued and demicontinuous in
\mathrm{C}_{N}
. By Corollary
8.22
, we know that, on each
{\mathcal{C}_{N}}
, the minimal selection
\hat{\boldsymbol{F}}{\vphantom{{\boldsymbol{F}}}}^{\circ}
is a subset of
{\hat{{\boldsymbol{F}}}_{N}}
and therefore, by Corollary
8.18
,
\hat{\boldsymbol{F}}{\vphantom{{\boldsymbol{F}}}}^{\circ}X={\boldsymbol{F}}X
for every
X\in{\mathcal{C}_{\infty}}
.

∎

Proof of Theorem
8.6
.

Let
\hat{{\boldsymbol{B}}}
be a law invariant maximal
\lambda
-dissipative extension of the Lagrangian representation
{\boldsymbol{B}}
of
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
with domain included in the convex set
\overline{{\mathcal{C}_{\infty}}}
, whose existence is given by Theorem
3.12
. Notice that
\iota^{2}(\hat{{\boldsymbol{B}}})
is maximal totally
\lambda
-dissipative and contains
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
so that it also contains
\operatorname{bar}\left({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}\right)
by Theorem
3.18
. We deduce that
\hat{{\boldsymbol{B}}}
is the Lagrangian representation of a
\lambda
-dissipative extension of
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}\cup\operatorname{bar}\left({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}\right)
.

We want to show that
\hat{{\boldsymbol{B}}}\subset\hat{\boldsymbol{F}}
and we split the argument in a few steps.

Claim 1:
for every
Y\in\mathrm{D}(\hat{{\boldsymbol{B}}})\cap\Big(\bigcup_{N\in{\mathfrak{N}}}\overline{\mathcal{D}_{N}}\Big)

and
W\in\hat{{\boldsymbol{B}}}Y
, we have
W\in\hat{\boldsymbol{F}}Y.

Let
Y
and
W
be as above and let
X\in\mathrm{D}(\hat{{\boldsymbol{F}}}_{\infty})
. We can find some
M,L\in{\mathfrak{N}}
such that
Y\in\mathrm{D}(\hat{{\boldsymbol{B}}})\cap\overline{\mathcal{D}_{M}}
and
X\in\mathrm{D}({\hat{{\boldsymbol{F}}}_{L}})
. In particular
Y\in\mathrm{D}(\hat{{\boldsymbol{B}}})\cap\overline{\mathcal{D}_{N}}
and
X\in\mathrm{D}({\hat{{\boldsymbol{F}}}_{N}})
for every
N\in{\mathfrak{N}}
such that
ML\mid N
(cf. Corollary
8.22
and Lemma
8.12
). By (
8.35
) we have

(8.51)

\langle X-Y,\hat{\boldsymbol{F}}{\vphantom{{\boldsymbol{F}}}}^{\circ}X-\Pi_{N}W\rangle_{\mathcal{X}}\leq\lambda|X-Y|_{\mathcal{X}}^{2}\quad\text{for every $N\in{\mathfrak{N}}$ such that $ML\mid N$}.

Passing to the limit as
N\to\infty
in
{\mathfrak{N}}
and using (
8.48
) we deduce that

(Y,W)\in\hat{\boldsymbol{F}}.

Claim 2:

\mathrm{D}(\hat{{\boldsymbol{B}}})\cap\Big(\bigcup_{N\in{\mathfrak{N}}}\overline{\mathcal{D}_{N}}\Big)=\mathrm{D}(\hat{{\boldsymbol{B}}})\cap\mathcal{X}_{\infty}
.

It is sufficient to prove that

\mathrm{D}(\hat{{\boldsymbol{B}}})\cap\overline{\mathcal{D}_{N}}=\mathrm{D}(\hat{{\boldsymbol{B}}})\cap\mathcal{X}_{N}
for every
N\in{\mathfrak{N}}

and since

\overline{\mathcal{D}_{N}}\subset\mathcal{X}_{N}

it is sufficient to prove the inclusion

(8.52)

\mathrm{D}(\hat{{\boldsymbol{B}}})\cap\mathcal{X}_{N}\subset\overline{\mathcal{D}_{N}}.

We first show that

(8.53)

\overline{\mathrm{D}(\hat{\boldsymbol{F}})}\cap\mathcal{X}_{N}\subset\overline{\mathcal{D}_{N}}.

Indeed, by Proposition
8.21
and Corollary
8.24
, for every

X\in\overline{\mathrm{D}(\hat{\boldsymbol{F}})}\cap\mathcal{X}_{N}
and
\tau>0
,

\boldsymbol{J}_{\tau}X
belongs to
\mathrm{D}({\hat{{\boldsymbol{F}}}_{N}})\subset\overline{\mathcal{D}_{N}}
: passing to the limit as
\tau\downarrow 0
, since
X\in\overline{\mathrm{D}(\hat{\boldsymbol{F}})}
, we conclude that
X

belongs to
\overline{\mathcal{D}_{N}}
as well, thus proving (
8.53
). Since
\mathrm{D}(\hat{{\boldsymbol{B}}})\subset\overline{\mathcal{D}_{\infty}}=\overline{\mathrm{D}(\hat{\boldsymbol{F}})}
, by (
8.53
), we get

\mathrm{D}(\hat{{\boldsymbol{B}}})\cap\mathcal{X}_{N}\subset\overline{\mathrm{D}(\hat{\boldsymbol{F}})}\cap\mathcal{X}_{N}\subset\overline{\mathcal{D}_{N}}
, which shows (
8.52
).

Claim 3:
\hat{\boldsymbol{B}}\subset\hat{\boldsymbol{F}}.

Setting
\hat{\boldsymbol{B}}_{0}:=\hat{\boldsymbol{B}}\cap(\mathcal{X}_{\infty}\times\mathcal{X})
,
the first two claims yield

\hat{\boldsymbol{B}}_{0}\subset\hat{\boldsymbol{F}}
.
On the other hand, the maximal
\lambda
-dissipativity and the law invariance of
\hat{\boldsymbol{B}}
show (cf. Theorem
3.4
) that

\mathcal{X}_{\infty}
is invariant under the action of the resolvent of
\hat{\boldsymbol{B}}
;
since
\mathcal{X}_{\infty}
is also dense in
\mathcal{X}
, we can apply
(
A.24
) of Lemma
A.15
obtaining that
\hat{\boldsymbol{B}}

coincides with the strong closure of
\hat{\boldsymbol{B}}_{0}
in
\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{X}
which is also contained
in
\hat{\boldsymbol{F}}
, since
\hat{\boldsymbol{F}}
is maximal
\lambda
-dissipative.

∎

8.4.
Examples and applications

Let us suppose that
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
satisfies the assumptions of Theorem

8.4
and let
\hat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
be the maximal
totally
\lambda
-dissipative MPVF induced by
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}.

Since
\mathrm{C}
is dense in
\mathrm{D}(\hat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})
, if we characterize the Lagrangian solutions to the flow generated by
\hat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}

starting from every measure of
\mathrm{C}
, we can then obtain
all the other evolutions by approximation.

We want to show that the evolution of every measure in
the core
\mathrm{C}
can be characterized in a metric way, involving only
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}.

Theorem 8.27
.

Under the assumptions of Theorem
8.4
, let
\mu_{0}\in\overline{\mathrm{C}_{N}}

for some
N\in{\mathfrak{N}}
and let

\mu:[0,+\infty)\to\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{X})
be a continuous curve starting from
\mu_{0}
.
The following properties are equivalent:

(1)

\mu
is a Lagrangian solution of the flow generated by
\hat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
(cf. Definition
4.1
);

(2)

\mu

is locally absolutely continuous in
(0,+\infty)
, it
takes values
in
\overline{\mathrm{C}_{N}}
, in particular

\mu_{t}\in\mathcal{P}_{f,N}(\mathsf{X})
for every
t\geq 0
, and

\mu
is a
\lambda
-
EVI
solution
for the restriction of
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
to
\mathrm{C}_{N}
;

(3)

\mu
is locally absolutely continuous
in
[0,+\infty)
and locally Lipschitz continuous in

(0,+\infty)
,
there exists a constant
C>0
such that
the Wasserstein velocity field
\boldsymbol{v}
of
\mu
(cf. Theorem
2.11
)
satisfies

(8.54)

I_{\lambda}(t)\Big(\int|\boldsymbol{v}_{t}|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\mu_{t}\Big)^{1/2}\leq C\quad\text{a.e.\penalty\ in }(0,1),

\mu_{t}\in\mathrm{D}(\hat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}_{N})\subset\mathrm{D}(\hat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})

for every
t>0
, and it holds

(8.55)

\boldsymbol{v}_{t}=\hat{\boldsymbol{f}}{\vphantom{\boldsymbol{f}}}^{\circ}[\mu_{t}]\quad\text{for $\mathscr{L}^{1}$-a.e.\penalty\ $t>0$},

where
\hat{\boldsymbol{f}}{\vphantom{\boldsymbol{f}}}^{\circ}
is the minimal selection map
induced by
(\hat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}\vphantom{{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}})^{\circ}
as in Theorem
3.20
and
I_{\lambda}(t)
is as in (
A.11
).

Proof.

We split the proof in various steps.

Claim 1.
(1)
\Leftrightarrow
(3)

To see that (3) implies (1), it is sufficient to notice that by (
8.55
)
\mu
satisfies the inclusion
(\boldsymbol{i}_{\mathsf{X}},\boldsymbol{v}_{t})_{\sharp}\mu_{t}\in\hat{{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}}[\mu_{t}]
for a.e.
t>0
, so that it is clearly a
\lambda
-EVI solution for
\hat{{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}}
(see also
[
27
, Theorem 5.4(1)]
); by Theorem
4.5
we get that
\mu
is a Lagrangian solution of the flow generated by
\hat{{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}}
. We are left to check that (1) implies (3).

Since
\mu_{0}\in\overline{\mathrm{C}_{N}}
, we can represent
\mu_{0}
as
\iota(X_{0})

for some
X_{0}\in\overline{{\mathcal{C}_{N}}}=\overline{\mathcal{D}_{N}}=\overline{\mathrm{D}(\hat{\boldsymbol{F}}_{N})}
(cf. Lemma
8.12
and Proposition
8.15
);
if
(\boldsymbol{S}_{t})_{t\geq 0}
is the semigroup generated by
\hat{\boldsymbol{F}}
we have

\mu_{t}=\iota(X_{t})
where
X_{t}=\boldsymbol{S}_{t}X_{0}
.

By Corollary
8.24
(1), the restriction of the resolvent
\boldsymbol{J}_{\tau}

of
\hat{\boldsymbol{F}}
to
\mathcal{X}_{N}
coincides with the resolvent of
\hat{\boldsymbol{F}}_{N}
: using the exponential formula (cf. Theorem
A.6
), we obtain that the restriction of
the semigroup
(\boldsymbol{S}_{t})_{t\geq 0}

to
\overline{\mathcal{D}_{N}}
coincides with the semigroup generated by
\hat{\boldsymbol{F}}_{N}.

Since the interior of the domain of
\hat{\boldsymbol{F}}_{N}
in
\mathcal{X}_{N}
is not empty (cf. Proposition
8.15
and Lemma
8.12
), we can
apply Theorem
A.7
obtaining that

\boldsymbol{S}_{t}X_{0}
is locally absolutely continuous in
[0,+\infty)
, it is locally Lipschitz in
(0,+\infty)
, it satisfies
I_{\lambda}(t)|\dot{X}_{t}|_{\mathcal{X}}\leq C
in
(0,1)

for a suitable constant
C
(so that we get (
8.54
)), it belongs to
\mathrm{D}(\hat{{\boldsymbol{F}}}_{N})
for every
t>0
,
and it solves the equation

\dot{X}_{t}=(\hat{{\boldsymbol{F}}}_{N})^{\circ}X_{t}\quad\text{for $\mathscr{L}^{1}$-a.e.\penalty\ $t>0$}.

Corollary
8.24
(2) then shows that
\dot{X}_{t}=(\hat{{\boldsymbol{F}}})^{\circ}X_{t}

as well, so that we get (
4.2
), with
\hat{\boldsymbol{f}}{\vphantom{\boldsymbol{f}}}^{\circ}
in place of
\boldsymbol{f}^{\circ}
,
and therefore (
8.55
): indeed the tangent space
\Tan_{\mu_{t}}\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{X})
(cf. Theorem
2.11
and
[
2
, Theorem 8.3.1, Propositions 8.4.5, 8.4.6]
) coincides with
L^{2}(\mathsf{X},\mu_{t};\mathsf{X})
being
\supp(\mu_{t})
of finite cardinality.

Claim 2:
(3)
\Rightarrow
(2)

We know that solves the continuity equation with velocity field
\boldsymbol{v}_{t}=\hat{\boldsymbol{f}}{\vphantom{\boldsymbol{f}}}^{\circ}[\mu_{t}]

so that, by Corollary
8.24
(2), we have

(\boldsymbol{i}_{\mathsf{X}},\boldsymbol{v}_{t})_{\sharp}\mu_{t}\in\hat{{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}}_{N}
.
Let
\Phi\in{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
with
\nu:=\mathsf{x}_{\sharp}\Phi\in\mathrm{C}_{N}
and let
t\in A(\mu)\subset[0,+\infty)
, where
A(\mu)
is the full
\mathscr{L}^{1}
-measure set given by Theorem
2.13
(6a). By Theorem
8.4
(2) we have that

(8.56)

\int_{\mathsf{X}\times\mathsf{X}}\langle\boldsymbol{v}_{t}(x),x-y\rangle\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{\mu}_{t}(x,y)\leq\int_{\mathsf{X}\times\mathsf{X}}\left(-\langle\boldsymbol{b}_{\Phi}(y),y-x\rangle+\lambda|x-y|^{2}\right)\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{\mu}_{t}(x,y)

for every
\boldsymbol{\mu}_{t}\in\Gamma_{f,N}(\mu_{t},\nu)
. Choosing
\boldsymbol{\mu}_{t}
optimal, by Theorem
2.13
(6a) we have that

\frac{\,\mathrm{d}}{\,\mathrm{d}t}\frac{1}{2}W_{2}^{2}(\mu_{t},\nu)=\left[(\boldsymbol{i}_{\mathsf{X}},\boldsymbol{v}_{t})_{\sharp}\mu_{t},\nu\right]_{r}\leq\int_{\mathsf{X}\times\mathsf{X}}\langle\boldsymbol{v}_{t}(x),x-y\rangle\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{\mu}_{t}(x,y).

On the other hand, since
\boldsymbol{\mu}_{t}
is concentrated on a map w.r.t.
\nu
, (
2.12
) gives that

\int_{\mathsf{X}\times\mathsf{X}}\langle\boldsymbol{b}_{\Phi}(y),y-x\rangle\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{\mu}_{t}(x,y)=[\Phi,{\mathsf{s}}_{\sharp}\boldsymbol{\mu}_{t}]_{r,0},

where
\mathsf{s}
is as in (
2.3
).
So that, using (
8.56
), we obtain that

\frac{\,\mathrm{d}}{\,\mathrm{d}t}\frac{1}{2}W_{2}^{2}(\mu_{t},\nu)\leq-[\Phi,{\mathsf{s}}_{\sharp}\boldsymbol{\mu}_{t}]_{r,0}+\lambda W_{2}^{2}(\mu_{t},\nu).

By passing to the supremum w.r.t.
\boldsymbol{\mu}_{t}\in\Gamma_{o}(\mu_{t},\nu)
and recalling Theorem
2.13
(2), we finally obtain

\frac{\,\mathrm{d}}{\,\mathrm{d}t}\frac{1}{2}W_{2}^{2}(\mu_{t},\nu)\leq-\left[\Phi,\mu_{t}\right]_{r}+\lambda W_{2}^{2}(\mu_{t},\nu);

this implies that
\mu
is a
\lambda
-EVI solution for the restriction of
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
to
\mathrm{C}_{N}
.

Claim 3.
(2)
\Rightarrow
(1)

We apply
[
27
, Lemma 5.3, (5.5a)]

obtaining that for every
t
in a set
A(\mu)\subset[0,+\infty)
of full
\mathscr{L}^{1}
-measure, every
\nu\in\mathrm{C}_{N}
and
\Phi\in{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}[\nu]
we have

(8.57)

\big[(\boldsymbol{i}_{\mathsf{X}},\boldsymbol{v}_{t})_{\sharp}\mu_{t},\nu\big]_{r}+\big[\Phi,\mu_{t}\big]_{r}\leq\lambda W_{2}^{2}(\mu_{t},\nu),

where
\boldsymbol{v}_{t}
is the Wasserstein velocity field of
\mu
.
Let
t\in A(\mu)
be fixed; restricting (
8.57
)
to all the measures
\nu
for which
\Gamma_{o}(\mu_{t},\nu)
contains
a unique element (denoted by
\boldsymbol{\mu}
),
Theorem
2.13
(4) yields

\displaystyle\big[(\boldsymbol{i}_{\mathsf{X}},\boldsymbol{v}_{t})_{\sharp}\mu_{t},\nu\big]_{r}

\displaystyle=\int\langle\boldsymbol{v}_{t}(x_{0}),x_{0}-x_{1}\rangle\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{\mu}(x_{0},x_{1}),

\displaystyle\big[\Phi,\mu_{t}\big]_{r}

\displaystyle=\int\langle\boldsymbol{b}_{\Phi}(x_{1}),x_{1}-x_{0}\rangle\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{\mu}(x_{0},x_{1}).

Proposition
8.17
and (
8.57
) then yield that
(\boldsymbol{i}_{\mathsf{X}},\boldsymbol{v}_{t})_{\sharp}\mu_{t}\in\hat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}_{N}[\mu_{t}].

Let us now consider the Lagrangian solution
\tilde{\mu}_{t}:=S_{t}(\mu_{0})

of the flow driven by
\hat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
.
By the first Claim,
we know that
\tilde{\mu}
is absolutely continuous,

\tilde{\mu}_{t}\in\mathrm{D}(\hat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}_{N})\subset\mathrm{D}(\hat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})\cap\overline{\mathrm{C}_{N}}
for
t>0
,
and satisfies (
8.55
).

We can then compute the derivative of
W_{2}^{2}(\mu_{t},\tilde{\mu}_{t})
:
for
\mathscr{L}^{1}
-a.e.
t>0
, we can choose an arbitrary
\boldsymbol{\mu}_{t}\in\Gamma_{o}(\mu_{t},\tilde{\mu}_{t})
, in particular
a coupling in
\mathcal{P}_{f,N}(\mathsf{X}\times\mathsf{X})
, obtaining, by Theorem
2.13
(6b),

\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\frac{1}{2}W_{2}^{2}(\mu_{t},\tilde{\mu}_{t})=\int\langle\boldsymbol{v}_{t}(x_{0})-\hat{\boldsymbol{f}}{\vphantom{\boldsymbol{f}}}^{\circ}[\tilde{\mu}_{t}](x_{1}),x_{0}-x_{1}\rangle\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{\mu}_{t}(x_{0},x_{1})\leq\lambda W_{2}^{2}(\mu_{t},\nu)

by
\lambda
-dissipativity of
\hat{{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}}_{N}
, since
(\boldsymbol{i}_{\mathsf{X}},\hat{\boldsymbol{f}}{\vphantom{\boldsymbol{f}}}^{\circ}[\tilde{\mu}_{t}])_{\sharp}\tilde{\mu}_{t}\in\hat{{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}}_{N}
by Corollary
8.24
(2). We thus have that
\mu_{t}=\tilde{\mu}_{t}
for every
t\geq 0

and
\boldsymbol{v}_{t}=\hat{\boldsymbol{f}}{\vphantom{\boldsymbol{f}}}^{\circ}[\mu_{t}]
.
∎

Remark 8.28
.

The example of
\frac{1}{2}
-dissipative PVF
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
, with
\mathsf{X}=\mathbb{R}
discussed in Remark
4.3
provides also a counterexample to the validity of the above Theorem
8.27
in case
\dim(\mathsf{X})=1
and
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
is not totally
\frac{1}{2}
-dissipative: the evolutions driven by
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
and
\hat{{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}}
should coincide by Theorem
8.27
, but this is impossible since
\hat{{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}}
is maximal totally
\frac{1}{2}
-dissipative and the evolution driven by
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
splits mass, a contradiction with Theorem
4.2
.

We can now fully justify the example given in the Introduction.

Example 8.29
.

Assume that
\dim\mathsf{X}\geq 2
and that
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
is a
\lambda
-dissipative
single-valued deterministic PVF induced by a map
\boldsymbol{f}:\mathcal{S}\left(\mathsf{X},\mathrm{C}\right)\to\mathsf{X}
, where
\mathrm{C}
is a core as in Definition
8.1
.
This means that

\boldsymbol{f}
induces a vector field
\boldsymbol{f}^{N}:\mathsf{C}_{N}\to\mathsf{X}^{N}

defined on
\mathsf{C}_{N}:=\mathscr{I}^{-1}_{N}({\mathcal{C}_{N}})
(where
{\mathcal{C}_{N}}
is as in (
8.18
)),
which is an open subset of
\mathsf{X}^{N}
, whose vectors
have distinct coordinates:
for every
{\boldsymbol{x}}=(x_{1},\dots,x_{N})\in\mathsf{C}_{N}
we have

\boldsymbol{f}^{N}({\boldsymbol{x}}):=(\boldsymbol{f}(x_{n},\iota\circ\mathscr{I}_{N}({\boldsymbol{x}})))_{n=1,\dots,N}.

Clearly
\boldsymbol{f}^{N}
is invariant with respect to permutations, in the sense that
\boldsymbol{f}^{N}(\sigma{\boldsymbol{x}})=\sigma\boldsymbol{f}^{N}({\boldsymbol{x}})
, for every
{\boldsymbol{x}}\in\mathsf{C}_{N}
and every
\sigma\in{\mathrm{Sym}(I_{N})}
.
If
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
is demicontinuous in
\mathrm{C}_{N}
,

\boldsymbol{f}^{N}
is demicontinuous (i.e. strongly-weakly continuous)
in
\mathsf{C}_{N}
.

The previous theorem shows that
starting from

\mu^{N}=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{n=1}^{N}\delta_{x^{N}_{n}}\in\mathrm{C}_{N}

the evolution
\mu^{N}_{t}=S_{t}(\mu^{N})
, at least for a short time,
has the form

\mu^{N}_{t}=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{n=1}^{N}\delta_{x^{N}_{n}(t)}\quad\text{where}\quad\dot{x}^{N}_{n}(t)=\boldsymbol{f}^{N}_{n}({\boldsymbol{x}}^{N}(t)).

Such an evolution admits a unique extension (see Theorem
8.5
) which in fact corresponds to the unique
maximal (and invariant by permutation) extension
of the
\lambda
-dissipative vector field

\boldsymbol{f}^{N}
to
\overline{\mathsf{C}_{N}}
.
It is then possible to follow the path of
each single particle by using the Lagrangian flow
starting from
\mu_{0}\in\mathrm{C}
and defining
N
locally Lipschitz curves

x^{N}_{n}(t)=\boldsymbol{s}_{t}(x^{N}_{n},\mu_{0})
.
If now
\mu^{N}\to\mu_{0}
as
N\to\infty

with a uniform control of the initial velocities,
i.e.

\sup_{N}\frac{1}{N}\sum_{n=1}^{N}|\boldsymbol{f}^{N}_{n}({\boldsymbol{x}}^{N})|^{2}<\infty,

then
the measures

\mu^{N}_{t}
will converge to
\mu_{t}=S_{t}(\mu_{0})

for every
t\geq 0
in
\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{X})
and,
by Theorem
4.9
,
the measures carried on the discrete trajectories

\frac{1}{N}\sum_{n=1}^{N}\delta_{x^{N}_{n}(\cdot)}\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathrm{C}([0,T];\mathsf{X}))

will converge to
\mathrm{s}_{\sharp}\mu_{0}

where
\mathrm{s}
is the Lagrangian map
starting from
\mu_{0}
as in (
4.14
).

Example 8.30

(A kinetic model of collective motion)
.

Consider
in the phase space
\mathsf{X}:=\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}

the evolution
of
N
-particles characterized
by position-velocity coordinates

(x_{n},v_{n})\in\mathsf{X}
,
n=1,\dots,N
, satisfying
the system

[
30
,
19
]

(8.58)

\left\{\begin{aligned} \dot{x}_{n}(t)&=v_{n}(t),\\
\dot{v}_{n}(t)&=(\alpha-\beta|v_{n}(t)|^{2})v_{n}(t)+\frac{1}{N}\sum_{m=1}^{N}\boldsymbol{h}(x_{n}(t)-x_{m}(t)),\end{aligned}\right.

with
\alpha\geq 0,\beta>0

and
\boldsymbol{h}:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
a given Lipschitz vector field.
For a given
\mu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{X})

we can consider the
lower semicontinuous and

(-\alpha)
-totally convex functional

\phi:\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{X})\to(-\infty,+\infty]

(8.59)

\phi(\mu):=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\Big(\frac{\beta}{4}|v|^{4}-\frac{\alpha}{2}|v|^{2}\Big)\,\mathrm{d}\mu(x,v),

whose proper domain is

\mathrm{D}(\phi):=\displaystyle\Big\{\mu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{X}):\int|v|^{4}\,\mathrm{d}\mu(x,v)<\infty\Big\}
.
The minimal selection of

-\boldsymbol{\partial}_{\mathrm{t}}\phi(\mu)

is given by
(\boldsymbol{i}_{\mathsf{X}},\boldsymbol{g})_{\sharp}\mu
with

(8.60)

\boldsymbol{g}(x,v;\mu):=\Big(0,(\alpha-\beta|v|^{2})v\Big)

with proper domain
\mathrm{D}(\boldsymbol{\partial}_{\mathrm{t}}\phi)=\displaystyle\Big\{\mu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{X}):\int|v|^{6}\,\mathrm{d}\mu(x,v)<\infty\Big\}.

We can also define the
deterministic PVF induced as in (
7.10
) by

\boldsymbol{h}:\mathcal{S}(\mathsf{X})\to\mathsf{X}

(8.61)

\boldsymbol{h}(x,v;\mu):=\Big(v,\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\boldsymbol{h}(x-y)\,\mathrm{d}\mu(y,w)\Big).

It is easy to check that
a collection of
N
particles

(x_{n}(t),v_{n}(t))
satisfies
(
8.58
) if and only if
the measure

\mu_{t}=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{n=1}^{N}\delta_{(x_{n}(t),v_{n}(t))}

is a Lagrangian solution
of the system (
4.10
)
associated with the
deterministic PVF

(8.62)

\boldsymbol{f}(x,v;\mu):=\boldsymbol{g}(x,v;\mu)+\boldsymbol{h}(x,v;\mu),\quad\mu\in\mathrm{D}(\boldsymbol{\partial}_{\mathrm{t}}\phi).

Since the Lagrangian representation
of
\boldsymbol{f}
corresponds to the sum
of a maximal
\alpha
-dissipative
operator
(the subdifferential of

\psi=\phi\circ\iota
)
and a Lipschitz operator,
it is maximal
\alpha
-dissipative
thanks to
[
17
, Lemma 2.4, Chapter II]
,
so that the deterministic PVF associated
with (
8.62
)
is totally
\alpha
-dissipative
and we can apply all the results of
Section
4
.

In the following we give an example of totally dissipative MPVF
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
having a core contained in its domain.

Example 8.31
.

Let
W:\mathsf{X}\to(-\infty,+\infty]
be a proper, lower semicontinuous, even and convex function and denote by
\mathrm{D}(W)
its proper domain. Let
\boldsymbol{B}\subset\mathsf{X}\times\mathsf{X}
be a maximal dissipative set (see Appendix
A
) and suppose that
0\in\operatorname{int}\left(\mathrm{D}(W)\right)
and
\operatorname{int}\left(\mathrm{D}(\boldsymbol{B})\right)\neq\emptyset
. Possible examples of
W
and
\boldsymbol{B}
are given by the indicator of a convex set in
\mathsf{X}
(or a function diverging at the boundary of a convex set) and the gradient of a convex function in
\mathsf{X}
(or its sum with a linear and antisymmetric function) respectively.
Let
\boldsymbol{u}_{W}
be an odd single-valued measurable selection of
\partial W
and let
\boldsymbol{v}_{\boldsymbol{B}}
be an arbitrary single-valued selection of
\boldsymbol{B}
. We define the set

E:=\left\{\mu\in\mathcal{P}_{c}(\mathsf{X})\,:\,\supp\mu\subset\operatorname{int}\left(\mathrm{D}(\boldsymbol{B})\right),\,\supp\mu-\supp\mu\subset\operatorname{int}\left(\mathrm{D}(W)\right)\right\},

where
\mathcal{P}_{c}(\mathsf{X})
denotes the subset of measures in
\mathcal{P}(\mathsf{X})
with compact support.
We define the single-valued probability vector field
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
as follows:

{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}[\mu]:=\begin{cases}\left(\boldsymbol{i}_{\mathsf{X}},-(\boldsymbol{u}_{W}\ast\mu)+\boldsymbol{v}_{\boldsymbol{B}}\right)_{\sharp}\mu,&\textrm{if }\mu\in E\\
\emptyset&\textrm{otherwise}\end{cases},\qquad\mu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{X}).

Notice that the convolution between
\boldsymbol{u}_{W}
and
\mu
is well posed since
\mu
is discrete; moreover
(\boldsymbol{u}_{W}\ast\mu)+\boldsymbol{v}_{\boldsymbol{B}}\in L^{2}(\mathsf{X},\mu;\mathsf{X})
if
\mu\in E
; indeed
\boldsymbol{v}_{\boldsymbol{B}}
and
\boldsymbol{u}_{W}
are both locally bounded in the interior of the respective domains (see Corollary
A.4
and Theorem
A.3
(3) and recall that
\operatorname{int}\left(\mathrm{D}(\partial W)\right)=\operatorname{int}\left(\mathrm{D}(W)\right)
), so that
\mathrm{D}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})=E
and
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}\subset\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{T\kern-1.5ptX})
. It is not difficult to check that
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
is totally dissipative: for every
\boldsymbol{\gamma}\in\Gamma(\mu,\nu)
and every
\mu,\nu\in E
,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathsf{X}\times\mathsf{X}}W(y_{1}-y_{2})\,\mathrm{d}(\nu\otimes\nu)(y_{1},y_{2})-\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathsf{X}\times\mathsf{X}}W(x_{1}-x_{2})\,\mathrm{d}(\mu\otimes\mu)(x_{1},x_{2})

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathsf{X}^{4}}\langle\boldsymbol{u}_{W}(x_{1}-x_{2}),(y_{1}-y_{2})-(x_{1}-x_{2})\rangle\,\mathrm{d}(\boldsymbol{\gamma}\otimes\boldsymbol{\gamma})(x_{1},y_{1},x_{2},y_{2})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathsf{X}^{3}}\langle\boldsymbol{u}_{W}(x_{1}-x_{2}),y_{1}-x_{1}\rangle\,\mathrm{d}\mu(x_{2})\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{\gamma}(x_{1},y_{1})

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathsf{X}^{3}}\langle\boldsymbol{u}_{W}(x_{2}-x_{1}),y_{2}-x_{2}\rangle\,\mathrm{d}\mu(x_{1})\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{\gamma}(x_{2},y_{2})

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathsf{X}\times\mathsf{X}}\langle(\boldsymbol{u}_{W}\ast\mu)(x),y-x\rangle\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{\gamma}(x,y),

where we have used Fubini’s theorem and the fact that
\boldsymbol{u}_{W}
is odd. This immediately gives that

(8.63)

\int_{\mathsf{X}\times\mathsf{X}}\langle(-\boldsymbol{u}_{W}\ast\mu)(x)+(\boldsymbol{u}_{W}\ast\nu)(y),x-y\rangle\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{\gamma}(x,y)\leq 0.

Thus

\displaystyle\int_{\mathsf{X}\times\mathsf{X}}\langle-(\boldsymbol{u}_{W}\ast\mu)(x)+\boldsymbol{v}_{\boldsymbol{B}}(x)+(\boldsymbol{u}_{W}\ast\nu)(y)-\boldsymbol{v}_{\boldsymbol{B}}(y),x-y\rangle\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{\gamma}(x,y)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathsf{X}\times\mathsf{X}}\langle(-\boldsymbol{u}_{W}\ast\mu)(x)+(\boldsymbol{u}_{W}\ast\nu)(y),x-y\rangle\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{\gamma}(x,y)

\displaystyle\quad+\int_{\mathsf{X}\times\mathsf{X}}\langle\boldsymbol{v}_{\boldsymbol{B}}(x)-\boldsymbol{v}_{\boldsymbol{B}}(y),x-y\rangle\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{\gamma}(x,y)

\displaystyle\leq 0,

where we have used (
8.63
) and the dissipativity of
\boldsymbol{B}
.

Given any unbounded directed subset
{\mathfrak{N}}\subset\mathbb{N}
, we can define
\mathrm{D}
as

\mathrm{D}:=\left\{\mu\in\mathcal{P}_{f,{\mathfrak{N}}}(\mathsf{X})\,:\,\supp\mu\subset\operatorname{int}\left(\mathrm{D}(\boldsymbol{B})\right),\,\supp\mu-\supp\mu\subset\operatorname{int}\left(\mathrm{D}(W)\right)\right\}.

Trivially, being
\mathrm{D}\subset\mathcal{P}_{c}(\mathsf{X})
, then

\mathrm{D}\subset\mathrm{D}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})\cap\mathcal{P}_{f,{\mathfrak{N}}}(\mathsf{X})
. Moreover, for any

N\in{\mathfrak{N}}
, the set
\mathrm{D}\cap\mathcal{P}_{f,N}(\mathsf{X})
is open in

\mathcal{P}_{f,N}(\mathsf{X})
and convex along couplings in
\mathcal{P}_{f,N}(\mathsf{X}\times\mathsf{X})
, being both
\operatorname{int}\left(\mathrm{D}(\partial W)\right)
and

\operatorname{int}\left(\mathrm{D}(\boldsymbol{B})\right)
convex sets (see Corollary
A.4
and
Theorem
A.3
(3)). Thus, setting
\mathrm{C}:=\mathrm{D}\cap\mathcal{P}_{\#{\mathfrak{N}}}(\mathsf{X})
and recalling
Lemma
8.2
, then Definition
8.1
is satisfied for
\mathrm{C}
.

Example 8.32
.

Assume
\dim\mathsf{X}\geq 2
.
Let
\mathsf{U}\subset\mathsf{X}
be an open convex
subset of
\mathsf{X}
containing
0

(e.g. an open ball of radious
r>0

centered at
0
)
and let
\mathrm{A}
be the set
of all measures
\mu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{X})
such that

\supp\mu-\int x\,\mathrm{d}\mu(x)\subset\mathsf{U}.

In the case
\mathsf{U}
is an open ball,
\mathrm{A}

imposes the constraint that
the support of
\mu
is contained in the ball with same
radious as
\mathsf{U}
centered at the barycenter of
\mu
.
We can then consider the
set
\mathrm{D}:=\bigcup_{N\in\mathbb{N}}(\mathrm{A}\cap\mathcal{P}_{f,N})

and inducing
a corresponding core
\mathrm{C}
as in Lemma

8.2
.

Let

\boldsymbol{f}:\mathcal{S}\left(\mathsf{X}\right)\to\mathsf{X}

be a map as in Theorem
7.6

inducing a
\lambda
-dissipative demicontinuous
PVF
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
by (
7.10
).

The restriction of
\boldsymbol{f}
to
\mathcal{S}\left(\mathsf{X},\mathrm{C}\right)

induces a unique maximal totally
\lambda
-dissipative
MPVF
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}^{\prime}
,
whose evolution corresponds to the evolution driven by
\boldsymbol{f}
and constrained by
\mathrm{A}
.

We conclude with an example
of two probability vector fields
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}},{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}}
generating the
same evolution semigroup.
The assumptions could be considerably refined: we just discuss a simple case,
for ease of exposition.

Example 8.33

(Superposition of PVFs)
.

Let
(\Theta,\mathcal{T},\mathfrak{m})

be a probability space and
let

\boldsymbol{f}:\mathsf{X}\times\Theta\to\mathsf{X}

be a
\mathcal{B}(\mathsf{X})\otimes\mathcal{T}
-measurable map satisfying
the properties

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{f}(\cdot,\theta):\mathsf{X}\to\mathsf{X}\quad\text{is $\lambda$-dissipative and demicontinuous for
$\mathfrak{m}$-a.e.\penalty\ $\theta\in\Theta,$}

\displaystyle\text{there exists $A>0$ such that }|\boldsymbol{f}(x,\theta)|\leq A(1+|x|^{2})\text{ for every }x\in\mathsf{X}\text{ and $\mathfrak{m}$-a.e.\penalty\ $\theta\in\Theta$.}

We denote by

\pi^{\mathsf{X}}:\mathsf{X}\times\Theta\to\mathsf{X}

the projection on the first component,

\pi^{\mathsf{X}}(x,\theta):=x
,
and we set

(8.64)

\displaystyle{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}[\mu]:={}

\displaystyle(\pi^{\mathsf{X}},\boldsymbol{f})_{\sharp}(\mu\otimes\mathfrak{m}),\quad\mu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{X}).

Clearly

|{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}[\mu]|_{2}^{2}=\int_{\mathsf{X}}\Big(\int_{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{f}(x,\theta)|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\mathfrak{m}(\theta)\Big)\,\mathrm{d}\mu(x)\leq A(1+\mathsf{m}_{2}^{2}(\mu))<\infty

so that
\mathrm{D}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}})=\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{X})
.
Using the plan
\Sigma:=(\mathsf{x}^{0},\boldsymbol{f}(\mathsf{x}^{0},\boldsymbol{i}_{\Theta}),\mathsf{x}^{1},\boldsymbol{f}(\mathsf{x}^{1},\boldsymbol{i}_{\Theta}))_{\sharp}(\boldsymbol{\mu}\otimes\mathfrak{m})
where
\boldsymbol{\mu}\in\Gamma_{o}(\mu_{0},\mu_{1})
, we see that

{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
is
\lambda
-dissipative.
Its barycentric selection (cf. (
2.13
))

{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}}:=\operatorname{bar}\left({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}\right)
is a deterministic
PVF induced by the
demicontinuous map

(8.65)

\boldsymbol{g}(x):=\int_{\Theta}\boldsymbol{f}(x,\theta)\,\mathrm{d}\mathfrak{m}(\theta).

{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}}
is a maximal totally
\lambda
-dissipative PVF (cf. Theorem
3.24
).
Whenever
\boldsymbol{f}(\cdot,\theta)
is not constant
in a set
\Theta_{0}\subset\Theta
of positive
\mathfrak{m}
-measure
(and therefore
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}\neq{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}}
), then

{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
cannot be totally
\lambda
-dissipative since this would lead to a contradiction with the maximality of its barycentric projection
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}}
. Applying

[
27
, Corollary 5.23, Theorem 5.27]
,
we know that
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
generates
a unique
\lambda
-EVI flow
whose trajectories
have the barycentric property, and therefore coincide with the
Lagrangian solutions
of the flow generated by
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}}
, i.e.
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
and

{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}}
generates the same evolution semigroup.
It would not be difficult to check that

{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}}
coincides with the
operator
\hat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}

of Theorem
8.4

constructed from the restriction of
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}

to the core of
discrete measures.

9.
Geodesically convex functionals with a core dense in energy are totally convex

In this section, we provide sufficient conditions for the total
(-\lambda)
-convexity property (cf. Section
5
),
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
, of a functional
\phi:\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{X})\to(-\infty,+\infty]
which is proper, lower semicontinuous and geodesically
(-\lambda)
-convex (see
[
2
, Definition 9.1.1]
) with proper domain
\mathrm{D}(\phi):=\{\mu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{X})\,:\,\phi(\mu)<+\infty\}
, where we assume
\dim(\mathsf{X})\geq 2
. This ensures the applicability of the results of Section
5
, in particular Theorem
5.6
.

Recall that

\phi:\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{X})\to(-\infty,+\infty]
is geodesically

(-\lambda)
-convex if for any
\mu_{0},\mu_{1}
in
\mathrm{D}(\phi)
there exists
\boldsymbol{\mu}\in\Gamma_{o}(\mu_{0},\mu_{1})
such that

\phi(\mu_{t})\leq(1-t)\phi(\mu_{0})+t\phi(\mu_{1})+\frac{\lambda}{2}t(1-t)W_{2}^{2}(\mu_{0},\mu_{1})\qquad\forall\,t\in[0,1],

where
\mu_{t}:=\mathsf{x}^{t}_{\sharp}\boldsymbol{\mu}
.

Theorem 9.1

(Geodesic convexity vs total convexity)
.

Assume that

\dim\mathsf{X}\geq 2
,
\phi:\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{X})\to(-\infty,+\infty]

is a proper l.s.c. geodesically
(-\lambda)
-convex functional such that

\mathrm{D}(\phi)
contains
a
{\mathfrak{N}}
-core
\mathrm{C}
(see Definition
8.1
) which is
dense in energy,
meaning that for every
\mu\in\mathrm{D}(\phi)
there exists
(\mu_{n})_{n}\subset\mathrm{C}
such that

\mu_{n}\to\mu\quad\text{ and }\quad\phi(\mu_{n})\to\phi(\mu).

Then
\phi
is totally
(-\lambda)
-convex (cf. Section
5
).

Proof.

Notice that
\phi
is geodesically (resp. totally)
(-\lambda)
-convex
if and only if
\phi_{\lambda}:=\phi+\frac{\lambda}{2}\mathsf{m}_{2}^{2}(\cdot)

is geodesically (resp. totally) convex. Moreover the
assumptions
of the present Theorem
hold for
\phi
if and only if they hold for
\phi_{\lambda}
. We can
thus prove the Theorem only in case
\lambda=0
.
We proceed in a
few steps, keeping the notation of Section

8.1
.
First of all, we introduce
a standard Borel space
(\Omega,{\mathcal{B}})
endowed with a nonatomic probability measure
\mathbb{P}

as in Definition
B.1

and let
\mathcal{X}:=L^{2}(\Omega,{\mathcal{B}},\mathbb{P};\mathsf{X})
. We lift
\phi
to the l.s.c. functional

\psi:\mathcal{X}\to(-\infty,+\infty]

defined
as

(9.1)

\psi(X):=\phi(\iota(X))\quad\text{for every }X\in\mathcal{X}.

(1)
The restriction of

\psi
to
{\mathcal{C}_{N}}

is continuous and locally convex.

By construction the function
\psi
is finite and lower
semicontinuous in
{\mathcal{C}_{N}}
. It is also clear, recalling
Lemma
, that for every
X\in{\mathcal{C}_{N}}

there is an open ball
\mathcal{B}
of
\mathcal{X}_{N}
and centered at
X
such that

\mathcal{B}\subset{\mathcal{C}_{N}}

and the restriction of
\psi
to
\mathcal{B}
is convex.
Since
\mathcal{B}
is open,
it follows that
\psi
is locally convex and continuous in

{\mathcal{C}_{N}}
: in particular it is convex along each segment contained
in
{\mathcal{C}_{N}}
.

(2)
For every
X_{0},X_{1}\in{\mathcal{C}_{N}}
we have

(9.2)

\psi((1-t)X_{0}+tX_{1})\leq(1-t)\psi(X_{0})+t\psi(X_{1}).

Let
X_{0},X_{1}\in{\mathcal{C}_{N}}
; setting
A:=\supp(\iota(X_{0}))

and
B:=\supp(\iota(X_{1}))
we can apply Proposition

6.4
and use the fact that
{\mathcal{C}_{N}}
is relatively open
to find
X_{1}^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{C}_{N}}
such that
