X_{1}(s):=(1-s)X_{1}+sX_{1}^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{C}_{N}}
for every
s\in[0,1]

and
X_{s,t}:=(1-t)X_{0}+tX_{1}(s)
belongs to
\mathcal{O}_{N}

for every
t\in[0,1]
and
s\in(0,1]
.
Since
\mathrm{C}_{N}
is convex along collisionless couplings, we deduce
that
X_{s,t}\in{\mathcal{C}_{N}}
for every
s,t\in(0,1)

and
\psi(X_{s,t})\leq(1-t)\psi(X_{0})+t\psi(X_{1}(s))
.
Passing to the limit as
s\downarrow 0
, using the the lower
semicontinuity
of
\psi
and its continuity
in
{\mathcal{C}_{N}}
we deduce
(
9.2
).

(3)
Let
K\in\mathbb{N}
,
X_{1},X_{2},\cdots X_{K}\in{\mathcal{C}_{N}}

and
\beta_{1},\cdots,\beta_{K}\geq 0
with
\sum_{k=1}^{K}\beta_{k}=1
.
For every
\varepsilon>0
there exist
X_{k}^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{C}_{N}}
with

|X_{k}-X_{k}^{\prime}|<\varepsilon
,
k=1,\cdots,K
, such that

\sum_{k=1}^{K}\beta_{k}X_{k}^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{C}_{N}}
.

It is sufficient to observe that the map
S_{K}:\mathcal{X}^{K}\to\mathcal{X}
,

S_{K}(X_{1},\cdots,X_{K}):=\sum_{k=1}^{K}\beta_{k}X_{k}
is linear, continuous,
and surjective, in particular it is an open map.
If
X_{1},X_{2},\cdots X_{K}\in{\mathcal{C}_{N}}
and

\mathcal{B}_{\varepsilon}
is an open ball of radious
\varepsilon

around the corresponding vector in
\mathcal{X}^{K}
and contained in
({\mathcal{C}_{N}})^{K}
,

S_{K}\big(\mathcal{B}_{\varepsilon})
is open in

\mathcal{D}_{N}=\operatorname{co}({\mathcal{C}_{N}})
so that its intersection with
the open and dense subset
{\mathcal{C}_{N}}
is not empty.

(4)
For every
K\in\mathbb{N}
,
X_{1},X_{2},\cdots X_{K}\in{\mathcal{C}_{N}}

and
\alpha_{1},\cdots,\alpha_{K}\geq 0
with
\sum_{k=1}^{K}\alpha_{k}=1

we have

(9.3)

\psi(\sum_{k=1}^{K}\alpha_{k}X_{k})\leq\sum_{k=1}^{K}\alpha_{k}\psi(X_{k}).

We argue by induction on the number
K
. By claim (2) the statement is true if

K=2
. Let us assume that it is true for
K\in\mathbb{N}

and let us consider
X_{k}\in{\mathcal{C}_{N}}
,
1\leq k\leq K+1
and
corresponding coefficients
\alpha_{k}
. It is not restrictive to assume

0<\alpha_{K+1}<1
and we set
\beta_{k}:=\alpha_{k}/(1-\alpha_{K+1})
,

1\leq k\leq K
,
so that
\beta_{k}\geq 0
and
\sum_{k=1}^{K}\beta_{k}=1
.

We can use the previous claim and for every
\varepsilon>0

we can find
X_{k}^{\prime}(\varepsilon)\in{\mathcal{C}_{N}}
with
|X_{k}^{\prime}(\varepsilon)-X_{k}|<\varepsilon

such that
X^{\prime}(\varepsilon):=\sum_{k=1}^{K}\beta_{k}X_{k}^{\prime}(\varepsilon)\in{\mathcal{C}_{N}}
.

Using claim (2) we get

\psi\big((1-\alpha_{K+1})X^{\prime}(\varepsilon)+\alpha_{K+1}X_{K+1}\big)\leq(1-\alpha_{K+1})\psi\big(X^{\prime}(\varepsilon)\big)+\alpha_{K+1}\psi\big(X_{K+1}\big).

Using the induction step we also get

(1-\alpha_{K+1})\psi\big(X^{\prime}(\varepsilon)\big)\leq\sum_{k=1}^{K}\alpha_{k}\psi\big(X_{k}^{\prime}(\varepsilon)\big).

Combining the two inequalities and passing to the limit as

\varepsilon\downarrow 0

using the lower semicontinuity of
\psi
and its continuity
in
{\mathcal{C}_{N}}
we conclude.

(5)
\psi
is convex in
\overline{\mathcal{D}_{N}}
.

Let us consider
the convex envelope of the restriction of
\psi
to
\mathcal{D}_{N}=\operatorname{co}({\mathcal{C}_{N}})

defined by

\psi_{N}(X):=\inf\Big\{\sum_{k=1}^{K}\alpha_{k}\psi(X_{k}):X_{k}\in{\mathcal{C}_{N}},\ \alpha_{k}\geq 0,\ \sum_{k=1}^{K}\alpha_{k}=1,\ \sum_{k=1}^{K}\alpha_{k}X_{k}=X,\ K\in\mathbb{N}\Big\},\quad X\in\mathcal{D}_{N}.

By the previous claim
\psi(X)\leq\psi_{N}(X)
for every
X\in\mathcal{D}_{N}.

We then consider the lower semicontinuous envelope

\bar{\psi}_{N}:\overline{\mathcal{D}_{N}}\to(-\infty,+\infty]

of
\psi_{N}

defined by

\bar{\psi}_{N}(X):=\inf\Big\{\liminf_{n\to\infty}\psi_{N}(X_{n}):X_{n}\in\mathcal{D}_{N},\ X_{n}\to X\quad\text{as }n\to\infty\Big\},\quad X\in\overline{\mathcal{D}_{N}}.

Since
\psi
is lower semicontinuous and
\psi_{N}
is continuous in
{\mathcal{C}_{N}}
,
we have

(9.4)

\psi(X)\leq\bar{\psi}_{N}(X)\quad\text{for every }X\in\overline{\mathcal{D}_{N}},\quad\bar{\psi}_{N}(X)=\psi_{N}(X)=\psi(X)\quad\text{if }X\in{\mathcal{C}_{N}}.

We want to show that
\psi\equiv\bar{\psi}_{N}
in
\overline{\mathcal{D}_{N}}.

Let us suppose that
X\in\overline{\mathcal{D}_{N}}
, with
\psi(X)<\infty.
We take

Y\in{\mathcal{C}_{N}}
, so that
X_{t}:=(1-t)X+tY\in\mathcal{D}_{N}
for every
t\in(0,1]

(since
\overline{\mathcal{D}_{N}}
is convex and its relative interior coincides with
\mathcal{D}_{N}
by Lemma
8.12
)
and
X_{t}\in{\mathcal{C}_{N}}
with possibly finite exceptions.
Therefore,
possibly replacing
Y
with
X_{t_{0}}
for a sufficiently small
t_{0}>0
,
it is not restrictive to assume that
X_{t}\in{\mathcal{C}_{N}}
for every
t\in(0,1]
and
\iota^{2}(X,Y)
is the unique optimal coupling between its marginals (see Lemma
) , so that

\psi
is convex along
(X_{t})_{t\in[0,1]}
being
\phi
geodesically convex. We deduce that

\bar{\psi}_{N}(X_{t})=\psi(X_{t})\leq(1-t)\psi(X)+t\psi(Y)\quad\text{for every }t\in(0,1],

so that
\bar{\psi}_{N}(X)\leq\liminf_{t\downarrow 0}\bar{\psi}_{N}(X_{t})\leq\psi(X)
.

(6)
\psi
is convex
.

Let
X,Y\in\mathrm{D}(\psi)
,
and let
\mu=\iota(X),\nu=\iota(Y)\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{X})
. We thus have that
\mu,\nu\in\mathrm{D}(\phi)\subset\overline{\mathrm{C}}
.

By density, we can find sequences
(\mu_{n})_{n},(\nu_{n})\subset\mathrm{C}

such that
W_{2}(\mu_{n},\mu)\to 0
,
W_{2}(\nu_{n},\nu)\to 0
,

\phi(\mu_{n})\to\phi(\mu)
and
\phi(\nu_{n})\to\phi(\nu)
as
n\to+\infty
.
By the last part of Theorem
B.5
, we can find sequences

(X_{n})_{n},(Y_{n})_{n}\subset{\mathcal{C}_{\infty}}

such that
\iota_{X_{n}}=\mu_{n}
,
\iota_{Y_{n}}=\nu_{n}
,
X_{n}\to X
and

Y_{n}\to Y
.
Since
X_{n}\in{\mathcal{C}_{M(n)}}
,

Y_{n}\in{\mathcal{C}_{N(n)}}
for some
M(n),N(n)\in{\mathfrak{N}}

and
{\mathfrak{N}}
is a directed set, we can find
P(n)\in{\mathfrak{N}}
such that

M(n)\mid P(n)
,
N(n)\mid P(n)
; so that

X_{n},Y_{n}\in\overline{\mathcal{D}_{P(n)}}
. By claim (5), we
we have that

\displaystyle\psi((1-t)X_{n}+tY_{n})\leq(1-t)\psi(X_{n})+t\psi(Y_{n}),\quad\text{for any }n\in\mathbb{N}.

Passing to the limit as
n\to\infty

and using the lower semicontinuity of
\psi
yield the sought convexity.
∎

Remark 9.2

(Geodesic convexity implies total convexity for continuous functionals)
.

Let
\phi:\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{X})\to\mathbb{R}
be a lower semicontinuous
and geodesically
(-\lambda)
-convex functional
which is approximable by discrete measures, i.e. for every

\mu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{X})
there exists a sequence

\mu_{n}\in\mathcal{P}_{\#\mathbb{N}}(\mathsf{X})
converging to
\mu
such that

\phi(\mu_{n})\to\phi(\mu)
(e.g.
\phi
is continuous).
Then

\phi
satisfies the assumptions of Theorem
9.1

with
\mathrm{C}=\mathcal{P}_{\#\mathbb{N}}(\mathsf{X})
.
This in particular gives that such kind of functionals
are totally
(-\lambda)
-convex and locally Lipschitz.

As a consequence, we notice that non totally
(-\lambda)
-convex
functionals cannot be approximated in the Mosco sense by everywhere
finite, continuous and geodesically
(-\lambda)
-convex functionals
defined on
\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{X})

(this is because total
(-\lambda)
-convexity
is preserved by the Mosco limit).

Remark 9.3
.

An analogous result as in Remark
9.2
has been obtained independently in
[
45
]
. There, the author proves the equivalence of geodesic convexity and total convexity, assuming that the functional is additionally differentiable, with no restrictions on
\dim\mathsf{X}
. Notice that, if the functional is just continuous, the result doesn’t hold in general if
\dim\mathsf{X}=1
, as shown in
[
45
, Example 3.9]
.

As previously mentioned, thanks to Theorem
9.1
we are
allowed to apply all the results obtained in Section
5

to the totally
(-\lambda)
-convex functional
\phi
. In particular,
we get existence and uniqueness of the
\lambda
-EVI solution for
the MPVF
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}:=-\boldsymbol{\partial}\phi
starting from

\mu_{0}\in\overline{\mathrm{D}(\phi)}
and its Lagrangian characterization as
the law of the semigroup generated by
-\partial\psi
, where
\psi
is defined as in (
9.1
).

We conclude the section by
showing that the total subdifferential

-\boldsymbol{\partial}_{\mathrm{t}}\phi:=\iota^{2}(-\partial\psi)
coincides with the
operator
\hat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
obtained by the
{\mathfrak{N}}
-core construction of
Theorem
8.4
.

Proposition 9.4
.

Let us suppose that

\dim\mathsf{X}\geq 2
,
\phi:\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{X})\to(-\infty,+\infty]

is a proper, l.s.c. geodesically
(-\lambda)
-convex functional such that

\mathrm{D}(\boldsymbol{\partial}\phi)
contains
a
{\mathfrak{N}}
-core
\mathrm{C}
which is dense in energy in the sense that for every
\mu\in\mathrm{D}(\phi)
there exists
(\mu_{n})_{n}\subset\mathrm{C}
s.t.

\mu_{n}\to\mu,\quad\phi(\mu_{n})\to\phi(\mu).

The maximal totally
\lambda
-dissipative

MPVF

\hat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
, obtained by Theorem
8.4

starting from the minimal selection
-\boldsymbol{\partial}^{\circ}\phi
restricted to
\mathrm{C}
,
coincides with
-\boldsymbol{\partial}_{\mathrm{t}}\phi
defined as in Section
5
.
Equivalently,
if
\psi:=\phi\circ\iota

and
\hat{\boldsymbol{F}}
is the Lagrangian representation of

\hat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
,
then

\hat{\boldsymbol{F}}=-\partial\psi.

Proof.

By Theorem
9.1
, we have that
\phi
is totally
(-\lambda)
-convex so that we can apply the results of Section
5
. By Propositions
5.2
and
5.3
we know that
\boldsymbol{\partial}^{\circ}\phi
coincides with

\boldsymbol{\partial}_{\mathrm{t}}^{\circ}\phi
and
\boldsymbol{\partial}_{\mathrm{t}}^{\circ}\phi
is totally
\lambda
-dissipative.

Theorem
8.6
shows that

\hat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
provides the unique maximal totally
\lambda
-dissipative
extension of the restriction of
\boldsymbol{\partial}_{\mathrm{t}}^{\circ}\phi

to
\mathrm{C}
with domain included in
\overline{\mathrm{C}}
. Therefore,
\hat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}}
must coincide with
\boldsymbol{\partial}_{\mathrm{t}}\phi
, being
\boldsymbol{\partial}_{\mathrm{t}}\phi
maximal totally
\lambda
-dissipative as well (cf. Proposition
5.2
) and observing that by Proposition
5.3
(3) we have
\mathrm{D}(\boldsymbol{\partial}_{\mathrm{t}}\phi)=\mathrm{D}(\boldsymbol{\partial}\phi)\subset\overline{\mathrm{C}}
.
∎

Appendix A
Dissipative operators in Hilbert spaces and extensions

In this section, we recall useful definitions, properties and results on
\lambda
-dissipative operators in Hilbert spaces used in Sections
3
and
8
, with
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
. Our main reference is
[
17
]
.

Let
\mathcal{H}
be a Hilbert space with norm
|\cdot|
and scalar product
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle
. Given
E\subset\mathcal{H}
, we denote by
\operatorname{co}(E)
the convex hull of
E
and
by
\overline{\operatorname{co}}\left(E\right)

its closure. Given an operator
\boldsymbol{B}\subset\mathcal{H}\times\mathcal{H}
(which we identify with its graph) we define its sections
\boldsymbol{B}x\equiv\boldsymbol{B}(x):=\{v\in\mathcal{H}:(x,v)\in\boldsymbol{B}\}
and its domain

\mathrm{D}(\boldsymbol{B}):=\{x\in\mathcal{H}:\boldsymbol{B}x\neq\emptyset\}
.
An operator
\boldsymbol{B}\subset\mathcal{H}\times\mathcal{H}

is
\lambda
-dissipative (
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
) if

(A.1)

\langle v-w,x-y\rangle\leq\lambda|x-y|^{2}\quad\text{for every }(x,v),\ (y,w)\in\boldsymbol{B}.

A
\lambda
-dissipative operator
\boldsymbol{B}
is maximal if it is maximal w.r.t. inclusion in the class of
\lambda
-dissipative operators or, equivalently, if

[
17
, Chap. II, Def. 2.2]

(A.2)

(x,v)\in\mathcal{H}\times\mathcal{H},\quad\langle v-w,x-y\rangle\leq\lambda|x-y|^{2}\quad\text{for every }(y,w)\in\boldsymbol{B}\quad\Rightarrow\quad(x,v)\in\boldsymbol{B}.

Remark A.1

(Dissipativity, monotonicity)
.

Let
\boldsymbol{B}\subset\mathcal{H}\times\mathcal{H}
; we define
-\boldsymbol{B}:=\{(x,-v):(x,v)\in\boldsymbol{B}\}
and we say that
\boldsymbol{B}
is
\lambda
-monotone if
-\boldsymbol{B}
is
(-\lambda)
-dissipative. It is easy to check that
\boldsymbol{B}
is
\lambda
-dissipative if and only if
\boldsymbol{B}^{\lambda}:=\boldsymbol{B}-\lambda\boldsymbol{i}_{\mathcal{H}}
is
0
-dissipative (or simply, dissipative) if and only if
-\boldsymbol{B}^{\lambda}
is
0
-monotone (or simply, monotone). The same holds for maximal
\lambda
-dissipativity, maximal dissipativity and maximal monotonicity (with analogous definition). Observe also that
\mathrm{D}(\boldsymbol{B})=\mathrm{D}(\boldsymbol{B}^{\lambda})=\mathrm{D}(-\boldsymbol{B}^{\lambda})
.

We list in the following theorems a few well known properties of
\lambda
-dissipative operators that have been extensively used in the previous sections. Since these results are more commonly known for
\lambda=0
(cf.
[
17
]
), we prefer to state them here in the general case. For this reason, in the proofs, we point out only the changes that have to be made compared to the case
\lambda=0
. Recall that
\lambda^{+}:=\lambda\vee 0
and we set
1/\lambda^{+}=+\infty
if
\lambda^{+}=0
.

Theorem A.2
.

Let
\boldsymbol{B}\subset\mathcal{H}\times\mathcal{H}
be a
\lambda
-dissipative operator. Then:

(1)

\boldsymbol{B}
is maximal if and only if the resolvent operator
\boldsymbol{J}_{\tau}:=(\boldsymbol{i}_{\mathcal{H}}-\tau\boldsymbol{B})^{-1}
is a
(1-\lambda\tau)^{-1}
-Lipschitz continuous map defined on the whole
\mathcal{H}
for every
0<\tau<1/\lambda^{+}
;

(2)

there exists a maximal extension
\hat{\boldsymbol{B}}
of
\boldsymbol{B}
(meaning that
\boldsymbol{B}\subset\hat{\boldsymbol{B}}
and
\hat{\boldsymbol{B}}
is maximal
\lambda
-dissipative) whose domain is included in
\overline{\operatorname{co}}\left(\mathrm{D}(\boldsymbol{B})\right)
.

Proof.

(1) We can use Remark
A.1
and apply
[
17
, Proposition 2.2]
to
-\boldsymbol{B}^{\lambda}
and then obtain that
\boldsymbol{B}
is maximal
\lambda
-dissipative if and only if
((1+\lambda\vartheta)\boldsymbol{i}_{\mathcal{H}}-\vartheta\boldsymbol{B})^{-1}
is a contraction on
\mathcal{H}
for every
\vartheta>0
. Since
x\mapsto x/(1-\lambda x)
is a bijection between
(0,1/\lambda^{+})
and
(0,+\infty)
, this is equivalent to say that
((1-\lambda\tau)^{-1}(\boldsymbol{i}_{\mathcal{H}}-\tau\boldsymbol{B}))^{-1}
is a contraction on
\mathcal{H}
for every
0<\tau<1/\lambda^{+}
which is to say that
\boldsymbol{J}_{\tau}
is a
(1-\lambda\tau)^{-1}
-Lipschitz map defined on the whole
\mathcal{H}
.

(2) This follows immediately from Remark
A.1
and
[
17
, Corollary 2.1]
.
∎

Theorem A.3
.

Let
\boldsymbol{B}
be a maximal
\lambda
-dissipative operator. Then:

(1)

\boldsymbol{B}
is closed in the strong-weak (or the weak-strong) topology in
\mathcal{H}\times\mathcal{H}
;

(2)

for every
x\in\mathrm{D}(\boldsymbol{B})
, the section
\boldsymbol{B}x
is closed and convex so that it contains a unique element of minimal norm denoted by
\boldsymbol{B}^{\circ}x
;

(3)

if
\operatorname{int}\left(\operatorname{co}(\mathrm{D}(\boldsymbol{B}))\right)\neq\emptyset
, then
\operatorname{int}\left(\mathrm{D}(\boldsymbol{B})\right)
is convex,
\operatorname{int}\left(\mathrm{D}(\boldsymbol{B})\right)=\operatorname{int}\left(\overline{\mathrm{D}(\boldsymbol{B})}\right)\neq\emptyset
and
\boldsymbol{B}
is locally bounded in the interior of its domain;

(4)

\overline{\mathrm{D}(\boldsymbol{B})}
is convex and for every
x\in\overline{\mathrm{D}(\boldsymbol{B})}
,
\boldsymbol{J}_{\tau}x\to x
as
\tau\downarrow 0
;

(5)

for every
0<\tau<1/\lambda^{+}
, the Moreau-Yosida approximation of
\boldsymbol{B}
,
\boldsymbol{B}_{\tau}:=\frac{\boldsymbol{J}_{\tau}-\boldsymbol{i}_{\mathcal{H}}}{\tau}
, is maximal
\frac{\lambda}{1-\lambda\tau}
-dissipative and
\frac{2-\lambda\tau}{\tau(1-\lambda\tau)}
-Lipschitz continuous. Moreover, for every
x\in\penalty\ \mathrm{D}(\boldsymbol{B})
,

\begin{split}&(1-\lambda\tau)|\boldsymbol{B}_{\tau}x|\uparrow|\boldsymbol{B}^{\circ}x|,\quad\text{as }\tau\downarrow 0,\\
&\boldsymbol{B}_{\tau}x\to\boldsymbol{B}^{\circ}x,\quad\text{as }\tau\downarrow 0,\\
&|\boldsymbol{B}_{\tau}x-\boldsymbol{B}^{\circ}x|^{2}\leq|\boldsymbol{B}^{\circ}x|^{2}-(1-2\lambda\tau)|\boldsymbol{B}_{\tau}x|^{2},\quad\text{for }0<\tau<1/\lambda^{+}.\end{split}

If
x\notin\mathrm{D}(\boldsymbol{B})
, then
|\boldsymbol{B}_{\tau}x|\to+\infty
. Finally,
\boldsymbol{B}_{\tau}\to\boldsymbol{B}
in the graph sense:

\text{for every }(x,v)\in\boldsymbol{B}\text{ there exists }(x_{\tau})_{\tau>0}\subset\mathcal{H}\text{ such that }x_{\tau}\to x,\,\boldsymbol{B}_{\tau}x_{\tau}\to v,\text{ as }\tau\downarrow 0.

(6)

\boldsymbol{B}^{\circ}
is a
principal selection
of
\boldsymbol{B}
i.e.

(A.3)

(x,v)\in\overline{\mathrm{D}(\boldsymbol{B})}\times\mathcal{H},\quad\langle v-\boldsymbol{B}^{\circ}y,x-y\rangle\leq\lambda|x-y|^{2}\quad\text{for every }y\in\mathrm{D}(\boldsymbol{B})\quad\Rightarrow\quad(x,v)\in\boldsymbol{B}.

Proof.

(1) and (2) follow immediately from (
A.2
).

(3) follows immediately by Remark
A.1
and
[
17
, Proposition 2.9]
.

(4) follows by Remark
A.1
and
[
17
, Theorem 2.2]
observing that

\lim_{\tau\downarrow 0}\boldsymbol{J}_{\tau}x=\lim_{\vartheta\downarrow 0}(1+\lambda\vartheta)(\boldsymbol{i}_{\mathcal{H}}+\vartheta(-\boldsymbol{B}^{\lambda}))^{-1}x=x.

(5) The Lipschitz constant of
\boldsymbol{B}_{\tau}
can be estimated by
\frac{1}{\tau}(L+1)
, where
L
is the Lipschitz constant of
\boldsymbol{J}_{\tau}
, so that the value of the constant follows by Theorem
A.2
(1). The fact that
\boldsymbol{B}_{\tau}
is
\lambda/(1-\lambda\tau)
dissipative is a consequence of the inequality

\langle\boldsymbol{B}_{\tau}x-\boldsymbol{B}_{\tau}y,x-y\rangle=\frac{1}{\tau}\langle\boldsymbol{J}_{\tau}x-\boldsymbol{J}_{\tau}y,x-y\rangle-\frac{1}{\tau}|x-y|^{2}\leq\frac{\lambda}{1-\lambda\tau}|x-y|^{2},

where we used the Lipschitz continuity of
\boldsymbol{J}_{\tau}
. Maximality of
\boldsymbol{B}_{\tau}
follows by Remark
A.1
and
[
17
, Proposition 2.6]
. The fact that
(1-\lambda\tau)|\boldsymbol{B}_{\tau}x|
is increasing and bounded from above by
|\boldsymbol{B}^{\circ}x|
follows precisely as in the proof of
[
17
, Proposition 2.6]
: exploiting the dissipativity inequality

\langle\boldsymbol{B}^{\circ}x-\boldsymbol{B}_{\tau}x,x-\boldsymbol{J}_{\tau}x\rangle\leq\lambda|x-\boldsymbol{J}_{\tau}x|^{2}

one gets that
|\boldsymbol{B}_{\tau}x|^{2}(1-\lambda\tau)\leq\langle\boldsymbol{B}^{\circ}x,\boldsymbol{B}_{\tau}x\rangle
for every
x\in\mathrm{D}(\boldsymbol{B})
. Substituting to
\boldsymbol{B}
, in the same inequality, the
\lambda/(1-\lambda\eta)
-dissipative operator
\boldsymbol{B}_{\eta}
, we get that

|\boldsymbol{B}_{\eta+\tau}x|^{2}(1-\lambda(\tau+\eta))\leq(1-\lambda\eta)\langle\boldsymbol{B}_{\eta}x,\boldsymbol{B}_{\eta+\tau}x\rangle\quad\text{ for every }x\in\mathcal{H}\text{ and every }0<\eta,\tau<1/\lambda^{+}.

This shows that the quantity
(1-\lambda\tau)|\boldsymbol{B}_{\tau}x|
is nondecreasing as
\tau\downarrow 0
for every
x\in\mathcal{H}
. This means in particular that there exists the limit
\ell:=\lim_{\tau\downarrow 0}|\boldsymbol{B}_{\tau}x|\in[0,+\infty]
. The above estimate also gives that

(A.4)

|\boldsymbol{B}_{\eta+\tau}x-\boldsymbol{B}_{\eta}x|^{2}\leq|\boldsymbol{B}_{\eta}x|^{2}-\frac{1-\lambda(\eta+2\tau)}{1-\lambda\eta}|\boldsymbol{B}_{\eta+\tau}x|^{2}\quad\text{ for every }x\in\mathcal{H},

so that
(\boldsymbol{B}_{\tau}x)_{\tau}
is Cauchy whenever it is bounded. Thus, if
x\in\mathrm{D}(\boldsymbol{B})
, then
(1-\lambda\tau)|\boldsymbol{B}_{\tau}x|\leq|\boldsymbol{B}^{\circ}x|
so that
\boldsymbol{B}_{\tau}x\to v
for some
v\in\mathcal{H}
. By (1),
(x,v)\in\boldsymbol{B}
and
|v|\leq|\boldsymbol{B}^{\circ}x|
which implies that
v=\boldsymbol{B}^{\circ}x
. On the other hand, if
x\notin\mathrm{D}(\boldsymbol{B})
, we have that
|\boldsymbol{B}_{\tau}x|\to+\infty
: indeed, if by contradiction
|\boldsymbol{B}_{\tau}x|
is bounded, then we have shown that
\boldsymbol{B}_{\tau}x
must converge to some
v\in\mathcal{H}
so that we also have
\boldsymbol{J}_{\tau}x=\tau\boldsymbol{B}_{\tau}x+x\to x
. Since
(\boldsymbol{J}_{\tau}x,\boldsymbol{B}_{\tau}x)\in\boldsymbol{B}
and
(\boldsymbol{J}_{\tau}x,\boldsymbol{B}_{\tau}x)\to(x,v)
, by (1) we deduce that
(x,v)\in\boldsymbol{B}
, a contradiction. Observe that passing to the limit as
\eta\downarrow 0
in (
A.4
), we get that
|\boldsymbol{B}_{\tau}x-\boldsymbol{B}^{\circ}x|^{2}\leq|\boldsymbol{B}^{\circ}x|^{2}-(1-2\lambda\tau)|\boldsymbol{B}_{\tau}x|^{2}
. To conclude the proof of (5) we only need to show the graph convergence of
\boldsymbol{B}_{\tau}
to
\boldsymbol{B}
. Let
(x,v)\in\boldsymbol{B}
and let us define
x_{\tau}:=x-\tau v
. Then
x_{\tau}\to x
and
\boldsymbol{J}_{\tau}x_{\tau}=x
. Then
\boldsymbol{B}_{\tau}x_{\tau}=(x-x_{\tau})/\tau=v
.

(6) Follows exactly as in
[
17
, Proposition 2.7]
: performing similar computations, we get

\frac{1}{2}\langle y_{1}-y_{2},x_{1}-x_{2}\rangle\leq-\langle y_{1}+y_{2},x-\boldsymbol{J}_{\tau}x\rangle+\lambda(|\boldsymbol{J}_{\tau}x-x_{1}|^{2}+|\boldsymbol{J}_{\tau}x-x_{2}|^{2})

for every
(x_{1},y_{1}),(x_{2},y_{2})\in M
, where

\boldsymbol{M}=\{(y,w)\in\overline{\mathrm{D}(\boldsymbol{B})}\times\mathcal{H}:\langle\boldsymbol{B}^{\circ}x-w,x-y\rangle\leq\lambda|x-y|^{2}\quad\text{ for every }x\in\mathrm{D}(\boldsymbol{B})\},

and
x:=(x_{1}+x_{2})/2
. Passing to the limit as
\tau\downarrow 0
we obtain that
\boldsymbol{M}
is
\lambda
-dissipative so that, since
\boldsymbol{B}\subset\boldsymbol{M}
, we get that
\boldsymbol{M}=\boldsymbol{B}
.
∎

For the next result we recall that a proper functional
\psi:\mathcal{H}\to(-\infty,+\infty]
is said to be
\lambda
-convex if the map
x\mapsto\psi(x)-\frac{\lambda}{2}|x|^{2}
is convex. Its Fréchet subdifferential
\partial\psi
is characterized by

(x,v)\in\partial\psi\quad\Leftrightarrow\quad x\in\mathrm{D}(\psi)\text{ and }\psi(y)-\psi(x)\geq\langle v,y-x\rangle+\frac{\lambda}{2}|x-y|^{2}\quad\text{ for every }y\in\mathcal{H}.

Corollary A.4
.

Let
\psi:\mathcal{H}\to(-\infty,+\infty]
be a proper, lower semicontinuous and
(-\lambda)
-convex function. Then
-\partial\psi
is a maximal
\lambda
-dissipative operator. Moreover, denoting by
\boldsymbol{B}:=-\partial\psi
, we have that

\lim_{\tau\downarrow 0}\frac{\psi(x)-\psi(\boldsymbol{J}_{\tau}x)}{\tau}=|\boldsymbol{B}^{\circ}x|^{2}\quad\text{ for every }x\in\mathrm{D}(\boldsymbol{B}),

\frac{1}{2\tau}|x-\boldsymbol{J}_{\tau}x|^{2}+\psi(\boldsymbol{J}_{\tau}x)<\frac{1}{2\tau}|x-y|^{2}+\psi(y)\quad\text{ for every }x,y\in\mathcal{H},\,y\neq\boldsymbol{J}_{\tau}x.

Proof.

Notice that
\psi_{\lambda}:=\psi+\frac{\lambda}{2}|\cdot|^{2}
is convex and that
\partial\psi_{\lambda}=\partial\psi+\lambda\boldsymbol{i}_{\mathcal{X}}
so that by
[
17
, Example 2.3.4]
and Remark
A.1
, the operator
-\partial\psi_{\lambda}
is maximal dissipative and thus
-\partial\psi
is maximal
\lambda
-dissipative. By definition of subdifferential of a
(-\lambda)
-convex function, we have that for every
0<\tau<1/\lambda^{+}
it holds

\displaystyle\psi(x)-\psi(\boldsymbol{J}_{\tau}x)

\displaystyle\geq\langle\boldsymbol{B}_{\tau}x,\boldsymbol{J}_{\tau}x-x\rangle-\frac{\lambda}{2}|\boldsymbol{J}_{\tau}x-x|^{2}=\tau|\boldsymbol{B}_{\tau}x|^{2}-\frac{\lambda}{2}|\boldsymbol{J}_{\tau}x-x|^{2},

\displaystyle\psi(\boldsymbol{J}_{\tau}x)-\psi(x)

\displaystyle\geq\langle\boldsymbol{B}^{\circ}x,x-\boldsymbol{J}_{\tau}x\rangle-\frac{\lambda}{2}|\boldsymbol{J}_{\tau}x-x|^{2}=-\tau\langle\boldsymbol{B}^{\circ}x,\boldsymbol{B}_{\tau}x\rangle-\frac{\lambda}{2}|\boldsymbol{J}_{\tau}x-x|^{2}.

Dividing the first (resp. the second) inequality by
\tau>0
(resp.
-\tau<0
) and passing to the
\liminf
(resp. to the
\limsup
) as
\tau\downarrow 0
, gives the desired equality thanks to Theorem
A.3
(5). The fact that the limit diverges outside the domain of
\boldsymbol{B}
follows again by Theorem
A.3
(5) and the first inequality above. The last assertion follows simply observing that
y\mapsto\Psi(\tau,x;y):=\frac{1}{2\tau}|x-y|^{2}+\psi(y)
is proper and strictly convex, so that
z
is a strict minimum point for
\Psi(\tau,x;\cdot)
if and only if
0\in\partial\Psi(\tau,x;z)
, which is satisfied if and only if
z=\boldsymbol{J}_{\tau}x
.
∎

Theorem A.5
.

Let
\boldsymbol{B}
be a maximal
\lambda
-dissipative operator and let
x_{0}\in\mathrm{D}(\boldsymbol{B})
. There exists a unique locally Lipschitz function
x:[0,+\infty)\to\mathcal{H}
, with
x(0)=x_{0}
, such that:

(1)

x(t)\in\mathrm{D}(\boldsymbol{B})
for every
t>0
;

(2)

\dot{x}(t)\in\boldsymbol{B}x(t)
for a.e.
t>0
;

(3)

the map
t\mapsto\boldsymbol{B}^{\circ}x(t)
is right continuous,
t\mapsto x(t)
is right differentiable at every
t\geq 0
and its right derivative at
t
coincides with
\boldsymbol{B}^{\circ}(x(t))
for every
t\geq 0
;

(4)

the function
t\mapsto e^{-\lambda t}|\boldsymbol{B}^{\circ}x(t)|
is decreasing in
[0,+\infty)
.

Moreover, if
x,y:[0,+\infty)\to\mathcal{H}
are solutions of the differential inclusion in (2), then

|x(t)-y(t)|\leq e^{\lambda t}|x(0)-y(0)|\quad\text{ for every }t\geq 0.

Proof.

The proof of the last assertion is trivial. The proof of the points (1),(2),(3) and (4) is completely analogous to the one of
[
17
, Theorem 3.1]
with only few differences that we point out in case
\lambda\neq 0
. In what follows, we take
0<\tau,\eta<1/\lambda^{+}
. To prove existence one starts from the approximate problems

\dot{x}_{\tau}-\boldsymbol{B}_{\tau}x_{\tau}=0

which have unique smooth solutions thanks to e.g.
[
17
, Theorem 1.6]
together with the estimate

(A.5)

|\boldsymbol{B}_{\tau}x_{\tau}(t)|=|\dot{x}_{\tau}(t)|\leq e^{\frac{\lambda t}{1-\lambda\tau}}|\boldsymbol{B}_{\tau}x_{0}|\leq\frac{e^{\frac{\lambda t}{1-\lambda\tau}}}{1-\lambda\tau}|\boldsymbol{B}^{\circ}x_{0}|\quad\text{ for every }t\geq 0,

still provided by
[
17
, Theorem 1.6]
and Theorem
A.3
(5). Performing the same computations of the proof of
[
17
, Theorem 3.1]
, using
\lambda
-dissipativity instead of monotonicity, one obtains

|x_{\tau}(t)-x_{\eta}(t)|\leq C(\lambda,t)|\boldsymbol{B}^{\circ}x_{0}|\sqrt{\tau+\eta}\quad\text{ for every }t\geq 0,

where
C(\lambda,t)
is a positive constant that depends in a continuous way only on
\lambda
and
t
. This proves that
x_{\tau}
converges locally uniformly to
x
on
[0,+\infty)
with the estimate

(A.6)

|x_{\tau}(t)-x(t)|\leq C(\lambda,t)|\boldsymbol{B}^{\circ}x_{0}|\sqrt{\tau}\quad\text{ for every }t\geq 0.

Since

|\boldsymbol{J}_{\tau}x_{\tau}-x_{\tau}|=\tau|\boldsymbol{B}_{\tau}x_{\tau}|\leq\tau\,\frac{e^{\frac{\lambda t}{1-\lambda\tau}}}{1-\lambda\tau}|\boldsymbol{B}^{\circ}x_{0}|,

we also get that
\boldsymbol{J}_{\tau}x_{\tau}
converges to
x
locally uniformly in
[0,+\infty)
and this, together with the estimate (
A.5
) and Theorem
A.3
(1), shows that
x(t)\in\mathrm{D}(\boldsymbol{B})
and
|\boldsymbol{B}^{\circ}x(t)|\leq e^{\lambda t}|\boldsymbol{B}^{\circ}x_{0}|
for every
t\geq 0
; in particular this proves (1). Since
|\dot{x}_{\tau}|
is uniformly bounded on every interval
[0,T]
by (
A.5
), it converges weakly
∗
in
L^{\infty}([0,T];\mathcal{H})
(and thus also weakly in
L^{2}([0,T];\mathcal{H})
) to a function
v\in L^{\infty}([0,T];\mathcal{H})
which turns out to be the almost everywhere derivative of
x
in
[0,T]
(cf.
[
17
, Appendix]
) so that, applying Theorem
A.3
(1) to the extension of
\boldsymbol{B}
to
L^{2}([0,T];\mathcal{H})
(see
[
17
, Examples 2.1.3, 2.3.3]
and Remark
A.1
), we obtain (2) and also the inequality

(A.7)

|\dot{x}(t)|\leq e^{\lambda t}|\boldsymbol{B}^{\circ}x_{0}|\quad\text{ for a.e.\penalty\ }t>0.

Observing now that, for every
t_{0}\geq 0
,
t\mapsto x(t+t_{0})
is a solution of (2) with initial datum
x(t_{0})
, we get that
|\boldsymbol{B}^{\circ}x(t+t_{0})|\leq e^{\lambda t}|\boldsymbol{B}^{\circ}x(t_{0})|
which proves (4). It remains only to prove (3). The right continuity of
t\mapsto|\boldsymbol{B}^{\circ}x(t)|
follows precisely as in
[
17
, Theorem 3.1]
: it is enough to prove it at
t=0
; if
0<t_{n}<1
is such that
t_{n}\downarrow 0
, then
|\boldsymbol{B}^{\circ}(x(t_{n}))|\leq e^{\lambda_{+}}|\boldsymbol{B}^{\circ}x_{0}|
by (4), so that, up to a unrelabeled subsequence,
\boldsymbol{B}^{\circ}(x(t_{n}))
converges weakly to some
v\in\mathcal{H}
. Since
x(t_{n})\to x_{0}
and thanks to Theorem
A.3
(1),
v
belongs to
\boldsymbol{B}x_{0}
. However
|v|\leq|\boldsymbol{B}^{\circ}x_{0}|
so that it must be
v=\boldsymbol{B}^{\circ}x_{0}
. The strong convergence follows observing that
\limsup|\boldsymbol{B}^{\circ}(x(t_{n}))|\leq|v|=|\boldsymbol{B}^{\circ}x_{0}|
. Being the limit independent of the subsequence, we obtain convergence of the whole sequence. We still follow the proof of
[
17
, Theorem 3.1]
to prove the right differentiability of
x
and the inclusion for its right derivative: for every
t_{0},h>0
we have that

|x(t_{0}+h)-x(t_{0})|=\left|\int_{t_{0}}^{t_{0}+h}\dot{x}(s)\,\mathrm{d}s\right|\leq\frac{e^{\lambda h}-1}{\lambda}|\boldsymbol{B}^{\circ}(x(t_{0}))|,

where we have applied (
A.7
) to
t\mapsto x(t+t_{0})
. If
t_{0}
is a point of differentiability for
x(t)
such that
\dot{x}(t_{0})\in\boldsymbol{B}x(t_{0})
, dividing by
h
and passing to the limit as
h\downarrow 0
in the above inequality, we get that
|\dot{x}(t_{0})|\leq|\boldsymbol{B}^{\circ}x(t_{0})|
so that
\dot{x}(t_{0})=\boldsymbol{B}^{\circ}x(t_{0})
. We can thus integrate this equality in
[t_{0},t_{0}+h]
for every
t_{0}\geq 0
and every
0<h<1
to obtain that

\lim_{h\downarrow 0}\frac{x(t_{0}+h)-x(t_{0})}{h}=\lim_{h\downarrow 0}\int_{0}^{1}\boldsymbol{B}^{\circ}x(t_{0}+sh)\,\mathrm{d}s=\boldsymbol{B}^{\circ}x(t_{0}),

where we used the right continuity of
t\mapsto\boldsymbol{B}^{\circ}(x(t))
and the dominated convergence theorem that we can apply since
|\boldsymbol{B}^{\circ}x(t_{0}+rh)|\leq e^{\lambda_{+}}|\boldsymbol{B}^{\circ}x(t_{0})|
by (4). This concludes the proof of (3).
∎

Theorem A.6
.

If
\boldsymbol{B}
is maximal
\lambda
-dissipative, there exists a semigroup of Lipschitz transformations
\boldsymbol{S}_{t}:\overline{\mathrm{D}(\boldsymbol{B})}\to\overline{\mathrm{D}(\boldsymbol{B})}
such that, for every
x\in\mathrm{D}(\boldsymbol{B})
, the curve
t\mapsto x(t):=\boldsymbol{S}_{t}x
is the unique solution of the differential inclusion
\dot{x}(t)\in\boldsymbol{B}x(t)
, for a.e.
t>0
, starting from
x
. Moreover, we have

(A.8)

|\boldsymbol{S}_{t}x-\boldsymbol{S}_{t}y|\leq e^{\lambda t}|x-y|\quad\text{ for every }x,y\in\overline{\mathrm{D}(\boldsymbol{B})}\text{ and every }t\geq 0.

Finally, for every
x\in\overline{\mathrm{D}(\boldsymbol{B})}
we have that

\boldsymbol{J}_{t/n}^{n}x\to\boldsymbol{S}_{t}x\quad\text{as }n\to+\infty

and for every
T\geq 0
there exist
N(\lambda,T)\in\mathbb{N}
,
C(\lambda,T)>0
(with
C(0,T)=2T
) such that

(A.9)

|\boldsymbol{J}_{t/n}^{n}x-\boldsymbol{S}_{t}x|\leq C(\lambda,T)\frac{|\boldsymbol{B}^{\circ}x|}{\sqrt{n}}\quad\text{ for every }0\leq t\leq T,\,n\geq N(\lambda,T),\,x\in\mathrm{D}(\boldsymbol{B}).

Proof.

The first assertion follows by extending by continuity the semigroup (whose existence follows by Theorem
A.5
) from
\mathrm{D}(\boldsymbol{B})
to the whole
\overline{\mathrm{D}(\boldsymbol{B})}
(see also
[
17
, Remark 3.2]
). The second assertion for
\lambda<0
follows immediately from
[
17
, Corollaries 4.3, 4.4]
applied to
-\boldsymbol{B}
. We only prove the second assertion in case
\lambda>0
following the same strategy of
[
17
, Corollaries 4.3, 4.4]
. We fix
x_{0}\in\mathrm{D}(\boldsymbol{B})
and we consider as in the proof of Theorem
A.5
the approximated problems

\dot{x}_{\tau}(t)-\boldsymbol{B}_{\tau}x_{\tau}(t)=0,\quad x_{\tau}(0)=x_{0},

where we are assuming from now on that
0<\tau<1/\lambda
.
By
[
17
, Theorem 1.7]
we have that

\displaystyle|x_{\tau}(t)-\boldsymbol{J}_{\tau}^{n}x_{0}|

\displaystyle\leq(1-\lambda\tau)^{-n}e^{\lambda t}|x_{0}-\boldsymbol{J}_{\tau}x_{0}|\left(\left(n-\frac{t}{\tau(1-\lambda\tau)}\right)^{2}+\frac{t}{\tau(1-\lambda\tau)}\right)^{1/2}

\displaystyle\leq|\boldsymbol{B}^{\circ}x_{0}|(1-\lambda\tau)^{-n-1}e^{\lambda t}\left(\left(\tau n-\frac{t}{1-\lambda\tau}\right)^{2}+\frac{t\tau}{1-\lambda\tau}\right)^{1/2},

where we have also used that
\boldsymbol{J}_{\tau}
is
(1-\lambda\tau)^{-1}
-Lipschitz continuous (see Theorem
A.2
(1)) and Theorem
A.3
(5). Using this inequality together with (
A.6
) with
\tau=t/n
we get that for every
T\geq 0
we can find an integer
N(\lambda,T)
and a positive constant
C(\lambda,T)
such that

|\boldsymbol{J}_{\tau}x_{0}-\boldsymbol{S}_{t}x_{0}|\leq C(\lambda,T)\frac{|\boldsymbol{B}^{\circ}x_{0}|}{\sqrt{n}}\quad\text{ for every }n\geq N(\lambda,T)\text{ and every }t\in[0,T].

This proves (
A.9
) and also the convergence of
\boldsymbol{J}_{t/n}^{n}x_{0}
to
\boldsymbol{S}_{t}x_{0}
, whenever
x_{0}\in\mathrm{D}(\boldsymbol{B})
. In case
y_{0}\in\overline{\mathrm{D}(\boldsymbol{B})}
and
x_{0}\in\mathrm{D}(\boldsymbol{B})
we can estimate

\displaystyle|\boldsymbol{J}_{t/n}^{n}y_{0}-\boldsymbol{S}_{t}y_{0}|

\displaystyle\leq|\boldsymbol{J}_{t/n}^{n}y_{0}-\boldsymbol{J}_{t/n}^{n}x_{0}|+|\boldsymbol{S}_{t}y_{0}-\boldsymbol{S}_{t}x_{0}|+|\boldsymbol{S}_{t}x_{0}-\boldsymbol{J}^{n}_{t/n}x_{0}|

\displaystyle\leq|x_{0}-y_{0}|\left((1-\lambda t/n)^{-n}+e^{\lambda t}\right)+|\boldsymbol{S}_{t}x_{0}-\boldsymbol{J}^{n}_{t/n}x_{0}|,

where we have used again Theorem
A.2
(1). Passing to the limit as
n\to+\infty
gives that

\limsup_{n\to+\infty}|\boldsymbol{J}_{t/n}^{n}y_{0}-\boldsymbol{S}_{t}y_{0}|\leq 2e^{\lambda t}|x_{0}-y_{0}|

ans passing to the
\inf
w.r.t.
x_{0}\in\mathrm{D}(\boldsymbol{B})
gives the sought convergence.
∎

The following result corresponds to
[
17
, Theorem 3.3]
and concerns the regularizing effect for the semigroup generated by maximal
\lambda
-dissipative operators whose domain has nonempty interior.

Theorem A.7
.

Let
{\boldsymbol{B}}
be a maximal
\lambda
-dissipative operator such that
\operatorname{int}\left(\mathrm{D}(\boldsymbol{B})\right)\neq\emptyset
and let
x_{0}\in\overline{\mathrm{D}(\boldsymbol{B})}
. Then the curve
x(t):=\boldsymbol{S}_{t}x_{0}
,
t\geq 0
(cf. Theorem
A.6
) has the following properties:

(1)

x
is locally absolutely continuous in
[0,+\infty)
and locally Lipschitz in
(0,+\infty)
;

(2)

x(t)\in\mathrm{D}(\boldsymbol{B})
for every
t>0
;

(3)

there exists a constant
C>0
(depending solely on
x_{0},\lambda
and
\boldsymbol{B}
) such that

(A.10)

I_{\lambda}(t)|\dot{x}(t)|\leq C\quad\text{ for a.e.\penalty\ $t\in(0,1)$},

where

(A.11)

I_{\lambda}(t):=\int_{0}^{t}\mathrm{e}^{\lambda(s-t)}\,\mathrm{d}s=\begin{cases}\frac{1-\mathrm{e}^{-\lambda t}}{\lambda}\quad&\text{ if }\lambda\neq 0,\\
t\quad&\text{ if }\lambda=0,\end{cases}\quad t\geq 0.

Proof.

The proof closely follows the one of
[
17
, Theorem 3.3]
and it is divided in several claims.

Claim 1.
For every
y\in\operatorname{int}\left(\mathrm{D}(\boldsymbol{B})\right)
there exist
\varrho,M>0
such that

\varrho|v|\leq\langle v,y-x\rangle+M(|x-y|+\varrho)+\lambda^{+}(|x-y|+\varrho)^{2}\quad\text{ for every }(x,v)\in\boldsymbol{B}.

Let
y\in\operatorname{int}\left(\mathrm{D}(\boldsymbol{B})\right)
and let
(x,v)\in\boldsymbol{B}
be fixed. By Theorem
A.3
(3), there exist
\varrho,M>0
such that, for every
z\in\mathcal{H}
with
|z|=1
and every
w\in{\boldsymbol{B}}(y-\varrho z)
, it holds
|w|\leq M
. Testing the
\lambda
-dissipativity of
{\boldsymbol{B}}
with
(x,v),(y-\varrho z,w)\in{\boldsymbol{B}}
, we get

\langle v-w,x-y+\varrho z\rangle\leq\lambda|x-y+\varrho z|^{2}

so that

\displaystyle\varrho\langle v,z\rangle

\displaystyle\leq\langle v,y-x\rangle+\lambda^{+}(|x-y|^{2}+2\varrho\langle x-y,z\rangle+\varrho^{2}|z|^{2})+M(|x-y|+\varrho|z|)

\displaystyle\leq\langle v,y-x\rangle+M(|x-y|+\varrho)+\lambda^{+}(|x-y|+\varrho)^{2}.

Passing to the supremum in
z\in\mathcal{H}
with
|z|=1
proves the claim.

We consider, as in the proof of Theorem
A.5
, the approximated problems

\dot{x}_{\tau}(t)-\boldsymbol{B}_{\tau}x_{\tau}(t)=0,\quad x_{\tau}(0)=x_{0},

where we are assuming from now on that
0<\tau<1/\lambda^{+}
.

Claim 2.
For every
T>0
, the curves
x_{\tau}
and
\boldsymbol{J}_{\tau}x_{\tau}
converge to
t\mapsto\boldsymbol{S}_{t}x_{0}
uniformly in
[0,T]
as
\tau\downarrow 0
.

Let us first show that
x_{\tau}
converges to
t\mapsto\boldsymbol{S}_{t}x_{0}
uniformly in
[0,T]
: let us denote by
(\boldsymbol{S}^{\tau}_{t})_{t\geq 0}
the semigroup associated by Theorem
A.6
to the maximal
\frac{\lambda}{1-\lambda\tau}
-dissipative operator
\boldsymbol{B}_{\tau}
(cf. Theorem
A.3
(5)), so that in particular
x_{\tau}(t)=\boldsymbol{S}^{\tau}_{t}x_{0}
for every
t\geq 0
.
For every
y_{0}\in\mathrm{D}(\boldsymbol{B})
and
t\in[0,T]
, we estimate

\displaystyle|x_{\tau}(t)-\boldsymbol{S}_{t}x_{0}|

\displaystyle\leq|\boldsymbol{S}^{\tau}_{t}x_{0}-\boldsymbol{S}^{\tau}_{t}y_{0}|+|\boldsymbol{S}^{\tau}_{t}y_{0}-\boldsymbol{S}_{t}y_{0}|+|\boldsymbol{S}_{t}y_{0}-\boldsymbol{S}_{t}x_{0}|

\displaystyle\leq e^{\frac{\lambda}{1-\lambda\tau}t}|x_{0}-y_{0}|+C(\lambda,t)|{\boldsymbol{B}}^{\circ}y_{0}|\sqrt{\tau}+e^{\lambda t}|x_{0}-y_{0}|

\displaystyle\leq\left(e^{\frac{\lambda^{+}}{1-\lambda\tau}T}+e^{\lambda^{+}T}\right)|x_{0}-y_{0}|+\sup_{t\in[0,T]}C(\lambda,t)|{\boldsymbol{B}}^{\circ}y_{0}|\sqrt{\tau},

where we have used (
A.8
) for
{\boldsymbol{B}}
and
{\boldsymbol{B}}_{\tau}
and (
A.6
).
Passing first to
\sup_{t\in[0,T]}
, then to the limit as
\tau\downarrow 0
and finally to the infimum w.r.t.
y_{0}\in\mathrm{D}(\boldsymbol{B})
, gives the sought uniform convergence of
x_{\tau}
to
t\mapsto\boldsymbol{S}_{t}x_{0}
in
[0,T]
. The argument for
\boldsymbol{J}_{\tau}x_{\tau}
is similar: for every
t\in[0,T]
and every
y_{0}\in\mathrm{D}(\boldsymbol{B})
we estimate

\displaystyle|\boldsymbol{J}_{\tau}x_{\tau}(t)-\boldsymbol{S}_{t}x_{0}|

\displaystyle\leq|\boldsymbol{J}_{\tau}x_{\tau}(t)-\boldsymbol{J}_{\tau}\boldsymbol{S}_{t}x_{0}|+|\boldsymbol{J}_{\tau}\boldsymbol{S}_{t}x_{0}-\boldsymbol{J}_{\tau}\boldsymbol{S}_{t}y_{0}|+|\boldsymbol{J}_{\tau}\boldsymbol{S}_{t}y_{0}-\boldsymbol{S}_{t}y_{0}|+|\boldsymbol{S}_{t}y_{0}-\boldsymbol{S}_{t}x_{0}|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{1-\lambda\tau}|x_{\tau}(t)-\boldsymbol{S}_{t}x_{0}|+\left(\frac{e^{\lambda t}}{1-\lambda\tau}+e^{\lambda t}\right)|x_{0}-y_{0}|+\tau|\boldsymbol{B}_{\tau}\boldsymbol{S}_{t}y_{0}|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{1-\lambda\tau}|x_{\tau}(t)-\boldsymbol{S}_{t}x_{0}|+\left(\frac{e^{\lambda t}}{1-\lambda\tau}+e^{\lambda t}\right)|x_{0}-y_{0}|+\frac{\tau e^{\lambda t}}{1-\lambda\tau}|{\boldsymbol{B}}^{\circ}y_{0}|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{1-\lambda\tau}\sup_{t\in[0,T]}|x_{\tau}(t)-\boldsymbol{S}_{t}x_{0}|+\left(\frac{e^{\lambda^{+}T}}{1-\lambda\tau}+e^{\lambda^{+}T}\right)|x_{0}-y_{0}|+\frac{\tau e^{\lambda^{+}T}}{1-\lambda\tau}|{\boldsymbol{B}}^{\circ}y_{0}|

where we have used the
(1-\lambda\tau)^{-1}
-Lipschitzianeity of
\boldsymbol{J}_{\tau}
coming from Theorem
A.2
(1), (
A.8
) for
\boldsymbol{B}
, the definition of
\boldsymbol{B}_{\tau}
, Theorem
A.3
(5) and Theorem
A.5
(4) applied to
\boldsymbol{B}
(notice that this is possible since
y_{0}\in\mathrm{D}(\boldsymbol{B})
). Passing first to
\sup_{t\in[0,T]}
, then to the limit as
\tau\downarrow 0
and finally to the infimum w.r.t.
y_{0}\in\mathrm{D}(\boldsymbol{B})
, concludes the proof of the claim.

Claim 3.
For every
T>0
there exists a constant
M>0
(not depending on
\tau
) such that
|\boldsymbol{B}_{\tau}x_{\tau}(T)|\leq M
for every
0<\tau<1/\lambda^{+}
.

We fix some
y\in\operatorname{int}\left(\mathrm{D}(\boldsymbol{B})\right)
and we apply Claim 1 to
(x,v):=(\boldsymbol{J}_{\tau}x_{\tau}(t),\boldsymbol{B}_{\tau}x_{\tau}(t))\in\boldsymbol{B}
, with
t\in[0,T]
and
0<\tau<1/\lambda^{+}
so that

\varrho\,|{\boldsymbol{B}}_{\tau}x_{\tau}(t)|\leq-\frac{1}{2}\frac{\,\mathrm{d}}{\,\mathrm{d}t}|x_{\tau}(t)-y|^{2}+M\varrho+M|\boldsymbol{J}_{\tau}x_{\tau}(t)-y|+\lambda^{+}\left(|\boldsymbol{J}_{\tau}x_{\tau}(t)-y|+\varrho\right)^{2}.

Integrating in
[0,T]
and using Theorem
A.5
(4) applied to
{\boldsymbol{B}}_{\tau}
, we get

\varrho\,|{\boldsymbol{B}}_{\tau}x_{\tau}(T)|\,I_{\frac{\lambda}{1-\lambda\tau}}(T)\leq\frac{1}{2}|x_{0}-y|^{2}+M\varrho T+\int_{0}^{T}\left[M|\boldsymbol{J}_{\tau}x_{\tau}(t)-y|+\lambda^{+}\left(|\boldsymbol{J}_{\tau}x_{\tau}(t)-y|+\varrho\right)^{2}\right]\,\mathrm{d}t.

By Claim 2, the right hand side of the previous inequality is uniformly bounded (w.r.t.
\tau\in(0,1/\lambda^{+})
) so that we conclude the proof of the claim.

Claim 4.
Proof of points (1), (2) and (3).

By Claim 3, we have that for every
t>0
, up to an unrelabeled subsequence,
{\boldsymbol{B}}_{\tau}x_{\tau}(t)\rightharpoonup v
for some
v\in\mathcal{H}
. By Claim 2, we have that
\boldsymbol{J}_{\tau}x_{\tau}(t)\to\boldsymbol{S}_{t}x_{0}
so that we deduce by Theorem
A.3
(1) that
\boldsymbol{S}_{t}x_{0}\in\mathrm{D}(\boldsymbol{B})
; this proves (2). We can then fix some
y\in\operatorname{int}\left(\mathrm{D}(\boldsymbol{B})\right)
and apply Claim 1 to
(x,v):=(x(t),\dot{x}_{+}(t))
,
t>0
, where
\dot{x}_{+}(t)
is the right derivative of
t\mapsto x(t)
at
t
. Indeed, since
\boldsymbol{S}_{t}x_{0}=\boldsymbol{S}_{t-\delta}(\boldsymbol{S}_{\delta}x_{0})
and
\boldsymbol{S}_{\delta}x_{0}\in\mathrm{D}(\boldsymbol{B})
for every
0<\delta<t
by (2), we can apply Theorem
A.5
(3) to get that
(x(t),\dot{x}_{+}(t))\in{\boldsymbol{B}}
. We then obtain

\varrho|\dot{x}_{+}(t)|\leq-\frac{1}{2}\frac{\,\mathrm{d}}{\,\mathrm{d}t}|x(t)-y|^{2}+M\varrho+M|x(t)-y|+\lambda^{+}(|x(t)-y|+\varrho)^{2}.

Integrating the above inequality in
[s,1]
for any
0<s<1
, we get

\displaystyle\varrho\int_{s}^{1}|\dot{x}_{+}(t)|\,\mathrm{d}t\leq\frac{1}{2}|x_{0}-y|^{2}+M\varrho+\int_{s}^{1}\left[M|x(t)-y|+\lambda^{+}(|x(t)-y|+\varrho)^{2}\right]\,\mathrm{d}t.

Thanks to (
A.8
) and Theorem
A.5
(4) we have that for every
t\in[s,1]
it holds

|x(t)-y|\leq e^{\lambda t}|x_{0}-y|+|\boldsymbol{S}_{t}y-y|\leq e^{\lambda^{+}}(|x_{0}-y|+|{\boldsymbol{B}}^{\circ}y|).

This proves that there exists some constant
C>0
(depending solely on
x_{0},\lambda,y,\varrho
and
M
) such that

\int_{s}^{1}|\dot{x}_{+}(t)|\,\mathrm{d}t\leq C\quad\text{ for every }s\in(0,1).

Being the constant independent on
s
, we conclude that
x
is absolutely continuous in
(0,1)
; using also Theorem
A.5
, this proves (1). To prove (3), it is enough to use the above estimate with Theorem
A.5
(3),(4).
∎

Proposition A.8
.

Let
{\boldsymbol{B}}
be a maximal
\lambda
-dissipative operator, let
\mathcal{Y}\subset\mathcal{H}
be a closed subspace and suppose that
\mathcal{Y}
is invariant for the resolvent of
{\boldsymbol{B}}
, i.e.
\boldsymbol{J}_{\tau}x\in\mathcal{Y}
for every
x\in\mathcal{Y}
. Then the operator
{\boldsymbol{B}}_{\mathcal{Y}}:={\boldsymbol{B}}\cap(\mathcal{Y}\times\mathcal{Y})
has the following properties:

(1)

{\boldsymbol{B}}_{\mathcal{Y}}
is maximal
\lambda
-dissipative in
\mathcal{Y}
;

(2)

the resolvent (resp. the semigroup) of
{\boldsymbol{B}}
coincides with the resolvent (resp. the semigroup) of
{\boldsymbol{B}}_{\mathcal{Y}}
when restricted to
\mathcal{Y}
.

(3)

\mathrm{D}({\boldsymbol{B}}_{\mathcal{Y}})=\mathrm{D}({\boldsymbol{B}})\cap\mathcal{Y}
;

(4)

\overline{\mathrm{D}({\boldsymbol{B}}_{\mathcal{Y}})}=\overline{\mathrm{D}({\boldsymbol{B}})}\cap\mathcal{Y}
;

(5)

({\boldsymbol{B}}_{\mathcal{Y}})^{\circ}x={\boldsymbol{B}}^{\circ}x
for every
x\in\mathrm{D}({\boldsymbol{B}}_{\mathcal{Y}})
.

Proof.

It is clear that the restriction of
\boldsymbol{J}_{\tau}
, the resolvent of
{\boldsymbol{B}}
, to
\mathcal{Y}
provides the resolvent operator for
{\boldsymbol{B}}_{\mathcal{Y}}
and it is a
(1-\lambda\tau)^{-1}
-Lipschitz map defined on the whole
\mathcal{Y}
: by Theorem
A.2
(1),
{\boldsymbol{B}}_{\mathcal{Y}}
is maximal
\lambda
-dissipative in
\mathcal{Y}
. This proves (i) and (ii), also using the exponential formula (cf. Theorem
A.6
). To prove (iii), it is enough to show the inclusion “
\supset
”: if
x\in\mathrm{D}({\boldsymbol{B}})\cap\mathcal{Y}
, then
(\boldsymbol{J}_{\tau}x-x)/\tau\in\mathcal{Y}
is bounded by Theorem
A.3
(5) and, by the same result together with (ii), it must be that
x\in\mathrm{D}({\boldsymbol{B}}_{\mathcal{Y}})
. The inclusion “
\subset
” in (iv) follows by (iii), while the inclusion “
\supset
” follows simply noticing that, if
x\in\overline{\mathrm{D}({\boldsymbol{B}})}\cap\mathcal{Y}
, then
\boldsymbol{J}_{\tau}x\to x
by Theorem
A.3
(4) and
\boldsymbol{J}_{\tau}x\in\mathrm{D}({\boldsymbol{B}})\cap\mathcal{Y}=\mathrm{D}({\boldsymbol{B}}_{\mathcal{Y}})
. Assertion (v) follows again by Theorem
A.3
(5).
∎

Corollary A.9
.

Let
{\boldsymbol{B}}
be a maximal
\lambda
-dissipative operator and suppose that
\mathcal{H}
has finite dimension. Then the conclusions of Theorem
A.7
hold.

Proof.

Up to a translation, we can assume that
0\in\mathrm{D}(\boldsymbol{B})
. Let
\mathcal{Y}
be the subspace generated by
\mathrm{D}(\boldsymbol{B})
. Since
\mathcal{H}
is finite dimensional, then
\mathcal{Y}
is closed. We can thus apply Proposition
A.8
and obtain that
{\boldsymbol{B}}_{\mathcal{Y}}:={\boldsymbol{B}}\cap(\mathcal{Y}\times\mathcal{Y})
is maximal
\lambda
-dissipative in
\mathcal{Y}
, has the same domain of
{\boldsymbol{B}}
and its semigroup coincides with the semigroup generated by
{\boldsymbol{B}}
. Since
\mathcal{H}
is finite dimensional, the relative interior of
\operatorname{co}(\mathrm{D}({\boldsymbol{B}}_{\mathcal{Y}}))
in
\mathcal{Y}
is nonempty and thus we conclude by Theorem
A.3
(3) that the relative interior of
\mathrm{D}({\boldsymbol{B}}_{\mathcal{Y}})
in
\mathcal{Y}
is nonempty, so that we can apply Theorem
A.7
to
{\boldsymbol{B}}_{\mathcal{Y}}
and obtain the conclusion of such theorem for the semigroup generated by
{\boldsymbol{B}}
.
∎

Corollary A.10
.

Let
\boldsymbol{B}_{1}
and
\boldsymbol{B}_{2}
be maximal
\lambda
-dissipative operators with
\overline{\mathrm{D}(\boldsymbol{B}_{1})}=\overline{\mathrm{D}(\boldsymbol{B}_{2})}
and let
\boldsymbol{S}^{1}_{t}
and
\boldsymbol{S}^{2}_{t}
be the semigroups of Lipschitz transformations associated to
\boldsymbol{B}_{1}
and
\boldsymbol{B}_{2}
respectively given by Theorem
A.6
. If for every
x\in\overline{\mathrm{D}(\boldsymbol{B}_{1})}=\overline{\mathrm{D}(\boldsymbol{B}_{2})}
there exists
\delta>0
such that
\boldsymbol{S}^{1}_{t}x=\boldsymbol{S}^{2}_{t}x
for every
0\leq t<\delta
, then
\boldsymbol{B}_{1}=\boldsymbol{B}_{2}
.

Proof.

This can be proven as in
[
17
, Theorem 4.1]
: let
x\in\mathrm{D}(\boldsymbol{B}_{1})
and let
y\in\mathrm{D}(\boldsymbol{B}_{2})
; by hypotesis, we can find some
\delta>0
such that
\boldsymbol{S}_{t}^{1}x=\boldsymbol{S}_{t}^{2}x
and
\boldsymbol{S}_{t}^{1}y=\boldsymbol{S}_{t}^{2}y
for every
0\leq t<\delta
. Thus, for every
0\leq t<\delta
, we have

\displaystyle\langle\frac{\boldsymbol{S}_{t}x-x}{t}-\frac{\boldsymbol{S}_{t}y-y}{t},x-y\rangle

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{t}|\boldsymbol{S}_{t}x-\boldsymbol{S}_{t}y||x-y|-\frac{1}{t}|x-y|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{e^{\lambda t}-1}{t}|x-y|^{2},

where we have used that
\boldsymbol{S}_{t}:=\boldsymbol{S}^{1}_{t}=\boldsymbol{S}^{2}_{t}
is
e^{\lambda t}
-Lipschitz by (
A.8
).
Passing to the limit as
t\downarrow 0
and using Theorem
A.5
(3), we get that

\langle\boldsymbol{B}_{1}^{\circ}x-\boldsymbol{B}_{2}^{\circ}y,x-y\rangle\leq\lambda|x-y|^{2}.

By (
A.2
) we get that
\mathrm{D}(\boldsymbol{B}_{1})=\mathrm{D}(\boldsymbol{B}_{2})
and thus that
\boldsymbol{B}_{1}^{\circ}=\boldsymbol{B}_{2}^{\circ}
. By (
A.3
) we thus get that
\boldsymbol{B}_{1}=\boldsymbol{B}_{2}
.
∎

The following proposition is a slight generalization of
[
3
, Lemma 2.3]
but we report its proof for the reader’s convenience.

Proposition A.11
.

Let
\boldsymbol{B}\subset\mathcal{H}\times\mathcal{H}
be maximal
\lambda
-dissipative and let
\boldsymbol{G}\subset\boldsymbol{B}
be s.t.
\mathrm{D}(\boldsymbol{G})
is dense in
\mathrm{D}(\boldsymbol{B})
. Then for every
x\in\operatorname{int}\left(\mathrm{D}(\boldsymbol{B})\right)
it holds

(A.12)

\boldsymbol{B}x=\overline{\operatorname{co}}\left(\left\{v\in\mathcal{H}\mid\exists(x_{n},v_{n})\in\boldsymbol{G}\text{ s.t. }x_{n}\to x,\,v_{n}\rightharpoonup v\right\}\right).

Proof.

Let
x\in\operatorname{int}\left(\mathrm{D}(\boldsymbol{B})\right)
and let us define

\boldsymbol{M}x:=\overline{\operatorname{co}}\left(\left\{v\in\mathcal{H}\mid\exists(x_{n},v_{n})\in\boldsymbol{G}\text{ s.t. }x_{n}\to x,\,v_{n}\rightharpoonup v\right\}\right).

If
(x_{n},v_{n})\in\boldsymbol{G}\subset\boldsymbol{B}
with
x_{n}\to x
and
v_{n}\rightharpoonup v
, by
\lambda
-dissipativity of
\boldsymbol{B}
, we have that

\langle v_{n}-w,x_{n}-y\rangle\leq\lambda|x_{n}-y|^{2}\quad\forall\,(y,w)\in\boldsymbol{B}.

Passing to the limit we get

\langle v-w,x-y\rangle\leq\lambda|x-y|^{2}\quad\forall\,(y,w)\in\boldsymbol{B},

so that
v\in\boldsymbol{B}x
by (
A.2
). This, together with the closure and convexity of
\boldsymbol{B}x
given by Theorem
A.3
(2), proves that
\boldsymbol{M}x\subset\boldsymbol{B}x
. Let us prove the other inclusion by contradiction: suppose that there is some
v\in\boldsymbol{B}x
s.t.
v\notin\boldsymbol{M}x
. The sets
\{v\}
and
\boldsymbol{M}x
are disjoint, closed, convex and
\{v\}
is also compact. By Hahn-Banach theorem we can find some
z\in\mathcal{H}
with
|z|=1
s.t.

(A.13)

\langle v,z\rangle>\langle u,z\rangle\quad\forall\,u\in\boldsymbol{M}x.

Since
x\in\operatorname{int}\left(\mathrm{D}(\boldsymbol{B})\right)
, if we define
z_{n}:=x+z/n
, we have that
z_{n}\in\operatorname{int}\left(\mathrm{D}(\boldsymbol{B})\right)
for
n
sufficiently large. We can thus find
x_{n}\in\mathrm{D}(\boldsymbol{G})
s.t.
|x_{n}-z_{n}|<n^{-2}
. Clearly
x_{n}\to x
and it is easy to check that
(x_{n}-x)/|x_{n}-x|\to z
. Since
x_{n}\in\mathrm{D}(\boldsymbol{G})
, we can find
v_{n}\in\boldsymbol{G}x_{n}
. Since
\boldsymbol{B}
is maximal, it is locally bounded (cf. Theorem
A.3
(3)) at
x
. Being
\boldsymbol{G}\subset\boldsymbol{B}
and being
x_{n}\to x
, the sequence
(v_{n})
is bounded so that, up to an unrelabeled subsequence, it converges weakly to some point
u\in\mathcal{H}
. By
\lambda
-dissipativity of
\boldsymbol{B}
we have

\langle v-v_{n},x-x_{n}\rangle\leq\lambda|x-x_{n}|^{2}\quad\forall\,n\in\mathbb{N},

so that, dividing by
|x_{n}-x|
and passing to the limit, we obtain

\langle v-u,z\rangle\leq 0,

a contradiction with (
A.13
) since, obviously,
u\in\boldsymbol{M}x
.
∎

The following proposition is an immediate consequence of
[
50
, Theorem 1]
and Remark
A.1
.

Proposition A.12
.

Let
\boldsymbol{B}\subset\mathcal{H}\times\mathcal{H}
be
\lambda
-dissipative with open non empty convex domain. Then there exists a unique maximal
\lambda
-disipative
\hat{\boldsymbol{B}}\supset\boldsymbol{B}
with
\mathrm{D}(\hat{\boldsymbol{B}})\subset\overline{\mathrm{D}(\boldsymbol{B})}
and it is characterized by

\hat{\boldsymbol{B}}=\left\{(x,v)\in\overline{\mathrm{D}(\boldsymbol{B})}\times\mathcal{H}\mid\langle v-w,x-y\rangle\leq\lambda|x-y|^{2}\quad\forall\,(y,w)\in\boldsymbol{B}\right\}.

As a consequence of Propositions
A.11
and
A.12
we can prove the following.

Theorem A.13
.

Let
\boldsymbol{B}\subset\mathcal{H}\times\mathcal{H}
be
\lambda
-dissipative with

C:=\overline{\mathrm{D}(\boldsymbol{B})}\text{ is convex},\quad\operatorname{int}\left(\mathrm{D}(\boldsymbol{B})\right)\neq\emptyset.

Then there exists a unique maximal
\lambda
-dissipative
\hat{\boldsymbol{B}}\supset\boldsymbol{B}
with
\mathrm{D}(\hat{\boldsymbol{B}})\subset C
and it is characterized by

(A.14)

\hat{\boldsymbol{B}}=\left\{(x,v)\in C\times\mathcal{H}\mid\langle v-w,x-y\rangle\leq\lambda|x-y|^{2}\quad\forall\,(y,w)\in\boldsymbol{B}\right\}.

Moreover, for every
x\in\operatorname{int}\left(\mathrm{D}(\hat{\boldsymbol{B}})\right)
it holds

(A.15)

\hat{\boldsymbol{B}}x=\overline{\operatorname{co}}\left(\left\{v\in\mathcal{H}\mid\exists(x_{n},v_{n})\in\boldsymbol{B}\text{ s.t. }x_{n}\to x,\,v_{n}\rightharpoonup v\right\}\right).

Finally

(A.16)

\operatorname{int}\left(C\right)=\operatorname{int}\left(\mathrm{D}(\hat{\boldsymbol{B}})\right)\subset\mathrm{D}(\hat{\boldsymbol{B}})\subset\overline{\mathrm{D}(\hat{\boldsymbol{B}})}=C.

Proof.

Let
\boldsymbol{B}^{\prime}
be a
\lambda
-dissipative maximal extension of
\boldsymbol{B}
with
\mathrm{D}(\boldsymbol{B}^{\prime})\subset C
, whose existence is granted by Theorem
A.2
(2); by
\lambda
-dissipativity of
\boldsymbol{B}^{\prime}
and since
\boldsymbol{B}\subset\boldsymbol{B}^{\prime}
, then
\boldsymbol{B}^{\prime}\subset\hat{\boldsymbol{B}}
, where
\hat{\boldsymbol{B}}
is defined as in (
A.14
). We need to prove the other inclusion.

Since
\mathrm{D}(\boldsymbol{B})\subset\mathrm{D}(\boldsymbol{B}^{\prime})\subset C
, we have that
\overline{\mathrm{D}(\boldsymbol{B}^{\prime})}=C
. Moreover, being
\boldsymbol{B}^{\prime}
maximal
\lambda
-dissipative and being the interior of its domain nonempty, we have by Theorem
A.3
(3) that

\operatorname{int}\left(\mathrm{D}(\boldsymbol{B}^{\prime})\right)\text{ is convex },\quad\operatorname{int}\left(\mathrm{D}(\boldsymbol{B}^{\prime})\right)=\operatorname{int}\left(\overline{\mathrm{D}(\boldsymbol{B}^{\prime})}\right)=\operatorname{int}\left(C\right).

It is then clear that
\boldsymbol{B}_{0}:=\boldsymbol{B}^{\prime}\cap(\operatorname{int}\left(\mathrm{D}(\boldsymbol{B}^{\prime})\right)\times\mathcal{H})
is
\lambda
-dissipative with open and nonempty convex domain so that, by Proposition
A.12
, there exists a unique maximal
\lambda
-dissipative
\boldsymbol{B}^{\prime\prime}\supset\boldsymbol{B}_{0}
with
\mathrm{D}(\boldsymbol{B}^{\prime\prime})\subset\overline{\mathrm{D}(\boldsymbol{B}_{0})}=\overline{\operatorname{int}\left(\mathrm{D}(\boldsymbol{B}^{\prime})\right)}=\overline{\operatorname{int}\left(C\right)}=C
(
C
is convex) and it is characterized by

(A.17)

\boldsymbol{B}^{\prime\prime}=\left\{(x,v)\in C\times\mathcal{H}\mid\langle v-w,x-y\rangle\leq\lambda|x-y|^{2}\quad\forall\,(y,w)\in\boldsymbol{B}_{0}\right\}.

Since
\boldsymbol{B}^{\prime}\supset\boldsymbol{B}_{0}
,
\boldsymbol{B}^{\prime}
is maximal
\lambda
-dissipative and
\mathrm{D}(\boldsymbol{B}^{\prime})\subset C
, it must be that
\boldsymbol{B}^{\prime}=\boldsymbol{B}^{\prime\prime}
.

By (
A.17
), we need to prove that

(A.18)

\hat{\boldsymbol{B}}\subset\left\{(x,v)\in C\times\mathcal{H}\mid\langle v-w,x-y\rangle\leq\lambda|x-y|^{2}\quad\forall\,(y,w)\in\boldsymbol{B}_{0}\right\}.

To this aim we apply Proposition
A.11
to the maximal
\lambda
-dissipative
\boldsymbol{B}^{\prime}
and its subset
\boldsymbol{B}
noticing that
\mathrm{D}(\boldsymbol{B})
is dense in
\mathrm{D}(\boldsymbol{B}^{\prime})
. In this way, we obtain that

(A.19)

\boldsymbol{B}_{0}y=\overline{\operatorname{co}}\left(\overline{\boldsymbol{B}}y\right),\quad y\in\mathrm{D}(\boldsymbol{B}_{0}),

where

\overline{\boldsymbol{B}}y=\left\{u\in\mathcal{H}\mid\exists(y_{n},u_{n})\in\boldsymbol{B}\text{ s.t. }y_{n}\to y,\,u_{n}\rightharpoonup u\right\}.

If
(x,v)\in\hat{\boldsymbol{B}}
and
(y,w)\in\mathrm{D}(\boldsymbol{B}_{0})\times\mathcal{H}
is such that
w\in\overline{\boldsymbol{B}}y
, we can find a sequence
(y_{n},u_{n})\in\boldsymbol{B}
s.t.
y_{n}\to y
and
u_{n}\rightharpoonup w
; then, by the very definition of
\hat{\boldsymbol{B}}
, we have

\langle v-u_{n},x-y_{n}\rangle\leq\lambda|x-y_{n}|^{2}\quad\forall\,n\in\mathbb{N},

so that, passing to the limit, we get

\langle v-w,x-y\rangle\leq\lambda|x-y|^{2}.

This proves that, if
(x,v)\in\hat{\boldsymbol{B}}
, then

(A.20)

\langle v-w,x-y\rangle\leq\lambda|x-y|^{2}\quad\forall\,w\in\overline{\boldsymbol{B}}y,\quad\forall\,y\in\mathrm{D}(\boldsymbol{B}_{0}).

Finally, if
(x,v)\in\hat{\boldsymbol{B}}
and
(y,w)\in\boldsymbol{B}_{0}
, we can find a sequence
(N_{n})_{n}\subset\mathbb{N}
, numbers
(\alpha_{i}^{n})_{i=1}^{N_{n}}\subset[0,1]
and points
(w_{i}^{n})_{i=1}^{N_{n}}\subset\overline{\boldsymbol{B}}y
s.t.

\sum_{i=1}^{N_{n}}\alpha_{i}^{n}=1\,\,\forall n\in\mathbb{N},\quad\lim_{n\to+\infty}\sum_{i=1}^{N_{n}}\alpha_{i}^{n}w_{i}^{n}=w.

By (
A.20
)

\langle v-w_{i}^{n},x-y\rangle\leq\lambda|x-y|^{2}\quad\forall i=1,\dots,N_{n},\quad\forall n\in\mathbb{N},

so that, multiplying by
\alpha_{i}^{n}
and summing up w.r.t.
i
we obtain

\langle v-\sum_{i=1}^{N_{n}}\alpha_{i}^{n}w_{i}^{n},x-y\rangle\leq\lambda|x-y|^{2}\quad\forall n\in\mathbb{N}.

Passing to the limit as
n\to+\infty
, we obtain

\langle v-w,x-y\rangle\leq\lambda|x-y|^{2},

so that (
A.18
) holds. Finally notice that (
A.15
) is already stated in (
A.19
) since we just proved that
\boldsymbol{B}^{\prime}=\boldsymbol{B}^{\prime\prime}=\hat{\boldsymbol{B}}
.
∎

As a consequence, we have the following corollary.

Corollary A.14
.

Let
\boldsymbol{B}\subset\mathcal{H}\times\mathcal{H}
be as in Theorem
A.13
and let
\boldsymbol{G}:\operatorname{int}\left(C\right)\to\mathcal{H}
be a single-valued selection of the maximal
\lambda
-dissipative extension
\hat{\boldsymbol{B}}
of
\boldsymbol{B}
. Then the unique maximal
\lambda
-dissipative extension of
\boldsymbol{G}
with domain included in
C
,
\hat{\boldsymbol{G}}
, coincides with
\hat{\boldsymbol{B}}
and in particular

(A.21)

(x,v)\in\hat{\boldsymbol{B}}\Leftrightarrow x\in C,\langle v-\boldsymbol{G}y,x-y\rangle\leq\lambda|x-y|^{2}\quad\forall y\in\operatorname{int}\left(C\right).

Let us consider a different situation when
we do not assume that
\mathrm{D}(\boldsymbol{B})

contains interior points
but there exists a subset
D
dense in
\mathrm{D}(\boldsymbol{B})

which is invariant with respect to the resolvent map
{\boldsymbol{J}_{\tau}}
, i.e.

(A.22)

\overline{D}\supset\mathrm{D}(\boldsymbol{B})\ \text{and}\quad\forall\,x\in D,\ 0<\tau<1/\lambda^{+}\quad\exists\,x_{\tau}\in D:\,\,x_{\tau}-\tau\boldsymbol{B}x_{\tau}\ni x.

Since
\boldsymbol{B}
is
\lambda
-dissipative, the point
x_{\tau}

solving the inclusion in
(
A.22
) is unique and defines a map
{\boldsymbol{J}_{\tau}}:D\to D\cap\mathrm{D}(\boldsymbol{B})
.

Lemma A.15
.

Let
\boldsymbol{B}\subset\mathcal{H}\times\mathcal{H}
be
\lambda
-dissipative
with
C:=\overline{D(\boldsymbol{B})}
convex, let us
assume that
D\subset\mathcal{H}
satistifies (
A.22
), and let us
set
\boldsymbol{B}_{0}:=\boldsymbol{B}\cap(D\times\mathcal{H})
. The following hold:

(1)

\boldsymbol{B}
admits a unique maximal
\lambda
-dissipative
extension
\hat{\boldsymbol{B}}
with
\mathrm{D}(\hat{\boldsymbol{B}})\subset C

characterized by

(A.23)

\displaystyle\hat{\boldsymbol{B}}=\Big\{

\displaystyle(x,v)\in C\times\mathcal{H}\mid\langle v-v_{0},x-x_{0}\rangle\leq\lambda|x-x_{0}|^{2}\text{ for every }(x_{0},v_{0})\in\boldsymbol{B}_{0}\Big\}.

(2)

If moreover the interior of
\overline{D}
contains
C
,
we have

(A.24)

\hat{\boldsymbol{B}}=\Big\{(x,v)\in\mathcal{H}\times\mathcal{H}:\exists\,(x_{n},v_{n})\in\boldsymbol{B}_{0}:x_{n}\to x,v_{n}\to v\quad\text{as }n\to\infty\Big\}.

Proof.

We first prove Claim (1).
Let
\boldsymbol{B}^{\prime}
be any maximal
\lambda
-dissipative extension of
\boldsymbol{B}
with domain
included in
C
(whose existence is granted by Theorem
A.2
(2)) and let
\boldsymbol{J}_{\tau}^{\prime}
be the resolvent
associated with
\boldsymbol{B}^{\prime}
. By
dissipativity of
\boldsymbol{B}^{\prime}
and since
\boldsymbol{B}_{0}\subset\boldsymbol{B}\subset\boldsymbol{B}^{\prime}
, we have that
\boldsymbol{B}^{\prime}\subset\hat{\boldsymbol{B}}
defined as in (
A.23
). We need to prove the other inclusion.

Clearly, the restriction of
\boldsymbol{J}_{\tau}^{\prime}
to

D

coincides with
{\boldsymbol{J}_{\tau}}
; since
\boldsymbol{J}_{\tau}^{\prime}

is Lipschitz and
D
is dense in
C
, it is the unique Lipschitz extension of

{\boldsymbol{J}_{\tau}}
to
\overline{D}\supset C
.

If
(x,v)\in\hat{\boldsymbol{B}}
, (
A.23
)
and the fact that for every
y\in D
,

\frac{1}{\tau}(\boldsymbol{J}_{\tau}y-y)\in\boldsymbol{B}\boldsymbol{J}_{\tau}y
yield by density that

(A.25)

\langle v-\tau^{-1}(\boldsymbol{J}_{\tau}^{\prime}y-y),x-\boldsymbol{J}_{\tau}^{\prime}y\rangle\leq\lambda|x-\boldsymbol{J}_{\tau}^{\prime}y|^{2}\quad\forall\,y\in\mathrm{D}(\boldsymbol{B}^{\prime}),\quad\forall\,0<\tau<1/\lambda^{+},

and passing to
the limit as
\tau\downarrow 0
we obtain that

(A.26)

\langle v-\boldsymbol{B}^{\prime\circ}y,x-y\rangle\leq\lambda|x-y|^{2}\quad\forall\,y\in\mathrm{D}(\boldsymbol{B}^{\prime}),

where we also used Theorem
A.3
(4), (5).
We can then apply (
A.3
) and conclude that
(x,v)\in\boldsymbol{B}^{\prime}
.

We prove Claim (2).
Since
\overline{\boldsymbol{B}_{0}}\subset\hat{\boldsymbol{B}}
, it is sufficient to prove the opposite inclusion

\hat{\boldsymbol{B}}\subset\overline{\boldsymbol{B}_{0}}
.
Let
(x,v)\in\hat{\boldsymbol{B}}
, let
0<\tau<1/\lambda^{+}
and set
y:=x-\tau v
.
Clearly
\boldsymbol{J}_{\tau}^{\prime}y=x
;
since
\overline{D}
contains a
neighborhood of every element of
\mathrm{D}(\hat{\boldsymbol{B}})\subset C
,
for sufficiently small
\tau>0

there exists a sequence
(y_{n})_{n}\subset D
converging to

y
as
n\to\infty
.
Setting
x_{n}:=\boldsymbol{J}^{\prime}_{\tau}y_{n}
and

v_{n}:=(x_{n}-y_{n})/\tau\in\boldsymbol{B}x_{n}
, we clearly have

\lim_{n\to\infty}x_{n}=x,

\lim_{n\to\infty}v_{n}=v
.
∎

Corollary A.16
.

Let
\boldsymbol{B}\subset\mathcal{H}\times\mathcal{H}
be
maximal
\lambda
-dissipative, let us
assume that
D\subset\mathcal{H}
satistifies (
A.22
) and the
interior of
\overline{D}
contains
C:=\overline{\mathrm{D}(\boldsymbol{B})}
. The following hold:

(1)

For every
x\in\mathrm{D}(\boldsymbol{B})
there exists a sequence

x_{n}\in D\cap\mathrm{D}(\boldsymbol{B})
converging to
x
such that

\boldsymbol{B}^{\circ}x_{n}\to\boldsymbol{B}^{\circ}x
as
n\to\infty
.

(2)

\boldsymbol{B}
can be determined by the restriction of the minimal
section
\boldsymbol{B}^{\circ}
to
D
i.e.

(A.27)

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{B}=\Big\{

\displaystyle(x,v)\in\overline{\mathrm{D}(\boldsymbol{B})}\times\mathcal{H}\mid\langle v-\boldsymbol{B}^{\circ}x_{0},x-x_{0}\rangle\leq\lambda|x-x_{0}|^{2}\text{ for every }x_{0}\in D\cap\mathrm{D}(\boldsymbol{B})\Big\}.

Proof.

We first prove Claim (1). Since
\boldsymbol{B}
is maximal
\lambda
-dissipative, the closure
of its domain
C
is convex (see Theorem
A.3
(4)).
We can thus apply the second claim of the previous Lemma

A.15

(in this case
\hat{\boldsymbol{B}}=\boldsymbol{B}
)
to find a sequence
(x_{n},v_{n})\in\boldsymbol{B}\cap(D\times\mathcal{H})

such that

x_{n}\to x
and
v_{n}\to\boldsymbol{B}^{\circ}x
.
Let us first prove that
\boldsymbol{B}^{\circ}x_{n}\rightharpoonup\boldsymbol{B}^{\circ}x
weakly in

\mathcal{H}
as
n\to\infty
: extracting an unrelabeled subsequence, since
|\boldsymbol{B}^{\circ}x_{n}|\leq|v_{n}|
is bounded,
we can suppose that there exists an increasing subsequence
k\mapsto n(k)

and an element
v\in\mathcal{H}
such that

\boldsymbol{B}^{\circ}x_{n(k)}\rightharpoonup v
as
k\to\infty
.
Since the graph of
\boldsymbol{B}
is strongly-weakly closed (cf. Theorem
A.3
(1)), we
deduce that
(x,v)\in\boldsymbol{B}
so that
|v|\geq|\boldsymbol{B}^{\circ}x|
.
On the other hand, the lower semicontinuity of the norm yields

|\boldsymbol{B}^{\circ}x|\leq|v|\leq\liminf_{k\to\infty}|\boldsymbol{B}^{\circ}x_{n(k)}|\leq\limsup_{k\to\infty}|\boldsymbol{B}^{\circ}x_{n(k)}|\leq\limsup_{k\to\infty}|v_{n(k)}|=|\boldsymbol{B}^{\circ}x|.

We deduce that
\boldsymbol{B}^{\circ}x_{n(k)}\rightharpoonup\boldsymbol{B}^{\circ}x

and
\lim_{k\to\infty}|\boldsymbol{B}^{\circ}x_{n(k)}|=|\boldsymbol{B}^{\circ}x|
so that the
convergence is also strong. Since the starting (unrelabeled) subsequence was arbitrary, we deduce the strong convergence of the whole sequence.

Claim (2) now follows easily by approximation using
the previous claim and Theorem
A.3
(6).
∎

Appendix B
Borel partitions and almost optimal couplings

In this appendix we summarize some of the results of
[
28
]
related to standard Borel spaces, Borel partitions and optimal couplings between probability measures that have been used throughout the whole paper. We refer to
[
28
, Section 3]
for the proofs.

Definition B.1
.

A
standard Borel space

(\Omega,{\mathcal{B}})
is a measurable space that is isomorphic (as a measurable space) to a Polish space. Equivalently, there exists a Polish topology
\tau
on
\Omega
such that the Borel sigma algebra generated by
\tau
coincides with
{\mathcal{B}}
. We say that a probability measure
\mathbb{P}
on
(\Omega,{\mathcal{B}})
is nonatomic if
\mathbb{P}(\{\omega\})=0
for every
\omega\in\Omega
(notice that
\{\omega\}\in{\mathcal{B}}
since it is compact in any Polish topology on
\Omega
).

If
(\Omega,{\mathcal{B}})
is a standard Borel space endowed with a nonatomic probability measure
\mathbb{P}
, we denote by
\mathrm{S}(\Omega,{\mathcal{B}},\mathbb{P})
the class of
{\mathcal{B}}
-
{\mathcal{B}}
-measurable maps
g:\Omega\to\Omega
which are essentially injective and measure-preserving, meaning that there exists a full
\mathbb{P}
-measure set
\Omega_{0}\in{\mathcal{B}}
such that
g
is injective on
\Omega_{0}
and
g_{\sharp}\mathbb{P}=\mathbb{P}
. If
\mathcal{A}\subset{\mathcal{B}}
is a sigma algebra on
\Omega
we denote by
\mathrm{S}(\Omega,{\mathcal{B}},\mathbb{P};\mathcal{A})
the subset of
\mathrm{S}(\Omega,{\mathcal{B}},\mathbb{P})
of
\mathcal{A}-\mathcal{A}
measurable maps.

We will often use the notation

I_{N}:=\{0,\dots,N-1\},\quad N\in\mathbb{N},\,N\geq 1

while
{\mathrm{Sym}(I_{N})}
denotes the set of permutations of
I_{N}

i.e. bijective maps
\sigma:I_{N}\to I_{N}
.
We will consider the partial order on
\mathbb{N}
given by

m\prec n\quad\Leftrightarrow\quad m\mid n

where
m\mid n
means that
n/m\in\mathbb{N}
. We write
m\mathrel{\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\prec$\cr\kern 4.30554pt\cr$\smash{\raisebox{2.79857pt}{\rotatebox{90.0}{\scalebox{1}[-1]{$\nshortmid$}}}}$\cr\kern-2.15277pt\cr}}}}n
if
m\prec n
and
m\neq n
.

This first result shows a correspondence between permutations and measure-preserving isomorphisms.

Lemma B.2
.

Let
(\Omega,{\mathcal{B}})
be a standard Borel space endowed with a nonatomic probability measure
\mathbb{P}
,
and let
\mathfrak{P}_{N}=\{\Omega_{N,k}\}_{k\in I_{N}}\subset{\mathcal{B}}
be a
N
-partition
of
(\Omega,{\mathcal{B}})
for some
N\in\mathbb{N}
, i.e.

\bigcup_{k\in I_{N}}\Omega_{N,k}=\Omega,\quad\Omega_{N,k}\cap\Omega_{N,h}=\emptyset\text{ if }h,k\in I_{N},\,h\neq k;

assume moreover that
\mathbb{P}(\Omega_{N,k})=\mathbb{P}(\Omega)/N
for every
k\in I_{N}
. If
\sigma\in{\mathrm{Sym}(I_{N})}
, there exists a measure-preserving isomorphism
g\in\mathrm{S}(\Omega,{\mathcal{B}},\mathbb{P};\sigma(\mathfrak{P}_{N}))
such that

(g_{k})_{\sharp}\mathbb{P}|_{\Omega_{N,k}}=\mathbb{P}|_{\Omega_{N,\sigma(k)}}\quad\forall k\in I_{N},

where
g_{k}
is the restriction of
g
to
\Omega_{N,k}
.

We introduce now the notion of
refined
standard Borel measure space which turns out to be useful when dealing with approximation of general measures with discrete ones.

Definition B.3
.

Let
(\Omega,{\mathcal{B}})
be a standard Borel space endowed with a nonatomic probability measure
\mathbb{P}
, and let
{\mathfrak{N}}\subset\mathbb{N}
be an unbounded directed set w.r.t.
\prec
. We say that a collection of partitions
(\mathfrak{P}_{N})_{N\in{\mathfrak{N}}}
of
\Omega
, with corresponding sigma algebras
{\mathcal{B}}_{N}:=\sigma(\mathfrak{P}_{N})
, is a
{\mathfrak{N}}
-segmentation of
(\Omega,{\mathcal{B}},\mathbb{P})
if

(1)

\mathfrak{P}_{N}=\{\Omega_{N,k}\}_{k\in I_{N}}
is a
N
-partition of
(\Omega,{\mathcal{B}})
for every
N\in{\mathfrak{N}}
,

(2)

\mathbb{P}(\Omega_{N,k})=\mathbb{P}(\Omega)/N
for every
k\in I_{N}
and every
N\in{\mathfrak{N}}
,

(3)

if
M\mid N=KM
then

\bigcup_{k=0}^{K-1}\Omega_{N,mK+k}=\Omega_{M,m}
,
m\in I_{M}
,

(4)

\sigma\left(\left\{{\mathcal{B}}_{N}\mid N\in{\mathfrak{N}}\right\}\right)={\mathcal{B}}
.

In this case we call
(\Omega,{\mathcal{B}},\mathbb{P},(\mathfrak{P}_{N})_{N\in{\mathfrak{N}}})
a
{\mathfrak{N}}
-refined standard Borel probability space
.

Proposition B.4
.

For any standard Borel space
(\Omega,{\mathcal{B}})
endowed with a nonatomic probability measure
\mathbb{P}
and any unbounded directed set
{\mathfrak{N}}\subset\mathbb{N}
w.r.t.
\prec
, there exists a
{\mathfrak{N}}
-segmentation of
(\Omega,{\mathcal{B}},\mathbb{P})
. If
{\mathfrak{N}}\subset\mathbb{N}
is an unbounded directed subset w.r.t.
\prec
, then there exists a totally ordered cofinal sequence
(b_{n})_{n}\subset{\mathfrak{N}}
satisfying

•

b_{n}\mathrel{\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\prec$\cr\kern 4.30554pt\cr$\smash{\raisebox{2.79857pt}{\rotatebox{90.0}{\scalebox{1}[-1]{$\nshortmid$}}}}$\cr\kern-2.15277pt\cr}}}}b_{n+1}
for every
n\in\mathbb{N}
,

•

for every
N\in\mathbb{N}
there exists
n\in\mathbb{N}
such that
N\mid b_{n}.

In particular,
for every
{\mathfrak{N}}
-refined standard Borel measure space
(\Omega,{\mathcal{B}},\mathfrak{m},(\mathfrak{P}_{N})_{N\in{\mathfrak{N}}})
it holds that

({\mathcal{B}}_{b_{n}})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
is a filtration on
(\Omega,{\mathcal{B}})
,

(B.1)

\text{for every $N\in{\mathfrak{N}}$ there exists $n\in\mathbb{N}$ such that }{\mathcal{B}}_{N}\subset{\mathcal{B}}_{b_{n}},

and

\sigma\left(\left\{{\mathcal{B}}_{b_{n}}\mid n\in\mathbb{N}\right\}\right)={\mathcal{B}}
.

For every every separable Hilbert space
\mathsf{X}
, we
thus have that

(B.2)

\bigcup_{N\in{\mathfrak{N}}}L^{2}(\Omega,{\mathcal{B}}_{N},\mathfrak{m};\mathsf{X})\text{ is dense in }L^{2}(\Omega,{\mathcal{B}},\mathfrak{m};\mathsf{X}).

The next theorem contains approximation results for couplings by means of maps in different situations.

Theorem B.5
.

Let
(\Omega,{\mathcal{B}},\mathbb{P},(\mathfrak{P}_{N})_{N\in{\mathfrak{N}}})
be a
{\mathfrak{N}}
-refined standard Borel probability space. Then:

(1)

For every
\boldsymbol{\gamma}\in\Gamma(\mathbb{P},\mathbb{P})
there exist a totally ordered strictly increasing sequence
(N_{n})_{n}\subset{\mathfrak{N}}
and maps
g_{n}\in\mathrm{S}(\Omega,{\mathcal{B}},\mathbb{P};{\mathcal{B}}_{N_{n}})
such that, for every separable Hilbert space
\mathsf{X}
and every
X,Y\in L^{2}(\Omega,{\mathcal{B}},\mathbb{P};\mathsf{X})
it holds

(B.3)

(X,Y)_{\sharp}(\boldsymbol{i}_{\Omega},g_{n})_{\sharp}\mathbb{P}\to(X\otimes Y)_{\sharp}\boldsymbol{\gamma}\text{ in }\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{X}^{2}).

(2)

If
\mathsf{X}
is a separable Hilbert space and
X,X^{\prime}\in L^{2}(\Omega,{\mathcal{B}},\mathbb{P};\mathsf{X})
, then for every
\boldsymbol{\mu}\in\Gamma(X_{\sharp}\mathbb{P},X^{\prime}_{\sharp}\mathbb{P})
there exist a totally ordered strictly increasing sequence
(N_{n})_{n}\subset{\mathfrak{N}}
and maps
g_{n}\in\mathrm{S}(\Omega,{\mathcal{B}},\mathbb{P};{\mathcal{B}}_{N_{n}})
such that

(B.4)

(X,X^{\prime}\circ g_{n})_{\sharp}\mathbb{P}\to\boldsymbol{\mu}\text{ in }\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{X}^{2}).

In particular, if
X_{\sharp}\mathbb{P}=X^{\prime}_{\sharp}\mathbb{P}
, there exist a totally ordered strictly increasing sequence
(N_{n})_{n}\subset{\mathfrak{N}}
and maps
g_{n}\in\mathrm{S}(\Omega,{\mathcal{B}},\mathbb{P};{\mathcal{B}}_{N_{n}})
such that

X^{\prime}\circ g_{n}\to X
in
L^{2}(\Omega,{\mathcal{B}},\mathbb{P};\mathsf{X})
as
n\to\infty
.

Finally, if
(\Omega,{\mathcal{B}})
is a standard Borel space endowed with a nonatomic probability measure
\mathbb{P}
,
\mathsf{X}
is a separable Hilbert space,
\mu,\nu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathsf{X})
and
X\in L^{2}(\Omega,{\mathcal{B}},\mathbb{P};\mathsf{X})
is s.t.
X_{\sharp}\mathbb{P}=\mu
, then, for every
\varepsilon>0
, there exists
Y\in L^{2}(\Omega,{\mathcal{B}},\mathbb{P};\mathsf{X})
s.t.
Y_{\sharp}\mathbb{P}=\nu
and

|X-Y|_{L^{2}(\Omega,{\mathcal{B}},\mathbb{P};\mathsf{X})}\leq W_{2}(\mu,\nu)+\varepsilon.
